Determinization of Min-Plus Weighted Automata is Decidable
Shaull Almagor, Guy Arbel, Sarai Sheinvald

Abstract
We show that the determinization problem for min-plus (tropical) weighted automata is decidable, thus resolving this long-standing open problem. In doing so, we develop a new toolbox for analyzing and reasoning about the run-structure of nondeterministic automata.



1
Introduction

Traditional automata accept or reject their input, and are therefore Boolean, in the sense that their language is a function
L:\Sigma^{*}\to\{0,1\}
.

Weighted Automata

[
16
,
31
,
7
,
1
]
(WFA, for short) extend Boolean automata to richer domains, so that the language of a WFA
\mathcal{A}
is a function
L_{\mathcal{A}}:\Sigma^{*}\to D
for some numeric domain
D
.
This is achieved by assigning
weights
from
D
to the transitions of the automaton, and defining two operations: one which aggregates weights along a run, and another that combines the weights along all runs to a value in
D
.

A natural class of domains over which WFAs are defined are
semirings
: algebraic structures with two operations,
\otimes
and
\oplus
, which naturally lend themselves as the two operations above. For a nondeterministic automaton over a semiring, the value of a run is the semiring product
\otimes
of the weights along the edges, and the value of a word is the semiring sum
\oplus
of the values of its runs.

Common examples of such semirings are the
(\min,+)
semiring
\langle\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\},\min,+\rangle
(also known as the
tropical
semiring, and sometimes considered as the dual
(\max,+)
semiring), and the rational field
\langle\mathbb{Z},+,\times\rangle
(and its
probabilistic
sub-semiring).
Applications of such weighted automata fall on a wide spectrum including verification, rewriting systems, tropical algebra, and speech and image processing (in the pre-LLM era) (we refer the reader to
[
11
,
16
,
1
]
and references therein).
Famously, tropical weighted automata have also been key to proving the star-height conjecture
[
22
,
18
,
19
,
27
]
.

For example, consider the tropical WFA in
Fig.

2
. For every word
w\in\{a,b\}^{*}
there are two runs: one cycles in
q_{a}
and counts the number of
a
’s, and the other cycles in
q_{b}
and counts the number of
b
’s. Thus, the weight of
w
is the minimum between the number of
a
’s and the number of
b
’s.

As with most computational models, reasoning about WFAs becomes easier on the deterministic fragment (e.g., equivalence of tropical WFAs is undecidable for nondeterminstic automata, but decidable for deterministic ones
[
26
,
1
]
).
Unfortunately, unlike Boolean automata, nondeterministic WFAs are strictly more expressive than their deterministic fragment (for most semirings).
Accordingly, a natural problem for WFAs is the
determinization problem
: given a WFA
\mathcal{A}
, is there a deterministic WFA
\mathcal{D}
such that
L_{\mathcal{A}}\equiv L_{\mathcal{D}}
. We focus on tropical WFAs.

The determinization problem was first raised for tropical WFAs in the 1990’s by Mohri
[
29
,
30
]
(and was alluded to already by Choffrut in 1977
[
9
]
, but not explicitly).
Despite much attention, the decidability of this problem remained open. Moreover, our ability to analyze the run structure of WFAs has made notoriously slow progress, despite seeing several exciting ideas over the years (e.g., the celebrated Forest-Factorization Theorem by Simon
[
32
]
).
Specifically, Mohri’s original work proposed a determinization algorithm for unambiguous WFAs
[
30
]
. This was later improved to finitely-ambiguous WFAs
[
25
]
, and much later to polynomially-ambiguous WFAs
[
24
]
. Crucially, all these works place strong restrictions on the nondeterminism, making their analysis possible.
Since then, the problem remained “wide open”
[
11
,
17
,
23
,
28
]
.

We remark that many recent works shed light on various intricacies of the run structure of tropical WFAs, e.g.,
[
14
,
10
,
12
,
13
,
4
,
8
,
3
]
.

Contribution and Paper organization

The centerpiece of our contribution is a proof that the determinization problem for tropical WFAs is decidable, thus resolving this longstanding open problem.
In obtaining this result, however, we develop several powerful new tools for reasoning about the run structure of WFAs, which are of independent interest.

The full detailed proof starts at
Section

8
, and is dauntingly long. This is in part due to our attempt to make it detailed and accessible.
In
Section

2
we define the model, and show a characterization of determinizability that is our starting point. In
Section

3
we briefly describe the tools and concepts we develop to approach the problem. In
Section

4.3
we present the outline of the main proof, but leave two significant holes in it. These holes are patched in
Sections

5
and
6
, where we explain our novel technique for analyzing runs of a WFA.
Throughout, we stick to intuition and leave the technicalities to the full proof.

It is worth noting our algorithm has no complexity bounds. Indeed, it is composed of two semi-algorithms, and relies on purely existential arguments arising from Ramsey-style reasoning. We believe, however, that our approach will pave the way to also tightening the complexity (we present a naive PSPACE-hard lower bound).

Related Work

Aside from the works mentioned above, we remark that WFAs are strongly tied with algebraic/logic tools such as Rational Series
[
16
,
15
]
and in particular a standard approach to reasoning about them is algebraic, e.g., with Simon’s factorization
[
32
]
. In contrast, our work stays entirely within the combinatorial approach. We posit that this combinatorial view is what allows us to “untangle” the various runs, and is what fails in previous approaches.

We should also mention that over the
(+,\times)
semiring, the technical setting changes dramatically – tools from real algebra arise, making the analysis entirely different. For
(+,\times)
semirings, determinization was raised as open in 2006 by
[
28
]
and resolved very recently
[
5
,
20
]
with clever usage of heavy linear-algebra machinery.

2
WFAs, Determinizability and Gaps

WFAs

A WFA
\mathcal{A}=\langle Q,\Sigma,Q_{0},\Delta\rangle
is a finite automaton where
Q
is a set of states,
\Sigma
is an alphabet,
Q_{0}\subseteq Q
are initial states,
and
\Delta\subseteq Q\times\Sigma\times\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}\times Q
is a
weighted
transition relation, assigning every
p,q,\sigma
a weight in
\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}=\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
. Intuitively, weight
\infty
means that there is no transition.

A
run
of
\mathcal{A}
a word
w
is a sequence of transitions reading
w
, and its
weight
is the sum of the weights along its transitions. We denote a run
\rho
from state
q
to state
p
reading
w
by
\rho:q\xrightarrow{w}p
, and its weight by
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)
.
The
weight of a word

w
, is the minimal weight of a run on
w
(where weight
\infty
signifies having no run on
w
). More generally, we denote the minimal weight of a run on
w
from state
q
to
p
by
\mathsf{mwt}(w,q\xrightarrow{w}p)
, and the minimal weight of a run between sets of states
Q^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}
by
\mathsf{mwt}(w,Q^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w}P^{\prime})
. An example of a WFA is depicted in
Fig.

2

A WFA is
deterministic
if it has a single initial state, and from every state there exists at most one finite-weight transition on each letter. It is
determinizable
if it has an equivalent deterministic WFA (i.e., they describe the same function).
It is well-known that not every WFA is determinizable
[
7
]
. In this paper, we show that determining whether a WFA is determinizable is decidable.

Figure 1
:
A tropical WFA that assigns to
w\in\{a,b\}^{*}
the minimum between the number of
a
’s and
b
’s in
w
.

Figure 2
:
A tropical WFA
\mathcal{A}
. All the states are initial.
Fig.

3
depicts a run DAG of
\mathcal{A}
.

Figure 3
:
After reading
bab^{2}ab^{3}ab^{4}ab^{5}a
, we arrive at the configuration
(0,0,5)
, as depicted. We then show how reading
b^{6}a
increases the weight of
r
by
1
. Note that this cannot be done with any shorter word.

We remark that there are several variants of WFAs: with or without initial and final weights, and with or without distinguished accepting states.
We show that the determinizability problems for these variations are are inter-reducible (see
Remark

8.1
). Thus,
our definition is of the simplest variant: no initial and final weights, and all states are accepting.

Characterizing Determinizability by Gaps

Intuitively, a WFA is determinizable if we can deterministically track its current
configuration
– a vector assigning to each state the minimal value it is reachable with (or
\infty
if it is not reachable). That is, we need to update the current configuration upon reading a new letter (while incurring some weight). Then, the question is whether the number of states needed for this is finite.

A naive approach would be to track the minimal weight to each state. For example, in the WFA of
Fig.

2
, given the word
aab
we can track the vectors
(0,0)\xrightarrow{a}(1,0)\xrightarrow{a}(2,0)\xrightarrow{b}(2,1)
(where the two components represent the minimal weight to
q_{a}
and to
q_{b}
, respectively).
Clearly, however, this requires an infinite state space. Also, it does not use the weights for each letter.
A simple optimization is therefore to always normalize the vectors such that the minimal state is
0
, and incur the normalization as weight. This makes sense, since we want to track the minimal runs.
Thus, we would track
aab
as

(0,0)\xrightarrow{a,0}(1,0)\xrightarrow{a,0}(2,0)\xrightarrow{b,1}(1,0)
, where in the last step we intuitively reach
(2,1)
, but “shift” all weights by
-1
, and therefore gain
1
weight along the run.

However, this still clearly yields an infinite state space. The question is now whether we can make this finite. Intuitively, the state space can be made finite if there is some bound
B
, such that states in the configuration that go above
B
in this normalized construction, i.e., go more than
B
above the minimal run, cannot later become a minimal run, and can therefore be treated as “unreachable”.

As it turns out, this is a precise characterization of determinization (it appears in
[
17
]
, and is folklore).
It is the starting point of our investigation, and we therefore make it explicit: for a bound
B\in\mathbb{N}
, a
B
-gap witness
is a pair of words
x,y\in\Sigma^{*}
and a state
q\in Q
such that upon reading
x
, we have
\mathsf{mwt}(x,Q_{0}\xrightarrow{x}q)>\mathsf{mwt}(x,Q_{0}\xrightarrow{x}Q)+B
, but
\mathsf{mwt}(xy,Q_{0}\xrightarrow{xy}Q)=\mathsf{mwt}(xy,Q_{0}\xrightarrow{x}q\xrightarrow{y}Q)
(see
Fig.

6
).
We then have the following.

Theorem 2.1
.

\mathcal{A}
is
non
determinizable if and only if for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a
B
-gap witness.

Determinizability is in RE (recognizable): for a WFA
\mathcal{A}
and a deterministic WFA
\mathcal{D}
, it is decidable whether they are equivalent
[
1
]
, so we can simply try all deterministic automata. Therefore, all that remains is to show that the existence of infinitely many
B
-gap witnesses, for increasing
B
, is also recognizable. This is the goal of our work.

At first glance, this problem seems easy: if there is a
B
-gap witness for very large
B
, surely we can apply some pumping argument to further increase this gap. This certainly holds for
Fig.

2
.
Unfortunately, things are far more involved, as runs can “undercut” one another.

Example 1
.

Consider the WFA in
Fig.

2
. Intuitively, upon reading
b^{k}
, state
p
gains weight
k
. Upon reading
a
, state
r
gains
1
, but the weight in
p
is “reset” to
0
due to the run from
q
, and the run in
r
is reset to the minimal run reaching
p
. In particular, reading
aa
resets all states to
0
.
The letter
c
“kills” all runs except that of
r
.

Note that while reading
b^{k}
for large
k
brings
p
very high, it is not a gap-witness, since we cannot make its run minimal.
In
Fig.

3
we illustrate that more complicated gap-witnesses, namely words of the form
(b^{n}a)_{n=1}^{\infty}
, can create large gaps, and form gap-witnesses with the suffix
c
, as they separate
r
from the other states. Therefore, this WFA is nondeterminizable.

We remark that
Fig.

2
can be used as a component in more elaborate constructions to require even more complicated gap-witnesses.

3
Constructions and Definitions

Our proof can be split into two significant elements: the definition of a well-behaved structure that allows a form of pumping, and a powerful set of techniques to find such well-behaved structures in a given WFA.
In this section we present the former well-behaved structures, and begin to present some of the challenges that arise from them.

3.1
The Baseline Augmented Construction (
Section

9
)

Recall from
Section

2
the idea of tracking configurations and shifting so that the minimal run is always
0
. Our first construction is technically simple, but turns out to be a very versatile tool: instead of normalizing to the minimal run, we allow normalizing to
any
run, referred to as the
baseline
run, and the choice of run is given as part of the word. We also keep track of the reachable set of states, as in the Boolean subset construction. We refer to this as the
Baseline-Augmented Subset Construction
, denoted
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.

More precisely, the states of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
(denoted
S
) are of the form
(q,p,T)\in Q\times Q\times 2^{Q}=S
, where
q
is the “usual” current state,
p
is the state in the baseline run, and
T
is the reachable set of states. The alphabet of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, denoted
\Gamma
, is the
transitions
of
\mathcal{A}
. Then, from state
s=(q,p,T)
, reading the transition (i.e., letter)
\gamma=(p,\sigma,c,p^{\prime})
, we have a transition to every
s^{\prime}=(q^{\prime},p^{\prime},T^{\prime})
where
(q,\sigma,d,q^{\prime})\in\Delta
, and
T^{\prime}
is updated deterministically.
Note that the letter
\sigma
(within
\gamma
) determines the options for the first component
q
, so that the first component simply tracks the “standard” runs of
\mathcal{A}
, while the second component
p
is determined by
\gamma
.
Together, we have a transition
(s,\gamma,d-c,s^{\prime})
in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, where the
weight
d-c
describes the weight
d
of the corresponding transition in
\mathcal{A}
, but “shifted” by the baseline weight
c
.

Intuitively, for a word
x\in\Sigma^{*}
, we can choose any run
\rho
of
\mathcal{A}
on
x
as baseline, and then
\rho
sets the “perspective” with which we consider the runs of
\mathcal{A}
on
x
. Crucially, these different perspectives do not affect the
gaps
between the runs.
Indeed, we prove (
Lemma

9.8
) that
\mathcal{A}
is determinizable if and only if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is, and from now on we proceed to work with
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.

3.2
Cactus Letters (
Sections

10
and
11
)

A key difficulty in reasoning about weighted runs is their behavior in complex paths (c.f.,
Example

1
). Our most fundamental tools to handle this are
stable cycles
and
cactus letters
. Intuitively, cactus letters extend the alphabet by capturing nested cycles that can be “easily pumped”. Unfortunately, their introduction leads to an
infinite alphabet
, which becomes a central challenge later on.

A
stable cycle
(
Section

10.1
) is a pair
(S^{\prime},w)
where
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
is a subset of the states of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and
w\in\Gamma^{*}
is a word, such that reading
w
from the states in
S^{\prime}
leads back to
S^{\prime}
(and therefore
w
can be repeatedly read, i.e., pumped), and where the baseline run on
w
is a minimal-weight cycle (with weight
0
). In particular, a stable cycle contains no negative cycles
1
1

1

The actual definition is somewhat more restrictive (
Definition

10.5
)
.

For example, in
Fig.

6
we illustrate a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},u)
. The baseline run that cycles on
r
gains weight
0
. The run from
q
to
p
gains negative weight, which is fine since it is not a cycle. The cycle from
t
to
t
, must be non-negative.

Figure 4
:
A
B
-gap witness.

Figure 5
:
A stable cycle. The baseline
r
is highlighted.

Figure 6
:
Potential and Charge, with baseline
r
.

For every stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
, we identify pairs of states
p,q\in S
called
grounded
(
Definition

10.7
) such that reading
w
a large number of times from
p
to
q
passes through a minimal cycle (with weight
0
), and so gains only bounded weight.
We introduce a new
cactus letter

\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
that has a transition only between grounded states of
(S^{\prime},w)
, and represents a long repetition of
w
’s. The choice of weight for these transitions is delicate, and is omitted here (see
Definition

11.1
).
Intuitively, reading
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
between grounded states represents pumping
w
while remaining bounded (due to the
0
-weight cycle), while for pairs that are not grounded, pumping
w
grows unboundedly (since all other cycles in
S^{\prime}
have positive weights).

Notice that we now have an infinite alphabet (since the words
w
can be arbitrarily long). We now want to allow nesting of cactus letters, describing increasingly complex runs, and so we repeat the process using the new alphabet, and do this inductively ad-infinitum. Thus, a word
w
in a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
may itself contain cactus letters. We end up with a WFA
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
over an infinite alphabet
\Gamma_{\infty}
, whose letters represent unboundedly “deep” nested-cactus letters.
We call a sequence of nested cactus letters a
chain
.

Fig.

7
depicts the formation of cactus letters and chains.

Figure 7
:
Cactus letters and cactus chains:
\alpha_{1}
is of depth
1
. The word
u_{1}\alpha_{1}v_{1}
(left) forms a cactus letter
\alpha_{2}
of depth
2
(center). Then,
u_{2}\alpha_{2}v_{2}
forms a cactus letter
\alpha_{3}
of depth
3
(right). Notice that
u_{2}
contains the cactus letter
\alpha_{4}
. The sequence
\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}
is a cactus chain, and so is
\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}
. This also illustrates why they are called “cactus letters”.

The combination of the baseline construction with cactus letters yields a powerful and robust tool for reasoning about the behavior of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
. Intuitively, it allows abstracting away parts of words as “pumpable”, as well as shifting the perspective by replacing baselines. Concretely, in
Section

12
we present three important tools:

A bound on chain depth

Cactus chains can be arbitrarily deep. In our effort to make the alphabet finite, our first important insight is that if cactus chains become too deep, then they must contain
degenerate
cactus letters. These are letters where, intuitively, all pairs are grounded. Degenerate letters can be pumped “for free”, in a sense that is made precise later.
We remark that the proof of this result is nontrivial and involves interesting analysis of the strongly connected components of graphs that arise from the grounded pairs of the cactus letters on each level of the chain (
Section

12.1.2
).

Baseline shifts

As mentioned in
Section

3.1
, by changing the baseline run we can shift the “perspective” on runs. In
Section

12.2
we present a formal framework to do so, which also applies to cactus letters. However, cactus letters do not have a natural perspective shift. Thus, we introduce a new type of letters there, called
rebase
letters, and another infinite hierarchy on top of the cactus hierarchy, which complicates things. We ignore such problems here, and leave them to the full proof.

Cactus unfolding

A crucial usage of cactus letters is going back and forth between the different levels of abstraction: we can
unfold
a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
to a word
w^{n}
in a way that captures all the “important” features of runs. This, however, needs to be done carefully, and depends not only on the cactus letter, but on its surrounding prefix and suffix, e.g.,
x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y
.
The framework in
Section

12.3
captures this precisely. We remark that unfolding a cactus may change the reachable set of states, since abstracting away runs inevitably eliminates some of the original runs. This nuance, referred to as
ghost states
(
\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}
) is considerably troublesome throughout the paper (see
Section

11.4
).

One result of this toolbox is that while
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}_{\infty}
are not equivalent (indeed, their alphabet is different), we can show that they are in fact equi-determinizable (
Corollary

12.26
), so in particular it suffices to reason about the nondeterminizability of
{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}_{\infty}
.

3.3
Potential and Charge (
Section

13
)

Recall that our goal is to reason about gap-witnesses. We now present two notions that take us a step closer. In a nutshell, the
potential (
\upphi
)
measures how far above the baseline run we can find “relevant” states, and the
charge (
\uppsi
)
captures the weight of the current minimal run.

We say that a state
q\in S
is
dominant
in configuration
\boldsymbol{c}
, if there exists a word
u
that has a finite-weight run from
q
, while all states below
q
in
\boldsymbol{c}
only have infinite-weight runs on
u
(i.e., do not have runs).
Intuitively,
q
is dominant in
\boldsymbol{c}
if there is a suffix that “kills” all the states below it.
We say that
q
is
maximal dominant
if
q
has the maximal weight in
\boldsymbol{c}
among all dominant states.
The
potential

\upphi(w)
of a word
w
is the weight of a maximal dominant state in the configuration that
w
reaches.
Note that this weight represents the gap between the dominant state and the (
0
-weight)
baseline
run (as opposed to the gap from the minimal state).

We define the
charge

\uppsi(w)
of
w
to be the minimal weight in the configuration that
w
reaches (negated, for technical reasons). Thus,
\uppsi(w)
reflects the distance between the minimal run and the baseline run.
In
Fig.

6
we illustrate these concepts. The run from
r
is baseline (notice that the baseline run might be above the potential). The weight of
q
determines the potential, since
u
sends all runs below it to
\infty
. The weight of
p
is minimal, and determines the charge.

A crucial property of the potential is that
\upphi(w\gamma)
cannot be much higher than
\upphi(w)
for a single letter
\gamma
. We refer to this property as
bounded growth
(
Lemma

13.3
). However, this property only holds when the alphabet is finite, which is no longer the case.
We return to this later on.

Intuitively, the “interesting” value we wish to reason about is not the gap of a dominant state from the baseline (namely
\upphi
), but the gap from the minimal run (to find a gap-witness), hence the definition of
\uppsi
.
The charge also has a “bounded growth” property, but it is of less interest. Importantly, the charge may
decrease
abruptly, if the minimal run cannot continue and a much higher run becomes minimal.
Such behavior is very problematic for our proof (
Definition

13.5
). This is dealt with later.

For the proof, we need both the potential and the charge to “play nice” with the tools we develop earlier (namely baseline shifts and cactus unfolding). Developing such tools is a significant part of the work (see
Section

13.2
).

The technical reason for defining potential and charge is to break down the overall problem into two distinct settings. This becomes clearer as we explain the proof structure below. We mention that this is an important separation, without which the proof becomes unmanageable.

3.4
A Witness for Nondeterminizability (
Section

14
)

We are now ready to present our witness for nondeterminizability. Intuitively, the witness is
a single word over
\Gamma_{\infty}

that (finitely) represents an infinite set of
B
-gap witnesses for unbounded values of
B
, and hence implies nondeterminizability (i.e., it is
sound
,
Lemma

14.2
).
Our main technical contribution in this work is to show that this characterization is
complete
, i.e., that if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
is nondeterminizable, then it has such a witness.

A
witness
(
Definition

14.1
) is a word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{*}
such that
w_{1}
and
w_{3}
are some prefix and suffix, and
w_{2}
is a “pumpable infix”. More precisely,
w_{2}
can be replaced by a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{2}}
such that
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
has finite weight in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
, whereas
w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{2}}w_{3}
does not.

The existence of a witness suggests that replacing
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{2}}
with
w_{2}^{k}
for some large
k
will cause the non-grounded pairs to induce runs with unboundedly large weight. In addition, there cannot be any grounded pairs on
w_{2}
, as otherwise
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{2}}
has finite weight-transitions on them so
w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{2}}w_{3}
would attain finite weight (this follows the fact that the states reachable by
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{2}}
are a subset of those reachable by
w_{2}
, which is not strictly true, but is made precise and correct in the detailed proof).

Thus,
w_{1}w_{2}^{k}w_{3}
is accepted only via non-grounded pairs. But the baseline run on
w_{2}
remains low (at
0
), which means that after
w_{1}w_{2}^{k}
, there is a huge gap, after which
w_{3}
causes the higher runs to continue with finite weight, and kills the baseline and lower runs. More precisely, these “higher states” are exactly the ghost-reachable states mentioned above: states that are not reachable via
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{2}}
, but are reachable via
w_{2}^{k}
with weight increasing as a function of
k
.
Therefore, a witness induces a family of
B
-gap witnesses for unbounded
B
.
In
Lemma

14.2
we give the precise details.

Once the definition of witness is set, we show an important result (
Lemma

14.4
): for a word
u\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}v
, if we replace
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
with
w^{k}
for any large-enough
k
, then either
\upphi(u\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}v)=\upphi(uw^{k}v)
, or there is a witness in
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}
.
The importance of this lemma is twofold. First, it (almost) shows that the potential “plays nice” with cactus unfolding, which is missing from the toolbox in
Section

13.2
, and second, it opens a line of results of the form “either something good happens, or there is a witness”.
The latter is of importance because many failed attempts at this problem were due to too many cases where things “go wrong”. Our techniques handle multiple such cases, but instead of things going wrong – we end up witnesses (implying nondeterminizability).

4
Nondeterminizable WFAs Have a Witness (
Section

20
)

We can now present the main proof structure.
In the following, we present the proof outline, emphasizing the novel ideas. There are, however, several major results that we use without proof now, and discuss them after the proof in
Sections

5
and
6
. Note that the detailed proof has the opposite order.
We remark that most details are left out of this sketch, but are referenced.

Consider a nondeterminizable WFA
\mathcal{A}
. We reason about
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
(as per their equi-determinizability) and show that it has a witness. This is done in two parts: first we prove in
Section

4.1
that
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in\Gamma^{*}\}=\infty
. That is, the potential over words in
\Gamma
(without cactus letters) is unbounded in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
. Next, we prove in
Section

4.2
that if the potential is unbounded, then
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
has a witness.

4.1
If
\mathcal{A}
is Nondeterminizable, Then the Potential is Unbounded

Since
\mathcal{A}
is nondeterminizable, then so is
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, and it therefore has
B
-gap witnesses for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, we have a sequence of words
\{x_{n}y_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that the minimal run on
x_{n}y_{n}
is at least
n
above the minimal run after reading
x_{n}
(where
n
serves as the increasing bound
B
).
Our first step is to change the baseline to a minimal run
\rho
on
x_{n}y_{n}
. This means that
\rho
now has weight
0
, so the minimal run on
x_{n}
becomes very negative (
Fig.

30
).
Observe that upon reading
y_{n}
, the charge
\uppsi
decreases significantly (since the minimal run gets closer to the baseline). This is a useful property which we call
discharging
, and is explained in
Section

6
. The implication of discharging is almost what we want: either the potential is unbounded, or there are words with infixes in which there are almost no decreasing runs (dubbed
D
-dip
,
Definition

16.13
). If the potential is unbounded, we are done.

A major step in the proof (
Section

16.2
) is to show that the
D
-dip property implies the existence of a special structure called an
increasing infix
, which we discuss in
Section

5
. When such an infix is found, it can be turned into a cactus letter, in a process we refer to as
budding
(
Section

17
). Moreover, the budding process lowers the
cost
– a concept we now discuss.

The
cost
of a word extends the notion of weight, by assigning a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
a cost much higher than that of
w
. In general, the cost upper-bounds the weight of any run on the word (in absolute value), not just the minimal one.
The property of increasing infixes is that they can be turned into cactus letters while reducing the cost.

We now go back to the
y_{n}
suffixes, which satisfy that
\uppsi(x_{n})\gg\uppsi(x_{n}y_{n})
. A crux of the proof is to replace each
y_{n}
with a word
y^{\prime}_{n}\in\Gamma_{\infty}
(i.e., with cacti) that satisfies these charge constraints, but also has
minimal cost
. Then, if we get a
D
-dip, we can find an increasing infix, and generate a new cactus. This reduces the cost of
y^{\prime}_{n}
, leading to a contradiction to its minimality.
Unfortunately, changing to
y^{\prime}_{n}
means that the alphabet is now infinite. This induces more complications, which we omit here.

We therefore conclude that the discharging sequence
y_{n}
cannot have
D
-dips, so we have unbounded potential. We remark that the last part relies heavily on our results on bounded cactus chains, and in particular we show that we only need to unfold a bounded number of “layers” in cactus letters before reaching a finite alphabet simultaneously on all the
y^{\prime}_{n}
suffixes.

4.2
If the Potential is Unbounded, then
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
Has a Witness

The second part resembles the structure of the first, and we therefore describe it even more briefly. Note, however, that the technical details of this part are actually more complicated than the first part above.

Since the potential is unbounded, we have a set of words
\{u_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
over
\Gamma
satisfying
\upphi(u_{n})\geq n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We again replace them by words
\{u^{\prime}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
over
\Gamma_{\infty}
with minimal cost. This type of sequence is referred to as
\upphi
-discharging
(due to the analogy with the first case).
Similar techniques to the first case (again, the devil is in the details), allow us to conclude that there is either a witness or a
D
-dip. In the former case we are done, and in the latter we reach a contradiction to the minimal cost.

4.3
Determinizability of WFA is Decidable

The decidability of determinizability now follows: we already mention above that determinizability is in RE. Thus, given a WFA
\mathcal{A}
, we can recognize whether it is
non
determinizable by enumerating all tuples
(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})
over
\Gamma_{\infty}
, and checking whether any of them form a witness (
Theorem

20.5
). By our proof above, if
\mathcal{A}
(and therefore
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
) is indeed nondeterminizable, then there is a witness.

5
Increasing Infix and Cactus Budding (
Sections

16
and
17
)

A separated increasing infix is a word
uxyv
with a highly-organized structure (
Fig.

8
). Intuitively, after reading
u
, the states fall into “sub-configurations”
V_{1},\ldots,V_{k}
, and this partition is kept after reading
x
and reading
y
. Moreover, each sub-configuration increases by a fixed amount, or stays
0
. The three main properties of this structure are:

1.

y
has much higher cost than
x
. In fact, higher than the cost of the cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
.

2.

The gaps between the sub-configurations are huge. We can pump
x
numerous times without the sub-configurations interfering with one another.

3.

The behavior on
x
and on
y
is similar enough so we can replace
y
with
x^{k}
for some
k
.

Intuitively, Conditions 2 and 3 allow us to pump
x
enough times so that it effectively becomes
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
and also “subsumes” the behavior of
y
(this intuition is made precise in the detailed proof).
Then, we can replace
uxyv
with
u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v
, while maintaining similar behavior.
Due to the huge cost of
y
(Condition 1), this reduces the overall cost, and “buds” a cactus, leading to the contradiction in the main proof.
This “similar behavior” mainly concerns the preservation of potential and charge, and is the focus of
Section

17
.

Figure 8
:
A separated increasing infix. The runs in
V_{1}
are all increased by
k_{1,x}
upon reading
x
, and are all increased by
k_{1,y}
upon reading
y
. Similarly for
V_{3}
with
k_{3,x}
and
k_{3,y}
. All the states in
V_{2}
(the baseline run among them) remain in the same height upon reading both
x
and
y
. The gaps between the sets is so large that we can pump
x
enough times without e.g., any state in
V_{1}
getting close to weight
0
.

The most involved part is showing that such infixes exist (under the
D
-dip assumption, which we only name here without a proper definition).
The first tool we develop to show their existence is a
Ramsey-style theorem
for infinite colored sequences (
Section

15
). Notably, this is one part where our proof is purely existential. Therefore, in order to obtain complexity bounds (via our methods), this needs to be turned to a quantitative argument.
The second tool we develop (
Sections

16.2.1
and
16.2
) is a technique for “untangling” runs, by considering infinite sequences of words that get longer and longer, and then “zooming-in” on their infixes, so that the infixes also get longer, but also get simpler. We elaborate on this technique in
Section

6
.

The bottom line of this part is that in the presence of
D
-dip we can find an increasing infix
uxyv
and safely replace it with the lower-cost
u\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}v
.

6
Discharging and Leveled Sequences (
Sections

18.2
and
19.2
)

One of our main conceptual contributions is a technique for “untangling” multiple runs, to find organized structures. We use this technique in several places (
Sections

18.2.1
,
18.2.2
,
19.2.1
and
19.2.2
), and we turn to briefly outline it, and then discuss its implications.

6.1
The Zooming Technique (Fair Decompositions)

Our goal is to find, under certain assumptions, an arbitrarily long infix
y
and a word
xyz
, whose runs have the following properties: upon reading
y
, there is a small set of runs that do not “interfere” with each other (dubbed
independent runs
,
Definition

18.1
), and all other runs are within some small interval from one of these runs (dubbed
G
-cover
,
Definition

18.4
). Moreover, the independent runs are extremely far apart, therefore allowing some pumping without risk of tangling runs.
These properties are not dissimilar to those of increasing infixes, but the actual definitions have important differences, so we separate them also here.

On top of these requirements, we have an additional property we wish
y
to satisfy, and this changes by instance. Specifically, the requirements are either that
\uppsi
or
\upphi
remain within a small “band” (
Leveled
or
\upphi
-Leveled
) or that
\uppsi
decreases /
\upphi
increases (
Discharging
/
\upphi
-Discharging
).

The assumption under which we find such a structure is that we start with an infinite sequence of (unboundedly long) words satisfying only the desired additional property, but not necessarily inducing independent runs.
Then, the key idea is to split an infix of each word into a number of parts, such that each part grows in length, the number of parts also grows, and the same general property can be found recursively in some inner part.
This can be thought of as splitting
n
into roughly
\sqrt{n}
parts of size
\sqrt{n}
(but is more involved, since we have other requirements from these parts). We refer to this as a
fair decomposition
(e.g.,
Definition

18.7
). Due to lack of space, illustrations of this are in the main proof (
Figs.

29
and
28
).

The main idea now is that if the independent runs do not form a
G
-cover, then there is a run that “drifts” far from other runs. We can then add it as an independent run and proceed inductively.
Naturally, all phases in this construction have careful details to consider (in particular the ghost runs mentioned above are a challenge).

6.2
From Decompositions to Unbounded Potential and Witness

The last key component of our proof (and the one most blatantly missing from the sketch in
Section

4.3
) is how to use this leveled / discharging structure to either obtain unbounded potential over
\Gamma
, or a witness. This is where all our definitions come together (
Lemmas

18.8
and
19.2
).
We illustrate the idea by showing how we obtain unbounded potential given a fair-decomposition where an infix has decreasing charge. Again, we gloss over most of the details.

Consider a word
xy\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{*}
where
y
is very long, and upon reading
y
there is a baseline run (of weight
0
) and the minimal run increases a lot, from below the baseline (i.e.,
\uppsi
decreases). Our goal is to show that
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in\Gamma^{*}\}=\infty
. This is achieved in five steps, as illustrated in
Fig.

9
.

Step 1: Flatten
x
.
Our first step is to
flatten
the prefix
x
, i.e., replace all its (nested) cactus letters by letters in
\Gamma
. This relies on our tools in
Section

12.3
. Crucially, after flattening we maintain most of the properties we start with. Note that this does not explicitly appear in
Fig.

9(a)
, since the alphabet is not depicted.

Step 2: Baseline shift.
Next, we take the run on
y
that decreases the most (the upper green run in
Fig.

9(a)
), and we change our perspective to make it the baseline (
Section

12.2
). Now, all the runs on the
y
suffix are non-decreasing (intuitively, they are non-decreasing with respect to the run we took), as in
Fig.

9(b)
.

Step 3: Replace
y
with
\alpha_{S^{\prime},y}
.
Since all the runs on
y
are non decreasing, then
y
is already close to being a stable cycle (
Section

10.1
). We show that it indeed is, and replace
y
with the cactus
\alpha_{S^{\prime},y}
for some
S^{\prime}
. This causes all the runs between non-grounded pairs to “jump to
\infty
”. In particular, since the minimal run was increasing, we show that it is between non-grounded pairs, and hence jumps to
\infty
. Moreover, we start with a
G
-cover, so all the runs near this minimal run also jump to
\infty
, and the next-lowest run (that could possibly not jump to
\infty
) has a huge gap above this sub-configuration.

Step 4: Baseline shift to minimal run.
We now almost have unbounded potential: some run above the minimal run stays bounded (namely the baseline run, or a lower run), but the minimal run increases unboundedly (as it is non-grounded). To conform with the definition of
\upphi
, we need the increase to be above the baseline run. We therefore shift the baseline again, this time starting from the minimal run on
x
. We remark that this step requires the introduction of some additional constructs (
jump letters

Section

11.3
).

(a)

Step 1

(b)

Step 2

(c)

Step 3

(d)

Step 4

Figure 9
:
The steps of
Section

6.2
(
Lemma

18.8
). The dashed lines are runs, the solid line is baseline.

By applying this technique to arbitrarily long
y
, and since
x
was flattened to be over
\Gamma
, we obtain our unbounded potential (
Fig.

9(d)
), with
\alpha_{S^{\prime},y}
(roughly) being the suffix that kills lower runs.
This completes the holes left in the main proof in
Section

4.3
, in very high level.

7
Discussion

The methods developed in this work represent a new type of reasoning for weighted automata: on top of standard (and even nested) “cycle analysis”, we introduce the concept of baseline shifts, allowing versatile manipulations on runs (e.g., making all runs non-negative by shifting on the most decreasing one). We combine this with the novel cactus letters (and their budding framework), which allow both abstracting away behaviors as well as unfolding to get back those behaviors. This results in a powerful toolbox for reasoning about the runs of a WFA.

Our Zooming technique is extensively used in this work, and we believe that it is generally useful for quantitative models. Indeed, it allows us to “group” runs together in a way that enables pumping. The latter is a central challenge in many proofs using quantitative models.

Overall, we expect the methods and techniques introduced in this work to have far-reaching applications, beyond resolving the long-standing open problem of WFA determinizability. For example, the seminal work proving that boundedness of WFAs is decidable
[
18
,
27
]
is completely subsumed by our framework (however, note that (1) we do not provide complexity bounds and (2) it can also be obtained by Simon’s Forest Factorization
[
32
]
).

Finally, now that the decidability of this problem is resolved, the race to establish its complexity can begin (with the current lower bound being PSPACE-hard as per
Appendix

D
).
An upper bound using our methods would require a quantitative Ramsey argument, as a first fundamental step (but would not suffice, as there are other existential steps).

In the next sections we present the complete work, with rigorous and detailed proofs (which account for the length of this paper). We start with a short reading guide.

8
Preliminaries

A Reading Guide

Due to the daunting length and complexity of our proof, we take several measures to assist the reader:

•

The current section is denoted in the header.

•

Proof environments are colored in
purple hue, to distinguish them from other text
.

•

We mark some results and definitions with

if they are key ideas, and with

if their proof has special insights. We recommend focusing on

statements, and on

proofs, for a first read.

•

Most of our definitions use english terms rather than symbols, to assist in remembering their meaning. Very few (and widely used) ones are left as symbols.

•

Lastly, we note that on most PDF readers, the keyboard shortcut “Alt + Left Arrow” or “Cmd + Left Arrow” goes back after following a hyperlink (e.g., a reference to a definition).

For an alphabet
\Sigma
, we denote by
\Sigma^{*}
(resp.
\Sigma^{+}
) the set of finite words (resp. non-empty finite words) over
\Sigma
.
For a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we denote its length by
|w|
and the set of its prefixes by
\mathsf{pref}(w)
. We write
w[i,j]=\sigma_{i}\cdots\sigma_{j}
for the infix of
w
corresponding to
1<i<j\leq|w|
.

We denote by
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}
the sets
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
and
\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
, respectively. We extend the addition and
\min
operations to
\infty
in the natural way:
a+\infty=\infty
and
\min\{a,\infty\}=a
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}
. By
\arg\min\{f(x)\mid x\in A\}
we mean the set of elements in
A
for which
f(x)
is minimal for some function
f
and set
A
.

Weighted Automata

A
(\min,+)
Weighted Finite Automaton
(WFA for short) is a tuple
\mathcal{A}=\langle Q,\Sigma,q_{0},\Delta\rangle
with the following components:

•

Q
is a finite set of
states
.

•

q_{0}\in Q
is the
initial
state.
2
2

2

Having a set of initial states does not add expressiveness, as it can be replaced by a single initial state that simulates the first transition from the entire set.

•

\Sigma
is an
alphabet
. We remark that we often use an
infinite alphabet

\Sigma
, hence we do not require it to be finite.

•

\Delta\subseteq Q\times\Sigma\times\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}\times Q
is a transition relation such that for every
p,q\in Q
and
\sigma\in\Sigma
there exists exactly
3
3

3

This is without loss of generality: if there are two transitions with different weights, the higher weight can always be ignored in the
(\min,+)
semantics. Missing transitions can be introduced with weight
\infty
.
one weight
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}
such that
(p,\sigma,c,q)\in\Delta
.

If for every
p\in Q
and
\sigma\in\Sigma
there exists at most one transition
(p,\sigma,c,q)
with
c\neq\infty
, then
\mathcal{A}
is called
deterministic
.

Remark 8.1

(On initial and final weights, and accepting states)
.

Weighted automata are often defined with initial and final weights, i.e.,

q_{0}
is replaced with an initial vector
\texttt{init}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}^{Q}
(and in particular may have several initial states with finite weight), and there are designated accepting states or a final weight vector
\texttt{fin}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}^{Q}
.
Then, the weight of a run also includes the initial weight and final weight (which may be
\infty
).

In
Appendix

B
we show that the determinization problem for this general model can be reduced to that of our setting. Therefore, it is sufficient to consider our model, without initial and final weights, and with a single initial state.
We remark that while replacing
init
and
fin
with initial and accepting states is straightforward, making
all
states accepting is nontrivial.

Runs

A
run
of
\mathcal{A}
is a sequence of transitions
\rho=t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{m}
where
t_{i}=(p_{i},\sigma_{i},c_{i},q_{i})
such that
q_{i}=p_{i+1}
for all
1\leq i<m
and
c_{i}<\infty
for all
1\leq i\leq m
.
We say that
\rho
is a run
on the word
w=\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{m}
from
p_{1}
to
q_{m}
, and we denote
\rho:p_{1}\xrightarrow{w}q_{m}
.
For an infix
x=w[i,j]
we denote the corresponding infix of
\rho
by
\rho[i,j]=t_{i},\ldots,t_{j}
(and sometimes by
\rho(x)
, if this clarifies the indices).
The
weight
of the run
\rho
is
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}
.

For a word
w
, the weight assigned by
\mathcal{A}
to
w
, denoted
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(w)
is the minimal weight of a run of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
. For convenience, we introduce some auxiliary notations.

For a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
and sets of states
Q_{1},Q_{2}\subseteq Q
, denote

\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(w,Q_{1}\to Q_{2})=\min\{\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\mid\exists q_{1}\in Q_{1},q_{2}\in Q_{2},\ \rho:q_{1}\xrightarrow{w}q_{2}\}

If
Q_{1}
or
Q_{2}
are singletons, we denote them by a single state (e.g.,
\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(w,P\to q)
for some set
P\subseteq Q
and state
q
).
We can now define

\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(w)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(w,q_{0}\to Q)

If there are no runs on
w
, then
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(w)=\infty
.
The function
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}:\Sigma^{*}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}
can be seen as the weighted analogue of the
language
of an automaton.
We omit the subscript
\mathcal{A}
when clear from context.

For a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
and a state
q\in Q
, of particular interest are runs from
q_{0}
to
q
on
w
that remain minimal throughout. Indeed, other runs are essentially “cut short” by some lower run. Formally, let
w=\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{n}
and consider a run
\rho:q_{0}\xrightarrow{w}q
with
\rho=t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{n}
and
t_{i}=(q_{i},\sigma_{i},e_{i},q_{i+1})
. We say that
\rho
is
seamless
if for every
1\leq j\leq n
it holds that
\mathsf{wt}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{j})=\mathsf{mwt}(\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{j},q_{0}\to q_{j+1})
.

We define
{\mathsf{max\_wt}(w)}
to be the maximal weight (in absolute value) occurring in any possible transition on the letters of
w
, i.e.,

{\mathsf{max\_wt}(w)}=\max\{|c|<\infty\mid\exists p,q\in Q,1\leq i\leq n,\ (p,\sigma_{i},c,q)\in\Delta\}

Additionally, the
maximal effect
of
w
is an upper bound on the change in weight that can happen upon reading
w
, defined as
{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{max\_wt}(\sigma_{i})}
.
Thus, any finite-weight run
\rho
on
w
satisfies
|\mathsf{wt}(\rho)|\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w)}\leq{\mathsf{max\_wt}(w)}|w|
.

We write
p\xrightarrow{w}q
when there exists some run
\rho
such that
\rho:p\xrightarrow{w}q
.
We lift this notation to concatenations of runs, e.g.,
\rho:p\xrightarrow{x}q\xrightarrow{y}r
means that
\rho
is a run on
xy
from
p
to
r
that reaches
q
after the prefix
x
. We also incorporate this to
\mathsf{mwt}
by writing e.g.,
\mathsf{mwt}(xy,q\xrightarrow{x}p\xrightarrow{y}r)
to mean the minimal weight of a run
\rho:q\xrightarrow{x}p\xrightarrow{y}r
.

For a set of states
Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q
and a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we define the
reachable set of states from
Q^{\prime}
upon reading
w
as
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(Q^{\prime},w)=\{q\in Q\mid\exists p\in Q^{\prime},\ p\xrightarrow{w}q\}
(where
\mathbb{B}
stands for “
\mathbb{B}
oolean”).

A WFA is
trim
if every state is reachable from
q_{0}
by some run. Note that states that do not satisfy this can be found in polynomial time (by simple graph search), and can be removed from the WFA without changing the weight of any accepted word. Throughout this paper, we assume that all WFAs are trim.

Determinizability

We say that WFAs
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are
equivalent
if
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}\equiv\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{B}}
. We say that
\mathcal{A}
is
determinizable
if it is equivalent to some deterministic WFA. Our central object of study is the following problem.

Problem 8.2

(WFA Determinizability)
.

Given a WFA
\mathcal{A}
over a finite alphabet
\Sigma
, decide whether
\mathcal{A}
is determinizable.

The determinization problem is PSPACE-hard. This does not seem to be published anywhere, and we therefore provide a proof in
Appendix

D
(we recommend reading this proof only after
Theorem

8.4
, as it relies on it).

Configurations

A
configuration
of
\mathcal{A}
is a vector
\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}^{Q}
which, intuitively, describes for each
q\in Q
the weight
\boldsymbol{c}(q)
of a minimal run to
q
thus far (assuming some partial word has already been read).
For a state
q
we define the configuration
\boldsymbol{c_{q}}
that assigns
0
to
q
and
\infty
to
Q\setminus\{q_{0}\}
.
Intuitively, before reading a word,
\mathcal{A}
is in the configuration
\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}}=\boldsymbol{c_{q_{0}}}
.

We adapt our notations to include a given starting configuration
\boldsymbol{c}
, as follows.
Given a configuration
\boldsymbol{c}
and a word
w
, they induce a new configuration
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}
by assigning each state the minimal weight with which it is reachable via
w
from
\boldsymbol{c}
. We denote this by

\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{c})(q)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(w,Q\to q)
for every
q\in Q
.
In particular,
\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{c}_{\texttt{init}})
is the configuration that
\mathcal{A}
reaches by reading
w
along a minimal run.

We use the natural component-wise partial order on configurations. That is, for two configuration
\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{d}
, we say that
\boldsymbol{d}
is
superior
to
\boldsymbol{c}
, denoted
\boldsymbol{c}\leq\boldsymbol{d}
, if
\boldsymbol{c}(q)\leq\boldsymbol{d}(q)
for every
q\in Q
. We also denote the
support
of a configuration by
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})=\{q\in Q\mid\boldsymbol{c}(q)<\infty\}
.

For a run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{w}q
and configuration
\boldsymbol{c}
, we define the
weight of
\rho
from
\boldsymbol{c}
to be
\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(\rho)=\boldsymbol{c}(p)+\mathsf{wt}(\rho)
. We similarly extend the notation
\mathsf{mwt}
to include configurations, by writing

\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(w,Q_{1}\to Q_{2})=\min\{\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(\rho)\mid\exists q_{1}\in Q_{1},q_{2}\in Q_{2},\ \rho:q_{1}\xrightarrow{w}q_{2}\}

(we remark that we never need both the subscript
\mathcal{A}
and
\boldsymbol{c}
together).
We say that
\rho
is
seamless from
\boldsymbol{c}
if for every
1\leq j\leq n
it holds that
\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{j})=\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{j},T\to q_{j+1})
where
T=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})
.

8.1
A Characterization of Determinizability

The following theorem gives an equivalent characterization of determinizability by means of runs, and of how far two potentially-minimal runs can get away from one another, which we commonly refer to as a
gap
.
Intuitively, we show that
\mathcal{A}
is determinizable if and only if there is some bound
B\in\mathbb{N}
such that if two runs on a word
x
obtain weights that are far from each other, but the “upper” run can still become minimal over some suffix,
then the runs are at most
B
apart. This is depicted in
Fig.

6
.

We remark that this theorem is folklore, but to our knowledge has not been stated explicitly anywhere. Moreover, since we often treat infinite alphabets, we need to tweak the statement accordingly.
A similar characterization (with finite alphabet) was given for
discounted-sum automata

[
2
]
.

Definition 8.3

(
B
-Gap Witness)
.

For
B\in\mathbb{N}
, a
B
-gap witness over alphabet
\Sigma^{\prime}
consists of a pair of words
x,y\in\Sigma^{\prime*}
and a state
q\in Q
such that

•

\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x\cdot y,q_{0}\to Q)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x\cdot y,q_{0}\xrightarrow{x}q\xrightarrow{y}Q)<\infty
, but

•

\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,q_{0}\to q)-\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,q_{0}\to Q^{\prime})>B
.

Then, the characterization is as follows.

Theorem 8.4
.

Consider a WFA
\mathcal{A}
, then the following hold.

1.

If there exists
B\in\mathbb{N}
such that there is no
B
-gap witness over
\Sigma
, then
\mathcal{A}
is determinizable.

2.

If there is a finite alphabet
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
such that for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
there is a
B
-gap witness over
\Sigma^{\prime}
, the
\mathcal{A}
is not determinizable.

We prove the theorem in
Appendix

A
. We remark that the proof there considers also initial and final weights, as it is later used in
Appendix

B
to establish
Remark

8.1
.

Observe that for WFAs over a finite alphabet,
Theorem

8.4
provides an exact characterization of determinizability.

9
The Baseline-Augmented Subset Construction

We now construct a WFA
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
that augments
\mathcal{A}
with additional information regarding its runs. This information is kept in both the states and in the alphabet.
Technically, the alphabet of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
consists of the
transitions
of
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.
\Delta
. Each state of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
contains information about the current state, the current reachable subset of states, as well as a special
baseline
state.
Intuitively, the baseline states aim to describe the minimal-weight run on the current word.

We proceed with the precise construction.
Let
\mathcal{A}=\langle Q,\Sigma,q_{0},\Delta\rangle
. We define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\langle S,\Delta,s_{0},\widehat{\Delta}\rangle
with the following components:

•

The states are

S=\{(q,p,T)\in Q\times Q\times 2^{Q}\mid q,p\in T\}
.
We refer to the components of a state
(q,p,T)\in S
as the
inner state

q
, the
baseline component

p
, and the
reachable subset

T
.

•

The alphabet of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is the set of transitions
\Delta
of
\mathcal{A}
. For clarity, we denote elements of
\Delta
using either explicit tuples, or by
\tau
. In practice, we consider reading only transitions of finite weight, but this is enforced in the transition relation.

•

The initial state is
(q_{0},q_{0},\{q_{0}\})
.

•

The transitions
\widehat{\Delta}
are defined as follows.
Consider states
(p_{1},q_{1},T_{1})
and
(p_{2},q_{2},T_{2})
in
S
and a letter
(q_{1},\sigma,c,q_{2})\in\Delta
with
c\neq\infty

(i.e., the transition in
\mathcal{A}
from
q_{1}
to
q_{2}
on some
\sigma\in\Sigma
).
If
T_{2}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(T_{1},\sigma)
, then consider the unique transition
(p_{1},\sigma,c^{\prime},p_{2})\in\Delta
. We add to
\widehat{\Delta}
the transition
((p_{1},q_{1},T_{1}),(q_{1},\sigma,c,q_{2}),c^{\prime}-c,(p_{2},q_{2},T_{2}))
.

Finally, we complete any undefined transitions with
\infty
.

To gain some intuition on the construction (specifically on the transition function), observe that for a state
(p_{1},q_{1},T_{1})
and letter
(q,\sigma,c,q^{\prime})
, the transition on the second and third component is entirely deterministic (assuming it has finite weight): any state
(p_{2},q_{2},T_{2})
reached must satisfy
T_{2}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(T_{1},\sigma)
, and it must hold that
q_{1}=q
and
q_{2}=q^{\prime}
.
The first component simulates
\mathcal{A}
: from
p_{1}
we can reach any state
p_{2}
that satisfies
(p_{1},\sigma,c^{\prime},p_{2})
for some
c^{\prime}
.
Finally, the cost of the transition is
c^{\prime}-c
: this reflects the fact that we “normalize” all weights so that if we had started at state
p
, the cost of the transition is
0
.

The remainder of our analysis is carried out on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
. We now show that this is sound and complete.

We now elaborate further on the behavior of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
. Recall that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
reads a sequence
x
of transitions of
\mathcal{A}
(and this sequence must form a run in order to have finite weight). Each state in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
keeps track of two runs of
\mathcal{A}
, and of the set of reachable states. This is done as follows.
Each transition
(q_{1},\sigma,c,q_{2})
that is read (as a letter for
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
) induces the letter in
\sigma\in\Sigma
that
\mathcal{A}
reads, but in addition “suggests” a transition on
\sigma
from
q_{1}
to
q_{2}
.
Then, this suggestion is only “accepted” in states whose baseline component is
q_{1}
, i.e.,
(p_{1},q_{1},T_{1})
for some
p_{1},T_{1}
with
p_{1},q_{1}\in T_{1}
.
This suggestion means that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
can now move to any state
p_{2}
reachable from
p_{1}
via
\sigma
, while the baseline component is now
q_{2}
.
Further observe that upon reading a word
x
, all the states reachable by
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
are of the form
(\cdot,p,T)
, i.e., share the same second and third components. Moreover, the possible reachable inner states (the first component) are exactly those in
T
, which are the states reachable by
\mathcal{A}
upon reading the letters in the word in
\Sigma^{*}
induced by
x
.
Thus,
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
essentially simulates the run tree of
\mathcal{A}
, with some additional information.

Of particular importance are states of the form
(q_{1},q_{1},T_{1})
, which are reachable and can accept the suggestion
(q_{1},\sigma,c,q_{2})
. Then,
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
may move to
(q_{2},q_{2},T_{2})
(as well as possibly to other states). Due to the weight normalization, the weight of this transition in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is
c-c=0
. Such states are central to our analysis.

Definition 9.1

(Baseline States and Baseline Runs)
.

A state in
S
is a
baseline
if it is of the form
(p,p,T)
for some
p\in T
.
A run
\rho=t_{1}\cdots t_{m}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
with
t_{i}=(s_{i},\tau_{i},d,s_{i+1})
is called a
baseline
if
s_{i}
is a baseline for all
i
.

Observation 9.2
.

Recall that the weight of a transition in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on letter
(p,\sigma,c,q)
is normalized by reducing
c
from any transition. In particular, a transition between baseline states
(p,p,T)\xrightarrow{\sigma}(q,q,T)
has weight
0
. Consequently, if
\rho
is a baseline run, then
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=0
. Note that there may still be other runs of both positive and negative weights.

The intuition behind the baseline run is that it forms a “point of view” (POV) of the various runs of
\mathcal{A}
on a given word, in the sense that the weights of the transitions in
\mathcal{A}
are shifted so that the baseline run has weight
0
, and all other runs are shifted accordingly. We formalize this intuition in
Section

9.1
.
In particular, this enables us to show that
\mathcal{A}
is determinizable if and only if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is determinizable.

9.1
Shifts Between
\mathcal{A}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}

Intuitively, the runs of
\mathcal{A}
and of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
have the same relative structure, up to the choice of baseline run dictated in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.
Indeed, for a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
and a run
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
, when
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
reads
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
, each run corresponds to a run of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
, with the weights shifted according to
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
. In particular, the difference in weights between any two runs of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
is the same as the difference in weights between the corresponding runs of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
.
Conversely, for a word
\widehat{w}\in\Delta^{*}
, we can associate a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
\widehat{w}
is a run of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
, and such that each run
\widehat{\rho}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on
\widehat{w}
corresponds to a run
\rho
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
, and such that the difference in weight is similarly maintained as above.

We now formalize this intuition.
Consider a word
4
4

4

Observe that the definition does not actually depend on
w
, as
w
is already induced by
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
.

w\in\Sigma^{*}
and a run
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}=t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{n}
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
with
t_{i}=(q_{i},\sigma_{i},c_{i},q_{i+1})
. We think of the run
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
as a word read by
\mathcal{A}
.
Consider a run
\widehat{\rho}=\widehat{r_{1}},\widehat{r_{2}},\ldots,\widehat{r_{n}}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
where for all
1\leq i<n
we have

\widehat{r_{i}}=((p_{i},q_{i},T_{i}),t_{i},d_{i},(p_{i+1},q_{i+1},T_{i+1}))\in\widehat{\Delta}

The
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
-shifted run of
\mathcal{A}
corresponding to
\widehat{\rho}
,
denoted
\mathsf{shift}^{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}}(\widehat{\rho})
,
is then the run
\rho=t^{\prime}_{1},t^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{n}
where
t^{\prime}_{i}=(p_{i},\sigma_{i},d_{i}+c_{i},p_{i+1})
.
Note that
\rho
follows the inner states of the run
\widehat{\rho}
, but has weights shifted by
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
.

We first observe that
\rho
is indeed a run of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
: the initial state of
\widehat{\rho}
is
s_{0}=(q_{0},q_{0},\{q_{0}\})
, and therefore the initial state of
\rho
is
q_{0}
. For the transitions, for every
1\leq i<n
, since
\widehat{r_{i}}\in\widehat{\Delta}
is a transition on the letter
t_{i}
, then (by the definition of
\widehat{\Delta}
) we have that
(p_{i},\sigma_{i},c^{\prime}_{i},p_{i+1})
for
c^{\prime}_{i}
such that
c^{\prime}_{i}-c_{i}=d_{i}
. But then
c^{\prime}_{i}=c_{i}+d_{i}
, so the transition
t^{\prime}_{i}
is indeed a valid transition in
\Delta
.

We now show that
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
-shifted runs maintain the weight differences of their origin runs. Intuitively, this holds because both runs are shifted by the same corresponding weights of
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
.

Proposition 9.3
.

Consider a run
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
of
\mathcal{A}
and two runs
\widehat{\rho}=\widehat{r_{1}},\widehat{r_{2}},\ldots,\widehat{r_{n}}
and
\widehat{\mu}=\widehat{x_{1}},\widehat{x_{2}},\ldots,\widehat{x_{n}}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
.
Let
\rho=r^{\prime}_{1},r^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{n}
and
\mu=x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n}
be

\mathsf{shift}^{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}}(\widehat{\rho})
and
\mathsf{shift}^{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}}(\widehat{\mu})
, respectively,
then for every
1\leq i\leq n
we have

\mathsf{wt}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\widehat{r_{1}},\widehat{r_{2}},\ldots,\widehat{r_{i}})-\mathsf{wt}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\widehat{\mu}=\widehat{x_{1}},\widehat{x_{2}},\ldots,\widehat{x_{i}})=\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(r^{\prime}_{1},r^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{i})-\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{i})

Proof.

The proof follows easily by induction on
i
. The base case is trivial: for
i=0
all runs accumulate
0
weight, so the differences are both
0
.
Assume correctness for
i-1
, we prove for
i
.
By the induction hypothesis, up to prefix
i
the differences are maintained, and thus it suffices to prove that

\mathsf{wt}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\widehat{r_{i}})-\mathsf{wt}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\widehat{x_{i}})=\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(r^{\prime}_{i})-\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x^{\prime}_{i})

Denote
\widehat{r_{i}}=((p_{i},q_{i},T_{i}),t_{i},d_{i},(p_{i+1},q_{i+1},T_{i+1}))
and
\widehat{x_{i}}=((p^{\prime}_{i},q_{i},T^{\prime}_{i}),t_{i},d^{\prime}_{i},(p^{\prime}_{i+1},q_{i+1},T^{\prime}_{i+1}))
(the second and third components are the same since both runs are runs on
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
)
with
t_{i}=(q_{i},\sigma_{i},c_{i},q_{i+1})
.
Then by the definition above we have

r^{\prime}_{i}=(p_{i},\sigma_{i},d_{i}+c_{i},p_{i+1})
and
x^{\prime}_{i}=(p^{\prime}_{i},\sigma_{i},d^{\prime}_{i}+c_{i},p^{\prime}_{i+1})
.
Thus, we need to prove that

d_{i}-d^{\prime}_{i}=d_{i}+c_{i}-(d^{\prime}_{i}+c_{i})

which indeed holds.
∎

Recall that a run is seamless if at all prefixes, it is the minimal run to its most recent state. By
Proposition

9.3
we immediately have the following.

Corollary 9.4
.

Consider a run
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
of
\mathcal{A}
and a run
\widehat{\rho}=\widehat{r_{1}},\widehat{r_{2}},\ldots,\widehat{r_{n}}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
. Let
\rho=r^{\prime}_{1},r^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{n}
be
\mathsf{shift}^{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}}(\widehat{\rho})
, then
\widehat{\rho}
is seamless if and only if
\rho
is seamless.

We now turn to the converse shift: from runs of
\mathcal{A}
to runs of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.
To this end, consider a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
and a run
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
. With every run
\rho
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
we associate the
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
-shifted run of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
corresponding to
\rho
denoted
\widehat{\rho}=\mathsf{shift}^{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}_{\mathcal{A}\to\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\rho)
as follows.

Let
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}=t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{n}
with
t_{i}=(q_{i},\sigma_{i},c_{i},q_{i+1})
and
\rho=r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{n}
with
r_{i}=(p_{i},\sigma_{i},d_{i},p_{i+1})
, then
\widehat{\rho}
is the run of
\mathcal{A}
on
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
defined by
\widehat{\rho}=\widehat{r_{1}},\widehat{r_{2}},\ldots,\widehat{r_{n}}
with

\widehat{r_{i}}=((p_{i},q_{i},T_{i}),t_{i},d_{i}-c_{i},(p_{i+1},q_{i+1},T_{i+1}))

and
T_{i}
is determined deterministically by
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
.

As above, we observe that
\widehat{r_{i}}
is a run of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
. The initial state of
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
and of
\rho
is
q_{0}
, so the initial state of
\widehat{\rho}
is
(q_{0},q_{0},\{q_{0}\})
, i.e., the initial state of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.
For the transitions, for every
1\leq i<n
, the transition
r_{i}
is valid in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on the internal state by the transition of
\rho
, and on the baseline and reachable set by the transitions (i.e., letters) of
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
. It remains to check that the weight of
r_{i}
matches the transitions in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, but this follows directly by the definition of
\widehat{\Delta}
.

The shift from
\mathcal{A}
to
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and from
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
to
\mathcal{A}
can be seen as inverse operators, in that when shifting from
\mathcal{A}
to
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, apart from the change in the state space, the weight of transitions in
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
is subtracted, and conversely, when going back from
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
to
\mathcal{A}
the weight of transitions in
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
is added (again, apart from the change in state space). We thus have the following

Observation 9.5
.

Consider a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
and a run
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
.

•

For runs
\rho,\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
, let
\widehat{\rho}=\mathsf{shift}^{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}_{\mathcal{A}\to\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\rho)
and let
\mu=\mathsf{shift}^{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}}(\widehat{\rho})
, then
\mu=\rho
.

•

For a run
\widehat{\rho}
of
\mathcal{A}
on
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
, let
\rho=\mathsf{shift}^{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}}(\widehat{\rho})
and
\widehat{\mu}=\mathsf{shift}^{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}_{\mathcal{A}\to\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\rho)
, then
\widehat{\mu}=\widehat{\rho}
.

Remark 9.6
.

Note that while
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
is a parameter in
\mathsf{shift}^{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}_{\mathcal{A}\to\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\rho)
, it is actually fixed in
\mathsf{shift}^{\rho_{\mathrm{base}}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}}(\widehat{\rho})
, as it is determined by
\widehat{\rho}
(it is the word upon which
\widehat{\rho}
runs).

We can now readily prove an analogue of
Proposition

9.3
.

Proposition 9.7
.

Consider a run
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
, and two runs
\rho=r^{\prime}_{1},r^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{n}
and
\mu=x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n}
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
.
Let
\widehat{\rho}=\widehat{r_{1}},\widehat{r_{2}},\ldots,\widehat{r_{n}}
and
\widehat{\mu}=\widehat{x_{1}},\widehat{x_{2}},\ldots,\widehat{x_{n}}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
be the respective
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
-shifted runs, then for every
1\leq i\leq n
we have

\mathsf{wt}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\widehat{r_{1}},\widehat{r_{2}},\ldots,\widehat{r_{i}})-\mathsf{wt}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\widehat{\mu}=\widehat{x_{1}},\widehat{x_{2}},\ldots,\widehat{x_{i}})=\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(r^{\prime}_{1},r^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{i})-\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{i})

Proof.

By
Proposition

9.3
the
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
-shifted runs of
\mathcal{A}
that correspond to
\widehat{\rho}
and
\widehat{\mu}
maintain the differences, but by
Observation

9.5
these runs are exactly
\rho
and
\mu
, respectively.
∎

We can now conclude that
\mathcal{A}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
are equi-determinizable, allowing us to proceed with an analysis of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
instead of
\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 9.8
.

\mathcal{A}
is determinizable if and only if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is determinizable.

Proof.

For the first direction, assume
\mathcal{A}
is not determinizable. By
Theorems

8.4
and
8.3
, for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
there exist words
x,y\in\Sigma^{*}
and a state
q\in Q
such that
\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x\cdot y,q_{0}\to Q)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x\cdot y,q_{0}\xrightarrow{x}q\xrightarrow{y}Q)<\infty
, and
\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,q_{0}\to q)-\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,q_{0}\to Q)>B
.

By
Proposition

9.7
, the same gap between runs exists between the corresponding runs after a
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
-shift for any
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
. Moreover, a minimal run in
\mathcal{A}
is shifted to a minimal run in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
. Thus, again by
Theorem

8.4
, we conclude that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is not determinizable.

The converse direction is similar: assume
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is not determinizable, then for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
there exist words
\widehat{x},\widehat{y}\in\Sigma^{*}
and a state
s\in S
such that
\mathsf{mwt}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\widehat{x}\cdot\widehat{y},s_{0}\to S)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\widehat{x}\cdot\widehat{y},s_{0}\xrightarrow{\widehat{x}}s\xrightarrow{\widehat{y}}S)<\infty
, and
\mathsf{mwt}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\widehat{x},s_{0}\to s)-\mathsf{mwt}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\widehat{x},s_{0}\to S)>B
.

By
Proposition

9.3
, the gap is maintained by the corresponding shifted runs. Note that here, the shift is dictated by the word being read, namely
\widehat{x}
(comparing the shifted runs of
s_{0}\xrightarrow{\widehat{x}}s
and
s_{0}\xrightarrow{\widehat{x}}Q
).
Thus, again by
Theorem

8.4
, we conclude that
\mathcal{A}
is not determinizable.
∎

10
Stable Cycles and Bounded Behaviors

A fundamental approach used in reasoning about weighted automata is that of
stabilization
. Intuitively, the idea is to symbolically represent a repeated application of a certain transition matrix, until entries either become bounded, unreachable, or tend to
\infty
. In essence, this is a form of “pumping”.
While useful in many setting, this form of stabilization seems to be too crude for showing that determinizability is decidable (evidently, all attempts for using it thus far have failed).

In this work, we use a much more delicate notion of stabilization, whereby we restrict attention to very specific subsets of states and transition matrices (i.e., words).
The basic structure that allows some form of pumping is that of
stable cycles
. Intuitively a stable cycle is a pair
(S^{\prime},w)
where
S^{\prime}
is a set of states and
w
is a word such that reading
w
any number of times from the states in
S^{\prime}
leads back to
S^{\prime}
, and the weighted behaviors of runs are “stable”, in the sense that we can identify which states generate minimal runs, and which tend to unbounded weights, regardless of the starting weights.
We make this notion precise in the following.

10.1
Stable Cycles

We start with a weaker definition than stable cycles.

Definition 10.1
.

Consider a set of states
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
and
w\in\Delta^{*}
. We say that
(S^{\prime},w)
is a
reflexive cycle
if there exists
q\in Q
and
T\subseteq Q
such that
S^{\prime}=\{(p,q,T)\mid p\in T\}
and
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w)\subseteq S^{\prime}
.

A state
s\in S^{\prime}
is a
reflexive state
if
s\xrightarrow{w}s
. We denote the set of reflexive states of
(S^{\prime},w)
as
\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w)=\{s\in S^{\prime}\mid s\xrightarrow{w}s\}
.

That is, the states in
S^{\prime}
share the last two components, and
T
is “saturated” in the sense that every state
p\in T
represented in
S^{\prime}
as
(p,q,T)
. Reflexivity requires that reading
w
from
S^{\prime}
leads back to
S^{\prime}
, or a subset thereof.
Note that if
(S^{\prime},w)
is a reflexive cycle, then so is
(S^{\prime},w^{n})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
(this is immediate by the requirement
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w)\subseteq S^{\prime}
).

A-priori,
(S^{\prime},w)
might not have reflexive states at all. Clearly, however, for
w^{n}
with
n
large enough, some states become reflexive. Our first two observation is that we can stabilize the reachability set and the reflexive states by taking a large-enough repetition of
w
.
To this end, let
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}=|S|!
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}=|S|\cdot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
.

A basic observation from Boolean automata is that the transition relation becomes idempotent after
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
iterations. More precisely, we have the following.

Proposition 10.2
.

For every word
w
, states
s,r\in S
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r
if and only if
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}n}}r
.

Proof.

For readers versed with the algebraic view of automata, this can be shown using the order of the transition monoid of the automaton (see e.g.,
[
6
]
).
We bring a state-based proof for completeness.

If
s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r
, then since
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}>|S|
there is a simple cycle in such a run, i.e.,
s\xrightarrow{w^{a}}r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{b}}r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}-a-b}}r
with
b<|S|
. But then
b
divides
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
, so we can repeat this cycle for additional
(n-1){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}/b
, thus yielding a run
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}n}}r
.

Conversely, if
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}n}}r
, since
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}=|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
, we can decompose
s
into
\frac{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}n}{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}=|S|n
segments of
w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}
.

\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}s_{1}\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}s_{2}\cdots\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}s_{|S|n}=r

Since there are more than
|S|
such segments, we can remove cycles and obtain a run

\mu:s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}t_{1}\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}t_{2}\cdots\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}t_{k}=r

with
k\leq|S|
.
If
k=|S|
, then
k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}={\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
, and therefore
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
and we are done.
Otherwise,
1\leq k<|S|
, and in particular since
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}>|S|
the prefix
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}t_{1}
contains a cycle of the form
t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{m}}t^{\prime}
for some
1\leq m<|S|
. Since
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}=|S|\cdot|S|!
, in particular
m
divides
|S|!={\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
, and therefore
m
divides
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}-k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
.
Thus, by repeating this cycle an additional
\frac{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}-k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}{m}
times, we obtain a run
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
, and we are done.
∎

Proposition 10.3
.

Consider a reflexive cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. For every
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{m})\subseteq\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})=\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n})
.
Additionally,
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n})
.

Proof.

Let
s\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{m})
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and take this
m
to be minimal.
Thus,
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{m}}s
for some run
\rho
. We start by showing that
s\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})
. First, note that if
m\leq|S|
, then
m|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}=|S|!
, so
\rho^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}/m}:s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}s
. Thus,
s\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})
.
Next, if
m>|S|
, then by the pigeonhole principle
\rho
has a cycle between some
w^{i}
and
w^{j}
.
That is, there are
1\leq i<j\leq m
and some state
s^{\prime}\in S
such that
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{i}}s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{j-i}}s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{m-j}}s
. We can now remove this cycle and obtain the run

\rho^{\prime}:s\xrightarrow{w^{m-(j-i)}}s
, contradicting the minimality of
m
.

Next, we show that
