\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})=\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n})
. The direction
\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})\subseteq\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n})
trivially holds, by repeating cycles (since
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
divides
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n
). The converse holds by the argument above, plugging
m={\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n
.

For the reachability part, by reflexivity we inductively have that
\{\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w^{k})\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
forms a decreasing chain, and in particular
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n})\subseteq\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
.
Conversely, consider
s\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
, then by a similar pigeonhole argument as above, there is
s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime}\in S^{\prime}
such that
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{i}}s^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{j}}s^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}-i-j}}s
for some
j<|S|
, and by repeating the
w^{j}
cycle we can obtain a run
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n}}s
, so
s\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n})
.
∎

Proposition

10.3
shows that all the reflexive cycles on any number of repetitions of
w
are already present for
w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
.

In the
(\min,+)
semantics, of particular importance are reflexive cycles whose cycles have minimal weight. We capture this as follows.

Definition 10.4
.

Consider a reflexive cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. We say that a state
s\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w)
is
minimal reflexive
if
\mathsf{mwt}(w,s\to s)=\min\{\mathsf{mwt}(w,r\to r)\mid r\in\textsc{RefStates}\}
.
We then define

\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w)=\{s\in S^{\prime}\mid s\text{ is minimal reflexive.}\}

That is, the cycle on
w
from
s
to
s
has minimal weight among all reflexive cycles on
w
.
Recall that
S^{\prime}=\{(p,q,T)\mid p\in T\}
for some
T\subseteq Q
. In particular,
(q,q,T)\in S^{\prime}
is the unique baseline state in
S^{\prime}
.
We now ask whether
(q,q,T)
is a minimal reflexive state, and whether its minimal reflexive run is a baseline (and therefore by
Observation

9.2
has weight
0
). Moreover, we ask whether this holds for
w^{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In case this holds, we say that
(S^{\prime},w)
is a
Stable Cycle
. We now formalize this.

Definition 10.5

(

Stable Cycle)
.

A reflexive cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
with baseline state
s=(q,q,T)
is a
stable cycle
if for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
it holds that
s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{n})
and
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{n},s\to s)=0
.

Note that if
(S^{\prime},w)
is a stable cycle, then the baseline run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{n}}s
is a cycle of minimal weight on any state
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
, and its weight is
0
.
There could be other runs from
S^{\prime}
on some
w^{n}
that are negative, but not cycles.

We now show an analogous result to
Proposition

10.3
, showing that the minimal reflexive states also become stable quickly, under the assumption that we have a stable cycle.

Proposition 10.6
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. For every
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{m})\subseteq\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})=\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n})

Proof.

Let
s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{m})
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
(not necessarily the baseline state), and take this
m
to be minimal.
Thus,
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{m}}s
for some minimal-weight run
\rho
. We start by showing that
s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})
. If
m\leq|S|
, then
m|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}=|S|!
, so
\rho^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}/m}:s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}s
.
By
Definition

10.5
we have
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=0
, so
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}/m})=0
as well. Since
(S^{\prime},w)
is stable, then in particular
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime})\geq 0
for every
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
, since the baseline state is minimal reflexive for
w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}
(indeed, for all repetitions of
w
) and has cycle weight
0
.
It follows that
s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})

Next, if
m>|S|
, then by the pigeonhole principle
\rho
has a cycle between some
w^{i}
and
w^{j}
.
That is, there are
1\leq i<j\leq m
and some state
s^{\prime}\in S
such that
\rho^{i}:s\xrightarrow{w^{i}}s^{\prime}
and
\rho^{j}:s\xrightarrow{w^{j}}s^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\rho^{j-i}:s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{j-i}}s^{\prime}
. Moreover,
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{j-i})\geq 0
. Indeed, otherwise enough repetitions of
\rho^{j-i}
would yield a negative cycle, contradicting the fact that the baseline state is minimal reflexive with weight 0 for
every
repetition of
w
.

We can now remove this cycle and obtain the run

\rho^{\prime}:s\xrightarrow{w^{m-(j-i)}}s
with weight at most
0
(and by the above – exactly
0
), contradicting the minimality of
m
.

Next, we show that
\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})=\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n})
.
The direction
\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})

\subseteq\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n})
holds by repeating cycles (since
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
divides
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n
), and by observing that negative cycles cannot occur in a stable cycle.
The converse holds by the argument above, plugging
m={\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot n
.
∎

10.2
States with Bounded Behaviors

Recall that our motivation in this section is to establish a “pumpable” structure. Let
(S^{\prime},w)
be a stable cycle, and consider the long-term behavior of runs on
w^{m}
for large
m
, starting from states in
S^{\prime}
.

Recall that for a state
s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w)
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(w,s\to s)=0
, and that this is the minimal weight of a cycle on
w
from
S^{\prime}
. Then, for every state
r\in S^{\prime}
, if either
s\xrightarrow{w}r
or
r\xrightarrow{w}s
, more repetitions of
w
intuitively do not increase (nor decrease) the weight further, since they incur weight
0
by cycling on
s
.
The converse is almost correct: we would want to say that runs that avoid
\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w)

must increase unboundedly. This is almost true, except that a state might not be minimal-reflexive for
w
, but becomes minimal reflexive for
w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
, so does not lead to unbounded weights.
In the following we show that this is indeed the case: runs that avoid
\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
inevitably yield increasing weights.
Moreover, we identify after how many repetitions of
w
these eventual behaviors stabilize.

We start with the definitions for the three types of “bounded behavior”: states that are reachable via
w
from a minimal-reflexive state are called
tethered
(as they are “tethered” to a
0
-weight cycle), states from which a minimal-reflexive state is reachable are called
plateau
, as they reach a
0
-weight cycle, and their combination is referred to as
grounded
, as follows.

Definition 10.7

(Tethered, Plateau and Grounded Pairs)
.

For a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
we define:

•

The
tethered pairs
as

\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w)=\{(s,r)\in S^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\mid s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w)\wedge s\xrightarrow{w}r\}

We refer to each such
r
as a
tethered state
.

•

The
plateau pairs
as

\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w)=\{(s,r)\in S^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\mid r\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w)\wedge s\xrightarrow{w}r\}

We refer to each such
s
as a
plateau state
.

•

The
grounded pairs
as

\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)=\{(s,r)\in S^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\mid\exists g\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})\text{ s.t. }s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r\}

For
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, the state
g\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
for which
\mathsf{wt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)
is minimal (among all states in
\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
) is the
grounding state of
(s,r)
.

Fig.

10
depicts the three types of pairs in
Definition

10.7
.

Figure 10
:
(s,r)
is a tethered pair (1), a plateau pair (2), and grounded pair with a grounding state
g
(3)

As a sanity-check, note that since
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w)\subseteq S^{\prime}
, then for every tethered, plateau, or grounded pair
(s,r)
, the fact that
r\in S^{\prime}
already follows from
s\in S^{\prime}
.

Similarly to
Propositions

10.3
and
10.6
, we show that the tethered and plateau pairs stabilize. This, however, no longer holds for any repetition of
w
, but for repetitions of
w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
, and the stabilization occurs at
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
(recall
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}=|S|\cdot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
), as follows.

Proposition 10.8
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. For every
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m\cdot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})\subseteq\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})=\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\cdot n}).

Moreover, for every
(s,r)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m\cdot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
it holds that

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m\cdot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to r)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(w^{n\cdot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)

Proof.

The proof proceeds in three steps.

Step 1:

Consider a tethered pair
(s,r)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
for some
m
, and let
m
be minimal for this pair. We claim that
m\leq|S|
. Indeed, similarly to the proofs of
Propositions

10.3
and
10.6
, if
m>|S|
, by the pigeonhole principle there exists a state
s^{\prime}
that repeats in the minimal weight run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}r
. We can thus write

\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{m_{1}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{m_{2}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{m_{3}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}r
with
m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}=m
. We then have that
s\xrightarrow{w^{(m_{1}+m_{3}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}r
. In order to reach a contradiction to the minimality of
m
, we need to show that
s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{(m_{1}+m_{3}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
, so that
(s,r)
is a tethered pair in
(S^{\prime},w^{(m_{1}+m_{3})}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})
. This, however, follows directly from
Proposition

10.6
, since
\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})=\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{(m_{1}+m_{3}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
. We can therefore assume
m\leq|S|
.

In addition, since stable cycles do not contain negative cycles, we have that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m_{2}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime})\geq 0
, so
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{(m_{1}+m_{3}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to r)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to r)
.

Step 2:

Next, we prove that
\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})\subseteq\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
for all
k\geq m
.
Indeed, consider a tethered pair
(s,r)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
, then
s\xrightarrow{w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}r
and
s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
.
By
Proposition

10.6
and since
k\geq m
we also have

s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})=\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{(k-m){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})=\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}).

In particular,
s\xrightarrow{w^{(k-m){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}s
, and therefore
s\xrightarrow{w^{k-m}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}s\xrightarrow{w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}r
, so
s\xrightarrow{w^{(k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})}}r
and thus
(s,r)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
.

Moreover, since
s\in\textsc{MinStates}(w^{(k-m){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
, it follows that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{(k-m){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to s)=0
, and from the run description above we get that

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to r)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to r)
(where we have an inequality instead of equality, since conceptually there could be other runs with lower weight).

Step 3:

We can now complete the proof. for a pair
(s,r)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
, we can assume by Step 1 that
m\leq|S|
, and this can only decrease the weight. By Step 2 we also have
(s,r)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, since
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}=|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}>m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
, and that this again decreases the weight, i.e.,

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to r)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{n{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r).

Conversely, if
(s,r)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
then since
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
is a multiple of
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
, by Step 2 we have
(s,r)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{n{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
for all
n\geq 1
.
To conclude the equality of weights, by Step 1 and Step 2 combined, we have that

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{n{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)
. Indeed, decreasing from
n{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
to at most
|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
decreases the weight (Step 1), and it further decreases to
|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}={\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
(Step 2).
∎

A similar claim holds for plateau states, with an analogous proof.

Proposition 10.9
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. For every
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m\cdot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})\subseteq\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})=\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\cdot n}).

Moreover, for every
(s,r)\in\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m\cdot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
it holds that

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m\cdot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to r)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(w^{n\cdot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)

Proof.

The proof proceeds in three steps.

Step 1:

Consider a plateau pair
(s,r)\in\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
for some
m
, and let
m
be minimal for this pair. We claim that
m\leq|S|
. Indeed, similarly to the proofs of
Propositions

10.3
and
10.6
, if
m>|S|
, by the pigeonhole principle there exists a state
s^{\prime}
that repeats in the minimal weight run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}r
. We can thus write

\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{m_{1}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{m_{2}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{m_{3}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}r
with
m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}=m
. We then have that
s\xrightarrow{w^{(m_{1}+m_{3}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}r
. In order to reach a contradiction to the minimality of
m
, we need to show that
r\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{(m_{1}+m_{3}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
, so that
(s,r)
is a plateau pair in
(S^{\prime},w^{(m_{1}+m_{3})}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})
. This, however, follows directly from
Proposition

10.6
, since
\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})=\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{(m_{1}+m_{3}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
. We can therefore assume
m\leq|S|
.

In addition, since stable cycles do not contain negative cycles, we have that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m_{2}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime})\geq 0
, so
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{(m_{1}+m_{3}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to r)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to r)
.

Step 2:

Next, we prove that
\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})\subseteq\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
for all
k\geq m
.
Indeed, consider a plateau pair
(s,r)\in\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
, then
s\xrightarrow{w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}r
and
r\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
.
By
Proposition

10.6
and since
k\geq m
we also have

r\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})=\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{(k-m){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})=\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}).

In particular,
r\xrightarrow{w^{(k-m){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}r
, and therefore
s\xrightarrow{w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}}r\xrightarrow{w^{k-m}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}}r
, so
s\xrightarrow{w^{(k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}})}}r
and thus
(s,r)\in\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
.

Moreover, since
r\in\textsc{MinStates}(w^{(k-m){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
, it follows that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{(k-m){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},r\to r)=0
, and from the run description above we get that

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to r)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to r)
(where we have an inequality instead of equality, since conceptually there could be other runs with lower weight).

Step 3:

We can now complete the proof. for a pair
(s,r)\in\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}})
, we can assume by Step 1 that
m\leq|S|
, and this can only decrease the weight. By Step 2 we also have
(s,r)\in\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, since
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}=|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}>m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
, and that this again decreases the weight, i.e.,

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}},s\to r)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{n{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r).

Conversely, if
(s,r)\in\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
then since
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
is a multiple of
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
, by Step 2 we have
(s,r)\in\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{n{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
for all
n\geq 1
.
To conclude the equality of weights, by Step 1 and Step 2 combined, we have that

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{n{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)
. Indeed, decreasing from
n{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
to at most
|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}
decreases the weight (Step 1), and it further decreases to
|S|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{n}}}={\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
(Step 2).
∎

The stabilization of
TthPairs
and
PltPairs
implies that the grounding state in grounded pairs does not have to be reachable and co-reachable with
w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
, as follows.

Proposition 10.10
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. For every
s,r,g\in S^{\prime}
with
g\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, if
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}i}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}j}}r
for some
i,j\geq 1
, then
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
.

Proof.

Since
g\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, then
(s,g)\in\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}i})=\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
(by
Proposition

10.9
) and that
(g,r)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}j})=\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
.
We thus have
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
, so by
Definition

10.7
we have
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
.
∎

The main result of this section aggregated the asymptotic behavior of grounded pairs, and shows that they stabilize. We give a brief overview of the argument.

Consider a pair of states
(s,r)
in a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. If
(s,r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, then every run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{m}}r
has only strictly positive cycles (focusing on the states after each
w
), and is therefore intuitively increasing. There may, however, be negative simple paths upon reading
w
, and the negative weight can depend on the length of
w
. Thus, it is guaranteed that every long-enough run has very large weight, but achieving this weight may take a while.

Conversely, if
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
and
g
is their grounding state (c.f.
Definition

10.7
), then it is possible to spend any number
m{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}>2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
of iterations of
w
between
r
and
s
by taking
0
-weight cycles on
g
. While this is optimal among runs that go through a grounding state, there may be other runs with lower weight that initially take a very negative transition. Nonetheless, after enough repetitions of
w
, the path through the grounding state becomes optimal altogether. This is captured as follows.

Lemma 10.11

(

Pumping Grounded Pairs)
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
M_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
(efficiently computable) such that for all
m\geq M_{0}
the following hold.
5
5

5

Note that Item 2 does not depend on
n
.

1.

If
(s,r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
then
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)>n
.

2.

If
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
with grounding state
g
, then

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(w^{M_{0}\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\xrightarrow{w^{M}}g\xrightarrow{w^{M}}r)

Moreover, we have
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m^{\prime}\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\xrightarrow{w^{M}}g\xrightarrow{w^{M}}r)
for every
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We first prove Item 1, and then use it to prove Item 2.

Proof of Item 1.

Let
(s,r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
. We construct a weighted directed graph
G=\langle V,E\rangle
whose vertices are
V=S^{\prime}
and for
p,q\in S^{\prime}
we add the edge
(p,q)
with weight
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}},p\to q)
(which might be
\infty
). Observe that there is a bijection between paths from
s
to
r
in
G
and runs of the form
\rho:s=p_{1}\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}p_{2}\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}\cdots\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}p_{k}=r
provided each section
p_{i}\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}p_{i+1}
in the run has minimal weight (indeed, otherwise it can be replaced by a lower-weight run).
In particular, for all
k
, the minimal weight of a run from
s
to
r
on
w^{k{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
is attained by a path in the graph.

Since
(S^{\prime},w)
is stable, this graph has no strictly negative cycles.
Since
(s,r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, then every cycle in the graph that is reachable from
s
and can reach
r
, has strictly positive weight. Indeed, otherwise there would be a run
s\xrightarrow{w^{k_{1}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}q\xrightarrow{w^{k_{2}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}q\xrightarrow{w^{k_{3}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
with
\mathsf{mwt}(q\xrightarrow{w^{k_{2}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}q)=0
. However, by
Proposition

10.6
this implies
q\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, which in turn implies by
Propositions

10.8
,
10.9
and
10.6
that
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
(as
(s,q)\in\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
and
(q,r)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
), but this contradicts the assumption that
(s,r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
.

We conclude that every cycle reachable between
s
and
r
has strictly positive weight. The claim readily follows from this, by noting that increasingly long runs have increasingly large weight (which corresponds to all the runs having large weight). We spell out the argument for completeness.

By Johnson’s algorithm
[
21
]
we can (efficiently) compute a new weight function on the edges
\mu:E\to\mathbb{N}
and two numbers
v_{s},v_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the weight of a path
s=p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}=r
in the original graph
G
is
v_{s}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}E(p_{i},p_{i+1})-v_{r}
. Note that in the new weights, there are no negative edges at all, and the property that there are no reachable
0
-cycles between
s
and
r
is maintained by Johnson’s construction. We can now readily see that in every segment of length
|S|
in the path there is at least one cycle, whose weight is at least
1
. Therefore, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, every path of length
|S|(|v_{s}|+|v_{r}|+n)
has weight at least
n
in
G
. Thus, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we can set
M_{0}=|S|(|v_{s}|+|v_{r}|+n)
, and the claim follows (note that we prove the claim already for
w^{m\cdot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
, but we take
w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
for uniformity with the other constants).

Note that in fact, we prove a slightly stronger result: for any pair
(s,r)
, if
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
does not traverse a
0
-weight cycle while reading some
w^{i}
infix, then
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)>n
. In particular, if
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\leq n
then it must make a
0
-weight cycle on some state
q\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
.
This observation is used in the next part of the proof.

Proof of Item 2.

Consider
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
and let
g
be the grounding state for
(s,r)
(see
Definition

10.7
).
We first note that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
it holds that there is always a run on
w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
that uses the grounding state as a
0
-cycle. Indeed,
s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{(m-1)2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{M}}r
is a run from
s
to
r
on
w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
. Since
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},g\to g)=0
it follows that the weight of this run is exactly
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)
. We thus have the inequality:

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r).

This concludes the “Moreover” part.
It remains to show that there is some
M_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
m\geq M_{0}
, the reverse inequality holds.

Fix
n=\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)
and let
M_{0}
be the constant constructed in the proof of Item 1 for this
n
. The observation at the end of the proof of Item 1 gives us that for every
m\geq M_{0}
, every run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
that does not visit a state in
\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
must have weight larger than
n
. By the inequality above, such runs cannot be minimal, and we can therefore ignore them.

Consider then a minimal-weight run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
that visits a state
q\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
, then we can write

\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{k_{1}\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}q\xrightarrow{w^{k_{2}\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
for some
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1
.
Then, by
Propositions

10.8
and
10.9
we have that
(s,q)\in\textsc{PltPairs}(w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to q)
and
(q,r)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},q\to r)
, and moreover:
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}},s\to q)=\mathsf{mwt}(w^{k_{1}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to q)
and similarly
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}},q\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(w^{k_{2}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},q\to r)
.
Thus, we have
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}q\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)
. However, recall that
g
is the grounding state, which by definition means that
g
minimizes this expression over all
q\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, and therefore
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)
. We thus have

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)

and we conclude the equality.
We can now either plug in
m=M_{0}
or leave
m\geq M_{0}
to get the two equalities of Item 2.
∎

11
The Cactus Extension

Consider a baseline-augmented subset construction
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\langle S,\Gamma,s_{0},\widehat{\Delta}\rangle
. Note that we now denote the alphabet by
\Gamma
; we no longer assume
\Gamma
is finite.
More precisely, we do not assume the precise transitions defined in
Section

9
, but the following:

•

The states are of the form
(q,p,T)
where
q,p\in T
.

•

The transitions are deterministic with respect to the second and third components (referred to as
baseline component
and
reachable set
, respectively).

In the following, we repeatedly extend the alphabet and transitions of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
to obtain a sequence of automata over the same state space with the properties above. We briefly sketch our approach.
Our extension consists of three phases. First, we introduce a
stabilization
operator, which adds letters referred to as
cactus letters
. Intuitively, a cactus letter is built by nesting stable cycles, where each stable cycle becomes a letter.
The next phase is a
rebase
operator, which further adds the
rebase letters
. Intuitively, these letters represent a stable cycle, but also shift the baseline to a new state (which is not possible in cactus letters).
We then repeatedly alternate adding cactus letters and rebase letters to an infinite limit.
Finally, we add another set of letters, referred to as
jump
letters. These allow us to change the baseline. The need for jump letters is technical, and we explain their usage in due course.

11.1
Cactus Letters

Cactus letters are defined as the infinite union of an inductively defined set. The induction steps are defined by adding new letters and transitions with the following
stabilization
operator.

Definition 11.1

(

Stabilization)
.

For each stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
we introduce a new letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
with the following transitions: for every
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
with grounding state
g
we have the transition
(s,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r),r)
. We refer to this operation as
stabilization
, and denote the new letters by
\mathsf{stab}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\Gamma)
and the new transitions by
\mathsf{stab}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(\widehat{\Delta})
.

Remark 11.2
.

The term
stabilization
is often used in the algebraic view of automata to denote a closure operation on the transition monoid of a WFA
[
28
,
13
]
whereby runs that increase too much are “sent to
\infty
”.
Our usage is technically different, but has a similar flavor, and hence we reuse the name.
Indeed, by
Lemma

10.11
, the effect of reading arbitrarily many repetitions of
w
from a state in a stable cycle is either bounded (for a grounded pair) or tends to infinity.

We define the stabilization by essentially considering
w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
. This, however, can be replaced by
w^{m2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, as we now show. Intuitively, the reason is that at
2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
most of the behaviors stabilize.

Proposition 11.3
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
, then for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
we have that
(S^{\prime},w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
is also a stable cycle, and the transitions defined on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
are identical to those of
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}
.
I.e, for every
s,r\in S
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},s\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}},s\to r)
.

Proof.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We first claim that
(S^{\prime},w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
is a stable cycle.
Since
(S^{\prime},w)
is reflexive, then
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w)\subseteq S^{\prime}
, so
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w^{k})\subseteq S^{\prime}
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and in particular for
k=m2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
.
Similarly, by
Definition

10.5
we have that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the baseline state
s\in S
satisfies
s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{n})
and has a
0
-cycle, and in particular this holds for multiples of
m2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
, so
(S^{\prime},w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
is a stable cycle.

Next, consider
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, then by definition we have
s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r
for
g\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
, but then we also have
s\xrightarrow{(w^{m2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{(w^{m2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r
via the run

s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{m4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}-2}}g\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r
,
so
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
. Moreover, by the run above we also have

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)\leq\mathsf{mwt}((w^{m2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)
.

Conversely, consider
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{m\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, then by
Proposition

10.10
we have
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, and by
Lemma

10.11
we have
\mathsf{mwt}((w^{m2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)
.

Since the transitions on cactus letters are only defined for grounded pairs, this concludes the proof.
∎

We now apply stabilization inductively, and take the infinite union, as follows.

Definition 11.4

(

Stabilization Closure)
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
we define

\mathsf{stab}_{k}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{k}=\langle S,\Gamma_{k},s_{0},\widehat{\Delta}_{k}\rangle
inductively as follows.

•

For
k=0
we define
\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma
,
\widehat{\Delta}_{0}=\widehat{\Delta}
.

•

For
k>0
we define
\Gamma_{k}=\Gamma_{k-1}\cup\mathsf{stab}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{k-1}}(\Gamma_{k-1})
and
\widehat{\Delta}_{k}=\widehat{\Delta}_{k-1}\cup\mathsf{stab}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{k-1}}(\widehat{\Delta}_{k-1})

•

For
k=\infty
we define
\Gamma_{\infty}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Gamma_{k}
and
\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\widehat{\Delta}_{k}
.

We refer to the letters in
\Gamma_{\infty}
as
cactus letters of
\Gamma
and we denote

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}=\mathsf{stab}_{\infty}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
. Notice that while the sets of letters and transitions of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
are infinite, its set of states is
S
.

Example 2

(Cactus Letters)
.

Suppose
\Gamma=\{a,b\}
, we construct some cactus letters (See
Fig.

11
).
For the sake of the example we do not have a concrete WFA, as we just illustrate the syntax of cactus letters.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}w_{1}=aaa}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}w_{4}=bbb}
, we define
{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{S_{1},w_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}\alpha_{4}=\alpha_{S_{4},w_{4}}}
.
We then recursively define
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}w_{2}=ab{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}\alpha_{1}}ab}
with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{S_{2},w_{2}}}
.
We go up another level:
{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}w_{3}=baa{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}\alpha_{4}}abba{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha_{2}}ba}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{S_{3},w_{3}}}
. We can consider another word outside, e.g.,
ab{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\alpha_{3}}aa
.
Note that we could write the entire word as a single expression, but it is very cumbersome:

ab\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\alpha_{S_{3},baa\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}\alpha_{S_{4},bbb}}\cdot abba\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha_{S_{2},ab\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.89,1}\alpha_{S_{1},aaa}}\cdot ab}}\cdot ba}}\cdot aa

Figure 11
:
Cactus letters and cactus chains:
\alpha_{1}
is of depth
1
. The word
u_{1}\alpha_{1}v_{1}
(left) forms a cactus letter
\alpha_{2}
of depth
2
(center). Then,
u_{2}\alpha_{2}v_{2}
forms a cactus letter
\alpha_{3}
of depth
3
(right). Notice that
u_{2}
contains the cactus letter
\alpha_{4}
. The sequence
\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}
is a cactus chain, and so is
\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}
.

Remark 11.5
.

Recall that in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, transitions between baseline states have weight 0. We remark that this is preserved in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
. Indeed, a transition on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
between baseline states
(p,p,T)
and
(q,q,T^{\prime})
implies that
p=q
, and that
w
prescribes a
0
-weight self loop on
p
, since
S^{\prime}
is a stable cycle and
p\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
(see
Definition

10.5
).

Consider a word
w\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{*}
, then
w
is a concatenation of letters from various
\Gamma_{k}
’s. In the following we define the
depth
of
w
as the maximal
k
used in the letters of
w
. This is illustrated in
Fig.

11
.

In addition, we define a
cost
of a word. Intuitively, the cost represents a measure of “length” of words, where cactus letters are defined to be extremely long, and dramatically increasing in length with each added depth. The notion of cost is central to our proof, and becomes clear later on.

Definition 11.6
.

The functions
\mathsf{cost}:\Gamma_{\infty}\to\mathbb{N}
and
0pt:\Gamma_{\infty}\to\mathbb{N}
are defined inductively on the structure of
\Gamma_{\infty}^{*}
in
Table

1
.

\mathsf{cost}(w)=

0pt(w)=

w=a\in\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma

{\mathsf{max\_wt}(a)}+1

1

w=\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}\in\Gamma_{\infty}

2^{16({\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|\cdot\mathsf{cost}(x))^{2}}

1+0pt(x)

w=\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{m}\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{*},\ m\geq 2

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathsf{cost}(\sigma_{i})

\max_{1\leq i\leq m}(0pt(\sigma_{i}))

Table 1
:
The cost and depth functions.

Note that
Definition

11.6
is indeed inductive, since if
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\in\Gamma_{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
w\in\Gamma_{k-1}^{*}
. That is, considering the “inner” letters of a cactus letter must decrease the depth, so the definition reaches a terminating condition.

Remark 11.7

(
\mathsf{cost}
is strictly increasing on prefixes)
.

The reason for
+1
in the cost of a single letter is just so that the cost function is always strictly positive. Then, since the cost of a word is defined as the sum of the costs of its letters, we have that if
x
is a prefix of
y
, then
\mathsf{cost}(x)<\mathsf{cost}(y)
.

The precise reason for the cost of
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
being defined as it is becomes apparent in later sections. For now, we show that the cost can be used to upper-bound the maximal weight.

Proposition 11.8
.

For every
w\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{*}
it holds that
{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w)}\leq\mathsf{cost}(w)
.

Proof.

The proof is immediate by induction according to
Table

1
, as follows.

•

If
w=a\in\Gamma_{0}
, then by definition
\mathsf{cost}(a)={\mathsf{max\_wt}(a)}+1={\mathsf{max\_eff}(a)}+1>{\mathsf{max\_eff}(a)}
.

•

If
w=\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
, let
p,q
such that
(p,\alpha_{S^{\prime},x},c,q)\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}
with maximal (finite)
c
in absolute value, i.e.,
|c|={\mathsf{max\_wt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},x})}
.
Since there is a finite-weight transition on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
from
p
to
q
, it follows from
Definition

11.1
that
c=\mathsf{mwt}(x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},p\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}q)
for some state
g
. In particular,
c\leq{\mathsf{max\_wt}(x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})}2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|x|
. By induction we have
{\mathsf{max\_wt}(x)}|x|\leq\mathsf{cost}(x)
, so

\begin{split}c&\leq{\mathsf{max\_wt}(x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})}2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|x|={\mathsf{max\_wt}(x)}2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|x|\\
&\leq\mathsf{cost}(x)2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\leq 2^{16{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|^{2}\mathsf{cost}(x)}=\mathsf{cost}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},x})=\mathsf{cost}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},x})\end{split}

where we use the fact that
{\mathsf{max\_wt}(x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})}={\mathsf{max\_wt}(x)}
, since
{\mathsf{max\_wt}()}
depends only of the set of letters occurring in the word, and the fact that
b2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\leq 2^{16{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|^{2}b}
, which holds for all
b\in\mathbb{N}
.

We conclude that
{\mathsf{max\_wt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},x})}|\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}|=c\leq\mathsf{cost}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},x})
, as required.

•

If
w=\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{m}
, we have by the induction hypothesis that

\mathsf{cost}(w)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{cost}(\sigma_{i})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(\sigma_{i})}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{max\_wt}(\sigma_{i})}={\mathsf{max\_eff}(w)}

where we use the fact that for single letters,
{\mathsf{max\_eff}(\sigma_{i})}={\mathsf{max\_wt}(\sigma_{i})}
.

∎

11.2
Rebase Letters

In light of
Remark

11.5
, we note that cactus letters maintain the baseline. However, later on in the proof (in
Section

19.1
), we need to change the baseline while reading a stable cycle.
To this end, we introduce an additional set of letters and transitions that allow to (intuitively) traverse a grounded pair by reading a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
, while changing the baseline to a new state. The weight of these transitions is then normalized by the weight of the transition on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
.

Definition 11.9

(Rebase)
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
be the stabilization closure of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.
Consider a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\in\Gamma_{\infty}
and write
S^{\prime}=\{(q,p,T)\mid q\in T\}
.
For each grounded pair
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
where
s=(q_{1},p,T)
and
r=(q_{2},p,T)
with the transition
(s,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},c,r)\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}
, we add a letter
\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}
with the following transitions:

\{((q^{\prime},q_{1},T),\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r},d-c,(q^{\prime\prime},q_{2},T))\mid((q^{\prime},p,T),\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},d,(q^{\prime\prime},p,T))\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}\}

We refer to the resulting WFA as
\mathsf{rebase}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty})
.

Figure 12
:
Rebase letter
\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}
with
s=(q,p,T)
and
r=(q^{\prime},p,T)
induced by the
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
transition from
(q,p)
to
(q^{\prime},p)
. The weight of the resulting transitions, as well as the baseline run, shifts down by
c
, in accordance with the new base transition from
q
to
q^{\prime}
.

We illustrate the behavior of a rebase letter in
Fig.

12
.

Remark 11.10

(Cactus as Rebase)
.

Consider a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
where
S^{\prime}=\{(q,p,T)\mid q\in T\}
. We observe that for the baseline state
s_{p}=(p,p,T)
, the transitions defined on the letter
\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s_{p}\to s_{p}}
are identical to those on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
.

Indeed,
(s_{p},\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},0,s_{p})\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}
, so plugging in
0
for
c
in
Definition

11.9
gives identical weights to those of
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
.

As a sanity check, note that the weight of the transition from
s
to
r
upon reading
\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}
is
0
(since
(s,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},c,r)\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}
, and by plugging in
q^{\prime}=q_{1}
and
q^{\prime\prime}=q_{2}
in
Definition

11.9
). Therefore, the property that baseline transitions have weight
0
is maintained.

Remark 11.11
.

Observe that rebase introduces new letters only for letters obtained by stabilization. Therefore, it is an idempotent operator. That is, if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
by applying rebase, then applying rebase on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
does not introduce new letters.

We now alternate the stabilization closure and rebase ad infinitum, as follows.

Definition 11.12

(Cactus Extension)
.

Define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{0}_{0}=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.
For every
k,j\in\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
we define

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{k}=\langle S,\Gamma^{j}_{k},s_{0},\widehat{\Delta}^{j}_{k}\rangle
inductively as follows.

•

We define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{0}_{k}=\mathsf{stab}_{k}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})
as per
Definition

11.4
.

•

For
j>0
we define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{0}=\mathsf{rebase}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j-1}_{\infty})
.

•

For
j>0
and
k>0
(including
k=\infty
) we define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{k}=\mathsf{stab}_{k}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{0})
.

•

For
j=\infty
and every
k
we define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{k}
as the union (of alphabets and transitions) of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{\infty}
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 11.13
.

Observe that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{k}=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\infty}
for all
k
, since applying stabilization on the infinite union does not add new letters (since stabilization is applied to words of some finite depth anyway, and is therefore already in the union).

Remark 11.14
.

Recall that the
\mathsf{cost}
and
0pt
functions (
Definition

11.6
) are defined with respect to the stabilization closure. Thus, when considered on a word in the scope of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{\infty}
for some
j>0
, the underlying alphabet
\Gamma
is actually
\Gamma^{j}_{0}
.
That is, rebase letters are considered as having depth
1
, like “regular” letters.
In the context of our results, we never use
\mathsf{cost}
and
0pt
over rebase letters. However, since we also establish a general toolbox, this is worth noticing.

11.3
Jump Letters

Our final type of letters is a technical addition that simplifies (some) proofs.
These letters, referred to as
jump letters
, allow us to change the baseline state without other effect.

Definition 11.15
.

Consider a cactus extension
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{k}
. For states
s=(q,p_{1},T),r=(q,p_{2},T)\in S
and
T\subseteq Q
(recall that
Q
is the state space of the original WFA
\mathcal{A}
) we introduce a
jump letter

\mathsf{jump}_{s\to r}
with the following transitions. For states
t=(q^{\prime},p_{1},T)
and
g=(q^{\prime},p_{2},T)
(note the same
first
and third component), we add the transition
(t,\mathsf{jump}_{s\to r},0,g)
.
For states not of the form
(\cdot,p_{1},T)
, the transitions have weight
\infty
.
Note that the transitions on
\mathsf{jump}_{s\to r}
are deterministic.

Thus, jump letters can change the baseline without further effect.
Note that since the baseline component is deterministic, then upon reading
\mathsf{jump}_{s\to r,T}
, all reachable states change their baseline.
Also observe that jump can only change the baseline component to a state in
T
. However, as we discuss in the following (
Section

11.4
), some states in
T
may be unreachable. And indeed,
\mathsf{jump}
letters may allow the baseline to be at an unreachable state, thus making the baseline run not seamless.

Remark 11.16
.

Note that in
Definition

11.15
, the component
q
in
s
and
r
is ignored (indeed, the same transitions are induced regardless of
q
). Thus, we sometimes denote the states
s,r
of
\mathsf{jump}_{s\to r}
as e.g.,
s=(\cdot,p_{1},T)
, where
\cdot
stands for some arbitrary state.

Finally, due to the “violent” nature of jump letters, we emphasize whenever they are present in a run, and in particular in a seamless run. We say that a run is
jump free
if its transitions do not contain a jump letter.
We do not annotate whether an alphabet contains jump letters or not, but we state so explicitly when relevant.

11.4
Ghost Reachable States

Consider a word
w
. Recall that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w)
is the set of states reachable from
s_{0}
by reading
w
.
In
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, before the introduction of cactus letters, it holds that

\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w)=\{(p,q,T)\mid p\in T\}
for some set
T
. That is, the reachable first components are exactly captured by the third component
T
.
After introducing cactus letters, however, this no longer holds. Indeed,
Definitions

11.12
and
11.9
limit the transitions to certain states (namely those obtained from grounded pairs). In particular, we have that

\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w)=\{(p,q,T)\mid p\in T^{\prime}\}
for some
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
, but equality does not necessarily hold.
Intuitively, the states in
T\setminus T^{\prime}
are
those for which repetitions of cactus infixes lead to unbounded increase in weight, and are therefore abstracted to
\infty
in the stabilization.

Nonetheless, our analysis (in particular in
Sections

14
and
16.2
) requires us to reason about these states, which we term
ghost states
.

Definition 11.17

(Ghost-Reachable States
\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}
)
.

Consider a state
s=(p_{1},q_{1},T_{1})
and word
w
with
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s,w)=\{(p_{2},q_{2},T^{\prime}_{1})\mid p_{2}\in T_{2}\}
for some
T_{2}\subseteq T^{\prime}_{1}
.
We define the set
ghost-reachable states of
w
from
s
as

\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s,w)=\{(p_{2},q_{2},T^{\prime}_{1})\mid p_{2}\in T^{\prime}_{1}\}
.

Note that

.

is determined by

, and therefore if
