.

Remark 11.18

(Stable Cycles Maintain Ghost States)
.

For every stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
we have
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime},w)=S^{\prime}
.
Indeed, recall that for a cycle to be stable, and in particular reflexive (
Definition

10.1
), it holds that
S^{\prime}=\{(p,q,T)\mid p\in T\}
for some
T
(i.e., the set of states is “saturated” with respect to
T
), and that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w)\subseteq S^{\prime}
. In particular, all states in
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w)
are of the form
(\cdot,q,T)
for some
q,T\in Q
.
Thus, we have
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime},w)=\{(p,q,T)\mid p\in T\}=S^{\prime}
.

We also lift the definition to runs, as follows.

Definition 11.19

(Ghost Run)
.

Consider words
w_{1},w_{2}
and states
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1})
and
q\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2})\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2})
.
We then say that a run
q\xrightarrow{w_{2}}p
on the infix
w_{2}
is a
ghost run from
w_{1}
to
w_{1}w_{2}
.

We note that a ghost run cannot visit the same state as a “real” run on any prefix of
w_{2}
, as that would entail
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2})
. Thus, ghost runs are “uninterrupted” by real ones.

12
A Toolbox for Cacti

Cactus, rebase and jump letters intuitively do two things: (1) abstract away parts of words as “pumpable” cycles, in a
nested
manner, and (2) shift the baseline run between states.
A central theme in our reasoning is to utilize these features by “folding” parts of words into a cactus, “unfolding” other parts, possibly in a nested fashion, and shifting the baseline to a certain run as a “point-of-view change”. These operations demonstrate the versatility of the cactus framework.
In this section, we present the toolbox that allows us to perform these operations. This is divided to three parts: a framework to bound the depth of “interesting” nested cacti (
Section

12.1
), a framework for shifting the baseline run (
Section

12.2
), and a framework for getting rid of all cacti and rebase letters by
flattening
(
Section

12.3
).

12.1
Degeneracy and A Bound on Cactus Depth

In this section, we consider words built up by repeatedly nesting cactus letters, as the following definition captures (see
Fig.

11
).

Definition 12.1

(Cactus Chain)
.

A
cactus chain
is a finite sequence of letters
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},w_{n}}
such that for every
1\leq i<n
we have
w_{i}=u_{i}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i+1},w_{i+1}}v_{i}
.

Cactus chains can be arbitrarily deep. Our goal in this section is to show that while this is the case, only a bounded number of “layers” can include interesting behaviors. In order to formalize this, we introduce the notions of
degenerate
and
non-degenerate
stable cycles, as follows.

Definition 12.2

((Non-)Degenerate Stable Cycle)
.

A stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
is
degenerate
if for every
s\in S^{\prime}
,
t\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, if there is a run
s\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k}}t
for some
k\geq 1
, then
(s,t)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
.

Otherwise,
(S^{\prime},w)
is
non-degenerate
, and a state
s\in S^{\prime}
such that there exists
t\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
and
k\geq 1
for which
s\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k}}t
and
(s,t)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
is called a
non-degenerate state
.

Intuitively, the “canonical” degenerate cycle is one where all the reflexive states are also minimal-reflexive (i.e., have a
0
-cycle on
w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
). Indeed, then any reflexive state can serve also as a grounding state. The definition of degeneracy generalizes this notion, by allowing some reflexive states that are not minimal, but they cannot be reached without going through a grounding state, and therefore their non-minimality does not really add any unboundedness. We formalize this in
Section

12.1.1
.

12.1.1
Boundedness of Degenerate Cycles

Proposition 12.3
.

Consider a degenerate stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
and
s,r\in S^{\prime}
. For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, if
s\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|k}}r
, then

|\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|k},s\to r)|\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w)
.

Proof.

We split the proof to the upper and lower bound, starting with the former.
Consider
s,r\in S^{\prime}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
s\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|k}}r
. We show that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|k},s\to r)\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w)
. Consider the minimal-weight run
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|k}}r
, i.e.,
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|k},s\to r)
.

By the pigeonhole principle there exists
t\in S^{\prime}
such that we can write
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k_{1}}}t\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k_{2}}}t\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k_{3}}}r
with
k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=|S|k
and
k_{2}>0
. In particular, we have
t\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k_{2}})=\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
(by
Proposition

10.3
).

Since
(S^{\prime},w)
is degenerate, it follows from
Definition

12.2
that
(s,t)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
. We claim that this implies
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
. Indeed, take the grounding state of
(s,t)
, i.e., some
t^{\prime}\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
such that
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}t
, then we can write

s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}t\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k_{3}}}r

and therefore
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{(2k_{3}+1){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
. By
Proposition

10.10
this implies
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
.
Now, by
Lemmas

10.11
and
11.8
, we have that

\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|k},s\to r)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r)\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w)

Concluding the upper bound.

We proceed with the lower bound, which is significantly simpler. Intuitively, in order for the weight to decrease so much, there must be a negative cycle, which cannot happen in a stable cycle. We now formalize this argument.

Recall that
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\gg|S|
. We prove a stronger claim than the lower bound: for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m},s\xrightarrow{m}r)\geq-|S|\mathsf{cost}(w)>-2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w)
.
To this end, assume otherwise by way of contradiction, and let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be the minimal number such that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{m},s\to r)<-|S|\mathsf{cost}(w)
. By
Proposition

11.8
it must hold that
m\geq|S|
, since
|{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w^{m})}|\leq m\mathsf{cost}(w)
.

Let
\rho
be a minimal-weight run such that
\rho:s\xrightarrow{w}s_{1}\xrightarrow{w}s_{2}\xrightarrow{w}\cdots\xrightarrow{w}s_{m-1}\xrightarrow{w}r
. Denote
s=s_{0}
and
r=s_{m}
. Since
m\geq|S|
, then by the pigeonhole principle there exist
0\leq i<j\leq m
such that
s_{i}=s_{j}
. Denote
t=s_{i}=s_{j}
, then
t\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w)
.
Now, if
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{j-i},t\to t)\geq 0
, then we can shorten the run
\rho
while still having value lower than
-|S|\mathsf{cost}(w)
, contradicting the minimality of
m
. However, if
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{j-i},t\to t)<0
, this contradicts the fact that
(S^{\prime},w)
is a stable cycle (
Definition

10.5
). Thus, we are done.
∎

A simple corollary of
Proposition

12.3
is that enough repetitions of
w
cause a repeated configuration, as follows.

Proposition 12.4
.

Consider a degenerate stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. There exist

m<n\leq(2+4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w))^{|S|^{2}}

such that for every
t\in S^{\prime}
and corresponding configuration
\boldsymbol{c_{t}}
we have

\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{t}},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|m})=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{t}},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|n})

Proof.

Fix
t\in S^{\prime}
.
By
Proposition

12.3
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
it holds that

\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{t}},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|k})\in(\{-2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w),\ldots,2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w)\}\cup\{\infty\})^{S}

Denote
D=\{-2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w),\ldots,2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w)\}\cup\{\infty\}
, then
|D|=4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w)+2
.
Taking this view on all states simultaneously, we have that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the vector

\mathcal{T}_{k}=(\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{t}},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|k}))_{t\in S}
is in
(D^{S})^{S}
.

Thus, by the pigeonhole principle, there exists
0\leq m<n\leq(4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w)+2)^{|S|^{2}}
such
\mathcal{T}_{m}=\mathcal{T}_{n}
, which proves the claim.
∎

Naturally, once a configuration repeats in all states, this cycle can be arbitrarily pumped without any effect on the weight, as follows.

Proposition 12.5
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
and
m<n\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
t\in S^{\prime}
it holds that
\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{t}},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}m})=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{t}},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}n})
. Then for every
t,r\in S^{\prime}
and every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(m+i(n-m))},t\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},t\to r)
.

Proof.

Denote
k=n-m
and
n_{i}=ik+m
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular,
n_{0}=m
and
n_{1}=n
. By induction it is immediate that
\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{t}},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}m})=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{t}},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}n_{i}})
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

The slightly more involved part of the proof is concluding that for every
t,r\in S^{\prime}
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(m+i(n-m))},t\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},t\to r)
.
Intuitively, this holds because in order for the configurations to repeat, either
r
is not reachable from
t
at all, or the weights remain bounded, meaning that
(t,r)
is a grounded pair. We now formalize this intuition.

First, if
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(m+i(n-m))},t\to r)=\infty
for some
i\in\mathbb{N}
, then in particular
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},t\to r)=\infty
. Indeed, if there is a finite-weight run from
t
to
r
on

w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
, then there is a reflexive state within this run, which can be pumped to a finite-weight run on
w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(m+i(n-m))}
(indeed, on any multiple of
w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
).

Next, assume
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(m+i(n-m))},t\to r)=c<\infty
, we claim that
(t,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
. Indeed, assume by way of contradiction that
(t,r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, then by
Lemma

10.11
there exists
M_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
m^{\prime}\geq M_{0}
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}m^{\prime}},t\to r)>c
. Take
m^{\prime}=m+M_{0}(n-m)
, then
m^{\prime}\geq M_{0}
, so this inequality holds. However, by first part of the proof (the repetition of configurations), we have that

\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{t}},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}m})=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{t}},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(m+i(n-m))})=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{t}},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(m+M_{0}(n-m))})

and therefore

c<\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}m^{\prime}},t\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(m+i(n-m))},t\to r)=c

which is a contradiction.

We conclude that
(t,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, and therefore by
Lemmas

10.11
and
11.1
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(m+i(n-m))},t\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},t\to r)
.
∎

We conclude this section with its main result, stating that for a degenerate cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
, we can find
m
that is not too large, such that the behavior of
w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|m}
is identical to
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
, but
w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|m}
has lower cost than
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
. Intuitively, this means that it is worthwhile replacing
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
with
w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|m}
. This, in turn, implies that in order to minimize the cost of a word, degenerate cycles can be eliminated.

Lemma 12.6
.

Consider a degenerate stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. There exists
m<(2+4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w))^{|S|^{2}}
such that
\mathsf{cost}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|m})<\mathsf{cost}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},w})
and for every
t,r\in S^{\prime}
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|m},t\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},t\to r)
.

Proof.

Combining
Propositions

12.4
and
12.5
(note that the constant
|S|
needs to be introduced in
Proposition

12.5
), there exist
m<(2+4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w))^{|S|^{2}}
such that for every
t,r\in S^{\prime}
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|m},t\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},t\to r)
.

It remains to work out the cost constraint, using
Definition

11.6
(
Table

1
).

\begin{split}&\mathsf{cost}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|m})=2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|m\mathsf{cost}(w)<2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|(2+4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathsf{cost}(w))^{|S|^{2}}\mathsf{cost}(w)\\
&<_{(\dagger)}2^{16({\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|\mathsf{cost}(w))^{2}}=\mathsf{cost}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},w})\end{split}

where the inequality
(\dagger)
is a crude upper bound that can be seen as follows: denote
|S|=s
and
\mathsf{cost}(w)=c
, then
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}s<{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{s^{2}}
, and we have

\begin{split}&2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}s(2+4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}c)^{s^{2}}c<2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}sc(8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}c)^{s^{2}}<2^{s^{2}}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{s^{2}}c^{s^{2}}(8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}c)^{s^{2}}=\\
&(16{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}c^{2})^{s^{2}}<2^{(16{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}c^{2})^{s^{2}}}=2^{16({\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}sc)^{2}}\end{split}

∎

12.1.2
Existence of Degenerate Cycles in Cactus Chains

Our goal in this section is to show that a deep-enough cactus chain must contain a degenerate stable cycle. Specifically, “deep-enough” is more than
|S|
. We need some definitions and result along the way.

We now define the
Minimal-Reflexive Graph
of a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. This directed graph captures the reachability relation between minimal-reflexive states, upon reading
w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
.

Definition 12.7

(
Min_Graph
)
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
. Its
minimal-reflexive graph
is the directed graph

\textsc{Min\_Graph}(S^{\prime},w)=\langle V,E\rangle
with vertices

V=\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
and

E=\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)\cap(\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})\times\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}))

That is, we have
(s,r)\in E
if
s,r\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
and there exists
g\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
such that
s\xrightarrow{w^{M}}g\xrightarrow{w^{M}}r
(as per
Definition

10.7
).
We present a simple but useful property of
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(S^{\prime},w)
, namely that it is transitively closed.

Proposition 12.8
.

For every stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
, the graph
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(S^{\prime},w)
is transitively closed. That is, if
(s,r)\in E
and
(r,t)\in E
, then
(s,t)\in E
.

Proof.

Assume
(s,r)\in E
and
(r,t)\in E
. Thus, there exist
g_{1},g_{2}\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
such that
s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g_{1}\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r
and
r\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g_{2}\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}t
. In particular,
s\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
and
r\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}t
.
Since
V=\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, then
s,r\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
. Since the minimal states stabilize at
w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
(
Proposition

10.6
) we also have
s,r\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
.
Thus, we have
(s,r),(r,t)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
(see
Definition

10.7
).
Since tethered states also stabilize at
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
(
Proposition

10.8
), we have in particular that
(s,r),(r,t)\in\textsc{TthPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, so
s\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}t
, and since
r\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, we conclude that
(s,t)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, so
(s,t)\in E
.
∎

We now decompose
Min_Graph
into (maximal)
Strongly-Connected Components (SCC)
. Recall that an SCC is
C\subseteq V
such that for every
s,r\in C
there is a path from
s
to
r
and from
r
to
s
, and that
C
is maximal with this property (with respect to containment). By
Proposition

12.8
, in the case of transitive graphs we can just define an SCC to be a maximal clique (with respect to containment).

We now turn to the main technical result of this section, relating the number of SCCs of consecutive levels in a cactus chain, where the inner cactus is non-degenerate.

Lemma 12.9

(

SCCs of Nested
Min_Graph
)
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
with
w=u\alpha_{B,x}v
. Then the number of SCCs of
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(B,x)
is at least the number of SCCs of
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(S^{\prime},w)
. Moreover, if
(S^{\prime},w)
is a non-degenerate stable cycle, then this number is strictly greater.

Proof.

Denote the SCCs of
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(S^{\prime},w)
by
C_{1},...,C_{m}
.
For each SCC
C_{i}
choose a representative
s_{i}\in C_{i}
. For every
1\leq i\leq m
, since
s_{i}\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
, then in particular there is a minimal reflexive run
s_{i}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}}s_{i}
. By
Definition

11.1
, this means that
s_{i}\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}s_{i}
. That is,
s_{i}\xrightarrow{(u\alpha_{B,x}v)^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}s_{i}
. We split the run as follows:

s_{i}\xrightarrow{u}t_{i}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{B,x}}r_{i}\xrightarrow{v(u\alpha_{B,x}v)^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}-1}}s_{i}

Again by
Definition

11.1
, we have
(t_{i},r_{i})\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(B,x)
, so there exists
g_{i}\in\textsc{MinStates}(B,x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
such that
t_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r_{i}
. We associate with each
s_{i}
the corresponding state
g_{i}
. Note that
g_{i}
is a vertex in
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(B,x)
.

We claim that for every
j\neq i
, if there is an edge
(g_{j},g_{i})
in
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(B,x)
, then there is also an edge
(s_{j},s_{i})
in
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(S^{\prime},w)
. Indeed,
By the assumption we have that
(g_{j},g_{i})\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(B,x)
.
We therefore have the following:

\begin{split}&s_{j}\xrightarrow{u}t_{j}\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g_{j}\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r_{j}\xrightarrow{v(u\alpha_{B,x}v)^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}-1}}s_{j}\\
&s_{i}\xrightarrow{u}t_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r_{i}\xrightarrow{v(u\alpha_{B,x}v)^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}-1}}s_{i}\\
&g_{j}\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}h\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g_{i}\end{split}

where
h
is the grounding state of
(g_{j},g_{i})
. In particular, we have
g_{j}\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g_{i}
, and therefore
t_{j}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g_{j}\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g_{i}
, so
t_{j}\xrightarrow{x^{3{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g_{i}
.
Since
g_{i}\in\textsc{MinStates}(B,x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})=\textsc{MinStates}(B,x^{3{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
(by
Proposition

10.6
), it follows that
t_{j}\in\textsc{PltPairs}(B,x^{3{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
(
Definition

10.7
). By the stabilization of Plateau pairs (
Proposition

10.9
) we have that
t_{j}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g_{i}
.
But then we can compose the run

s_{j}\xrightarrow{u}t_{j}\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r_{i}\xrightarrow{v(u\alpha_{B,x}v)^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}-1}}s_{i}

So
s_{j}\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}s_{i}
.
Again by the stabilization of plateau pairs (
Proposition

10.9
) we have that
s_{i}\in\textsc{PltPairs}(S^{\prime},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
and therefore
s_{j}\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}s_{i}
. But
s_{i}\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
, and it can serve as a grounding state. We therefore have
s_{j}\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}s_{i}\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}s_{i}
, so
(s_{j},s_{i})\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, and is therefore an edge in
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(S^{\prime},w)
.
We can now prove the desired properties.

All the
g_{i}
are in different SCCs of
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(B,x)

Assume by way of contradiction that there are
i\neq j
such that
g_{i}
and
g_{j}
are in the same SCC
D
of
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(B,x)
.
By
Proposition

12.8
we have that
(g_{j},g_{i})
and
(g_{i},g_{j})
are edges in
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(B,x)
, and by the above we get that
(s_{j},s_{i})
and
(s_{i},s_{j})
are edges in
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(S^{\prime},w)
, which contradicts the fact that
s_{j},s_{i}
are in difference SCCs.

We conclude that each
g_{i}
lies in a separate SCC, so the number of SCCs in
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(B,x)
is at least that of
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(S^{\prime},w)
.

If
(S^{\prime},w)
is non-degenerate, there is some
g^{\prime}\in\textsc{MinStates}(B,x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
that is not in any of the SCCs of the
g_{i}
.

We now proceed to the case where
(S^{\prime},w)
is non-degenerate, and we exhibit at least one additional SCC in
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(B,x)
.

Since
(S^{\prime},w)
is non-degenerate (
Definition

12.2
), it has a non-degenerate state
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
. That is, there is some (non-minimal) reflexive state
r\in\textsc{RefStates}(S^{\prime},w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
with
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k}}r
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
(s^{\prime},r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
. Similarly to the previous case, we expand the run above as

s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{u}t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{B,x}}r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{vw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k-1}}r

By
Definition

11.1
we have
(t^{\prime},r^{\prime})\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(B,x)
, so there exists a state
g^{\prime}\in\textsc{MinStates}(B,x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
such that

t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r^{\prime}
.
We therefore have

s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{u}t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{vw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k-1}}r

We claim that
g^{\prime}
is not in any SCC of
g_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq m
. Assume otherwise by way of contradiction, then by
Proposition

12.8
there exists some
g_{i}
such that
(g^{\prime},g_{i})
and
(g_{i},g^{\prime})
are edges in
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(B,x)
. Thus,
(g_{i},g^{\prime}),(g^{\prime},g_{i})\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(B,x)
, and in particular
g_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g_{i}
. We now have the following runs:

\begin{split}&s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{u}t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{vw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k-1}}r\\
&s_{i}\xrightarrow{u}t_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r_{i}\xrightarrow{vw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k-1}}s_{i}\\
&g_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g^{\prime}\\
&g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g_{i}\end{split}

We can therefore obtain the runs:

\begin{split}&s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{u}t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r_{i}\xrightarrow{vw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k-1}}s_{i}\\
&s_{i}\xrightarrow{u}t_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g_{i}\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{vw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}k-1}}r\\
\end{split}

That is,
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}s_{i}\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
. Since
s_{i}\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
, then by
Proposition

10.10
we have
(s^{\prime},r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, in contradiction to the non-degeneracy assumption.

We conclude that
g^{\prime}
is not in any of the SCCs of the
g_{i}
, and therefore
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(B,x)
has strictly more SCCs than
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(S^{\prime},w)
.
∎

Since
\textsc{Min\_Graph}(S^{\prime},w)
can have at most
|S|
SCCs, an immediate corollary of
Lemma

12.9
is that a cactus chain of depth more than
|S|
must have a degenerate cycle.

Corollary 12.10
.

Consider a cactus chain
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},w_{n}}
. If
n>|S|
then there exists
1\leq i\leq n
such that
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}}
is a degenerate stable cycle.

Finally, by
Lemma

12.6
, if a cactus chain
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},w_{n}}
contains a degenerate stable cycle
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}}
, then we can replace this degenerate stable cycle by
w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|m}
for some
m
while maintaining equivalent transitions, but lowering the cost. Thus, minimal-cost cactus chains do not contain degenerate cycles. In particular, by
Corollary

12.10
, their depth is bounded by
|S|
. We conclude this section with this important result.

Corollary 12.11

(

Minimal-Cost Cactus Chains are Not Deep)
.

Consider a cactus chain
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},w_{n}}
of minimal cost, i.e.,

\mathsf{cost}(\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}})=\min\{\mathsf{cost}(\gamma)\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}\mid\forall s,r\in S,\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},s\to r)=\mathsf{mwt}(\gamma,s\to r)\}

Then
n\leq|S|
. In particular,
0pt(\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}})\leq|S|
.

12.2
Baseline Shift

In
Section

9.1
we establish the correspondence between the runs of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and of
\mathcal{A}
. In particular, this shows that regardless of the choice of baseline run, the run structure of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
(i.e., the differences between runs) is fixed.

In this section, we show a similar result in the presence of cactus and rebase letters. Since these letters are not present in
\mathcal{A}
, we show this directly on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
. More precisely, we show that for every word
w\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}^{\infty}
(without jump letters), and for every run
\rho_{0}
on
w
, we can construct a new word
w^{\prime}
with the same “inner letters” as
w
, and establish a bijection between runs on
w
and runs on
w^{\prime}
, such that differences are maintained and such that the run corresponding to
\rho_{0}
is the baseline run (and therefore has weight
0
throughout).

The intuition behind the construction is simple: given the run
\rho_{0}
, we only need to change the letters of
w
so that their baseline component follows the state component dictated by
\rho_{0}
. Then,
\rho_{0}
(or rather, the corresponding run that uses the same state components) becomes a baseline run, and all other runs shift their weights accordingly. In order to change the letters in this manner, cactus letters may become rebase letters (and some rebase letters become cactus letters).

The formal construction mainly tests the limits of the English and Greek alphabets, as well as the reader’s patience, but is otherwise simple, as follows.

Consider a word
w=\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{k}
where for every
1\leq i\leq k
we have that

\tau_{i}\in\{(p_{i-1},\sigma_{i},c_{i},p_{i}),\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}},\beta_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i},s_{i}\to r_{i}}\}

i.e., each letter is either in
\widehat{\Delta}
, a cactus, or a rebase.
Let
\rho_{0}=t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}
be a run of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
on
w
, and denote

t_{i}=((q_{i-1},p_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\tau_{i},d_{i},(q_{i},p_{i},T_{i}))
(where the
d_{i}
are the weights).

We define the word
w^{\prime}=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{k}
, where we split the definition of
\gamma_{i}
to cases according to whether
\tau_{i}
is a standard letter or a cactus/rebase.

•

If
\tau_{i}=(p_{i-1},\sigma_{i},c_{i},p_{i})
, then
d_{i}=c^{\prime}_{i}-c_{i}
where
c^{\prime}_{i}
is the weight of the transition
(q_{i-1},\sigma_{i},c^{\prime}_{i},q_{i})\in\widehat{\Delta}
(by
Section

9
).
In this case, we define
\gamma_{i}=(q_{i-1},\sigma_{i},c^{\prime}_{i},q_{i})
.

•

If
\tau_{i}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}}
or
\tau_{i}=\beta_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i},r_{i}\to s_{i}}
, denote
S^{\prime}_{i}=\{(q,p,T)\mid q\in T\}
for some
p,T
. We define
\gamma_{i}=\beta_{S^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i},(q_{i-1},p,T)\to(q_{i},p,T)}
. In case
q_{i-1}=q_{i}=p
, then we identify
\gamma_{i}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}}
as per
Remark

11.10
.

In order to show that
\gamma_{i}
is a well-defined rebase letter, we need to show that
((q_{i-1},p,T),(q_{i},p,T))\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i})
.
Again by
Remark

11.10
we can uniformly consider
\tau_{i}
as
\tau_{i}=\beta_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i},r_{i}\to s_{i}}
(identifying
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}}
with a rebase letter on the baseline state). Denote
r_{i}=(q^{\prime},p,T)
and
s_{i}=(q^{\prime\prime},p,T)
.

Since
t_{i}=((q_{i-1},p_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\tau_{i},d_{i},(q_{i},p_{i},T_{i}))
then by
Definition

11.9
we have
p_{i-1}=q^{\prime}
and
p_{i}=q^{\prime\prime}
, as well as
T_{i-1}=T_{i}=T
. So we can write

t_{i}=((q_{i-1},q^{\prime},T),\tau_{i},d_{i},(q_{i},q^{\prime\prime},T))
. Still by
Definition

11.9
(and since
d_{i}<\infty
) we have
((q_{i-1},p,T),\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}},e_{i},(q_{i},p,T))
for some
e_{i}
, and in particular
((q_{i-1},p,T),(q_{i},p,T))\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i})
, otherwise there would not be a transition as per
Definition

11.1
.

Having defined
w^{\prime}
, it now remains to show a bijection between runs on
w
and on
w^{\prime}
.

Proposition 12.12
.

In the notations above, for every sequence of states
f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\in Q
(states of
\mathcal{A}
), the following are equivalent.

•

\eta=x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
with
x_{i}=((f_{i-1},p_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\tau_{i},a_{i},(f_{i},p_{i},T_{i}))
is a run of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
on
w
.

•

\mu=y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
with
y_{i}=((f_{i-1},q_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\gamma_{i},a_{i}-d_{i},(f_{i},q_{i},T_{i}))
is a run of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
on
w^{\prime}
(recall that
d_{i}
are the weights in
\rho_{0}
).

Proof.

Consider a sequence of states
f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\in Q
, and
\eta
and
\mu
as in the statement. We show that the transitions
x_{i}
have finite weight if and only if the transition
y_{i}
have finite weight, thus concluding the claim.
For every
1\leq i\leq k
the following hold:

•

If
\tau_{i}=(p_{i-1},\sigma_{i},c_{i},p_{i})
, then the transition
x_{i}
is in
\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}^{\infty}
if and only if

a_{i}=c^{\prime\prime}_{i}-c_{i}
where
c^{\prime\prime}_{i}
is the weight of the transition
(f_{i-1},\sigma_{i},c^{\prime\prime}_{i},f_{i})\in\widehat{\Delta}
. Since
\gamma_{i}=(q_{i-1},\sigma_{i},c^{\prime}_{i},q_{i})
(and in particular has the same letter
\sigma_{i}
), this happens if and only if

((f_{i-1},q_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\gamma_{i},c^{\prime\prime}_{i}-c^{\prime}_{i},(f_{i},q_{i},T_{i}))\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}^{\infty}

Recall that by construction we have
d_{i}=c^{\prime}_{i}-c_{i}
, and therefore

c^{\prime\prime}_{i}-c^{\prime}_{i}=c^{\prime\prime}_{i}-c_{i}+c_{i}-c^{\prime}_{i}=c^{\prime\prime}_{i}-c_{i}-(c^{\prime}_{i}-c_{i})=a_{i}-d_{i}

and so the transition above is
y_{i}
.

•

If
\tau_{i}=\beta_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i},r_{i}\to s_{i}}
(we again use
Remark

11.10
to consider cactus and rebase jointly), denote
S^{\prime}_{i}=\{(q,p,T)\mid q\in T\}
and
r_{i}=(q^{\prime},p,T)
and
s_{i}=(q^{\prime\prime},p,T)
. Since
(r_{i},s_{i})\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i})
, we have
(r_{i},\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}},b^{\prime}_{i},s_{i})\in\Delta_{\infty}^{\infty}
for some
b^{\prime}_{i}<\infty
.
As in the construction of
w^{\prime}
, by
Definition

11.9

we have that
x_{i}\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}^{\infty}
if and only if

p_{i-1}=q^{\prime}
,
p_{i}=q^{\prime\prime}
and
T_{i-1}=T_{i}=T
, and
a_{i}=b_{i}-b^{\prime}_{i}
where
b_{i}
is such that
((f_{i-1},p,T),\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}},b_{i},(f_{i},p,T))\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}^{\infty}
(and in particular these states are a grounded pair).

As we show in the construction of
w^{\prime}
, we have
((q_{i-1},p,T),\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}},e_{i},(q_{i},p,T))
for some
e_{i}
.
By
Definition

11.9
, the above holds if and only if

((f_{i-1},q_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\beta_{S^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i},(q_{i-1},p,T)\to(q_{i},p,T)},b_{i}-e_{i},(f_{i},q_{i},T_{i}))\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}^{\infty}

Note that
d_{i}=e_{i}-b^{\prime}_{i}
, and therefore
a_{i}-d_{i}=b_{i}-b^{\prime}_{i}-(e_{i}-b^{\prime}_{i})=b_{i}-e_{i}
.
Therefore, this transition is exactly
y_{i}
.

∎

We wrap up this section by introducing the baseline-shift operator, and providing a concise formalism for using it.
For a word
w
and
\rho_{0}
as above, we denote the constructed word
w^{\prime}
by

\mathsf{bshift}[{w}/{\rho_{0}}]
.
We overload this notation to runs: for a run
\eta=x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
with
x_{i}=((f_{i-1},p_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\gamma_{i},a_{i},(f_{i},p_{i},T_{i}))
we denote by
\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{0}}]
the run
\mu=y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
with
y_{i}=((f_{i-1},q_{i-1},T_{i-1}),\gamma_{i},a_{i}-d_{i},(f_{i},q_{i},T_{i}))
. Note that a run already encapsulates the information about the word, and therefore we do not need to specify
w
in the latter notation.

Proposition

12.12
readily implies our two main results of this section, namely that the baseline shift of
\rho_{0}
by itself is a seamless run, and that baseline shifts maintain the gaps between runs.

Corollary 12.13
.

Consider a word
w
and a run
\rho_{0}
on
w
, then
\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{0}}/{\rho_{0}}]
is a seamless run (and in particular gains
0
weight in every transition).

Corollary 12.14
.

Consider a word
w
and a run
\rho_{0}
on
w
. Let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
be runs on
w
, and consider the shifted runs
\mu_{1}=\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{1}}/{\rho_{0}}]
and
\mu_{2}=\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{2}}/{\rho_{0}}]
then for every
0\leq i\leq|w|
it holds that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{1}[1,i])-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{2}[1,i])=\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{1}[1,i])-\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{2}[1,i])
.

12.3
Cactus Flattening

Intuitively, cactus (and rebase) letters encapsulate many repetitions of the same word, where rebase also allows a change in the baseline. Naturally, we wish to consider the corresponding words that are obtained by repeatedly “unfolding” cactus and rebase letters, in a process we refer to as
flattening
.
We make extensive use of flattening throughout the paper.

Recall that for a word
u
the value
{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}
is an upper-bound on the absolute value of the change in weight that a run can incur upon reading
u
.

Definition 12.15

(

Cactus-Unfolding)
.

Consider a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
, a word
u=x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
and
F\in\mathbb{N}
.

By
Lemma

10.11
there exists
M_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
k\geq M_{0}
and every
s,r\in S^{\prime}
with
(s,r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{k\cdot 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s\to r)>F
.

The
Unfolding of
u
with constant
F
is then
\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)=xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y
, and any word
xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}m}y
with
m\geq M_{0}
is
an unfolding of
u
with constant
F
.

Remark 12.16

(“The” v.s. “an” unfolding)
.

We remark that the distinction between “the” unfolding and “an” unfolding merely means that we can add repetitions of
w
(in multiples of
2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
), and still be referred to as an unfolding.

In the following, we mostly refer to
M_{0}
as the number of repetitions, but we sometimes need to increase this number, in which case we remark that all the results still hold.

Intuitively, while
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
makes transitions
s\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}}r
attain value
\infty
if
(s,r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, the unfolding repeats
w
enough times such that these runs may exist, but attain a value as high as we like (namely
F
).
We always choose
F
large enough such that any continuation of such runs is irrelevant for the value of the word. Therefore, the values of runs after unfolding are the same as those before unfolding. We capture this as follows.

Proposition 12.17
.

Consider a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
, a word
x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
and
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y)}
.

Let
\rho
be a seamless run on
x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
, then there exists a
seamless
run
\mu
on
\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)=xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y
such that

\mu[1,|x|]=\rho[1,|x|]
, and for every
i\geq 0
, if
z=x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y[1,i]
is a prefix of
x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
and the corresponding prefix of
\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)
is
z^{\prime}=x\cdot w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot y[1,i]
, then it holds that

\mathsf{wt}(\rho(z))=\mathsf{wt}(\mu(z^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

Let
u=x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
and
v=\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)=xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y
, and let
\rho
be a seamless run on
u
.
Denote
\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x}s\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}}r\xrightarrow{y}q

Since
s\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}}r
then by the definition of transitions on cactus letters (
Definition

11.1
) we have that
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
. Let
g
be the corresponding grounding state, i.e.,
g\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})

and
s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r
.
In particular,
\mathsf{mwt}(g\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g)=0
. Take
\eta:s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g
and
\xi:g\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r
to be runs of minimal weights (from/to their respective states), and
\zeta:g\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g
with weight
0
.
We construct
\mu
as the following concatenation of runs:

\mu:\rho(x)\eta\zeta^{(2M_{0}-2)}\xi\rho(y)

and therefore

\mu:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x}s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{(2M_{0}-2){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r\xrightarrow{y}q

First, note that
\mu
and
\rho
are identical on their
x
prefix. That is,

\mu[1,|x|]=\rho[1,|x|]
, as required.

Next, let
i\geq 0
and denote
z=x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y[1,i]
and
z^{\prime}=x\cdot w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot y[1,i]
.
Recall (from
Definition

11.1
) that
\mathsf{wt}(s\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}}r)=\mathsf{mwt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r)
. Since
\zeta:g\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g
has weight
0
, and
\xi
and
\eta
are of minimal weight, it follows that
\mathsf{wt}(s\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}}r)=\mathsf{wt}(\eta\zeta^{(2M_{0}-2)}\xi)
. Thus, upon reaching
r
, both
\rho
and
\mu
have the same weight.

Since the suffix read from
r
in both
z
and
z^{\prime}
is
y[1,i]
and the runs are identical at this suffix (namely
\rho(y)
), it follows that

\mathsf{wt}(\rho(z))=\mathsf{wt}(\mu(z^{\prime}))
.

Finally, it is left to prove that
\mu
is seamless (and recall that
\rho
is seamless).
Assume by way of contradiction that
\mu
is not seamless, and let
\mu^{\prime}
be a run on some prefix
u
of
\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)
such that both
\mu(u):s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}s_{1}
and
\mu^{\prime}(u):s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}s_{1}
but

\mathsf{wt}(\mu^{\prime}(u))<\mathsf{wt}(\mu(u))
.
That is,
\mu^{\prime}
arrives at
s_{1}
with smaller weight than
\mu
, thus “breaking”
\mu
.
We can assume without loss of generality that
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are identical from
s_{1}
(i.e., after reading
u
). Indeed, by concatenating the suffix of
\mu
to the prefix
\mu^{\prime}(u)
, the obtained run remains strictly below
\mu
from
s_{1}
and on, and also “breaks”
\mu
at
s_{1}
. We can further assume that
s_{1}
is the last state in both runs, i.e.,
q
. In particular
\mathsf{wt}(\mu^{\prime})<\mathsf{wt}(\mu)
.

Denote by
p_{1},p_{2}
the states visited by
\mu^{\prime}
after reading
x
and
xw^{2M_{0}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
respectively (we implicitly think of
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
as states at specific indices, to avoid cumbersome notations). See
Fig.

13
.

(a)

The cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
induces ghost states and runs. The baseline run is in blue, and the green run is seamless.

(b)

After unfolding,
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
is replaced by repetitions of
w
. The ghost runs now become concrete, and are far above the original runs. The updated green run is still seamless.

Figure 13
:
Cactus letter unfolding, before (
Fig.

13(a)
) and after (
Fig.

13(b)
).

There are now two possible cases: either
(p_{1},p_{2})\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, or not. Note that
p_{1},p_{2}\in S^{\prime}
(since
p_{1}\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},x)\subseteq S^{\prime}
and
p_{2}\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},w)\subseteq S^{\prime}
by
Definition

10.5
).

If
(p_{1},p_{2})\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, then
p_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}}p_{2}
. Moreover, the weight of this transition on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
is
\mathsf{mwt}(p_{1}\xrightarrow{w^{2M_{0}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}p_{2})
(by
Definitions

11.1
and
10.11
).
Thus, the weight gained by
\mu^{\prime}
over the infix is at least
\mathsf{mwt}(p_{1}\xrightarrow{w^{2M_{0}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}p_{2})
.
We can now consider the run
\rho^{\prime}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x}p_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}}p_{2}\xrightarrow{y}q
obtained by replacing the infix of
\mu^{\prime}
on
w^{2M_{0}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
with the transition on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
. It is immediate that

\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime})=\mathsf{wt}(\mu^{\prime})<\mathsf{wt}(\mu)=\mathsf{wt}(\rho)
, but this is a contradiction to the fact that
\rho
is seamless.

The most involved case is when
(p_{1},p_{2})\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
, where we heavily rely on the choice of
M_{0}
(the above does not actually use
M_{0}
, if examined carefully).

Note that in this case, there is no transition on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
from
p_{1}
to
p_{2}
, and we cannot readily obtain a run
\rho^{\prime}
as above.
However, by
Lemma

10.11
and the choice of
M_{0}
in
Definition

12.15
we have that
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2M_{0}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},p_{1}\xrightarrow{}p_{2})>F
.
In particular, the weight gained by
\mu^{\prime}
in the infix between
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
is more than
2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
, and therefore we have

\mathsf{wt}(\mu^{\prime})>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(y)}\geq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}

However,
\mathsf{wt}(\mu)=\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
and therefore
\mathsf{wt}(\mu^{\prime})>\mathsf{wt}(\mu)
, in contradiction to our assumption.

Thus, we conclude that
\mu
is seamless.
∎

Naively, we would want a converse of
Proposition

12.17
, i.e., that a seamless run on an unfolded word induces an equivalent seamless run on the folded word. Unfortunately, this is generally not true. Indeed there may be seamless runs that do not use grounded pairs, and therefore attain a huge weight on the unfolded word, but are simply not reachable in the folded word. Therefore, the converse needs to be stated carefully, as follows.

Proposition 12.18
.

Consider a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
, a word
x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
and
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y)}
. Let
\mu
be a seamless run on
\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)=xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y
, then the following hold.

1.

If
\mathsf{wt}(\mu)\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
then there exists a seamless run
\rho
on
x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y
such that

\mu[1,|x|]=\rho[1,|x|]
, and for every
i\geq 0
, if
z=x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y[1,i]
is a prefix of
x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
and the corresponding prefix of
\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)
is
z^{\prime}=x\cdot w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot y[1,i]
, then it holds that

\mathsf{wt}(\rho(z))=\mathsf{wt}(\mu(z^{\prime}))
.

2.

If
\mathsf{wt}(\mu)>{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
, then
\mathsf{wt}(\mu)>F-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y)}
.

Proof.

The proof can be viewed as a “reverse” proof of
Proposition

12.17
.

Consider a seamless run
\mu
on
\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)=xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y
.
Write
\mu:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x}s\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}r\xrightarrow{y}q
. By
Lemma

10.11
, if such that
\mathsf{wt}(\mu)\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
then it must hold that
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
. Indeed, otherwise the weight gained by
\mu
in the infix
s\xrightarrow{2^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}r
would be more than
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
(by
Definition

11.12
), which would in turn imply that
\mathsf{wt}(\mu)>{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
, which is a contradiction.

Since
(s,r)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
and
\mu
is seamless, we can assume without loss of generality that

\mu:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x}s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{(2M_{0}-2){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r\xrightarrow{y}q

where
g
is the grounding state of
(s,r)
, and the infix
s\xrightarrow{w^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{(2M_{0}-2){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}g\xrightarrow{w^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r
of
\mu
gains weight
\mathsf{mwt}(s\xrightarrow{w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}}r)
(as per
Lemma

10.11
).
We can now readily construct
\rho
by replacing this infix with a transition on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
with the same weight.

The fact that
\rho
is seamless now comes “for free” from
Proposition

12.17
. Indeed, if
\rho
is not seamless, we can take a lower-weight seamless run, and construct from it a seamless run
\mu^{\prime}
that would “break”
\mu
, contradicting the assumption that
\mu
is seamless.

For the second case, if
\mathsf{wt}(\mu)>{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
then again by
Lemma

10.11
it now follows that
(s,r)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},w)
(by the same analysis as above). By
Definition

11.12
we then have
\mathsf{mwt}(w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},s\to r)>F
, so
\mathsf{wt}(\mu)>F-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(y)}\geq F-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
.
∎

The combination of
Propositions

12.17
and
12.18
gives us the following useful tool.

Lemma 12.19

(

The Effect of Unfolding)
.

Consider a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
, a word
x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y
and
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y)}
, and let
\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)=xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y
.
Let
y^{\prime}
be a prefix of
y
, and consider the configurations
\boldsymbol{c_{1}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y^{\prime})
and
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y^{\prime})
, then
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{1}})\subseteq\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{2}})
and for every state
q\in S
we have the following.

1.

If
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{1}})
then
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{1}}(q)
.

2.

If
q\notin\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{1}})
then
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(q)>F-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
(and could be
\infty
).

Proof.

By
Proposition

12.17
we immediately get that
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{1}})\subseteq\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{2}})
and moreover, for every
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{1}})
we have
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{1}}(q)
, since a seamless run to
q
on
x\cdot\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\cdot y^{\prime}
induces a seamless run of the same weight on
xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y^{\prime}
.

Next, consider
q\notin\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{1}})
. If
q\notin\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{2}})
then we are done. Otherwise, it must hold that
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(q)>{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
. Indeed, if (by way of contradiction)
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(q)\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y)}
then by
Proposition

12.18
we have
\boldsymbol{c_{1}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(q)
and in particular
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{1}})
, which is a contradiction.

It then follows (still by
Proposition

12.18
) that
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(q)>F-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\alpha_{S^{\prime},x},y)}
, and we are done.
∎

Our next task is to eliminate rebase letters. Intuitively, a rebase letter is similar to a cactus letter that allows a change of baseline runs (with restrictions on what changes are allowed).
Thus, we replace a rebase letter with the corresponding cactus letter, surrounded by jumps to change the baseline.

Definition 12.20

(Rebase Removal)
.

Consider a rebase letter
\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}
and a word
x\cdot\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}\cdot y
where
s=(q_{1},p,T)
and
r=(q_{2},p,T)
.
Let
t_{1}=(\cdot,q_{1},T)
,
t_{p}=(\cdot,p,T)
and
t_{2}=(\cdot,q_{2},T)
where
\cdot
stands for some arbitrary state (c.f.,
Remark

11.16
)

The
Rebase Removal
of
x\cdot\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}\cdot y
is
\mathsf{rebase\_rm}(x,\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r},y)=x\cdot\mathsf{jump}_{t_{1}\to t_{p}}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\mathsf{jump}_{t_{p}\to t_{2}}y
.

Rebase removal is depicted in
Fig.

14
.
We claim that applying rebase removal doesn’t change the structure of the run tree, in the sense that the gaps between all runs remain the same. The following proposition captures this.

Figure 14
:
The rebase letter
\beta_{S^{\prime},w,t\to r}
is replaced by the sequence
\mathsf{jump}_{t\to p}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\mathsf{jump}_{p\to r}
. The baseline changes

Proposition 12.21
.

Consider a rebase letter
\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}
with
s=(q_{1},p,T)
and
r=(q_{2},p,T)
. Denote
t_{1}=(\cdot,q_{1},T)
,
t_{p}=(\cdot,p,T)
and
t_{2}=(\cdot,q_{2},T)
as per
Definition

12.20
. Let
c\in\mathbb{N}
be the weight of the transition
(s,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},c,r)
.

For every
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}\in Q
(states of the original WFA
\mathcal{A}
), the transition

((q^{\prime},q_{1},T),\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r},d-c,(q^{\prime\prime},q_{2},T))

exists if and only if the
6
6

6

Observe that this is indeed a single run, since each part of it is a single transition.
run

(q^{\prime},q_{1},T)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{jump}_{t_{1}\to t_{p}}}(q^{\prime},p,T)\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}}(q^{\prime\prime},p,T)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{jump}_{t_{p}\to t_{2}}}(q^{\prime\prime},q_{2},T)

exists and has weight
d
.

Moreover, there are no other runs from
(q^{\prime},q_{1},T)
to
(q^{\prime\prime},q_{2},T)
on
\mathsf{jump}_{t_{1}\to t_{p}}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\mathsf{jump}_{t_{p}\to t_{2}}
.

Proof.

The proof is actually immediate by the respective definitions, but does require untangling each definition.

First, observe that every transition of the form
(s^{\prime},\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r},e,r^{\prime})
implies by
Definition

11.9
that
s^{\prime}=(q^{\prime},q_{1},T)
and
r^{\prime}=(q^{\prime\prime},q_{2},T)
for some
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}\in Q
. Thus, these are the only types of transition that can be considered. Therefore, consider some
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}\in Q
.

Recall that
c
is the weight of
(s,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},c,r)
. Still by
Definition

11.9
, we have that

((q^{\prime},q_{1},T),\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r},d-c,(q^{\prime\prime},q_{2},T))

if and only if
((q^{\prime},p,T),\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},d,(q^{\prime\prime},p,T))
.
By
Definition

11.15
, this holds if and only if we have the following run

(q^{\prime},q_{1},T)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{jump}_{t_{1}\to t_{p}}}(q^{\prime},p,T)\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}}(q^{\prime\prime},p,T)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{jump}_{t_{p}\to t_{2}}}(q^{\prime\prime},q_{2},T)

Where the transitions on jump letters have weight
0
, and the transition on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
has weight
d
. This concludes the claim.
∎

By applying
Proposition

12.21
to entire runs, we obtain a bijection between runs on
u
and on
\mathsf{rebase\_rm}(u)
which, intuitively, preserves the gaps between runs. More precisely, by carefully tracking indices and state names, we have the following.

Corollary 12.22
.

Consider a word
x\cdot\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}\cdot y
with
s=(q,p,T)
and
r=(q^{\prime},p,T)
.
There exists a constant
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that the following are equivalent for every sequence of transitions
\rho=t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{k}
where
t_{i}=(s_{i},\sigma_{i},d_{i},s_{i+1})

and
s_{i}=(q_{i},p_{i},T_{i})
for all
i
.

•

\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x}s_{1}\xrightarrow{\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}}s_{2}\xrightarrow{y}s_{3}
is a run on
x\cdot\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}\cdot y
.

•

The sequence of transition

\rho^{\prime}=t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{|x|},t^{\prime}_{1},t^{\prime}_{2},t^{\prime}_{3},t_{|x|+1},\ldots,t_{k}

with

\begin{split}&t^{\prime}_{1}=(s_{|x|+1},\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,q,T)\to(\cdot,p,T)},0,(q_{|x|+1},p,T))\\
&t^{\prime}_{2}=((q_{|x|+1},p,T),\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},c,(q_{|x|+2},p,T))\\
&t^{\prime}_{3}=(q_{|x|+2},\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p,T)\to(\cdot,q^{\prime},T)},0,s_{|x|+2})\end{split}

is a run on
\mathsf{rebase\_rm}(x,\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r},y)
, i.e.,

\rho^{\prime}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x}s_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,q,T)\to(\cdot,p,T)}}s^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}}s^{\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p,T)\to(\cdot,q^{\prime},T)}}s_{2}\xrightarrow{y}s_{3}

And observe that in case both runs exist, the weights of the runs are identical up to
s_{1}
, and from
s_{2}
the run
\rho^{\prime}
has weight higher by
c
than
\rho
.

Having established how to remove cactus letters and rebase letters, we can now recursively apply these definition to
flatten
any word
u\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty})^{*}
back to a word in
\Gamma^{*}
(with jump letters). Before defining the flattening precisely, note that if
u\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty})^{*}
then in fact
u\in(\Gamma_{k}^{j})^{*}
for some finite
k,j
(indeed, the infinite indices are reached by infinite unions, but cannot be attained in a single finite word).

We remark that the order with which one removes cactus letters (e.g., leftmost first, or highest-depth first) may result in different flattenings. We fix as a canonical order the highest-depth first approach (and left to right among those), but this is arbitrary. It is captures below with the requirement that the prefix
x
is of “lower” rank.

Definition 12.23

(Flattening)
.

Consider a word
u\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty})^{*}
and
F\in\mathbb{N}
.
We define the
flattening of
u
with constant
F
, denoted
\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F)
, inductively as follows.

•

Base case:
If
u\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}=\Gamma^{*}
, then
\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F)=u
.

•

Cactus Case:
If
u\in(\Gamma^{j}_{k})^{*}\setminus(\Gamma^{j}_{k-1})^{*}
for
j\geq 0
and
k\geq 1
, write
u=x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y
such that
x\in(\Gamma^{j}_{k-1})^{*}
and
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\in\Gamma^{j}_{k}\setminus\Gamma^{j}_{k-1}
, then we define

\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F)=\mathsf{flatten}(\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F)\wr F+2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)})

•

Rebase case:
If
u\in(\Gamma^{j}_{0})^{*}\setminus(\Gamma^{j-1}_{\infty})^{*}
for
j\geq 1
, write
u=x\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}y
such that
x\in(\Gamma^{j-1}_{\infty})^{*}
and
\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}\in\Gamma^{j}_{0}\setminus\Gamma^{j-1}_{\infty}
, then we define
\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F)=\mathsf{flatten}(\mathsf{rebase\_rm}(x,\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r},y)\wr F)
.

Observe that
Definition

12.23
always terminates. Indeed, by induction after every application of the inductive cases, the number of maximal-depth cactus letters, or number of rebase letters of highest rank
j
, decreases by one. Note that each application of
\mathsf{unfold}
or
\mathsf{rebase\_rm}
may create additional cacti and rebase letters that were nested, but these are of lower ranks.

We illustrate the flattening process of the cactus in
Fig.

11
in
Fig.

15
.

Figure 15
:
Illustration of flattening the cactus in
Fig.

11
. The horizontal dotted lines represent many more iterations.

Also note that in the recursive cactus case, we increase
F
by
2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}
. This ensures that if there are “inner letters” of higher cost that are nested in the cactus, their weight is also accounted for.

The main characterization of flattening is that it maintains the weights to the reachable states, makes ghost states reachable, but gives the latter very high weight, as we now formulate.

Lemma 12.24
.

Consider a word
u\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty})^{*}
and
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{c}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},u)
and
\boldsymbol{d}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F))
.
Then

\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u)=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})\subseteq\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F))=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule
