width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F))=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u)

and for every
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d})
, if
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})
then
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\boldsymbol{d}(q)
, and otherwise
\boldsymbol{d}(q)\geq\max\{\boldsymbol{c}(p)\mid p\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})\}+F
.

Proof.

The containment/equality chain is immediate by the construction of unfolding and rebase removal. The interesting part of the proof is the weight accounting.
The claim is proved by induction on the word
u
, as per
Definition

12.23
.

Base case:

For the base case, if
u\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
, then
\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F)=u
and the claim is trivial, since
\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{d}
.

Cactus case:

For the cactus case, write
u=x\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}y
and denote
u^{\prime}=\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},y\wr F+2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)})
.
Define
\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{d}
as in the premise, and additionally
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},u^{\prime})
.
Let
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F))
.

If
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})
, then in particular there is a seamless run
\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}q
, so by
Proposition

12.17
there is a seamless run with the same weight
\mu:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime}}q
. Assume
\rho
has minimal weight, then since
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}
(which holds regardless of minimality) it follows by
Proposition

12.18
that
\mu
is also minimal weight (otherwise there would be a lower run to
q
).
Thus, we have
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q)
and in particular
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}})
.
We have that
\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr F)=\mathsf{flatten}(u^{\prime}\wr F+2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)})
, so by
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}})
we can apply the induction hypothesis to obtain
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q)=\boldsymbol{d}(q)
, but then
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\boldsymbol{d}(q)
, so this case is proved. Intuitively, all we show here is that if
q
is already reachable, then this property is maintained through the inductive step (which is fairly obvious by the definition of unfolding).

Now assume
q\notin\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})
, we split to two cases. If
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}})
then
q\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u)\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u)
but
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u^{\prime})
.
In particular,
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q)\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u^{\prime})}
.
Also, by the induction hypothesis, we have
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q)=\boldsymbol{d}(q)
.
By
Proposition

12.18
, there is no seamless run
\mu:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u^{\prime}}q
with
\mathsf{wt}(\mu)\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}
(otherwise we would have
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u)
), and therefore (by the second item of
Proposition

12.18
) we have

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{d}(q)=&\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q)=\mathsf{mwt}(u^{\prime},s_{0}\to q)>F+2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}=\\
&F+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}\geq F+\max\{\boldsymbol{c}(p)\mid p\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})\}\end{split}

as required.
Intuitively, this case amounts to showing that at the first level of the unfolding where
q
becomes reachable, its weight is very high.

The remaining case is if
q\notin\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}})
. Then, by the induction hypothesis we have

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{d}(q)\geq&\max\{\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(p)\mid p\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})\}+F+2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}>\\
&F+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}\geq F+\max\{\boldsymbol{c}(p)\mid p\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})\}\end{split}

Intuitively, this case just shows that if
q
is not yet reachable even in the current unfolding level, then by the end of the unfolding its weight is extremely high.

Rebase case:

The rebase case is immediate by
Corollary

12.22
. Indeed, the reachable set of configurations does not change, nor do the weights.
∎

Conceptually, flattening is simply recursive unfolding (as depicted in
Figs.

13
and
15
), with some adjustments to account for rebase letters.

Propositions

12.17
,
12.18
,
12.22
and
12.24
give us the tools to reason about the flattening of words, and show that they maintain the gaps in runs. In
Appendix

C
we introduce some additional reasoning to eliminate jump letters, and show the following.

Theorem 12.25
.

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
is determinizable if and only if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is determinizable.

By combining this with
Lemma

9.8
we also have the following.

Corollary 12.26
.

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
is determinizable if and only if
\mathcal{A}
is determinizable.

13
Dominance, Potential and Charge

The construction in
Section

11
can be thought of as a pumping argument, in that we make sure that states that can be (relatively) easily pumped unboundedly are out of the way. This construction is now equipped with a variety of tools from
Section

12
.
Our next task is to define certain measures of configurations, that essentially refine the notion of
B
-gap witness (
Definition

8.3
), and refer specifically to the cactus extension. We present two such measures, the
potential
and
charge
, and then provide a toolbox to work with them.

In the following section we refer to
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\infty}
(sometimes with jump letters). Specifically, recall that in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\infty}
, the weight of baseline transitions is
0
, and that there is at most one baseline run on each word (see
Remark

11.5
).

13.1
Dominant States, Potential and Charge – Definitions

Consider a configuration
\boldsymbol{c}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\infty}
.
Our first definition describes when a state
q
is still “relevant” in
\boldsymbol{c}
, in the sense that some suffix read from
\boldsymbol{c}
may make the run starting from
q
minimal, or at least better (i.e., lower) than states that are currently lower. We make this precise as follows.

Definition 13.1

(

Dominant State)
.

Consider a configuration
\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}^{S}
. We say that a state
q\in S
is
dominant
if there exists
w\in\Sigma^{*}
such that

\mathsf{mwt}(w,q\to S)<\infty

and for every
p\in S
, if
\boldsymbol{c}(p)<\boldsymbol{c}(q)
then

\mathsf{mwt}(w,p\to S)=\infty
.

We say that
q
is
maximal dominant
if
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\max\{\boldsymbol{c}(p)\mid p\text{ is dominant}\}
. We then denote this maximal value by
\mathsf{dom\_val}(\boldsymbol{c})=\boldsymbol{c}(q)
and the set of maximal dominant states by
\mathsf{max\_dom}(\boldsymbol{c})
.

Intuitively,
q
is dominant if it can yield a run with finite weight, whereas all states below
q
only yield infinite weight runs (i.e, they cannot complete a valid run).

Note that states
q^{\prime}
with
\boldsymbol{c}(q^{\prime})\geq\boldsymbol{c}(q)
are not considered in the definition, and may yield even lower runs than those starting from
q
. Thus, we distinguish the maximal dominant state as well.
Also note that dominance seems strongly tied to the notion of gap witnesses (
Definition

8.3
). However, there is an important difference: the baseline run can be chosen to be some arbitrary run, which may cause the potential to be large, but such that way below the baseline there is another accepting run, and therefore there are no gap-witnesses.
The precise connection between dominance and gap witnesses/determinizability is made in
Section

14
.

Next, recall that some states in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\infty}
are baseline, and that a run is a baseline if all the states along it are baselines. We wish to extend the definition of a baseline run to words. Naively, one could require that a word has a baseline run. This, however, is trivial – every word has a baseline run (this follows from the definition of the baseline-augmented subset construction in
Section

9
).
Instead, we require that the baseline run is
seamless
(c.f.
Section

8
), i.e., that for every state
q
visited by the baseline run after a prefix, the baseline run is also minimal to that state (although there may be other, lower runs, that go through other states).

Thus, we say that
w

has a seamless baseline run
if its baseline run is seamless.
Recall that in the baseline-augmented subset construction, the weight of a baseline run is
0
, as baseline transitions are normalized to weight 0. This special status of the baseline run, provided it is seamless, allows us to capture the growth of other runs. We measure this in two ways: the difference between the highest dominant state to the baseline run is the
potential
, and the difference between the baseline run and the current minimal state is the
charge
7
7

7

The term “potential” makes sense as it refers to states that can potentially become minimal. The name “charge” was chosen to keep in line with the physics-based naming.
. Recall that
\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{c}_{\texttt{init}})
is the configuration reached by
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\infty}
after reading
w
. We then have the following.

Definition 13.2

(

Potential and Charge)
.

Consider a word
w
that has a seamless jump-free baseline run and let
\boldsymbol{c}_{w}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{c}_{\texttt{init}})
.

1.

The
potential
of
w
is
\upphi(w)=\mathsf{dom\_val}(\boldsymbol{c}_{w})
.

2.

The
charge
of
w
is
\uppsi(w)=-\min\{\boldsymbol{c}(q)\mid q\in Q\}
.

Note that
\upphi(w)
and
\uppsi(w)
are always nonnegative (when they are defined), since the minimal run is of weight at most
0
, due to the seamless baseline run.

13.2
A Toolbox for Potential and Charge

We turn to give some fundamental results about potential and charge, as well as link them to the tools of
Section

12
– specifically to baseline shift and unfolding.

Observe that if
w\cdot\sigma
has a seamless baseline run, then so does
w
. In the following lemmas, we show that the difference in potential and in charge between
w\cdot\sigma
and
w
is bounded from above. This, however, requires that the alphabet is finite, and therefore the bound is not uniform for all words. Nonetheless, this suffices for our needs.

Lemma 13.3

(

Potential Bounded Growth)
.

For every finite alphabet
\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\infty}_{\infty}
there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
w\in\Gamma^{*}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma
, if
w\cdot\sigma
has a seamless baseline run then
\upphi(w\cdot\sigma)-\upphi(w)\leq k
.

Proof.

Intuitively, the proof is simple: since
\Gamma
is finite, the maximal weight that can be accumulated in a single step is bounded.
Thus, if
\mathsf{max\_dom}(w\cdot\sigma)
is much higher than
\mathsf{max\_dom}(w)
, then the predecessor of
\mathsf{max\_dom}(w\cdot\sigma)
must have already started higher than
\mathsf{max\_dom}(w)
, and is either a dominant state itself, or some state slightly lower but still higher than
\mathsf{max\_dom}(w)
is dominant. This contradicts the maximality of
\mathsf{max\_dom}(w)
.
We turn to formalize this argument.

Consider a finite alphabet
\Gamma
, and let
W
be the maximal weight (in absolute value) that appears in a transition on a letter
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

Consider a word
w\cdot\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
that has a seamless baseline run (whose prefix on
w
is therefore also a seamless baseline run), and assume by way of contradiction that
\upphi(w\cdot\sigma)-\upphi(w)>W
.
Let
p=\mathsf{max\_dom}(w)
and
p^{\prime}=\mathsf{max\_dom}(w\cdot\sigma)
, and let
\boldsymbol{c}_{w}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}})
and
\boldsymbol{c}_{w\sigma}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\sigma,\boldsymbol{c}_{w})
.
Consider the predecessor
q
of
p^{\prime}
. That is,
\mathsf{mwt}(w\sigma,q_{0}\xrightarrow{w}q\xrightarrow{\sigma}p^{\prime})=\mathsf{mwt}(w\sigma,q_{0}\to p^{\prime})
. We claim that
\boldsymbol{c}_{w}(q)>\boldsymbol{c}_{w}(p)
.
Indeed, more generally, if
r
is a state for which
\boldsymbol{c}_{w}(p)\geq\boldsymbol{c}_{w}(r)
, then
r
cannot yield any finite-weight run to
p^{\prime}
on
\sigma
, otherwise

\begin{split}&\mathsf{mwt}(w\sigma,q_{0}\xrightarrow{w}r\xrightarrow{\sigma}p^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{mwt}(w\sigma,q_{0}\to r)+W=\boldsymbol{c}_{w}(r)+W\leq\boldsymbol{c}_{w}(p)+W=\\
&\upphi(w)+W<\upphi(w\sigma)=\mathsf{mwt}(w\sigma,q_{0}\xrightarrow{w}q\xrightarrow{\sigma}p^{\prime})=\mathsf{mwt}(w\sigma,q_{0}\to p^{\prime})\end{split}

(13.1)

which would be a contradiction.

Since
p^{\prime}=\mathsf{max\_dom}(w\cdot\sigma)
then by
Definition

13.1
there exists a word
z\in\Sigma^{*}
(note that
z
is not assumed to be over
\Gamma
) such that

\mathsf{mwt}(z,p^{\prime}\to Q)<\infty

and for every
q^{\prime}
with
\boldsymbol{c}_{w\sigma}(q^{\prime})<\boldsymbol{c}_{w\sigma}(p^{\prime})
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(z,q^{\prime}\to Q)=\infty
.
In particular, we have that

\mathsf{mwt}(\sigma z,q\to Q)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(\sigma z,q\xrightarrow{\sigma}p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{z}Q)<\infty
.

Let
r
be a state attaining
\min\{\boldsymbol{c}_{w}(r)\mid\mathsf{mwt}(\sigma z,r\to Q)<\infty\}
. By similar argument to
Eq.

13.1
it must hold that
\boldsymbol{c}_{w}(r)>\boldsymbol{c}_{w}(p)
(since any transition from
r
on
\sigma
can accumulate at most
W
, and therefore cannot surpass
\boldsymbol{c}_{w}(p^{\prime})
if starting below
\boldsymbol{c}_{w}(p)
).
It follows that
r
is a dominant state in
\boldsymbol{c}
, contradicting the assumption that
p=\mathsf{max\_dom}(w)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 13.4

(Charge Bounded Growth)
.

For every finite alphabet
\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\infty}_{\infty}
there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
w\in\Gamma^{*}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma
, if
w\cdot\sigma
has a seamless baseline run then
\uppsi(w\cdot\sigma)-\uppsi(w)<k
.

Proof.

The proof idea is dual to that of
Lemma

13.3
(but simpler): the minimal (negative) weight that can be accumulated in a single step is bounded from below, and therefore the minimal run cannot drop too much in a single step.

Consider a finite alphabet
\Gamma
, and let
W
be the maximal weight (in absolute value) that appears in a transition on a letter
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Consider a word
w\cdot\sigma\in\Gamma^{*}
that has a seamless baseline run (whose prefix on
w
is therefore also a seamless baseline run).

Let
p
and
p^{\prime}
be the states attaining
-\uppsi(w)
and
-\uppsi(w\sigma)
, respectively. In particular, it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(w,q_{0}\to Q)=\mathsf{mwt}(w,q_{0}\to p)=-\uppsi(w)
and
\mathsf{mwt}(w\sigma,q_{0}\to Q)=\mathsf{mwt}(w\sigma,q_{0}\to p^{\prime})=-\uppsi(w\sigma)
.
We then have

\mathsf{mwt}(w\sigma,q_{0}\to p^{\prime})\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w,q_{0}\to Q)+\mathsf{mwt}(\sigma,Q\to Q)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w,q_{0}\to p)-W

We thus have
-\uppsi(w\sigma)\geq-\uppsi(w)-W
, so
\uppsi(w\sigma)-\uppsi(w)\leq W
, concluding the claim.
∎

In contrast to
Lemma

13.4
,
\uppsi
can
decrease
abruptly, if a minimal run cannot continue, so a much higher run becomes minimal.
The following definition, when applicable, guarantees this doesn’t happen.

Definition 13.5

(Bounded-Decrease Charge)
.

Consider a word
xyz
where
\uppsi
is defined, and let
B\in\mathbb{N}
.
We say that the infix
y
has
B
bounded-decrease charge in
xyz
if for every prefix
u\sigma
of
y
it holds that
\uppsi(xu)-\uppsi(xu\sigma)<B
.

A useful property linking charge with potential is that when the charge does not have bounded decrease, it implies that the potential is unbounded. For this result, we restrict the alphabet to
\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
, which is our main focus for most of the paper.
More precisely, we have the following.

Lemma 13.6

(

Unbounded Charge Decrease Implies Unbounded Potential)
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
be a finite alphabet, and assume that for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
there exist a word
u\in\Gamma^{\prime*}
and a letter
\sigma\in\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\uppsi(u)-\uppsi(u\sigma)\geq B
.
Then
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
.

Proof.

Intuitively, since
\sigma
causes a large jump in the charge, this means that
\sigma
cannot be read by the minimal run after
u
, and that the only run that can read it is much higher. This already suggests that the potential is unbounded.
Technically, all that needs to be done is to perform a baseline shift (
Section

12.2
) on a minimal run on
u
. This, in turn, also requires us to first flatten (
Section

12.3
) this prefix, so that we remain in
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
.
We recommend following this argument, as gentle preparation to the upcoming horrors of

Lemma

18.8
.
We illustrate the proof in
Fig.

16
.

(a)

Step 1

(b)

Step 2

(c)

Step 3

Figure 16
:
The proof flow of
Lemma

13.6

Denote by
W_{\max}
the maximal weight appearing in a transition over any letter in
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Let
P\in\mathbb{N}
, we show that the potential can be greater than
P
. By the assumption, there exist
u,\sigma
such that
\uppsi(u)\geq\uppsi(u\sigma)+P+2W_{\max}
.

Since
\uppsi
is the negation of the minimal weight, there exists a seamless run
\rho_{1}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}S
such that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{1})=-\uppsi(u)=\mathsf{mwt}(u,s_{0}\to S)
.
Let
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},u)
, and consider the minimal state
s_{1}
that can read
\sigma
. That is,
s_{1}\in\arg\min\{s\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}})\mid\mathsf{mwt}(\sigma,s_{1}\to S)<\infty\}
. It must hold that
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s_{1})>\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{1})+P
as otherwise (since
W_{\max}
bounds the weight incurred on
\sigma
) the minimal run on
u\sigma
has weight at most

-\uppsi(u)+P+W_{\max}<-\uppsi(u)+P+2W_{\max}\leq-\uppsi(u\sigma)

where the last inequality follows by our requirement on
\uppsi(u\sigma)
. But this is a contradiction, since
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0},u\sigma)=-\uppsi(u\sigma)
.
Put simply – if the charge has a large jump on
\sigma
, then the minimal state that can read
\sigma
is much higher than the minimal state up to
\sigma
. Note that
s_{1}
is therefore dominant (
Definition

13.1
).
Let
\rho_{2}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}s_{1}
be a seamless run to
s_{1}
(see
Fig.

16(a)
).

We now turn to flattening the prefix (
Definition

12.23
).
Let
x_{0}=\mathsf{flatten}(u\wr 2W_{\max}|u\sigma|)
. Since
\Gamma^{\prime}
does not contain rebase letters, we have
x_{0}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
(since flattening may only introduce jump letters in case of rebase letters).
Let
\mu_{1}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x_{0}}S
be a seamless run such that
\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{1})=-\uppsi(x_{0})
and similarly
\mu_{2}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x_{0}}s_{1}
a seamless run such that
\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{2})=\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{2})
. These exist by

Lemma

12.24
.
By the same lemma, for every state
s^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{mwt}(x_{0},s_{0}\to s^{\prime})<\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{2})
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(\sigma,s^{\prime}\to S)=\infty
i.e.,
s_{1}
is still the minimal state from which
\sigma
can be read, and
\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{2})-\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{1})\geq P
(see
Fig.

16(b)
).

We now perform a baseline shift on
\mu_{1}
.
By
Corollary

12.14
, we have

P\leq\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu_{2}}/{\mu_{1}}])-\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu_{1}}/{\mu_{1}}])=\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu_{2}}/{\mu_{1}}])

(where
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu_{1}}/{\mu_{1}}])=0
as it is the baseline run, as per
Corollary

12.13
).
Essentially, all that remains to do is adapt
\sigma
to the baseline shift, so that it can be read from the “shifted
s_{1}
” but not from any state below it. This is achieved using jump letters, as follows.

Denote the state reached by
\rho_{1}
as
(p_{1},q_{1},T_{1})
and
s_{1}=(p^{\prime}_{1},q_{1},T_{1})
(since the last two components are deterministic, they are the same for both states). Keep in mind that there is a finite weight transition from
s_{1}
on
\sigma
to some state. Note that
\mu_{1}
also ends in
(p_{1},q_{1},T_{1})
, and
\mu_{2}
ends in
(p^{\prime}_{1},q_{1},T_{1})
(since flattening does not change the reachable states and the baseline component).

Therefore, after the baseline shift we have that
\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu_{1}}/{\mu_{1}}]:s_{0}\to(p_{1},p_{1},T_{1})
and
\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu_{2}}/{\mu_{1}}]:s_{0}\to(p^{\prime}_{1},p_{1},T_{1})
.
We now consider the suffix
\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p_{1},T_{1})\to(\cdot,q_{1},T_{1})}\cdot\sigma
. There exists some
r^{\prime}\in S
such that

s_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\mu_{1}}]}(p^{\prime}_{1},p_{1},T_{1})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p_{1},T_{1})\to(\cdot,q_{1},T_{1})}}(p^{\prime}_{1},q_{1},T_{1})\xrightarrow{\sigma}r^{\prime}

However, for any state below
(p^{\prime}_{1},p_{1},T_{1})
in the configuration
\boldsymbol{c}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\mu_{1}}])
, there is no finite-weight run on
\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p_{1},T_{1})\to(\cdot,q_{1},T_{1})}\cdot\sigma
. Indeed, such a run implies a run from a state lower than
s_{1}
on
\sigma
, which contradicts the definition of
s_{1}
(see
Fig.

16(c)
).

We conclude that
\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\mu_{1}}])\geq P
, so we are done (note that we do not have equality, since we do not assume that
s_{1}
is maximal dominant, only dominant).
∎

Our next simple result is that both potential and charge are monotone with respect to the initial configuration, as follows.

Lemma 13.7
.

Consider configurations
\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{d}
where
\boldsymbol{c}\leq\boldsymbol{d}
such that
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d})
, there is a single baseline state
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})
, and
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\boldsymbol{d}(q)=0
.

Then for every
w\in\Sigma^{*}
with a seamless baseline run from
\boldsymbol{c}
it holds that:

1.

The base run of
\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{d})
is seamless.

2.

\upphi(\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{c}))\leq\upphi(\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{d}))
.

3.

\uppsi(\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{c}))\geq\uppsi(\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{d}))

Proof.

Since
\boldsymbol{c}\leq\boldsymbol{d}
, then for every run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{y}q
we have
\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(\rho)\leq\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\rho)
, and in particular

\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(y,P\to R)\leq\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(y,P\to R)\quad\text{ for every }P,R\subseteq Q.

(13.2)

Using this, we prove the three claims.

1.

Consider the baseline run
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
on
w
from
\boldsymbol{c}
. Since transitions between baseline states have weight
0
, and since
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\boldsymbol{d}(q)=0
for their single baseline state
q
, it follows that for every prefix
x
of
w
we have

\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{|x|})=\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{|x|})=0
.

Since
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
is seamless from
\boldsymbol{c}
, then for every prefix
x
of
w
and baseline state
q_{|x|}
reached by
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
after reading
x
we have

\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{|x|})=\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(x,Q\to q_{|x|})
.
By
Eq.

13.2
we have

\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{|x|})=\mathsf{wt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{|x|})=\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(x,Q\to q_{|x|})\leq\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(x,Q\to q_{|x|})

so
\rho_{\mathrm{base}}
is seamless from
\boldsymbol{d}
as well.

2.

Denote
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{c})
and
\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{d})

By
Eq.

13.2
we have that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}\leq\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}
.
Consider the state
s=\mathsf{max\_dom}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}})
and let
z\in\Sigma^{*}
be a suffix such that
\mathsf{mwt}(z,s\to Q)<\infty

and

\mathsf{mwt}(z,s^{\prime}\to Q)=\infty

for every
s^{\prime}
such that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})<\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(s)
, as per
Definition

13.2
.
Since
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}})
(following
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d})
), it holds that

\mathsf{mwt}(z,s^{\prime}\to Q)<\infty
.
Let
s^{\prime\prime}
be a state that attains the minimum
\min\{\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}}(z,s^{\prime\prime}\to Q)<\infty\mid s^{\prime\prime}\in Q\}
. That is,
s^{\prime\prime}
is dominant with respect to
z
.
Notice that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(s)\leq\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(s^{\prime\prime})
since
s=\mathsf{max\_dom}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}})
, but
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}\leq\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}
, so
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(s)\leq\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}(s^{\prime\prime})
.
In particular,

\mathsf{dom\_val}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}})=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(s)\leq\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}(s^{\prime\prime})\leq\mathsf{dom\_val}(\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}})

3.

Again denote
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{c})
and
\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(w,\boldsymbol{d})
. Since
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}\leq\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}
and there are seamless baseline runs to
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}
and
\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}
, we have
\min\{\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q)\mid q\in Q\}\leq\min\{\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}(q)\mid q\in Q\}
, so by
Definition

13.2
we have
\uppsi(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}})\geq\uppsi(\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}})
.

∎

We now turn to link the potential with baseline shift (
Section

12.2
).

Proposition 13.8
.

Consider a word
u
such that
\upphi(u)
is defined, and let
\rho
be a seamless run on
u
. For every prefix
v
of
u
, we have that
\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}])=\upphi(v)-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(v))
.

Proof.

The idea is quite intuitive: we shift all so that
\rho
becomes baseline, so while the potential itself may change, it is still attained by the same dominating state, and the difference in weight is exactly
\rho(v)
.
The only nontrivial part of the proof is translating a “separating suffix”
z
that works for
v
to one that works for
\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}])
. This is achieved using jump letters.

Let
\rho_{0}
be the baseline run over
v
, so that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{0})=0
.
Let
\boldsymbol{c}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},v)
and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}])
, and let
q=\mathsf{max\_dom}(\boldsymbol{c})
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\upphi(v)
. Thus, there exists
z\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty}
(possibly with jump letters) such that
\mathsf{mwt}(z,q\to S)<\infty
and for every
p
with
\boldsymbol{c}(p)<\boldsymbol{c}(q)
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(z,p\to S)=\infty
. Let
\mu
be a seamless run
\mu:s_{0}\xrightarrow{v}q
. We now consider the corresponding “shifted” state
q^{\prime}\in S
such that
\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu}/{\rho}]:s_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}]}q^{\prime}
.
By
Corollary

12.14
we have that
\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu}/{\rho}]
is also a seamless run (since its gaps from other runs are the same as those of the seamless
\mu
), and in addition

\upphi(v)=\mathsf{wt}(\mu)-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{0})=\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu}/{\rho}])-\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{0}}/{\rho}])=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q^{\prime})+\mathsf{wt}(\rho)

where the latter equality, namely
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=-\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{0}}/{\rho}])
follows from
Proposition

12.12
.

It thus remains to prove that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q^{\prime})=\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}])
. To this end, consider the suffix
\mathsf{jump}_{q^{\prime}\to q}
(note that by
Proposition

12.12
) this is indeed a legal jump letter (and in particular has cost
0
from
q^{\prime}
to
q
), then we have

\mathsf{mwt}(\mathsf{jump}_{q^{\prime}\to q}\cdot z,q^{\prime}\to S)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(\mathsf{jump}_{q^{\prime}\to q}\cdot z,q^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{jump}_{q^{\prime}\to q}}q\xrightarrow{z}S)<\infty

On the other hand, still by
Proposition

12.12
, for every state
s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}})
, if
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})<\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q^{\prime})
, then the “corresponding state”
s\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})
, i.e., the state such that a seamless run
\eta:s_{0}\xrightarrow{v}s
has
\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho}]:s_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}]}s^{\prime}
, satisfies
\boldsymbol{c}(s)<\boldsymbol{c}(q)
. Moreover, we have that
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{jump}_{q^{\prime}\to q}}s
, and this is the only transition from
s^{\prime}
on
\mathsf{jump}_{q^{\prime}\to q}
(this is immediate by the shape of the states, as described in
Proposition

12.12
).
We therefore have

\mathsf{mwt}(\mathsf{jump}_{q^{\prime}\to q}\cdot z,s^{\prime}\to S)=\mathsf{mwt}(\mathsf{jump}_{q^{\prime}\to q}\cdot z,s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{jump}_{q^{\prime}\to q}}s\xrightarrow{z}S)=\infty

So indeed
q^{\prime}
is dominant. We therefore have
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q^{\prime})\leq\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}])
, so we conclude

\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}])\geq\upphi(v)-\mathsf{wt}(\rho)
.

In order to obtain equality, we could use the same technique as above. However, a simpler argument is just to shift again to the original baseline. That is, we apply the proof thus far to the word
\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}]
and shift to the run
\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{0}}/{\rho}]
. We thus have

\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}]}/{\rho_{0}}])\geq\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}])-\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{0}}/{\rho}])

However, recall from above that
-\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{0}}/{\rho}])=\mathsf{wt}(\rho)
, and observe that nesting the baseline shift operator is equivalent to applying the outermost one, so since
\rho_{0}
is the baseline run on
v
, we have

\mathsf{bshift}[{\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}]}/{\rho_{0}}]=\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho_{0}}]=v

We thus get
\upphi(v)\geq\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{v}/{\rho}])+\mathsf{wt}(\rho)

which concludes the second inequality.
∎

Our last result of this section is that the charge is invariant under cactus unfolding (
Definition

12.15
). Intuitively, this is because the charge is attained via a grounded pair, whereas unfolding mainly changes non-grounded pairs.

Proposition 13.9
.

Consider a word
u=x\cdot\alpha_{B,w}\cdot y
and let
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\cdot\alpha_{B,w}\cdot y)}
. Let
M_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
xw^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}y=\mathsf{unfold}(x,\alpha_{B,w},y\wr F)
.
Then for every prefix
u
of
y
it holds that

\uppsi(x\cdot w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot u)=\uppsi(x\cdot\alpha_{B,w}\cdot u)
.

Proof.

Consider the configurations
\boldsymbol{c_{1}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(x\cdot\alpha_{B,w}\cdot u)
and
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(x\cdot\alpha_{B,w}\cdot u)
. Let
p_{1},p_{2}\in S
such that
\boldsymbol{c_{1}}(p_{1})=-\uppsi(x\cdot\alpha_{B,w}\cdot u)
and
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(p_{2})=-\uppsi(x\cdot w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot u)
.
In particular, we have
p_{1}\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{1}})
and
p_{2}\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{2}})
.
By
Lemma

12.19
we have
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(p_{1})=\boldsymbol{c_{1}}(p_{1})
, so

-\uppsi(x\cdot w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot u)\leq-\uppsi(x\cdot\alpha_{B,w}\cdot u)
.

Conversely, if
\boldsymbol{c_{1}}(p_{2})=\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(p_{2})
, then we have
-\uppsi(x\cdot w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot u)\geq-\uppsi(x\cdot\alpha_{B,w}\cdot u)
and we are done.

Otherwise (by way of contradiction), we have
\boldsymbol{c_{1}}(p_{2})\neq\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(p_{2})
, so again by
Lemma

12.19
it follows that
p_{2}\notin\mathsf{supp}(c_{1})
, and therefore

\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(p_{2})>F-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\cdot\alpha_{B,w}\cdot y)}\geq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x\cdot\alpha_{B,w}\cdot y)}>0

and in particular (since the baseline run exists and has weight
0
) it follows that
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(p_{2})
is not minimal in
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}
, and therefore is not the charge, which contradicts the assumption.
∎

Ideally, we would now want to prove a similar result regarding potential. Unfortunately, such a result does not generally hold. Nonetheless, in
Section

14
, we introduce concepts that allow us to obtain a strong-enough similar result.

14
A Witness for Nondeterminizability

We are now ready to describe the type of witnesses we use for nondeterminizability. Intuitively, a witness consists of a prefix
w_{1}
, a “pumpable infix”
w_{2}
, and a suffix
w_{3}
, where the idea is that after enough iterations of
w_{2}
the potential grows unboundedly, and
w_{3}
serves to separate the dominant state from the baseline, thus demonstrating unbounded gaps, which imply nondeterminizability by
Theorem

8.4
.

In this section, after defining witnesses, we prove that they provide a sound characterization for nondeterminizability: if a witness exists, then
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
is nondeterminizable. Moreover, we show that checking whether a given input is indeed a witness, is decidable.
The converse, namely that witnesses are a complete characterization, is in fact the main result of this entire paper.

There are two cruxes to the approach. First,
w_{1},w_{2}
and
w_{3}
are over our cactus extension, and thus capture nested cycles. Indeed, it is well known that single cycles (even non-simple) in the original WFA
\mathcal{A}
may not suffice to demonstrate unbounded gaps (e.g.,
Example

1
).
Second, we require
w_{3}
to essentially cause a jump from finite to infinite weight. This may seem overly strict.
Indeed, in some cases all runs have finite weight, and such a separation is impossible in
\mathcal{A}
, despite it being nondeterminizable (e.g., if all states are accepting and the transition function is total).
However, since the witness is over a cactus alphabet, it actually captures an “asymptotic” behavior, and in particular may induce
\infty
weight transitions, which we rely on. This becomes clearer after the definition and the proof of
Lemma

14.2
.

Consider the WFA
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}=\langle S,\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty},s_{0},\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}^{\infty}\rangle
, and recall that
\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty}
is obtained by alternating the stabilization closure (
Definition

11.4
) and rebase (
Definition

11.9
) (and it also includes jump letters, as per
Definition

11.15
).

For generality and brevity, we define witnesses parameterized by a type
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For this paper, however, we only use types
0
and
1
.
We explain the intuition behind the definition in the proof of
Lemma

14.2
below.

Definition 14.1

(

Witness)
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a
witness of type
k
consists of three words
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}
such that the following hold.

1.

w_{1},w_{2}\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{k})^{*}
,
w_{3}\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty})^{*}
and there is a seamless baseline run on
w_{1}w_{2}
.

2.

Let
S_{1}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})
, then
(S_{1},w_{2})
is a stable cycle with corresponding cactus letter
\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}
(recall that
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}
are the ghost-reachable states, as defined in
Definition

11.17
).

3.

\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2})
(i.e.,
w_{2}
cycles on the reachable states).

4.

\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3},s_{0}\to S)<\infty
whereas
\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}w_{3},s_{0}\to S)=\infty
.

We can now give the formal justification for witnesses, namely that they imply nondeterminizability. In the proof we also give further intuition about the details of the definition.

Lemma 14.2

(

)
.

If there is a type-
k
witness for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\infty}
is not determinizable.

Proof.

We start with some intuition regarding the proof and the definition of witnesses.
The “core” of
Definition

14.1
is Requirement
4
: the word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
can be read with finite weight, but
w_{1}\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}w_{3}
cannot. Recall that
\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}
allows finite runs only on grounded pairs, whose weight is bounded when reading
w_{2}^{*}
. In addition,
\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}
has a seamless baseline run. Since we also require a seamless baseline run on
w_{1}w_{2}
, it follows that there is a run of weight
0
on
w_{1}(w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})^{*}
. However, this run, and indeed – all runs between grounded pairs – are “killed” by
w_{3}
(otherwise we would have finite weight runs on
w_{1}\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}w_{3}
).
Therefore, all the finite weight runs on
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
must traverse, along the
w_{2}
infix, pairs of non-grounded states (this is not strictly true, because the grounded pairs require
w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
, which is why we need to pump
w_{2}
, hence Requirement 3).
Since runs on grounded pairs increase arbitrarily, we can construct arbitrarily-high gap witnesses, implying nondeterminizability.
This is illustrated in
Fig.

17
.
We now turn to the formal details.

We show that the existence of a type-
k
witness implies the gap criterion of nondeterminizability as per
Theorem

8.4
. That is, assume there exists a type-
k
witness
(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})
, then we claim that for every gap
G\in\mathbb{N}
there exist words
x,y\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty})^{*}
and a state
s\in S
such that

\mathsf{mwt}(xy,s_{0}\to S)=\mathsf{mwt}(xy,s_{0}\xrightarrow{x}s\xrightarrow{y}S)<\infty
, and
\mathsf{mwt}(x,s_{0}\to s)-\mathsf{mwt}(x,s_{0}\to S)>G
.

Let
G\in\mathbb{N}
.
As per
Definition

14.1
, denote
S_{1}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})
, then
(S_{1},w_{2})
is a stable cycle corresponding to
\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}
. Denote by
W={\mathsf{max\_eff}(w_{1}\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}w_{3})}
, and recall that
W
is an upper bound on the weight (in absolute value) of any run of any prefix of
w_{1}\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}w_{3}
.
By
Lemma

10.11
there exists
M_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
m\geq M_{0}
and every pair of states
(r,s)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S_{1},w_{2})
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}m},r\to s)>G+W
.

Figure 17
:
The structure of a witness, where

S_{1}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.20749pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.20749pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.20749pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.20749pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},\widetilde{w_{1}})
. Notice that the grounded-pairs of
\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}
get killed by
w_{3}
, but there are ghost runs that survive
w_{3}
.

Note that by Requirement 3 we have
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{1},w_{1}w_{2}^{n})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Denote

S_{2}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}})\quad\text{ and }\quad U_{2}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2})\setminus S_{2}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})\setminus S_{2}

By Requirement 4, we have
\mathsf{mwt}(w_{3},S_{2}\to S)=\infty
(otherwise there is a finite-weight run on
w_{1}\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}w_{3}
). Since we also have
\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3})<\infty
, it follows that there exists
s\in U_{2}
such that

\mathsf{mwt}(w_{3},s\to S)<\infty
and moreover, we can assume
s
is minimal, i.e.,

\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}w_{3},s_{0}\to S)=\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}w_{3},s_{0}\xrightarrow{w_{1}w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}s\xrightarrow{w_{3}}S)

In particular, by our choice of
s
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(w_{3},s\to S)<\infty
.

Next, for every
r\in S_{1}
such that
r\xrightarrow{w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}s
we have
(r,s)\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S_{1},w_{2})
(otherwise we would have
s\in S_{2}
, but
s\in U_{2}
). Thus, we have
\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},s_{0}\to s)>-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w_{1})}+G+W\geq G
.
Since
w_{1}w_{2}
has a seamless baseline run, we have

\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},s_{0}\to S)\leq 0
.
We therefore have that

\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},s_{0}\to s)-\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},s_{0}\to S)>G-0=G

Thus, for
x=w_{1}w_{2}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
and
y=w_{3}
, the gap requirements hold. Moreover, the family of witnesses is over the alphabet of
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}
, and in particular finite.
Since this is true for all
G
, we conclude from
Theorem

8.4
that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
is nondeterminizable.
∎

Algorithmically,
Lemma

14.2
is not of much use unless we can detect when words form a witness. Fortunately, this is easy (intuitively – all the conditions are easy to check, the only mildly-nontrivial part is computing the transition weights on cactus letters).

Theorem 14.3
.

The following problem is decidable: given a WFA
\mathcal{A}
and words
(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty}
, decide whether
(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})
is a witness.

Proof.

We describe an algorithm to check whether the words form a witness. First, for every letter
\sigma
occurring in
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}
, if
\sigma\in\Gamma_{0}^{0}
, then we know its associated transitions by constructing
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
from
\mathcal{A}
.

If
\sigma=\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}\in\Gamma_{k}^{j}
for some
k,j
, we need to compute its transitions between all pairs of states. This is done inductively as follows: first compute the transitions on the letters of
x
(which are of a lower rank). Next, compute the grounded pairs of
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
by first computing the set
\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
, which may form the grounding states, and then checking for each
g\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
which states
(s,r)\in S^{\prime}
satisfy
s\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{x^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}r
, as per
Definition

10.7
(which is a simple reachability query).
The weights of the transitions between grounded states
(s,r)
are then computable as per
Definition

11.1
(by iterating over the possible grounding states).
In addition, we similarly compute the transitions on
\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}
.

If
\sigma=\mathsf{rebase}_{S^{\prime},x,s\to r}
, we first verify that
(s,r)
are indeed a grounded pair, as above, and the readily compute the transitions and their weights (inductively) by
Definition

11.9
.

Thus, we have all the transitions weights on the letters of
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}
. We can now easily check the conditions of
Definition

14.1
: condition 1 is syntactic, there is nothing to check. Condition 2 can be checked as follows:
S_{1}
can be computed as per
Definition

11.17
from the transitions, and checking that the cycle is stable is a simple reachability query as per
Definition

10.5
.
Condition 3 is checked by composing the transitions on
w_{1}
and on
w_{1}w_{2}
and checking reachability equivalence. Finally, since we already computed the transitions over
\alpha_{S_{1},w_{2}}
, we can evaluate both expressions in Condition 4, and we are done.
∎

Our next result is that either a type-0 witness exists, or stable cycles can be safely unfolded without affecting the potential. Recall that type-0 means that we allow nesting of cacti, but no rebase or jump letters on the
w_{1},w_{2}
parts of the witness.
Intuitively, this result is as close as we can get to an analogue of
Proposition

13.9
for Potential.

Lemma 14.4

(

Unfolding Maintains Potential)
.

Either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-
0
witness, or the following holds.
Consider an initial state
8
8

8

When applying this lemma, we sometimes use a different initial state than that of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
. We therefore allow any initial state, but still call it
s_{0}
for clarity.

s_{0}
and
u,\alpha_{B,x},v\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
(where
(B,x)
is a stable cycle).
Let
F\geq 2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u\alpha_{B,x}v)}
and
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v=\mathsf{unfold}(u,\alpha_{B,x},v\wr F)
as per
Definition

12.15
, then for every prefix
v^{\prime}
of
v
we have:

1.

\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{s_{0}}},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{s_{0}}},u\alpha_{B,x}v^{\prime})
.

2.

\upphi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})=\upphi(u\alpha_{B,x}v^{\prime})
(if the potential is defined, with respect to the given initial state
s_{0}
).

Proof.

Item 1 follows immediately from
Lemma

12.19
. Indeed, let
\boldsymbol{d}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{s_{0}}},u\alpha_{B,x}v^{\prime})
and
\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{s_{0}}},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})
, then we have

\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d})\subseteq\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}})
and for every
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{d})
we have
\boldsymbol{d^{\prime}}(q)=\boldsymbol{d}(q)
(and anyway
\boldsymbol{d}(q)=\infty
otherwise).

It is now tempting to use
Lemma

13.7
and use the superiority of the configurations to conclude the proof. Unfortunately, the condition there also requires that the configurations have equal supports, which fails in our case. We therefore need to work much harder to show the lemma.

Intuitively, the proof proceeds as follows. We consider the unfolding
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v=\mathsf{unfold}(u,\alpha_{B,x},v\wr F)
, and specifically its prefix
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
.
By
Proposition

12.17
, the unfolding preserves the reachable configuration, i.e. the weights with which states in
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x})
are reached. However, we might also get finite weights on other states in
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x})
, due to the unfolding.
The question now is whether these newly reachable states interfere with the potential.

In order to reason about the potential, we consider a maximal dominant state
q
, and split to two cases. The first case is when
q
is in
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x})
(namely – very high, reached by pumping
x
on non-grounded pairs).
Then, we show that we can construct a type-0 witness. Specifically, the witness is
(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},z)
, where
z
is the suffix that shows
q
is dominant. Proving that this is indeed a witness is technical, and uses the intricacies of our definitions of stable cycles and grounded states.
The intuition, however, is not too complicated: if the dominant state is high, then replacing
x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
by its corresponding cactus letter sends all states above
q
to
\infty
, and the remaining states are sent to
\infty
by
z
.

The second case is when
q
is in
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x})
, namely low, reachable by grounded states. Then we use the unfolding toolbox of
Section

12.3
to show that the potential is indeed maintained.

We now turn to formalize this argument.
Consider
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v=\mathsf{unfold}(u,\alpha_{B,x},v\wr F)
, and assume that the potential is defined (otherwise we are done).
Consider the following configurations and sets of states:

\begin{array}[]{ l l l }\boldsymbol{c_{1}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{s_{0}}},u)&\boldsymbol{c_{2}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{s_{0}}},u\alpha_{B,x})&\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{s_{0}}},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})\\
S_{1}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u)&S_{2}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x})&S^{\prime}_{2}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})\\
\end{array}

Note that
S_{2}=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{2}})
and
S^{\prime}_{2}=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}})
.
By
Lemma

12.19
we have that

S_{2}\subseteq S^{\prime}_{2}
, for every
s\in S_{2}
it holds that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(s)=\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(s)
and
for every
s^{\prime}\notin S_{2}
it holds that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(s^{\prime})>\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(s)
(where
s^{\prime}\notin S_{2}
means either
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
, or
s^{\prime}\notin S^{\prime}_{2}
).

Consider a maximal-dominant state
q\in S
in
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}
.
By
Definition

13.1
, this means that there exists
z\in\Gamma^{\infty}_{\infty}
such that

\mathsf{mwt}(z,q\to S)<\infty

whereas for every
p\in S
with
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(p)<\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(q)
we have

\mathsf{mwt}(z,p\to S)=\infty
. We now split to cases according to whether
q\in S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
(i.e.,
q
is “very high”) or
q\in S_{2}
(i.e.,
q
is “low”).

Dominant State is Very High

If
q\in S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
, we claim that
(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},z)
is a type-0 witness.
Indeed, following the requirements in
Definition

14.1
, we have:

1.

ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
and
z\in\Gamma^{\infty}_{\infty}
.

2.

Since
\alpha_{B,x}
is a cactus letter then by

we have that
\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}
