width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})
, since
(B,x)
is a stable cycle.

3.

By
Proposition

10.2
we have that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})
.

4.

Since
q\in S^{\prime}_{2}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})
then by Item 3 above we have
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})
. In particular,
\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},s_{0}\to q)<\infty
. Since
z
satisfies that
\mathsf{mwt}(z,q\to S)<\infty
, we conclude that:

\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot z,s_{0}\to S)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},s_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{mwt}(z,q\to S)<\infty

It therefore remains to prove that
\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}\cdot z,s_{0}\to S)=\infty
.
To this end, we claim that for every state
p
, if
\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}},s_{0}\to p)<\infty
, then
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(p)<\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(q)
(from which we can obtain the desired result).
Indeed, if
\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}},s_{0}\to p)<\infty
then there exists a run

\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}s_{1}\xrightarrow{x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}}p

By
Definition

11.1
, this implies that
(p^{\prime},p)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}})
. Then, however, it follows by
Proposition

10.10
that
(s_{1},p)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})
(by using the same grounding state of
(p^{\prime},p)
).
Recall that by
Proposition

11.3
, the letters
\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}
and
\alpha_{B,x}
have the exact same transitions. That is,
(s_{1},p)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(B,x)
.
Thus, we have the run

s_{0}\xrightarrow{u}s_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{B,x}}p

so
p\in S_{2}
.
As noted above, all the states in
S_{2}
the configurations
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}
and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}
coincide, so
\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(p)=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(p)
. On the other hand,
q\in S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
, and again as noted above this means that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(q)>\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(p)=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(p)
.

We are therefore in the following setting:

•

If
\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}},s_{0}\to p)<\infty
, then
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(p)<\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(q)
, but then
\mathsf{mwt}(z,p\to S)=\infty
, so

\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}\cdot z,s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}}p\xrightarrow{z}S)=\infty

•

If
\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}},s_{0}\to p)=\infty
then trivially

\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}\cdot z,s_{0}\xrightarrow{ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}}p\xrightarrow{z}S)=\infty

We conclude that anyway we have

\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}\cdot\alpha_{B,x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}\cdot z,s_{0}\to S)=\infty

This shows that all the requirements of
Definition

14.1
hold, so there is a type-0 witness.

Dominant State is Low

If
q\notin S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
, then
q\in S_{2}
(since
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}})=S^{\prime}_{2}
). Then, by the observations above, we have
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{2}}(q)
. We claim that for every prefix
v^{\prime}
of
v
it holds that
\upphi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})=\upphi(u\alpha_{B,c}v^{\prime})
.
Indeed, fix such a prefix
v^{\prime}
and denote

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{c_{3}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},u\alpha_{B,x}v^{\prime})=\mathsf{next\_conf}(c_{2},v^{\prime})\quad S_{3}=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{3}})\\
&\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})=\mathsf{next\_conf}(c^{\prime}_{2},v^{\prime})\quad S^{\prime}_{3}=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{3}})\end{split}

Similarly to the above, we have
S_{3}\subseteq S^{\prime}_{3}
, and due to the choice of
F
as the unfolding constant (
Definitions

12.15
and
12.19
) for every
p\in S_{3}
it holds that
\boldsymbol{c_{3}}(p)=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(p)
, and for every
p\in S^{\prime}_{3}\setminus S_{3}
we have
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(p)>\max\{\boldsymbol{c_{3}}(r)\mid r\in S_{3}\}+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u\alpha_{B,c}v)}
.

Let
p,p^{\prime}
be maximal dominant states in
\boldsymbol{c_{3}}
and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}
, respectively. We claim that
p,p^{\prime}\in S_{3}
. Indeed,
p\in S_{3}
by definition. If
p^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{3}\setminus S_{3}
, then there exists a suffix
z
such that
\mathsf{mwt}(z,p^{\prime}\to S)<\infty
and for every
r^{\prime}
with
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(r^{\prime})<\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(p^{\prime})
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(z,r^{\prime}\to S)=\infty
. In particular, for every
r^{\prime}\in S_{3}
this holds.
Since
p^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{3}\setminus S_{3}
, then
p^{\prime}\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime}_{2},v^{\prime})\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S_{2},v^{\prime})
. But then
v^{\prime}z
is a suffix that exhibits a dominant state in
S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
, and in particular the maximal dominant state of
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{2}}
is in
S^{\prime}_{2}\setminus S_{2}
, so we are back in the previous case of a very high dominant state (which we assume does not hold now).

Thus,
p^{\prime}\in S_{3}
. It now readily follows that
\upphi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})=\upphi(u\alpha_{B,c}v^{\prime})
: restricted to
S_{3}
, the configurations
\boldsymbol{c_{3}}
and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}
are equivalent. Moreover, for every
r^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{3}\setminus S_{3}
we have
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(r^{\prime})>\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(p^{\prime})
, so does not come into consideration for the dominance of
p^{\prime}
.

It thus follows that
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(p^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c_{3}}(p)
(in fact, we can assume without loss of generality that
p=p^{\prime}
, since we can choose any maximal-dominant state, if there are several), and since the baseline run is reachable and its state is also in
S_{3}
, we conclude that

\upphi(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{3}}(p^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c_{3}}(p)=\upphi(u\alpha_{B,c}v^{\prime}).

∎

Remark 14.5

(The Prefix “Either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness, or…”)
.

Most of our reasoning about potential in the coming sections is based on
Lemma

14.4
.
Therefore, the prefix “Either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness, or the following holds” appears in many other arguments. Since having such a witness immediately implies nondeterminziability (by
Lemma

14.2
), this condition can be read as “either we are done, or the following holds”.

15
A Ramsey Theorem for Colored Infixes

In this section we present a general Ramsey-type theorem, concerning finite coloring of an infinite set of words. Specifically, we assume that we have an infinite set of words such that all the infixes of each word are colored.
We show that we can find arbitrarily-long words with arbitrarily-many indices such that all infixes between these indices are identically colored.

These results are then used to reason about the types of runs between configurations in
Section

16.2.1
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
denote
\mathfrak{I}_{n}=\{(i,j)\mid 1\leq i\leq j\leq n\wedge i,j\in\mathbb{N}\}
denote the set of finite
intervals
of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
(think of
(i,j)
as denoting the set
\{i,i+1,\ldots,j\}
). Note that for
n=\infty
, the set
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
has all finite intervals.
Fix a finite set
\mathfrak{C}
of
colors
.
An
interval coloring
is a function
\lambda:\mathfrak{I}_{n}\to\mathfrak{C}
that assigns a color to each interval.

Fix a finite alphabet
\Sigma
. An
infix-colored set
is an infinite set
C=\{(w_{1},\lambda_{1}),(w_{2},\lambda_{2}),\ldots\}
where
w_{i}\in\Sigma^{*}
and
\lambda_{i}:\mathfrak{I}_{|w_{i}|}\to\mathfrak{C}
is an interval coloring for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Intuitively, the following lemma shows that given an infix-colored set
C
, there is a single infinite word
w
and a corresponding interval coloring such that
w
has infinitely many prefixes in
C
, and their infix coloring coincides with that of
w
. Technically, we state the lemma slightly differently.
For
\lambda:\mathfrak{I}_{k}\to\mathfrak{C}
and
k^{\prime}<k
, we denote
\lambda|_{k^{\prime}}
as
\lambda
restricted to the domain
\mathfrak{I}_{k^{\prime}}
.
We denote by
\Sigma^{\omega}
is the set of infinite words over
\Sigma
.

Lemma 15.1
.

Let
C
be an infix-colored set, then there exists an infinite word
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
and an interval coloring
\lambda_{\omega}:\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\to\mathfrak{C}
such that for every finite prefix
v
of
w
there exists
(x,\lambda^{\prime})\in C
such that
v
is a prefix of
x
and
\lambda_{\omega}|_{|v|}\equiv\lambda^{\prime}|_{|v|}

Proof.

Consider a pair
(w,\lambda)\in C
, then for every prefix
v
of
w
, we can consider the pair
(v,\lambda|_{|v|})
, which is a coloring of
v
that coincides with
\lambda
over the relevant indices.

We now construct a finitely-branching forest
9
9

9

The graph is a forest since
\epsilon
may appear with different colorings.
(i.e., a union of trees)
F=\langle V,E\rangle
à la König, as follows.
The vertices of the tree are
F=\{(v,\lambda)\mid v\in\Sigma^{*},\lambda:\mathfrak{I}_{|v|}\to\mathfrak{C}\}
, i.e., each vertex is an infix-colored word. The edges are

E=\{((v,\lambda),(v\sigma,\lambda^{\prime})\mid\lambda^{\prime}|_{|v|}\equiv\lambda,\ \sigma\in\Sigma)\}

That is, we connect a colored word
v
to any word obtained by adding a single letter
\sigma
to
v
, such that the coloring of
v\sigma
coincides with that of
v
on the relevant indices.

Note that all the outgoing edges from vertices
(v,\lambda)
with
|v|=n
are to vertices
(v^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
with
|v^{\prime}|=n+1
.
Since there are only finitely many vertices
(v,\lambda)
with
|v|=n
(since
\mathfrak{C}
is finite), then clearly the branching degree of the tree is also finite.

We now restrict
F
to the sub-forest
F^{\prime}
obtained by keeping only the vertices in
F
from which there is a path to some
(x,\lambda^{\prime})\in C
(observe that the elements of
C
are also vertices in
F
by definition).
Since
C
is infinite and
\Sigma
is finite, it follows that
C
contains arbitrarily long words. Since we keep all vertices along paths from
\epsilon
(with some coloring) to words in
C
, it follows that
F^{\prime}
is a finitely-branching forest with arbitrarily long paths.

By König’s lemma, it follows that there is an infinite path in
F^{\prime}
. We claim that such a path induces an infinite word as required.
Indeed, we can assume without loss of generality that the infinite path begins in
(\epsilon,\lambda_{\epsilon})
(otherwise we add prefixes to it until reaching
\epsilon
). We can therefore write the path as
(\epsilon,\lambda_{\epsilon}),(\sigma_{1},\lambda_{1}),(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2},\lambda_{2}),\ldots
, i.e., the
n
-th vertex in the path is
(\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{n},\lambda_{n})
.

We now define the infinite word
w
and
\lambda_{\omega}
as follows.
w=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots
(i.e.,
w[n,n]=\sigma_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
). For every
(i,j)\in\mathfrak{I}_{\omega}
we define
\lambda_{\omega}(i,j)=\lambda_{j}(i,j)
.

It remains to show that
(w,\lambda_{\omega})
satisfies the required conditions. Consider a finite prefix
v
of
w
, then in particular
(v,\lambda_{|v|})\in F^{\prime}
(since
w
is described by a path in
F^{\prime}
), and
\lambda_{|v|}\equiv\lambda_{\omega}|_{|v|}
.
Moreover, by the definition of
F^{\prime}
, it follows that there is a path in
F
from
(v,\lambda_{|v|})
to some
(x,\lambda^{\prime})\in C
. By the way we construct
F
, we have that
\lambda^{\prime}|_{|v|}\equiv\lambda_{|v|}
. Putting these together, we have
\lambda_{\omega}|_{|v|}=\lambda^{\prime}|_{|v|}
, as required.
∎

We can now show that every infix-colored set
C
has words that can be decomposed to arbitrarily many infixes such that all infixes have the same coloring, and moreover – this coloring is idempotent with respect to concatenation. In addition, we are able to place some constraints on the cost of these infixes, where “cost” here is a general extension of the length of a word.
Naturally, our usage is with the cost functions of
Definition

11.6
.

Formally, a
prefix-cost function
is a function
\mathsf{pc}:\Sigma^{+}\to\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
without any restrictions.
A letter-cost function is
\mathsf{lc}:\Sigma^{+}\to\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
such that
\mathsf{lc}
is “strictly increasing on prefixes”, i.e., if
x
is a strict prefix of
y
then
\mathsf{lc}(x)<\mathsf{lc}(y)
(c.f.,
Remark

11.7
).
We also denote
w[i,j]=\sigma_{i}\cdots\sigma_{j}
for
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
. Before we proceed, we recall a specific theorem by Ramsey that we use in the proof.
For a set
A
, write
(A)_{n}=\{A^{\prime}\subseteq A:|A^{\prime}|=n\}

Theorem 15.2

(Ramsey, 1930)
.

Let
f:(\mathbb{N})_{n}\to\mathfrak{C}
, then there exists an infinite set
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
on which
f
is homogeneous, i.e.,
f
is constant on
(A)_{n}
.

Theorem 15.3

(

A Ramsey Theorem for Infix Coloring)
.

Consider an infix-colored set
C
, a prefix-cost function
\mathsf{pc}
and a letter-cost function
\mathsf{lc}
.
For every
L\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a pair
(v,\lambda)
and an index set
1\leq i_{0}<i_{1}<\ldots<i_{L}\leq|v|+1
such that there are infinitely many pairs
(w_{i},\lambda_{i})\in C
(for
i\in\mathbb{N}
) where
v
is a prefix of
w_{i}
, and the following hold:

1.

\lambda\equiv\lambda_{i}|_{|v|}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. I.e., the coloring of
v
matches that of the
w_{i}
.

2.

For every
0\leq j<k\leq L
we have
\lambda(i_{0},i_{1}-1)=\lambda(i_{j},i_{k-1})
, i.e., all the infixes between the indices in the set have the same color.

3.

\mathsf{pc}(v[i_{0},i_{1}-1])<\mathsf{lc}(v[i_{1},i_{L}])
, i.e., the first infix is much “cheaper” than the remaining infix.

Proof.

Let
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
and
\lambda_{\omega}
be the infix-colored infinite word obtained as per
Lemma

15.1
. Note that
\lambda_{\omega}:(\mathbb{N})_{2}\to\mathfrak{C}
, and we can therefore apply
Theorem

15.2
to
\lambda_{\omega}
.
Thus, there exists an infinite subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and a color
r\in\mathfrak{C}
such that
\lambda_{\omega}(i,j)=r
for all
i<j\in A
. Sort
A
as
i_{0}<i_{1}<\ldots
.

Recall that the cost function
\mathsf{lc}
strictly increasing on prefixes, and in particular strictly increasing with
length
along any infix of
w
.
Since
A
is infinite, then there exists
L\in\mathbb{N}
large enough such that
\mathsf{pc}(w[i_{0},i_{1}-1])<\mathsf{lc}(w[i_{1},i_{L}])
(note that
\mathsf{pc}(w[i_{0},i_{1}-1])
is a constant).

Take
v=w[1,i_{L}]
. By the above, Requirement
3
holds (since
v
is identical to
w
up to index
i_{L}
).
Requirement
2
holds by the application of
Theorem

15.2
, since all the infixes with indices in
A
are colored
r
.
The existence of infinitely many pairs in
C
where
v
is a prefix follows from
Lemma

15.1
. Indeed, for every prefix
x
of
w
such that
v
is a prefix of
x
, there exists
(w_{i},\lambda_{i})\in C
such that
x
is a prefix of
w_{i}
and
\lambda_{\omega}|_{|x|}\equiv\lambda_{i}|_{|x|}
. In particular, since there are arbitrarily long such prefixes
x
, it follows that there are infinitely many such pairs
(w_{i},\lambda_{i})
. This also shows that Requirement
1
holds, concluding the proof.
∎

16
Separated Increasing Infixes

In this section we define word infixes whose runs exhibit a very “organized” behavior, which are later used in our proof. We then show that such infixes exist.

16.1
Separated Increasing Infixes – Definition

We start with an intuitive overview of the properties we require, follows by the formal definition.

Figure 18
:
A Separated Increasing Infix from State
p
. After reading
u
, the configuration is separated to sub-configurations corresponding to the
V_{i}
. Reading
x
maintains this partition and the exact distances within each set. The same holds for
y
, but with different shifts.
In addition,
y
is much longer than
x
(or rather, has much higher cost). Moreover, the gaps between the sub-configurations are larger still – much larger than the maximal effect of
x
and
y
.

Consider the WFA
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{0}=\langle S,\Gamma^{0}_{\infty},s_{0},\widehat{\Delta}^{0}_{\infty}\rangle
. That is, we focus on the construction with cactus letters, but without rebase or jump.
An separated increasing infix (from state
p
) consists of a concatenation
uxyv
with the following structure (see
Fig.

18
):

•

Reading
u
from
p
reaches a configuration that is partitioned to “sub-configurations” that have a huge gap between them, we call the state sets in these sub configurations
V_{1},\ldots,V_{\ell}
.

•

Next, reading
x
maintains the structure of these
V_{j}
sets, and increases all the states in each set
V_{j}
by a constant
k_{j,x}\geq 0
. In particular, the entire configuration maintains its support after
x
.

•

Reading
y
has very similar behavior to
x
, in the sense that the
V_{j}
are maintained, and each is increased by a constant
k_{j,y}
, with the property that
k_{j,y}=0
if and only if
k_{j,x}=0
.

However, an additional property of
y
is that it is
very
long (or rather, very costly). Specifically, its cost (in the sense of
Definition

11.6
) is larger than the cost of a cactus letter on
x
. This means that replacing the infix
xy
by the cactus
\alpha_{B,x}
decreases the overall cost (which indeed we do in
Lemma

16.6
).

Despite the huge cost of
y
, the gaps between the
V_{j}
are even higher, so much so that “pumping”
x
should be safe. These notions are made concrete throughout the section.

•

Finally,
v
is just a harmless suffix.

We now turn to the formal definition, starting with some notations.
For a word
w
, recall that
{\mathsf{max\_wt}(w)}
is the maximal absolute value of a weight over any transition on a letter of
w
.
Further recall that
{\mathsf{max\_wt}(w)}\cdot|w|
upper-bounds the maximal finite change in weights that can be incurred upon reading
w
.
For a state
p\in S
, let
\boldsymbol{c_{p}}
be the configuration that assigns
p
weight
0
and
\infty
otherwise.

Definition 16.1

(

Separated Increasing Infix from
p
)
.

Consider a state
p\in S
and a word
w\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
decomposed as
w=u\cdot x\cdot y\cdot v
.
Denote
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{p}},u)
and similarly
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{p}},ux)
and
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{p}},uxy)
.
We say that
w
is a separated increasing infix from
p
if the following conditions hold.

1.

\mathsf{cost}(y)>2^{16({\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|\mathsf{cost}(x))^{2}}

(where
\mathsf{cost}
is as per
Definition

11.6
).

2.

\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{ux}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}})=B
for some subset
B\subseteq S
. That is, the subsets of states reached after
u,ux,uxy
are the same.

3.

B
can be partitioned to
B=V_{1}\cup\ldots\cup V_{\ell}
for some
\ell
such that the following holds.

(a)

For every
1\leq j<\ell
, every
s\in V_{j},s^{\prime}\in V_{j+1}
and every
\xi\in\{u,ux,uxy\}
we have

\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(s^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(s)>8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(|S|+1){\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}^{2}
.

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
todo:

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=

ACCOUNTING: If this constant changes, the accounting in the definition of
\mathsf{sparse}
needs to be updated, along with every part influenced by it. These parts are next to comments labeled ACCOUNTING-1

That is, the values assigned to states in
V_{j+1}
are significantly larger than those assigned to
V_{j}
in the configurations
\boldsymbol{c_{u}},\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
and
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}
.

(b)

For every
1\leq j\leq\ell
there exist
k_{j,x},k_{j,y}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
s\in V_{j}
we have
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+k_{j,x}
and
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}(s)=\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)+k_{j,y}
.

Moreover,
k_{j,x}=0
if and only if
k_{j,y}=0
.

4.

If
p
is a baseline state, then there is a seamless baseline run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{uxyv}p
from
\boldsymbol{c_{p}}
(and so
\rho
is constantly of weight
0
in all prefixes).

The partition and gaps described in
Items

3
and
3a
give rise to a restriction on the runs on the infixes
x,y
. Specifically, there are only runs from
V_{j}
to
V_{j^{\prime}}
for
j\geq j^{\prime}
, and there is always a run from
V_{j^{\prime}}
to
V_{j^{\prime}}
. In addition, the constants
k_{j,x}
and
k_{j,y}
are bounded.

Proposition 16.2
.

Consider a separated increasing infix
uxyv
from state
p
, and let
B=V_{1}\cup\ldots\cup V_{\ell}
and
k_{j,x},k_{j,y}
as per
Definition

16.1
. Then the following hold for every
1\leq j\leq\ell
,.

•

k_{j,x}\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
and
k_{j,y}\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(y)}
.

•

For every
z\in\{x,y\}
and
s\in S
, if
s\in V_{j}
then there exists
s^{\prime}\in V_{j}
such that
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{z}s
.

•

For every
z\in\{x,y\}
and
s,s^{\prime}\in S
such that
s\xrightarrow{z}s^{\prime}
, if
s^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
then
s\in V_{j}
for some
j\geq j^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We prove the claim for
z=x
, and the case of
y
identical up to working with
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}
instead of
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
.

We start with the first item. Assume by way of contradiction that there exists
1\leq j\leq\ell
such that
k_{j,x}>{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
, and let
j
be maximal with this property (i.e., the “highest” part in the partition where this holds). We depict the proof in
Fig.

19
.

Figure 19
:
The part
V_{j}
and the state
s
whose minimal run stems from
V_{j^{\prime}}
, implying a small gap.

Consider a state
s\in\arg\max\{\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q)\mid q\in V_{j}\}
. We then have
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)>\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. This means that the minimal seamless run
\rho:s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x}s
is from
s^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
with
j^{\prime}>j
. Indeed, no run from any state in
V_{j}
or lower can get to weight
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. In particular, we get
j<\ell
.
We therefore have
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}(s)}\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
.

In the following, it is useful to think of
j^{\prime}
as
j+1
(although we do not actually assume that).
Consider
k_{j^{\prime},x}
. By the maximality assumption on
j
, we have that
k_{j^{\prime},x}\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s^{\prime})\leq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
. This, however, contradicts the gap in
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
: we have that
s^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
and
s\in V_{j}
, but

\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)\leq 2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}\ll 8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(|S|+1){\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}^{2}

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
todo:

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=

ACCOUNTING: check

in contradiction to
Item

3a
of
Definition

16.1
.
We conclude that
k_{j,x}\leq{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
for all
j
, and similarly for
y
.

We now proceed to the latter two items.
Let
1\leq j\leq\ell
and
s\in V_{j}
and let
s^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
such that
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x}s
.
Assume by way of contradiction that
j^{\prime}<j
, then by the gap criterion
Item

3a
, since the gap is much larger than
{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
, we have

\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)\leq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}<\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)
, but this is a contradiction to
Item

3b
of
Definition

16.1
.
Thus,
j^{\prime}\geq j
, as required.

Next, assume by way of contradiction that for every
s^{\prime}
such that
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x}s
we have
s^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
for
j^{\prime}>j
. As we show above, this immediately implies
k_{k,x}>{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}
, which cannot hold.
∎

Our next observation is that an increasing infix does not allow negative cycles on
x^{k}
for any
k
. Note that negative runs on
x
may occur. For example, a state in
V_{7}
may have a negative run on
x
, but to a state in
V_{3}
, which is much lower and therefore this negative run is not seamless, and thus does not really “matter”.

Proposition 16.3
.

Consider a separated increasing infix
uxyv
from
p\in S
. In the notations of
Definition

16.1
, for every
r\in B
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{k},r\to r)\geq 0
.

Proof.

Assume by way of contradiction that the claim does not hold, i.e.,
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{k},r\to r)<0
for some
r\in B
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let

\rho:r=r_{0}\xrightarrow{x}r_{1}\xrightarrow{x}r_{2}\cdots r_{k-1}\xrightarrow{x}r_{k}=r
be a run such that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)<0
. In particular, there exists some
1\leq i_{0}\leq k
such that
\mathsf{mwt}(x,r_{i_{0}-1}\to r_{i_{0}})<0
.

By
Item

3
of
Definition

16.1
, for every
0\leq i\leq k
we have
r_{i}\in V_{j_{i}}
for some
1\leq j_{i}\leq\ell
.
By
Proposition

16.2
, we have
j_{i-1}\geq j_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
, since there are only runs from
V_{j}
to “lower” parts.
Since
r_{0}=r_{k}
, it therefore follows that all the states must be in the same part
V_{j}
.

Let
k_{j,x}
be the corresponding constant to
V_{j}
. Observe that for every
r^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime}\in V_{j}
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(x,r^{\prime}\to r^{\prime\prime})\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime\prime})-\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime})+k_{j,x}
(otherwise we would have
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(r^{\prime\prime})<\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime\prime})+k
).
Thus, as a telescopic sum, and since
r_{k}=r_{0}=r
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathsf{mwt}(x,r_{i-1}\to r_{i})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r_{i})-\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r_{i-1})+k_{j,x})\\
&=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r_{k})-\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r_{0})+k\cdot k_{j,x}=k\cdot k_{j,x}\end{split}

This contradicts the negativity of
\rho
, and we are done.
∎

We now have that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the minimal weight that can be attained by a run over
x^{k}
cannot be too small (as the run cannot contain negative cycles). Specifically, we have the following.

Corollary 16.4
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and run
\rho:B\xrightarrow{x^{k}}B
we have
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\geq-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}|S|
.

We now lift
Definition

16.1
to a set of states, obtaining the main definition of this section.

Definition 16.5

(Separated Increasing Infix)
.

Consider a set of states
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
and a word
w
decomposed as
w=u\cdot x\cdot y\cdot v
.
We say that
w
is a separated increasing infix from
S^{\prime}
if
w
is a separated increasing infix from every
p\in S^{\prime}
and in addition
(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime},u),x)
is a stable cycle.

The next central lemma is key to our proofs, and captures our usage of separated increasing infixes. Intuitively, it states that given a separated increasing infix
u\cdot x\cdot y\cdot v
, we can replace it with
u\cdot\alpha_{\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.10419pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.10419pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.10419pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.10419pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime},u),x}\cdot v
, and then many “nice” properties hold. Specifically, the cost of the word becomes lower, its potential becomes higher, and it induces higher runs. A caveat to the lemma is that these guarantees might not hold, but in such a case we have a type-
0
witness (this is based on
Lemma

14.4
).

Lemma 16.6

(

Increasing Infix to Cactus)
.

Either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-
0
witness, or the following holds.
Consider a separated increasing infix
S^{\prime},w
where
w=u\cdot x\cdot y\cdot v
and
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
. Write
B=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime},u)
and let
w^{\prime}=u\alpha_{B,x}v
, then we have:

1.

\mathsf{cost}(w)>\mathsf{cost}(w^{\prime})
.

2.

For every
r\in S^{\prime},t\in S
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(w,r\to t)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{\prime},r\to t)
.

3.

For every
w_{1},w_{2}\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
with
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1})\subseteq S^{\prime}
we have
\upphi(w_{1}ww_{2})\leq\upphi(w_{1}w^{\prime}w_{2})
(if the potential is defined).

Proof.

The first requirement follows immediately from the
Definition

16.1
: since
\mathsf{cost}(y)>2^{16({\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|\mathsf{cost}(x))^{2}}=\mathsf{cost}(\alpha_{B,x})
(by
Definition

11.6
) so

\begin{split}&\mathsf{cost}(w)=\mathsf{cost}(u)+\mathsf{cost}(x)+\mathsf{cost}(y)+\mathsf{cost}(v)>\\
&\mathsf{cost}(u)+\mathsf{cost}(x)+\mathsf{cost}(\alpha_{B,x})+\mathsf{cost}(v)\geq\mathsf{cost}(u)+\mathsf{cost}(\alpha_{B,x})+\mathsf{cost}(v)=\mathsf{cost}(w^{\prime})\end{split}

The remainder of the proof is broken into two steps.

Step 1: From
y
to
x^{*}

The first step is to replace
y
with many iterations of
x
. Intuitively, we imagine that
x
is already replaced with
\alpha_{B,x}
, and we unfold it.

Let
w_{1},w_{2}\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
with
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1})\subseteq S^{\prime}
, and consider the word
\mathsf{unfold}(w_{1}u,\alpha_{B,x},vw_{2}\wr F)=w_{1}ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}vw_{2}
for some
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w_{1}u\alpha_{B,x}yvw_{2})}
.
We assume without loss of generality that
M_{0}
is large enough so that for every state
r\in S^{\prime}
it holds that
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v
is also an unfolding
\mathsf{unfold}(u,\alpha_{B,x},v\wr F)
when starting
10
10

10

Note that the initial state
r
is implicit in
Definition

12.15
, but the unfolding may depend on it nonetheless.
from
r
, and that

M_{0}>{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}
. Note that
y
does not appear in the latter unfolding – this is deliberate, and makes the conclusion of the proof simpler.

We can assume these requirements on
M_{0}
by
Remark

12.16
, which states that increasing the number of repetitions of
x
retains all the properties of unfolding.

We show an analogue of the properties of the lemma. That is, we show:

•

For every
r\in S^{\prime},t\in S
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(uxyv,r\to t)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v,r\to t)
.

•

\upphi(w_{1}uxyvw_{2})\leq\upphi(w_{1}ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}vw_{2})
(if the potential is defined).

We start with the first property.
The intuitive idea is as follows. We repeat the infix
x
many times. By the increasing infix property, for each
V_{j}
in the partition, this either causes all states to increase, or stay in place. However, this is only true as long as the
V_{j}
sub-configurations stays far from one another. At some point, it could be that e.g.,
V_{2}
rises a lot, while
V_{3}
stays in place, and when the weights “cross”, the states in
V_{3}
now become minimal.
Now, while state in
V_{3}
grow when reading
x
, it may still be the case that
V_{3}
generates
negative
runs to states in
V_{2}
. Before pumping
x
, this had no effect. Now, however, it generates lower runs.

Nonetheless, these negative runs are “local”, since
V_{3}
cannot generate any negative cycle in
V_{2}
(otherwise
V_{2}
would have a negative cycle itself). Since the gap we start with is huge, we are guaranteed that even after these local decreasing runs, the overall configuration increases in comparison to the first
x
.
Moreover, even when removing
y
, the negative effect this might create is not large enough to overcome the gaps we create by pumping
x
, so we end up with a superior configuration. This idea is depicted in
Fig.

20

An important point to stress is that the pumped runs of the
V_{j}
sets might indeed cross each other, but if this happens, it must be so far above the original configuration, that it maintains the overall increase.

Figure 20
:
Replacing
y
with
x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
. Visually, the increase seems “steeper”, but it is the same slope as
x
, only much much longer. Also notice that runs from
V_{1}
eventually cross those of
V_{2}
(and so some runs might be overtaken by
V_{2}
), but this happens way above the original
V_{1}
states.

We now turn this wild hand-waving to a precise argument.
Let
r\in S^{\prime}
.
We start by analyzing runs on the words
uxy
and
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
. Specifically, we show that

\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{r}},uxy)\leq\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{r}},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})
.
Using the notations of
Definition

16.1
(with
r
as the starting state),
since
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{ux}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}})=B
, then by induction we also have that
B=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{ux^{k}}})
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Consider some
r^{\prime}\in B
, and assume
r^{\prime}\in V_{j}
in the partition given by
Item

3
of
Definition

16.1
.
By
Proposition

16.2
, there exist
k_{j,x},k_{j,y}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k_{j,x}=0
if and only if
k_{j,y}=0
if and only if
k_{j,x}+k_{j,y}=0
.

Let

\rho:r\xrightarrow{u}p_{0}\xrightarrow{x}p_{1}\xrightarrow{x}p_{2}\cdots p_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}-1}\xrightarrow{x}p_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}=r^{\prime}

be a minimal-weight run. By
Proposition

16.2
, for all
0\leq i\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}
we have that
p_{i}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
for
j^{\prime}\geq j

(otherwise there is a transition from lower
V_{j^{\prime\prime}}
to a higher one, which is a contradiction).
Denote
\boldsymbol{d_{i}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(r,ux^{i})
. We aim to show that restricted to
V_{j}
, the configuration
\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}
is superior to
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}
.
Note that for every
0\leq i<2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}
we have
\boldsymbol{d_{i-1}}\leq\boldsymbol{d_{i}}
.
Indeed, by
Definition

16.1
each
V_{j}
can only (weakly) increase. This, however, is not enough to prove our claim, since once
i
gets high enough, the gaps are no longer maintained and the
V_{j}
sets may “interfere” with each other, and prevent certain states from strictly increasing. Then, removing
y
may decrease things, which can be problematic.

We consider two cases.

•

If
p_{i}\in V_{j}
for all
0\leq i\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}
. In particular, the minimal run leading to each
p_{i}
stems from
V_{j}
, when starting at
\boldsymbol{d_{i-1}}
.
We claim (by induction) that
\boldsymbol{d_{i}}(s)=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+ik_{j,x}
for all
i
.

The base case
i=0
is trivial, since
\boldsymbol{d_{0}}=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}
. Assume correctness for
i
, we prove for
i+1
.
By the induction hypothesis, we have that
\boldsymbol{d_{i}}(s)=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+ik_{j,x}
.
By the observation above, we have that

\boldsymbol{d_{i+1}}(s)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{d_{i}}}(x,V_{j}\to s)
.
Since this expression depends only on states in
V_{j}
, then by the above we can restrict attention to
V_{j}
, and therefore write

\boldsymbol{d_{i+1}}(s)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c_{u}}}(x,V_{j}\to s)+ik_{j,x}=\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s)+ik_{j,x}=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)+(i+1)k_{j,x}

which completes the induction.

Now, if
k_{i,j}=0
then for every
s^{\prime}\in V_{j}
we have
\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}(s^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(s^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}(s^{\prime})
(since by
Definition

16.1
we also have
k_{j,y}=0
). So the “sub-configurations” of
uxy
and
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
restricted to
V_{j}
are equal. In particular,
\mathsf{mwt}(uxy,r\to r^{\prime})=\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},r\to r^{\prime})
.

If
k_{j,x}>0
, then by our choice that
M_{0}>{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}
and the inductive claim, we have

\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}(r^{\prime})>\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime})+2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}k_{j,x}\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}

However, by
Definition

16.1
and
Proposition

16.2
we have

\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}(r^{\prime})\leq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime})+k_{j,x}+k_{k,y}\leq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(y)}=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}

and we conclude that restricted to
V_{j}
, the sub-configuration of
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
is superior to that of
uxy
. In particular,
\mathsf{mwt}(uxy,r\to r^{\prime})<\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},r\to r^{\prime})
.

•

The second case is when there exists
0\leq i\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}
such that
p_{i}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
for some
j^{\prime}>j
.
Intuitively, in this case the run on
x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
goes so high that it mingles with e.g.,
V_{j+1}
. From there, it must remain much higher than any weight that
xy
can attain starting from
V_{j}
(and in particular the weight of reaching
r^{\prime}
)

By
Corollary

16.4
, for every
s\in V_{j^{\prime\prime}}
with
j^{\prime\prime}>j
and every
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c_{u}}}(x^{k},s\to B)\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}|S|
.
As observed above, for every
i^{\prime}
we have
\boldsymbol{d_{i^{\prime}-1}}\leq\boldsymbol{d_{i^{\prime}}}
, and therefore we in particular have

\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{d_{i}}}(x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}-i},p_{i}\to B)\geq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}|S|

but by
Item

3a
of
Definition

16.1
, for the maximal state
s^{\prime}\in V_{j}
we have

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s)-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}|S|>\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})+8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(|S|+1){\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}^{2}-{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x)}|S|\gg\\
&\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(s^{\prime})+{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}\geq\mathsf{mwt}_{\boldsymbol{c_{u}}}(xy,V_{j}\to B)\end{split}

Since
p_{0}\xrightarrow{xy}r^{\prime}
and
p_{0}\in V_{j}
, it follows that
\mathsf{mwt}(uxy,r\to r^{\prime})<\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}},r\to r^{\prime})
.

We remark that this latter case cannot occur if
k_{j,x}=0
, but we do not use this fact.

Since the above works for every
j
and every
r^{\prime}
, we conclude that
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}\leq\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}}
.
Therefore, we immediately have that

\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}},v)\leq\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}},v)
. Recall that this is all under
r
as the initial state, and works for every
r\in S^{\prime}
.
We can conclude the first property we want to prove, namely:
for every
r\in S^{\prime},t\in S
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(uxyv,r\to t)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v,r\to t)
.

Obtaining the second item is now easy. Observe that since both
x
and
y
are cycles on
B
, i.e.,
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(B,x)=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(B,y)=B
, then

\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}},v))=\mathsf{supp}(\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{d_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}},v))

Also, by the first property above, we can also prefix
w_{1}
and still maintain configuration superiority. That is,

\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},w_{1}uxyv)\leq\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{s_{\texttt{init}}},w_{1}ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v)

and again the configurations have the same support.
This brings us under the conditions of
Lemma

13.7
(two support-equivalent configurations with a superiority relation). Applying the lemma with the suffix
w_{2}
, we have

\upphi(\mathsf{next\_conf}(\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},w_{1}uxyv),w_{2}))\leq\upphi(\mathsf{next\_conf}(\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}}w_{1}ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v),w_{2}))

And since we start from
\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}}
, this simplifies to
\upphi(w_{1}uxyvw_{2})\leq\upphi(w_{1}ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}vw_{2})
, as required.

Step 2: from
x^{*}
to
\alpha_{B,x}

Using the previous step, we now need to prove the following.

•

For every
r\in S^{\prime}
,
t\in S
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v,r\to t)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(u\alpha_{B,x}v,r\to t)
.

•

\upphi(w_{1}ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}vw_{2})\leq\upphi(w_{1}u\alpha_{B,x}vw_{2})
.

Intuitively, we already repeat
x
enough times so that all its behaviors are “stabilized”, and can be replaced by
\alpha_{B,x}
.

Technically, all the work is already done by
Lemma

14.4
, as follows.
Recall that
M_{0}
is chosen so that for every state
r\in S^{\prime}
it holds that
ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v
is an unfolding
\mathsf{unfold}(u,\alpha_{B,x},v\wr F)
with
F
large enough.
We are therefore within the conditions of
Lemma

14.4
, with
r
as the initial state. We then have that either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness (in which case we are done), or from Item 1
Lemma

14.4
in we get in particular that

\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{r}},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}v})\leq\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{r}},u\alpha_{B,x}v)
(by applying the lemma with the suffix
v
). This in means that for every
t\in S
we have

\begin{split}&\mathsf{mwt}(ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}v,r\to t)=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{r}},ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}v})(t)\leq\\
&\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{r}},u\alpha_{B,x}v)=\mathsf{mwt}(u\alpha_{B,x}v,r\to t)\end{split}

concluding the first item, and combined with Step 1 concludes Item 2 of our proof.

Finally, to conclude the potential inequality we again apply
Lemma

14.4
. This time, we recall that
M_{0}
is also chosen such that
M_{0}=\mathsf{unfold}(w_{1}u,\alpha_{B,x},vw_{2}\wr F)
for
F
large enough. Again we are within the scope of
Lemma

14.4
, this time with the initial state
s_{0}
(i.e., the “real” initial state).
Thus, again either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness (in which case we are done), or
\upphi(w_{1}ux^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}vw_{2})=\upphi(w_{2}u\alpha_{B,x}vw_{2})
, and combined with the potential inequality of Step 1, we conclude Item 3 of our proof for the specific
w_{1},w_{2}
we start with.
Since the above works for every
w_{1},w_{2}
, we are done.

Note that while the proof requires us to first fix
w_{1},w_{2}
in order to choose
M_{0}
, eventually we end up with the word
u\alpha_{B,x}v
, which satisfies the claim regardless of
w_{1},w_{2}
.
∎

16.2
Existence of Separated Increasing Infixes

We now turn to show that separated increasing infixes exist. The overall approach is to analyze the run structure of words, combined with the Ramsey-style argument we show in
Theorem

15.3
.
In the remainder of the section, we fix a finite alphabet
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty}
and a corresponding constant
W_{\max}
that is the maximal weight (in absolute value) appearing in any transition on the letters in
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

In the following we focus on words over
\Gamma^{\prime}
decomposed as
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
. The intuition behind this decomposition is the following:
w_{1}
is a prefix leading to some set of states.

w_{2}
separates several outgoing runs from each state after
w_{1}
to a large gap.

w_{3}
is a very long infix that maintains a separation between runs (and induces a separated increasing infix in some form), and
w_{4}
is a suffix.

Remark 16.7

(Implicit Decomposition to
(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})
)
.

When we write
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
we assume that the decomposition is explicit (instead of writing
(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})
). When we wish to consider the entire word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
we explicitly mention that we consider it “as a concatenation”.

We start with several definitions regarding the runs structure of different parts of this decomposition, mainly focused around
w_{3}
.

Definition 16.8

(Independent Runs)
.

Consider a word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, a state
s\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})
and two seamless runs
\rho_{1}:s\xrightarrow{w_{2}}p_{1}\xrightarrow{w_{3}}q_{2}
and
\rho_{2}:s\xrightarrow{w_{2}}p_{3}\xrightarrow{w_{3}}q_{4}
. We say that
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are
independent with respect to
s
and
w_{3}
if there exists
G\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every prefix
u
of
w_{3}
we have
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{w_{2}u}_{2})-\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{w_{2}u}_{1})\geq G
where
\rho^{w_{2}u}_{i}
is the prefix of
\rho_{i}
on the word
w_{2}u
.

In this case we also say that
\rho_{2}
is above
\rho_{1}
with gap
G
(we also use “not within gap
G
” when the order is not important).

Similarly, a set of runs
\{\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell}\}
from
s
on
w_{2}w_{3}
is
independent with gap
G
if its runs are pairwise-independent with gap
G
(a singleton set
\{\rho_{1}\}
is independent by definition).

Observe that two seamless runs with distinct weights in some prefix, must be state-disjoint from that prefix and on (otherwise at least one of them is not seamless). It follows that for a state
s
, every set of independent runs is of size at most
|S|
.

Our overall approach in the next few sections is to “untangle” runs on words in order to obtain a very structured behavior. To do so, we consider a sequence of words
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
with
w_{3}
increasing in length. This is captured by our first definition.

Definition 16.9

(

Elongating Words Sequence)
.

A function from
\mathbb{N}
to
\Gamma^{\prime*}
is an
elongating words sequence
, denoted
\textsf{words}:\mathbb{N}\to\Gamma^{\prime*}
, if for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
such that
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
has a seamless baseline run and
|w_{3}|\geq m
.

For elongating words sequences
\textsf{words},\textsf{words}^{\prime}
, we say that
\textsf{words}^{\prime}
is a
faithful restriction
of
words
if for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
t\in\mathbb{N}
with
\textsf{words}^{\prime}(m)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
and
\textsf{words}(t)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
such that
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1}
and
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
as a concatenation.

Intuitively, a faithful restriction
\textsf{words}^{\prime}
of
words
only returns “similar” words to
words
, but
words
may return things that are not returned by
\textsf{words}^{\prime}
. We typically use this when we modify
words
to obtain other function in a way that is not too “disruptive”.

Since
|w_{3}|\geq m
in an elongated words sequence, we can obtain a faithful restriction of it by truncating each
w_{3}
to be of length exactly
m
, and assigning the suffix of
w_{3}
to
w_{4}
. For convenience, we often make this assumption in this section (but drop it in following sections). We explicitly define it as follows.

Definition 16.10

(Exact Words Sequence)
.

An elongated words sequence is
exact
, denoted
\mathsf{xwords}
, if for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathsf{xwords}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
we have
|w_{3}|=m
.

We now specify two important possible properties of elongating word sequences. The first concerns the gaps between independent runs.

Definition 16.11

(
\ell
-Sparse)
.

For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, an exact word sequence
\mathsf{xwords}
is
\ell
-sparse
if for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\mathsf{xwords}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and
for every

Intuitively, an
\ell
-sparse function returns words where the gaps between independent runs are large (and grow with
m
). We assume that when
\mathsf{xwords}
is
\ell
-sparse, the sets

.

Notice that the requirement on

is that all words have the same number of independent runs. We show that in fact it is sufficient that infinitely many words have

.

16{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(|S|+1)(mW_{\max})^{2}

.
Then there is a faithful restriction
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}
of
\mathsf{xwords}
that is
\ell
-sparse.

Proof.

Consider
m\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathsf{xwords}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
.
For every
n<m
, consider the word
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
where
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1}
,
w^{\prime}_{2}=w_{2}
,
w^{\prime}_{3}=w_{3}[1,n]
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=w_{3}[n+1,m]w_{4}
(i.e., we move letters from
w_{3}
to
w_{4}
so that
|w^{\prime}_{3}|=n
).
Clearly we can replace
\mathsf{xwords}(n)
by
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
and obtain a faithful restriction. Moreover, the sparsity requirement holds in
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
, since the independent runs on
w_{3}
can be shortened to independent runs in
w^{\prime}_{3}
, and the gap requirement for
m
is stricter than for
n<m
.

Thus, if there are infinitely many
m
for which the sparsity requirement holds, we can “fill the gaps” between these values of
m
by shortening the respective
w_{3}
’s.
∎

The next property concerns words that have a long infix in which no run decreases too much.

A function

is
D
-dip
if

is
D
-dip for every

.

Intuitively, a word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
is a dip word if every infix of
w_{3}
induces “dips” (i.e. decreasing runs) losing at most
D
weight (
D
stands for “Dip”, also in the following).
Thus, the overall trend of runs on
w_{3}
in
words
is non-decreasing, with some small exceptions.

Our final definition for this section concerns the states that are not part of the independent runs. Specifically, we measure whether such states are close to independent runs prescribed by the
\ell
-sparse function.
If all states are within gap
G
of an independent run, we say that the function is a
G
-cover
.

16.2.1
Existence of Increasing Infixes Assuming A
G
-Cover

Fix some dip level
D
. We now turn to analyze the setting where a
G
-cover exists, and we show that under this assumption, a separated increasing infix exists. This is the first instance where we use a mild version of our
Zooming
technique (
Section

6.1
).

Lemma 16.15

(

From
D
-Dip and Cover to Increasing Infix)
.

Let
D,\ell,G\in\mathbb{N}
such that there exists a function
\mathsf{xwords}
that is
\ell
-sparse,
D
-dip and a
G
-cover.

Then there exists some
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\mathsf{xwords}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and a decomposition
w_{3}=u^{\prime}\cdot x\cdot y\cdot z^{\prime}
such that
u\cdot x\cdot y\cdot z
is a separated increasing infix from

\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})

for
u=w_{2}\cdot u^{\prime}
and

.

We prove the lemma in the remainder of the section.
Let
G\in\mathbb{N}
as assumed in the lemma.
Our first step is to define coloring functions on infixes of infinitely many
w_{3}
words, so that we are in our Ramsey framework (
Section

15
).

We again start with some definitions. Consider a state
p
and the configuration
\boldsymbol{c_{p}}
that assigns
0
to
p
and
\infty
elsewhere.
Motivated by increasing infixes, we consider two characteristics of infixes.
The first characteristic, dubbed
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(x)
, stores the order of the states in the configuration reached from
\boldsymbol{c_{p}}
by
x
, and also stores which states are within distance
G
from each other.

Definition 16.16

(
\mathsf{diff\_type}
)
.

For a constant
G\in\mathbb{N}
and a word
x
, let
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(x,\boldsymbol{c_{p}})
.
The
G
-capped difference type
of
x
from
p
is

\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(x)=\langle\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{x}}),\leq_{x},\mathsf{near}_{p,G}(x)\rangle
where
\leq_{x}\subseteq\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{x}})
is a partial order on the reachable states defined by
s_{1}\leq_{x}s_{2}
if
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(s_{1})\leq\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(s_{2})
, and

\mathsf{near}_{x,G}=\{(s_{1},i,s_{2})\mid i=|\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(s_{2})-\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(s_{1})|\in\{0,\ldots,G\}\}

We denote the set of all possible
\mathsf{diff\_type}
by
\mathsf{DIFF\_TYPES}_{p,G}=\{\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(x)\mid x\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty})^{*}\}

Orthogonally to
\mathsf{diff\_type}
, our second characteristic measures the weight gained along a run, capped between two specific valued.

Definition 16.17

(
\mathsf{gain\_type}
)
.

For a constant
D\in\mathbb{N}
and words
u,v
, we define the
D
-capped gain type
of
(u,v)
as
\mathsf{gain\_type}_{D}(u,v)=\{(s_{1},i,s_{2})\in S\times\{-D,\ldots,D\cdot|S|\}\times S\mid s_{1}\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},u)\wedge\exists\rho:s_{1}\xrightarrow{v}s_{2}.\ \mathsf{wt}(\rho)=i\}
.

We denote the set of all possible
\mathsf{gain\_type}
by
\mathsf{GAIN\_TYPES}_{D}=\{\mathsf{gain\_type}_{D}(u,v)\mid u,v\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty})^{*}\}

That is, the gain type consists of triples of the form
(s_{1},i,s_{2})
where
s_{1}
is reachable from
s_{0}
via
u
, and there is a run that gains weight
i\in\{-D,\ldots,D\cdot|S|\}
from
s_{1}
to
s_{2}
.
Notice that here we allow a small decrease of at most
-D
, but no further, and we keep track of an increase up to
D\cdot|S|
.

Crucially, note that for a given
p,G
and
D
, both
\mathsf{DIFF\_TYPES}_{p.G}
and
\mathsf{GAIN\_TYPES}_{D}
are finite.

Recall that for increasing values of
m
, the length of the
w_{3}
infix of
\mathsf{xwords}(m)
also increases.
We construct an infix-colored set (as in
Section

15
) as follows.
For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
\mathsf{xwords}(n)=w^{n}_{1}w^{n}_{2}w^{n}_{3}w^{n}_{4}
and note that since
|w^{n}_{3}|=n
, then these words are unique
11
11

11

A nit-picking note: the word
w^{n}_{1}w^{n}_{2}w^{n}_{3}w^{n}_{4}
is unique with respect to this particular decomposition (see
Remark

16.7
. It may be equal to some other
w^{k}_{1}w^{k}_{2}w^{k}_{3}w^{k}_{4}
as a concatenation.

We now construct the infix-colored set
C=\{(w_{3}^{n},\lambda_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as follows. Our color set consists of tuples of a
\mathsf{gain\_type}
and two
\mathsf{diff\_type}
for each reachable state (and a placeholder
\bot
for unreachable states). More precisely, the color set is

\begin{split}\mathfrak{C}=\{(\chi,(\tau_{p,1},\tau_{p,2})_{p\in S})\mid\chi\in\mathsf{GAIN\_TYPES}_{D}\wedge\forall p\in S.\ \tau_{p,1},\tau_{p,2}\in\mathsf{DIFF\_TYPES}_{p,G}\cup\{\bot\}\}\end{split}

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we define a coloring function
\lambda_{n}:\mathfrak{I}_{|w_{3}^{n}|}\to\mathfrak{C}
as follows: let
1\leq i\leq j\leq|w_{3}^{n}|
(i.e.,
(i,j)\in\mathfrak{I}_{|w_{3}^{n}|}
in the notation of
Section

15
), let
u=w_{3}[1,i-1]
and
v=w_{3}[i,j]
then

\lambda_{n}(i,j)=(\chi,(\tau_{p,1},\tau_{p,2})_{p\in S})
where

\chi=\mathsf{gain\_type}_{D}(w_{1}w_{2}u,w_{1}w_{2}uv)
and for every
p\in S
we have

(\tau_{p,1},\tau_{p,2})=(\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{2}u),\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{2}uv))\text{ if }p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})

and
(\tau_{p,1},\tau_{p,2})=(\bot,\bot)
otherwise.

Intuitively, each infix of
w_{3}
is colored by three elements: the gain difference upon reading the infix, and the configurations before and after reading the infix for each state
p
reachable after reading
w_{1}
(including ghost states), described by their
G
-capped difference, so that we only have finitely many colors.
Recall that the assumption of the lemma that
\mathsf{xwords}
is a
G
-cover states that every run from
p
on
w_{2}x
remains within gap
G
of an independent run in
I_{p}
. Tracking
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}
therefore suffices to track all relevant information about the runs from
p
.

\mathsf{pc}(x)=2^{16({\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}|S|\mathsf{cost}(x))^{2}}
.

of

).

Fix

Proposition 16.18
.

Requirement 2 of
Definition

16.1
holds.

That is, denote
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{p}},u)
,
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{p}},ux)
, and
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{p}},uxy)
, then
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{ux}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}})
.

Proof.

By Item
2
of
Theorem

15.3
we have that
\lambda(x)=\lambda(y)
(we slightly abuse notation and color the infix word instead of the index pair). By the choice of our coloring function, this implies that

\mathsf{gain\_type}_{D}(w_{1}u,w_{1}ux)=\mathsf{gain\_type}_{D}(w_{1}ux,w_{1}uxy)
and for every

we have
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{1}w_{2}u)=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{1}w_{2}ux)
and

. The latter two equalities therefore imply

\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{2}u)=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{2}ux)=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{2}uxy)

we immediately get that the first components are equal, i.e.,
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{ux}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}})
.
∎

Let

and let
I_{p}
be a set of independent runs as assumed by the
\ell
-sparsity of
\mathsf{xwords}
.
From
Definition

16.11
, the gap between the independent runs in
I_{p}
is at least
16{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(|S|+1)(mW_{\max})^{2}
, and since
|w_{3}|=m>2G
, the gap is (much) greater than
2G
.

Consider a configuration
\boldsymbol{c}
and states
p,q\in S
, we write
p\sim_{\boldsymbol{c}}q
if
|\boldsymbol{c}(p)-\boldsymbol{c}(q)|\leq 2G
. We start by claiming that for every prefix
v
of
xy
, for
\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{p}},uv)
the relation
\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}}
is an equivalence relation.
Indeed, it is trivially reflexive and symmetric. Moreover, if
|\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(p)-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(q)|\leq 2G
, then by
the assumption that
\mathsf{xwords}
is a
G
-cover (
Definition

16.14
),
there exists some states
r,r^{\prime}
that are along independent runs in
I_{p}
satisfying
|\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(p)-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(r)|\leq G
and
|\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(r^{\prime})|\leq G
.
However, since the gap between independent runs in
I_{p}
is much greater than
2G
,
it follows that
r=r^{\prime}
.
That is, two equivalent states are within gap
G
of a corresponding state in an independent run. In particular, this entails the transitivity of
\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}}
.

Consider
B=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{u}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{ux}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{uxy}})
, we partition
B
according to the equivalence classes of these configurations, as follows.
Let
I_{p}=\{\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{k}\}
be the independent runs from
p
, and assume that
\rho_{i+1}
is above
\rho_{i}
for all
1\leq i<k
.
Denote by
\rho_{i}(v)
the state of
\rho_{i}
after reading the prefix
v
.
For
\xi\in\{u,ux,uxy\}
we define a partition
B=V^{\xi}_{1}\cup\ldots\cup V^{\xi}_{k}
by setting
V^{\xi}_{i}=\{q\mid q\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}}\rho_{i}(\xi)\}
, i.e., the set of states that are equivalent to
\rho_{i}(\xi)
. We now show that these partitions are not dependent on
\xi
.

Proposition 16.19
.

The partitions induced for each
\xi\in\{u,ux,uxy\}
are identical. That is,
V^{\xi}_{i}=V^{\zeta}_{i}
for every
\xi,\zeta\in\{u,ux,uxy\}
.

Proof.

Recall that we obtain
x,y
using our Ramsey argument, and due to its coloring we have

\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{2}u)=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{2}ux)=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{2}uxy)

First, observe that
\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}}
is the same relation for all
\xi
. Indeed, the relation
\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}}
only depends on
\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}
, and the way we obtained our observation above (that
\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}}
is an equivalence relation for all prefixes
v
of
xy
) in fact shows that for every
\xi
, the states corresponding to
\{\rho_{i}(\xi)\}_{i=1}^{k}
are representatives of the
k
equivalence classes.

Thus, all that remains to be proved is that there is no permutation in the order of the
V_{i}
between the infixes.
That is, consider e.g.,
V^{u}_{1}
and
V^{ux}_{1}
. These sets are equivalence classes of
\sim_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
(which as we just showed is independent of
\xi
). However, it is not a-priori guaranteed that
V^{u}_{1}=V^{ux}_{1}
, rather it could be that e.g.,
V^{u}_{1}=V^{ux}_{3}
.
Intuitively, however, this cannot happen because the equivalence of
\mathsf{diff\_type}
implies in particular an equivalent ordering of the states (i.e.,
\leq_{u},\leq_{ux}
and
\leq_{uxy}
, as per
Definition

16.16
).
We show the equivalence of
V^{u}_{i}
and
V^{ux}_{i}
, the proof for
V^{ux}_{i}
and
V^{uxy}_{i}
is word-for-word identical, up to replacing
u
by
ux
and
ux
by
uxy
.

Assume by way of contradiction that there exists some permutation
\pi
of
\{1,\ldots,k\}
such that
\pi
is not the identity permutation, and
V^{u}_{i}=V^{ux}_{\pi(i)}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
Since
\pi
is not the identity, it has an inversion – some
1\leq j<h\leq k
such that
\pi(j)>\pi(h)
.
Let
q\in V^{u}_{j}
and
r\in V^{u}_{h}
, then
q\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}}\rho_{j}(u)
and
r\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}}\rho_{h}(u)
, and therefore
q\leq_{u}r
(since
\rho_{j}(u)\leq_{u}\rho_{h}(u)
and the gap between them is much greater than
2G
),

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
disable,inline,color=red!25,size=

and moreover there is a strict inequality in the weights:
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q)<\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r)
.
However, we then have
q\in V^{ux}_{\pi(j)}
and
r\in V^{ux}_{\pi(h)}
, and with the same argument, namely
\rho_{\pi(h)}(ux)\leq_{ux}\rho_{\pi(j)}(ux)
, we have
r\leq_{ux}q
(again with a strict inequality in weights).

This is in contradiction to the fact that
\leq_{u}\equiv\leq_{ux}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to show that Item

of

holds, for the partition

(we can omit the superscript due to

).

and

for some

, we claim that for every

it holds that

.

is at least

and that

and

(since

is an infix of

). Note that

, then we have that

\begin{split}&16{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(|S|+1)(mW_{\max})^{2}=8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(|S|+1)(mW_{\max})^{2}+8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(|S|+1)(mW_{\max})^{2}\\
&>8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(|S|+1)(mW_{\max})^{2}+16G\geq 8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(|S|+1)(|xy|W_{\max})^{2}+16G\geq\\
&8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}(|S|+1){\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)}^{2}+16G\end{split}

We turn to show part

in Requirement

. That is, we need to show that for every

there exist

such that for every

, and that in addition

.
The existence of

and by

. The harder part here is showing that these constants are non-negative.

. Also recall that

.
