, we can conclude that for every
1\leq t<L
, the partition induced by
\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{ux\cdot y_{1}\cdots y_{t}}}}
is also
V_{1}\cup\cdots\cup V_{k}
, with the same ordering as above.

Let
1\leq j\leq k
and consider the “minimal” state
q\in V_{j}
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q)=\min\{\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r)\mid r\in V_{j}\}
. Note that
q
is also minimal after
ux
and after
uxy_{1}\cdots y_{t}
for every
t
, since the ordering of the states in the corresponding configurations are the same (due to the equivalence in
\mathsf{diff\_type}
).
By the minimality of
q
, for every
r\in V_{j}
we have
0\leq\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q)\leq 2G
. By the equivalence in
\mathsf{diff\_type}
(specifically, since the gaps are tracked up to size
2G
), we also have for every
\xi\in\{u,ux,uxy_{1},\ldots,uxy_{1}\cdots y_{L-1}\}
that
\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q)
. By rearranging, we have

\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(r)=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r)+(\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q))
. In particular, by setting
k_{j,x}=(\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(q))
(and similarly
k_{j,y}
), we have the result for
integer
constants. It remains to prove the non-negativity of the constant, and the special condition for
0
.

To this end, we start by observing that for any infix
\xi
as above and state
q\in V_{j}
, there are no runs leading to
q
from any state in
V_{i}
with
i<j
, and there is a minimal run to
q
from some state in
V_{j}
(as well as possibly-minimal runs from “above”
V_{j}
). We prove this in two parts.

Intuitively, if
i<j
, then
\rho_{i}
is far below
\rho_{j}
along all of
uxy
, and therefore no state in
\rho_{i}
can yield a run to a corresponding state of
\rho_{j}
, as that would “drag down”
\rho_{j}
too close to
\rho_{i}
. By extension, no state that is equivalent to
\rho_{i}
can reach a state that is equivalent to
\rho_{j}
.
We formalize this intuition, the approach is illustrated in
Fig.

22
.

Figure 22
:
The red arrow is the assumption by contradiction. The green lines signify “small” differences. If the red arrow is a run, then it has a small gap, which pulls together
\rho_{j}
and
\rho_{i}
, leading to a contradiction.

Proposition 16.20
.

Let
\sigma\in\Gamma^{\prime}
be the next letter read after a prefix
v
of
xy
, let
i<j
and let
r,q\in S
with
r\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}}\rho_{i}(uv)
and
q\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}}\rho_{j}(uv\sigma)
, then there is no transition
(r,\sigma,c,q)\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}^{\infty}
with
c<\infty
.

Proof.

Let
r,q\in S
with
r\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}}\rho_{i}(uv)
and
q\sim_{\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}}\rho_{j}(uv\sigma)
, and assume by way of contradiction that there is a transition
(r,\sigma,c,q)\in\widehat{\Delta}_{\infty}^{\infty}
with
c<\infty
(and in particular
c\leq W_{\max}
). It follows that
\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(r)\leq W_{\max}
. Further assume that
r
is the minimal state with this property with respect to
\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}
. It follows that
|\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(r)|\leq W_{\max}
(intuitively, we are merely avoiding the case where the absolute value grows because
q
is dragged even further down below
r
, which would only simplify things for us).

Observe that
|\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(\rho_{j}(uv))-\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(\rho_{j}(uv\sigma))|\leq W_{\max}
, as this is a single transition in a seamless run.
We then have

\begin{split}&|\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(\rho_{j}(uv))-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(\rho_{i}(uv))|=\\
&|\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(\rho_{j}(uv))-\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(\rho_{j}(uv\sigma))+\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(\rho_{j}(uv\sigma))-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(\rho_{i}(uv))|\leq\\
&|\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(\rho_{j}(uv))-\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(\rho_{j}(uv\sigma))|+|\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(\rho_{j}(uv\sigma))-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(\rho_{i}(uv))|\leq\\
&W_{\max}+|\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(\rho_{j}(uv\sigma))-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(\rho_{i}(uv))|=\\
&W_{\max}+|\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(\rho_{j}(uv\sigma))-\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(q)+\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(r)+\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(\rho_{i}(uv))|\leq\\
&W_{\max}+|\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(\rho_{j}(uv\sigma))-\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(q)|+|\boldsymbol{c_{uv\sigma}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(r)|+|\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(\rho_{i}(uv))|\leq\\
&W_{\max}+2G+W_{\max}+2G\leq 2W_{\max}+4G\\
\end{split}

This, however, is a contradiction, since by the second inequality of
Eq.

16.1
we have (in particular)

|\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(\rho_{j}(uv))-\boldsymbol{c_{uv}}(\rho_{i}(uv))|>8W_{\max}+16G
.

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
todo:

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=

ACCOUNTING: Checked

∎

A corollary of
Proposition

16.20
is that any run on infix
\xi
reaching
q\in V_{j}
must start from some
r\in V_{i}
with
i\geq j
. We now claim that at least one minimal run actually comes from
V_{j}
.

Proposition 16.21
.

Let
\zeta,\xi\in\{u,ux,uxy_{1},\ldots,uxy_{1}\cdots y_{L-1}\}
such that
\xi
is a strict prefix of
\zeta
and
\zeta=\xi\cdot\chi
, and let
q\in V_{j}
for some
j
.
There exists
r\in V_{j}
and a run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{\xi}r\xrightarrow{\chi}q
such that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\mathsf{mwt}(\zeta,p\to q)
.

Proof.

Figure 23
:
The highlighted areas are “near”
\rho_{j^{\prime}}
(above) and
\rho_{j}
(below). The red run is
\mu
, and seems to cross on the letter
\sigma
between the areas, which cannot happen. Thus,
q^{\prime\prime}
is actually reachable from
r^{\prime\prime}
within the lower area.

The proof idea is depicted in
Fig.

23
.
Assume by way of contradiction that the claim does not hold, and let
\mu:p\xrightarrow{\xi}r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\chi}q
be a minimal-weight run to
q
with
r^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
for
j^{\prime}>j
. Recall that in every configuration along
uxy
, every state is within gap
G
of an independent run in
I_{p}
(since
\mathsf{xwords}
is a
G
-cover). Since
r^{\prime}\in V_{j^{\prime}}
and
q\in V_{j}
, then at some point along the run
\mu
the states got into within gap
G
of
\rho_{j}
(whereas
\mu
starts within gap
G
of
\rho_{j^{\prime}}
).
Take
\mu
to be the minimal-weight run with a maximal-length suffix near
\rho_{j}
. That is,
\mu
gets within gap
G
of
\rho_{j}
the earliest among all minimal-weight runs. Write

\mu:p\xrightarrow{\xi}r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\theta}q^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sigma}q^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\eta}q
where
q^{\prime}
is not near
\rho_{j}
, and the suffix
q^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\eta}q
is near
\rho_{j}
. Note that
\sigma
is the last letter before getting near
\rho_{j}
.

Consider the transition
(q^{\prime},\sigma,c,q^{\prime\prime})
, then
|c|<W_{\max}
. However, by the gaps in
I_{p}
from
Eq.

16.1
we have
\boldsymbol{c_{\xi\theta}}(q^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi\theta}}(\rho_{j}(\xi\theta))>W_{\max}+2G
.

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
todo:

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=

ACCOUNTING: Checked

Thus, a single transition cannot decrease the weight enough to attain the value already assigned to
q^{\prime\prime}
in
\boldsymbol{c_{\xi\theta}}
. It follows that there exists another run from
p
that reaches
q^{\prime\prime}
with weight
\boldsymbol{c_{\xi\theta}}
, and the last transition to
q^{\prime\prime}
must be from some state
r^{\prime\prime}
that is near
\rho_{j}
(note that it cannot come from any lower independent run due to
Proposition

16.20
).

This, however, contradicts the maximality of the suffix
\eta
, since the new run has a lower weight and a longer suffix.

Thus, there is a minimal weight run whose states along the entire suffix
\chi
remain near
\rho_{j}
, namely a run from
V_{j}
to
V_{j}
.
∎

Proposition 16.22
.

Let
\xi,\zeta\in\{u,ux,uxy_{1},\ldots,uxy_{1}\cdots y_{L-1}\}
such that
\xi
is a prefix of
\zeta
, and let
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}})
, then
\boldsymbol{c_{\zeta}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(q)\geq 0
.

Moreover, if
\boldsymbol{c_{\zeta}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(q)=0
, then
\boldsymbol{c_{\zeta^{\prime}}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi^{\prime}}}(q)=0

for every
\xi^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime}
such that
\xi^{\prime}
is a prefix of
\zeta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Denote
\zeta=\xi\chi
. Recall that
\xi
and
\zeta
have the same color, and in particular
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{2}\xi)=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{2}\zeta)
.
Assume by way of contradiction that
\boldsymbol{c_{\zeta}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(q)<0
with
q\in V_{j}
for some
1\leq j\leq k
. We can assume without loss of generality that
q
is the minimal state in
V_{j}
, i.e., that
\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(q)=\min\{\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(r)\mid r\in V_{j}\}
.
Indeed, the gaps between the states in
V_{j}
are identical in
\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}
and in
\boldsymbol{c_{\zeta}}
, and therefore for every
r\in V_{j}
it holds that
\boldsymbol{c_{\zeta}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(r)<0
, so we can take
q
to be the minimal state.

By
Proposition

16.21
, there is some
r\in V_{j}
and a minimal-weight run
\rho:r\xrightarrow{\chi}q
. Since
q
is minimal, it follows that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\boldsymbol{c_{\zeta}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(r)<0
.

Recall that
\mathsf{xwords}
(and therefore
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
) is
D
-dip (
Definition

16.13
).
In particular
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\boldsymbol{c_{\zeta}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(r)>-D
.
Further recall that the
\mathsf{gain\_type}
component of our Ramsey-coloring tracks runs of weights in
\{-D,\ldots,D\cdot|S|\}
. Thus, we have
(r,\mathsf{wt}(\rho),q)\in\mathsf{gain\_type}(w_{1}w_{2}\xi,w_{1}w_{2},\xi\chi)
.
Looking at the
\mathsf{gain\_type}
component of the idempotent coloring, we have that for every infix
y_{i}
for
1\leq i<L
, since
y_{i}
is colored the same as
\chi
, then
(r,\mathsf{wt}(\rho),q)\in\mathsf{gain\_type}(w_{1}w_{2}uxy_{1}\cdots y_{i-1},w_{1}w_{2}uxy_{1}\cdots y_{i})
.

We claim that this decreasing run induces a longer decreasing run from
V_{j}
to
V_{j}
. Specifically, consider the configuration
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
. Since
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{2}ux)=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{p,G}(w_{2}\xi)
then

\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(q)

and reading
y_{1}
from
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}
also admits the run
\rho
, so
\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(q)>\boldsymbol{c_{uxy_{1}}}(q)
.
But
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy_{1}}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{uxy_{1}}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(q)
, so we can repeat this argument for
y_{2}
, and by induction for all
1\leq i<L
.

Since
L>D\cdot|S|>D
, it follows that
\boldsymbol{c_{uxy_{1}\cdots y_{L-1}}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{ux}}(q)<-D
(since
q
decreases by at least
1
with every
y_{i}
).
By
Proposition

16.21
and the minimality of
q
, we can trace a single run
\rho^{\prime}:r^{\prime}\xrightarrow{y_{1}\cdots y_{L-1}}q
from some
r^{\prime}\in V_{j}
with
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime})<-D
. This is in contradiction to the fact that
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
is
D
-dip. We therefore conclude that there are no such negative runs.

Finally, consider the case where
\boldsymbol{c_{\zeta}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(q)=0
. Following the same reasoning as above (i.e., taking a minimal run from
V_{j}
to the minimal
q
satisfying this), we can conclude by a similar induction that for every
\xi\in\{u,ux,uxy_{1},\ldots,uxy_{1}\cdots y_{L-1}\}
and
r\in V_{j}
, we have
\boldsymbol{c_{\xi}}(r)=\boldsymbol{c_{u}}(r)
, so the configuration restricted to
V_{j}
remains unchanged throughout the run on
xy
, as required.
∎

Proposition

16.22
then concludes part
(b)
in Requirement
3
of
Definition

16.1
.

Finally, in order to conclude the requirements of
Definition

16.5
, we need to show that

is a stable cycle (
Definition

10.5
). Recall that

.

Proposition 16.23
.

(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime},u),x)
is a stable cycle.

Proof.

We start by showing that

This is straightforward, but subtle.

Recall that the states of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
are tuples of the form
(p_{1},p_{2},T)
where
p_{2}
and
T
behave deterministically. Since
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},u)=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},ux)
(a corollary of
Proposition

16.18
, by taking union over all
p\in S^{\prime}
), it follows that all states in this set share the
p_{2}
and
T
components.

.
By the assumption of
Lemma

16.15
, the entire word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
has a seamless baseline run, which therefore has weight
0
by definition. It follows that
\mathsf{mwt}(x,s\to s)=0
, and therefore
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{n},s\to s)\leq 0
by induction for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We show that
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{n},s\to s)=0
and that there are no negative cycle in a single argument.

Assume by way of contradiction that there is some
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
p_{1}\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime},u)
with a negative reflexive run
\rho^{\prime}:p_{1}\xrightarrow{x^{k}}p_{1}
with
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime})=d<0
,
We can assume without loss of generality that
k\leq|S|
. Indeed, otherwise the run has the form
\rho^{\prime}:p_{1}\xrightarrow{x^{j}}r\xrightarrow{x^{\ell}}r\xrightarrow{x^{t}}p_{1}
, and either the cycle on
r
is also negative, in which case we can look at that cycle instead, or the cycle on
r
is positive, in which case we can shorten
\rho^{\prime}
and still have a negative run.

Write
\rho^{\prime}:p_{1}\xrightarrow{x}p_{2}\xrightarrow{x}\cdots\xrightarrow{x}p_{k-1}\xrightarrow{x}p_{k}
with
p_{k}=p_{1}
.
Since
(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime},u),x)
is a reflexive cycle, then in particular
p_{j}\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime},u)
for all
1\leq j\leq k
. Since
x
is an infix of
w_{3}
, and
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
is
D
-dip, then in particular
\mathsf{mwt}(x,p_{j}\to p_{j+1})>-D
for all
1\leq j<k
. We claim that in addition it holds that

\mathsf{mwt}(x,p_{j}\to p_{j+1})\leq|S|D
.
Indeed, assume by way of contradiction that
\mathsf{mwt}(x,p_{j}\to p_{j+1})>|S|D
for some
1\leq j<k
. Since
k\leq|S|
and each sub-run on
x
decreases by at most
D
, the total weight of
\rho^{\prime}
cannot be negative, in contradiction to the assumption.

Thus,
-D\leq\mathsf{mwt}(x,p_{j}\to p_{j+1})\leq|S|D
, and it follows that
(p_{j},\mathsf{mwt}(x,p_{j}\to p_{j+1}),p_{j+1})\in\mathsf{gain\_type}(w_{1}w_{2}u,w_{1}w_{2}ux)
for all
1\leq j<k
.
By the equivalent coloring of
x
and each of the
y_{i}
, for every
1\leq i<L
, it also holds that

(p_{j},\mathsf{mwt}(x,p_{j}\to p_{j+1}),p_{j+1})\in\mathsf{gain\_type}(w_{1}w_{2}uxy_{1}\cdots y_{i-1},w_{1}w_{2}uxy_{1}\cdots y_{i})

This means that we can “replicate” the run
\rho^{\prime}
using a sequence of at most
|S|
subwords from
y_{i}
. E.g., a run
p_{1}\xrightarrow{y_{1}}p_{2}\xrightarrow{y_{3}}\cdots\xrightarrow{y_{k}}p_{k}
can be constructed such that each transition on
y_{j}
gains weight
\mathsf{mwt}(x,p_{j}\to p_{j+1})
.

Now, since
L>|S|D
, we can concatenate at least
D
copies of this replication of
\rho^{\prime}
, so the corresponding run has weight at most
-D
(since
\rho^{\prime}
is negative and has weight at most
-1
). This is a contradiction to
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
being
D
-dip.

We therefore see that there are no negative reflexive cycles in
(\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime},u),x)
, and it is therefore a stable cycle.
∎

This concludes the proof of
Lemma

16.15
. Admittedly, even the most tenacious reader may have (justifiably) forgotten by now that we are within a proof of a lemma.

16.2.2
Existence of Increasing Infixes Without A Sparse
G
-Cover

In
Lemma

16.15
we establish the existence of a separated increasing infix under the assumption that there is a sparse
G
-cover.
In this section we show that this assumption in fact follows from the existence of a
D
-dip
\mathsf{xwords}
function, and can therefore be dropped.

More precisely, the existence of a sparse
G
-cover for some
G
can be obtained without any assumptions (and
D
-dip is an “orthogonal” assumption).
Intuitively, the idea is that if a seamless run stays far enough away from every independent run, then it induces another independent run. This allows us to apply an inductive argument to show the claim.
We start by showing that sparsity is enough to ensure a
G
-cover.

Lemma 16.24

(

Zooming on
\mathsf{xwords}
)
.

Let
\mathsf{xwords}
be an
\ell
-sparse function for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then there exists a faithful restriction
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}
of
\mathsf{xwords}
that is an
\ell^{\prime}
-sparse
G
-cover for some
G,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Recall that for every state
p
and a set of independent runs
I_{p}
, it holds that
|I_{p}|\leq|S|
. Also recall that the parameter
\ell
in
\ell
-sparse is
\ell=|\bigcup_{p}I_{p}|
, and therefore
\ell\leq|S|^{2}
.

We prove the lemma by reverse induction on
\ell
, starting from
|S|^{2}
and down to
1
. Note that there must be at least one independent run, since the baseline run is seamless in the word
\mathsf{xwords}
for all
m
(
Definition

16.9
).

Base case:
\ell=|S|^{2}
.

Intuitively, in this case every seamless run is independent, and therefore every state along a seamless run is within gap
0
of an independent run.

More precisely, consider a word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}=\mathsf{xwords}(m)
for some
m
.
For every
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})
let
I_{p}
be a set of independent runs. Since
\ell=|S|^{2}
, it holds that
|I_{p}|=|S|
.
For every prefix
x
of
w_{3}
consider the configuration
\boldsymbol{c_{w_{2}x}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{p}},w_{2}x)
, then it follows that
\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{w_{2}x}})=S
(by size considerations) and for every
q\in S
we have that
\boldsymbol{c_{w_{2}x}}(q)
is along some seamless run from
p
, which is therefore one of the runs in
I_{p}
, so we conclude the lemma with
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}=\mathsf{xwords}
and
G=0
.

Induction step:
\ell<|S|^{2}
.

We assume correctness of the claim for
\ell+1
, and we prove for
\ell
. We obtain
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}
by modifying
\mathsf{xwords}
, and the construction is split to two cases. We sketch the intuition before proceeding with the technical details.

First, consider the case where
\mathsf{xwords}
is already a
G
-cover for some
G\in\mathbb{N}
. In fact, we relax the definition further and only require that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}=\mathsf{xwords}(m)
, states visited after reading a prefix
x
of
w_{3}
of length at least
\frac{1}{2}m
are
G
-covered. This seemingly-overcomplicated assumption then helps us in the second case, where we can require something more strict.
We now split
w_{3}
in the middle, obtaining
w_{3}=uv
where
u=\lfloor\frac{1}{2}m\rfloor
and
v=\lceil\frac{1}{2}m\rceil
, we denote
t=|v|
.
We then define
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}(t)=w_{1}^{\prime}w_{2}^{\prime}w_{3}^{\prime}w_{4}^{\prime}
where
w_{1}^{\prime}=w_{1}
,
w_{2}^{\prime}=w_{2}u
,
w_{3}^{\prime}=v
and
w_{4}^{\prime}=w_{4}
. We show that
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}
is a
G
-cover, which follows trivially from
\mathsf{xwords}
begin a
G
-cover.
Note that this case does not require the induction hypothesis.

In the second case, we assume the complement of the first case. That is, for every
G\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}=\mathsf{xwords}(m)
there exists a prefix
x
of
w_{3}
with
|x|\geq\frac{1}{2}m
and states
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})
and
q
such that after reading
w_{2}x
from
p
, the state
q
is not within gap
G
from any independent run in
I_{p}
.

We apply this assumption for a large enough constant
G
, which then allows us to observe that in order to create a gap of at least
G
from all independent runs, the seamless run leading up to
q
must be very far from all independent runs for a long infix. Therefore, this run is a new independent run in the infix, and we can now use the induction hypothesis.

We now turn to the formal details, with depictions in
Figs.

24
and
25
.

Figure 24
:
The first case in the proof of
Lemma

16.24
. The highlighted regions are within
G
of the independent runs. Note that the red run goes outside this region, but only in the first half of
w_{3}
.

Figure 25
:
The second case in the proof of
Lemma

16.24
. The red run goes far from the independent runs, and to do so it must be far for a long suffix (
v
).

For the first case, assume that there exists
G\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}=\mathsf{xwords}(m)
, for every state
p\in S
and corresponding set of independent runs
I_{p}
, and for every prefix
x
of
w_{3}
with
|x|\geq\frac{1}{2}|w_{3}|
, if
\boldsymbol{c_{w_{2}x}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(w_{2}x,\boldsymbol{c_{p}})
then for every state
q
,
\boldsymbol{c_{w_{2}x}}(q)
is within gap
G
of some independent run in
I_{p}
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{N}
, we construct
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}(t)
as follows. Plugging
m=2t
, consider
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}=\mathsf{xwords}(2t)
and write
w=uv
with
|u|=|v|=t
. We now set
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}(t)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with
w_{1}^{\prime}=w_{1}
,
w_{2}^{\prime}=w_{2}u
,
w_{3}^{\prime}=v
and
w_{4}^{\prime}=w_{4}
. We claim that
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}
is indeed a faithful restriction of
\mathsf{xwords}
that is an
\ell
-sparse
G
-cover for some
G
.

Indeed, by the decomposition (specifically,
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1}
and
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
) we have that
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}
is a faithful restriction of
w
. In particular, this implies that the baseline run is seamless.

\ell\geq 1
). For brevity, we assume
\mathsf{xwords}
itself is already
\ell
-sparse.

Crucially, note that taking a faithful restriction maintains the property of being a
D
-dip.

We can now apply
Lemma

16.24
, to obtain a sparse
G
-cover
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}
. Again, since
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}
is a faithful restriction of
\mathsf{xwords}
, then
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}
is also
D
-dip. Thus, we have the following.

Corollary 16.25
.

If there exists an elongated word sequence
\mathsf{xwords}
that is a
D
-dip for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
, then there is a faithful restriction
\mathsf{xwords}^{\prime}
of
\mathsf{xwords}
that is a
D
-dip
\ell
-sparse
G
-cover for some
\ell,G\in\mathbb{N}
.

Then, by combining
Corollary

16.25
with
Lemma

16.15
we have the following.

Corollary 16.26

(

D
-Dip to Increasing Infix)
.

If there exists an elongated word sequence
\mathsf{xwords}
that is
D
-dip for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
, then there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\mathsf{xwords}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and a decomposition
w_{3}=u^{\prime}xyv^{\prime}
such that
uxyv
is a separated increasing infix from
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})
for
u=w_{2}u^{\prime}
and
v=v^{\prime}w_{4}
.

17
Cactus Budding

In this section we discuss how a cactus chain (
Definition

12.1
) may grow a new cactus at its end (
bud
, in cactus terms). The overall intuition is that a chain may bud a new inner cactus if doing so both decreases the cost of the chain (as per
Definition

11.6
), and does not decrease the potential.
To this end, the main tool at our disposal is our study of Separated Increasing Infixes (
Definition

16.5
).
As usual, there are several definitions and technical tools to develop before getting to the main result.

Definition 17.1

(Superior Stable Cycle)
.

Consider two stable cycles
(S^{\prime},u),(S^{\prime},v)
sharing the same first component
S^{\prime}
.
We say that
(S^{\prime},v)
is
superior
to
(S^{\prime},u)
if for every
q,p\in S^{\prime}

we have
\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},u},q\to p)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},v},q\to p)
.

Our first (simple) observation is that a word that “almost” induces a stable cycle, and satisfies a superiority condition with respect to a stable cycle, actually induces a superior stable cycle.

Proposition 17.2
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},u)
and let
v\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
(i.e., without rebase and jump letters) be a word such the following hold.

1.

For every
q,p\in S^{\prime}
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(u,q\to p)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(v,q\to p)
.

2.

For the baseline state
g\in S^{\prime}
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(v,g\to g)=0
.

Then
(S^{\prime},v)
is a stable cycle, and it is superior to
(S^{\prime},u)
.

Proof.

By the condition on the baseline state
g

and since
v
does not have jump letters,
the baseline run
\rho:g\xrightarrow{v}g
exists and
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=0
.
Therefore in order to show that
(S^{\prime},v)
is a stable cycle, all that remains is to prove that there are no negative cycles on
v^{k}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},v)\subseteq S^{\prime}
.

The latter is simple: if
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},v)
then
\mathsf{mwt}(v,S^{\prime}\to q)<\infty
, but then
\mathsf{mwt}(u,S^{\prime}\to q)<\infty
, so
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},u)\subseteq S^{\prime}
(since
(S^{\prime},u)
is a stable cycle), so
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},v)\subseteq S^{\prime}
.
Before addressing the main claim, we prove a slightly more general property: for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
r_{0},r_{k}\in S^{\prime}
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(u^{k},r_{0}\to r_{k})\leq\mathsf{mwt}(v^{k},r_{0}\to r_{k})
.
Indeed, let
\rho:r_{0}\xrightarrow{v^{k}}r_{k}
such that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\mathsf{mwt}(v^{k},r_{0}\to r_{k})
, and write
\rho:r_{0}\xrightarrow{v}r_{1}\cdots r_{k-1}\xrightarrow{v}r_{k}
. By the premise we have

\begin{split}&\mathsf{mwt}(v^{k},r_{0}\to r_{k})=\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\mathsf{mwt}(v,r_{i}\to r_{i+1})\geq\\
&\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\mathsf{mwt}(u,r_{i}\to r_{i+1})\geq\mathsf{mwt}(u^{k},r_{0}\to r_{k}).\end{split}

Now, assume by way of contradiction that
\mathsf{mwt}(s\to s,v^{k})<0
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then by the property above we have
\mathsf{mwt}(s\to s,u^{k})<0
in contradiction to the fact that
(S^{\prime},u)
is a stable cycle.

We conclude that
(S^{\prime},v)
is a stable cycle.
Similarly, we can now prove that it is superior to
(S^{\prime},u)
. Consider
p,q\in S^{\prime}
. If
\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},v},p\to q)=\infty
, there is nothing to prove. Thus, assume
\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},v},p\to q)<\infty
. By
Definition

11.1
, we have that
(p,q)\in\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},v)
and there exists
g\in S^{\prime}
such that

\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},v},p\to q)=\mathsf{mwt}(v^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},p\xrightarrow{v^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{v^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}q)
.
By the property above, we now have

\begin{split}&\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},v},p\to q)=\mathsf{mwt}(v^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},p\xrightarrow{v^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{v^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}q)=\mathsf{mwt}(v^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},p\to g)+\mathsf{mwt}(v^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},g\to q)\geq\\
&\mathsf{mwt}(u^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},p\to g)+\mathsf{mwt}(u^{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},g\to q)=\mathsf{mwt}(u^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},p\xrightarrow{u^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}g\xrightarrow{u^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}q)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},u},p\to q)\end{split}

where the last inequality is by
Definition

11.1
, keeping in mind that the grounding state for
p,q
with
u
may differ from
g
, but that
g\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},u^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}})
(that is, it is a possible grounding state), so we indeed have the inequality.
We thus have that
(S^{\prime},v)
is superior to
(S^{\prime},u)
.
∎

Our next observation is that superiority “propagates” through to outer-cactus letters.

Proposition 17.3
.

Consider a stable cycle
(S^{\prime},w)
such that
w=w_{1}\alpha_{B,u}w_{2}
with
B=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})
. Let
(B,v)
be a superior stable cycle to
(B,u)
, then
(S^{\prime},w_{1}\alpha_{B,v}w_{2})
is a superior stable cycle to
(S^{\prime},w)
.

Proof.

Write
w^{\prime}=w_{1}\alpha_{B,v}w_{2}
, we prove that
w^{\prime}
satisfies the conditions of
Proposition

17.2
with respect to
(S^{\prime},w)
.
Let
p,q\in S^{\prime}
. If
\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}\alpha_{B,v}w_{2},p\to q)=\infty
, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, let
r,t\in B
such that

\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}\alpha_{B,v}w_{2})=\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}\alpha_{B,v}w_{2},p\xrightarrow{w_{1}}r\xrightarrow{\alpha_{B,v}}t\xrightarrow{w_{2}}q)

Since
(B,v)
is superior to
(B,u)
, we now have

\begin{split}&\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}\alpha_{B,v}w_{2})=\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}\alpha_{B,v}w_{2},p\xrightarrow{w_{1}}r\xrightarrow{\alpha_{B,v}}t\xrightarrow{w_{2}}q)\\
&=\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1},p\to r)+\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{B,v},r\to t)+\mathsf{mwt}(w_{2},t\to q)\\
&\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1},p\to r)+\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{B,u},r\to t)+\mathsf{mwt}(w_{2},t\to q)\\
&=\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}\alpha_{B,v}w_{2},p\xrightarrow{w_{1}}r\xrightarrow{\alpha_{B,u}}t\xrightarrow{w_{2}}q)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(w_{1}\alpha_{B,u}w_{2})\end{split}

satisfying the first item in
Proposition

17.2
.

Finally, since
\alpha_{B,v}
is a stable cycle, and since
w_{1}\alpha_{B,u}w_{2}
has a seamless baseline run with weight
0
, then so does
w_{1}\alpha_{B,v}w_{2}
(since
\alpha_{B,v}
also has a
0
baseline transition).
We conclude that
w^{\prime}=w_{1}\alpha_{B,v}w_{2}
satisfies the conditions in
Proposition

17.2
, and therefore
(S^{\prime},w^{\prime})
is a superior stable cycle to
(S^{\prime},w)
.
∎

Next, we show that the charge
decreases
with superiority. Intuitively, this is because the charge measures how negative runs can be come, and superiority increases all the runs.

Proposition 17.4
.

Consider stable cycles
(B,u),(B,v)
such that
(B,v)
is superior to
(B,u)
. Then for every
x,y
we have that
\uppsi(x\alpha_{B,v}y)\leq\uppsi(x\alpha_{B,u}y)
.

Proof.

Let
q\in S
such that
\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x\alpha_{B,v}y}q
satisfies
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=-\uppsi(x\alpha_{B,v}y)
and
\rho
is seamless.
Write
\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x}r\xrightarrow{\alpha_{B,v}}t\xrightarrow{y}q
, then by superiority and since
\rho
is seamless, we have

\begin{split}&-\uppsi(x\alpha_{B,v}y)=\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\mathsf{mwt}(x,s_{0}\to r)+\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{B,v},r\to t)+\mathsf{mwt}(y,t\to q)\geq\\
&\mathsf{mwt}(x,s_{0}\to r)+\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{B,u},r\to t)+\mathsf{mwt}(y,t\to q)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(x\alpha_{B,u}y,s_{0}\to q)\geq-\uppsi(x\alpha_{B,u}y)\end{split}

So
\uppsi(x\alpha_{B,v}y)\leq\uppsi(x\alpha_{B,u}y)

and we are done.
∎

Our next major goal is to show a dual result for the potential (namely that the potential increases with superiority), and moreover – that this carries through cactus chains (
Definition

12.1
).
Specifically, we do not prove this claim in full generality. Rather, we focus on cactus chains that end in an increasing infix (
Definition

16.5
), and show how they induce superior cactus chains with increasing potential.

Recall that separated increasing infixes (
Definition

16.5
) can be “folded” into stable cycles, as per
Lemma

16.6
.
We make use of this fact in order to “bud” a new cactus from an increasing infix, while maintaining nice properties, as follows (see depiction in
Fig.

26
).

Figure 26
:
Budding a cactus from an increasing infix. Observe that
y
is depicted as longer than
x
, to reflect its higher cost.

Lemma 17.5

(

Increasing Infix Budding)
.

Either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness, or the following holds.
Consider a cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
where
w=uxyz
is a separated increasing infix from
S^{\prime}
. Let
w^{\prime}=u\alpha_{B,x}v
where
B=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime},u)
. Then
(S^{\prime},w^{\prime})
is a stable cycle superior to
(S^{\prime},w)
, and for every
w_{1},w_{2}\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
we have
\upphi(w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}w_{2})\leq\upphi(w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w^{\prime}}w_{2})
, if the potential is defined.

Proof.

The fact that
(S^{\prime},w^{\prime})
is a superior stable cycle follows directly from
Lemma

16.6
: we show there that either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness, or for every
r\in S^{\prime}
and
t\in S
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(w,r\to t)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(w^{\prime},r\to t)
. Moreover, the baseline run exists in
(S^{\prime},w^{\prime})
, since
\alpha_{B,x}
maintains the baseline run that is present in
xy
.
Therefore, we can use
Proposition

17.2
and we have that
(S^{\prime},w^{\prime})
is a stable cycle superior to
(S^{\prime},w)
.

The interesting part of the lemma is obtaining the potential inequality.
Intuitively, we first unfold both
w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}w_{2}
and
w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w^{\prime}}w_{2}
with a high enough constant. This leaves us with many repetitions of
w
and of
w^{\prime}
, respectively. By
Lemma

14.4
, we know that the potential is maintained by this unfolding.
We then use
Lemma

16.6
again, this time deriving the potential inequality
\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}ww^{\prime}_{2})\leq\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}w^{\prime}_{2})
for every
w^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{2}
.
We then use this to replace each
w
by
w^{\prime}
in the unfolding, while maintaining the potential inequality, which allows us to conclude the proof. We refer to the latter argument as
synchronous unfolding
, and reuse it later.
We turn to the precise details.

Consider
w_{1},w_{2}\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
such that
\upphi(w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}w_{2})
is defined. Note that this only means that the baseline run is seamless, and this is preserved in all the words we consider henceforth, so we do not mention this every time we discuss the potential.

Let
F\geq\max\{2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}w_{2})},2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w^{\prime}}w_{2})}\}
, and consider
\mathsf{unfold}(w_{1},\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},w_{2}\wr F)=w_{1}w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}w_{2}
and
\mathsf{unfold}(w_{1},\alpha_{S^{\prime},w^{\prime}},w_{2}\wr F)=w_{1}w^{\prime 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}w_{2}
. By
Remark

12.16
we can indeed assume that
M_{0}
is the same for both unfoldings (by taking the maximum of the two unfolding constants).

By
Lemma

14.4
, either
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness, in which case we are done, or we have

\upphi(w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}w_{2})=\upphi(w_{1}w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}w_{2})\quad\text{ and }\quad\upphi(w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w^{\prime}}w_{2})=\upphi(w_{1}w^{\prime 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}w_{2})

By
Lemma

16.6
, for every
w^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{2}\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
such that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w^{\prime}_{1})\subseteq S^{\prime}
, it holds that
\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}ww^{\prime}_{2})\leq\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}w^{\prime}_{2})
.

We claim that

\upphi(w_{1}w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}w_{2})\leq\upphi(w_{1}w^{\prime 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}w_{2})
. In order to show this, define for every
0\leq j\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}
the word
e_{j}=w_{1}w^{j}w^{\prime 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}-j}w_{2}
, then this is equivalent to showing that
\upphi(e_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})\leq\upphi(e_{0})
. We prove that for every
0<j\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}
it holds that
\upphi(e_{j})\leq\upphi(e_{j-1})
, which implies the claim.

Indeed, consider such
j
and write
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1}w^{j-1}
and
w^{\prime}_{2}=w^{\prime 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}-j}w_{2}
. Note that
e_{j}=w^{\prime}_{1}ww^{\prime}_{2}
whereas
e_{j-1}=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}w^{\prime}_{2}
.
Since
(S^{\prime},w)
is a stable cycle and
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1})\subseteq S^{\prime}
, then in particular
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w^{\prime}_{1})\subseteq S^{\prime}
. Therefore, as we mention above, we have

\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}ww^{\prime}_{2})\leq\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}w^{\prime}_{2})
, i.e.,
\upphi(e_{j})\leq\upphi(e_{j-1})
, concluding the claim.
We now conclude the proof with:

\upphi(w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}w_{2})=\upphi(w_{1}w^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}w_{2})\leq\upphi(w_{1}w^{\prime 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}w_{2})=\upphi(w_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w^{\prime}}w_{2})

We remark that the latter argument is the
synchronous unfolding
mentioned above.
∎

The results in this section so far pertain to stable cycles, and can be viewed as if they occur at the “apex” of a cactus
chain
(recall
Definition

12.1
).
We now turn to lift these results to the entire cactus chain.

Definition 17.6

(Superior Cactus Chain)
.

Consider two cactus chains
\Theta_{1}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},...,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},w_{n}}
and
\Theta_{2}=\beta_{S^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{1}},...,\beta_{S^{\prime}_{n},w^{\prime}_{n}}
.
We say that
\Theta_{2}
is
superior
to
\Theta_{1}
if
\beta_{S^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i}}
is superior to
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i},w_{i}}

for every
1\leq i\leq n
.

In case a cactus chain ends with a separated increasing infix, we call it
pre-bud
, with the intuition that we can now bud a new cactus letter at its end. The cactus chain obtained by budding this increasing infix is referred to as a
post-bud
chain, as follows.

Definition 17.7

(Pre-bud and Post-bud Cactus Chain)
.

A cactus chain
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},...,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},w_{n}}
is
pre-bud
if
w_{n}=uxyv
is a separated increasing infix from
S^{\prime}_{n}
. Denote
w_{i}=u_{i}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i+1},w_{i+1}}v_{i}
for every
1\leq i<n
as per
Definition

12.1
.

The corresponding
post-bud
cactus chain
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{1}},...,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},w^{\prime}_{n}}
is defined as follows:

•

w^{\prime}_{n}=u\alpha_{B,x}v
with
B=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime}_{n},u)
.

•

For every
1\leq i<n
we define
w^{\prime}_{i}=u_{i}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{i+1},w^{\prime}_{i+1}}v_{i}
.

It is easy to see (by induction) that the post-bud cactus chain is indeed a valid cactus chain, as per
Definition

12.1
. The only non trivial part is the base case, which is also immediate by the definition of an increasing infix from
S^{\prime}
(
Definition

16.5
).

Intuitively, the post-bud cactus chain can be elongated by placing
\alpha_{B,x}
as a new element, but at this point we do not do that, in order to keep the number of elements in the pre-bud and post-bud the same, so that they can be compared by the superiority relation.
Indeed, our main result of this section is that the post-bud chain is superior to the pre-bud chain, and also has increased potential.

Lemma 17.8

(

The Post-Bud Chain is Superior)
.

Either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness, or the following holds.
Let
\Theta_{1}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},...,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},w_{n}}
be a pre-bud cactus chain and
\Theta_{2}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{1}},...,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},u\alpha_{B,x}v}
its post-bud cactus chain, then
\Theta_{2}
is superior to
\Theta_{1}
and for every
z_{1},z_{2}
with
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},z_{1})\subseteq S^{\prime}_{1}
it holds that

\upphi(z_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}}z_{2})\leq\upphi(z_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{1}}z_{2})
, if the potential is defined.

Proof.

The proof is by induction over the length of the chain. In a nutshell, the base case is exactly
Lemma

17.5
, whereas the inductive step follows by a similar “synchronized unfolding” argument as in the proof of
Lemma

17.5
.

Before starting the induction, we assume that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
does not have a type-0 witness. If it does, we are done. Accordingly, whenever we use results that are prefixed by this assumption, we ignore it (c.f.,
Remark

14.5
).

Induction base:
n=1

In this case
\Theta_{1}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}}
with
w_{1}=uxyv
an increasing infix from
S^{\prime}_{1}
. By
Lemma

17.5
for
w^{\prime}_{1}=u\alpha_{B,x}
we have that
(S^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{1})
is a superior stable cycle to
(S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1})
and for every
z_{1},z_{2}\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
with
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},z_{1})\subseteq S^{\prime}_{1}
it holds that
\upphi(z_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}}z_{2})\leq\upphi(z_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{1}}z_{2})
, if the potential is defined. This concludes the base case.

Inductive step:

Assume correctness for
n
, we prove for
n+1
.
Consider the pre-bud chain
\Theta_{1}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{0},w_{0}},\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},...,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},w_{n}}
and its post-bud chain
\Theta_{2}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{0},w^{\prime}_{0}}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},...,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},u\alpha_{B,x}v}
.

Observe that
Definition

17.7
is inductive from the end of the chain to its start. In particular, we have that
\Theta^{\prime}_{1}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},...,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},w_{n}}
is a pre-bud chain and its corresponding post-bud chain is conveniently
\Theta^{\prime}_{2}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}},...,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{n},u\alpha_{B,x}v}
.
By the induction hypothesis, it holds that
\Theta^{\prime}_{2}
is superior to
\Theta^{\prime}_{1}
. In particular,
(S^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{1})
is superior to
(S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1})
. By the definition of the post-bud chain (
Definition

17.7
), we have that
w_{0}=u_{0}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}}v_{0}
and
w^{\prime}_{0}=u_{0}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{1}}v_{0}
.
By the superiority-propagation of
Proposition

17.3
it follows that
(S^{\prime}_{0},w^{\prime}_{0})
is superior to
(S^{\prime}_{0},w_{0})
, as required.

It remains to show the potential inequality. Let
z_{1},z_{2}\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
with
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},z_{1})\subseteq S^{\prime}_{0}
. We need to show that
\upphi(z_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{0},w_{0}}z_{2})\leq\upphi(z_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{0},w^{\prime}_{0}}z_{2})
.
We proceed with the synchronous unfolding argument, similarly to
Lemma

17.5
, with the main difference being that the “inner” potential inequality is now obtained by the induction hypothesis.

Let
F\geq\max\{2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(z_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{0},w_{0}}z_{2})},2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(z_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{0},w^{\prime}_{0}}z_{2})}\}
,
and consider
\mathsf{unfold}(z_{1},\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{0},w_{0}},z_{2}\wr F)=z_{1}w_{0}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}z_{2}

and
\mathsf{unfold}(z_{1},\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{0},w^{\prime}_{0}},z_{2}\wr F)=z_{1}{w^{\prime}_{0}}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}{M_{0}}}z_{2}
.
By
Remark

12.16
we can indeed assume that
M_{0}
is the same for both unfoldings (by taking the maximum of the two unfolding constants).

By
Lemma

14.4
we have

\upphi(z_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{0},w_{0}}z_{2})=\upphi(z_{1}w_{0}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}z_{2})\quad\text{ and }\quad\upphi(z_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{0},w^{\prime}_{0}}z_{2})=\upphi(z_{1}{w^{\prime}_{0}}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}z_{2})

It is therefore enough to show that
\upphi(z_{1}w_{0}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}z_{2})\leq\upphi(z_{1}{w^{\prime}_{0}}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}z_{2})
.
Recall that
w_{0}=u_{0}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w_{1}}v_{0}
and
w^{\prime}_{0}=u_{0}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{1}}v_{0}
(as per
Definition

17.7
).
For every
0\leq j\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}
, define the word
e_{j}=z_{1}(u_{0}w_{0}v_{0})^{j}(u_{0}w^{\prime}_{0}v_{0})^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}-j}z_{2}
, then we want to prove
\upphi(e_{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}})\leq\upphi(e_{0})
.
We prove that for every
0<j\leq 2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}
it holds that
\upphi(e_{j})\leq\upphi(e_{j-1})
, which implies the claim.

Indeed, consider such
j
and write
z^{\prime}_{1}=z_{1}(u_{0}w_{0}v_{0})^{j-1}
and
z^{\prime}_{2}=(u_{0}w^{\prime}_{0}v_{0})^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}-j}z_{2}
.
Note that
e_{j}=z^{\prime}_{1}w_{0}z^{\prime}_{2}
and
e_{j-1}=z^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{0}z^{\prime}_{2}
.
Further note that since
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime}_{0},u_{0})\subseteq S^{\prime}_{1}
, and
w_{0}
and
w^{\prime}_{0}
are stable cycles on
S^{\prime}_{1}
, and
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime}_{1},v_{0})\subseteq S^{\prime}_{0}
, and
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},z_{1})\subseteq S^{\prime}_{0}
by the assumption, it holds that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},z^{\prime}_{1})\subseteq S^{\prime}_{1}
.
Thus, by the induction hypothesis (applied with
z^{\prime}_{1}
and
z^{\prime}_{2}
), we have that

\upphi(z^{\prime}_{1}w_{0}z^{\prime}_{2})\leq\upphi(z^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{0}z^{\prime}_{2})
. That is,
\upphi(e_{j})\leq\upphi(e_{j-1})
, from which we conclude that
\upphi(z_{1}w_{0}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}z_{2})\leq\upphi(z_{1}{w^{\prime}_{0}}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}z_{2})
.
Thus, we conclude the proof with:

\upphi(z_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{0},w_{0}}z_{2})=\upphi(z_{1}w_{0}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}z_{2})\leq\upphi(z_{1}{w^{\prime}_{0}}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}z_{2})=\upphi(z_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{0},w^{\prime}_{0}}z_{2})

∎

18
On Charge and Unbounded Potential

In this section we continue our examination of organized structures in runs on arbitrarily long words, akin to
Section

16.2
(which we also rely on).
Our focus now is on the behavior of the charge (
\uppsi
) and its implication on the potential (
\upphi
) (see
Definitions

13.2
and
13.2
).

The structure of this section resembles that of
Section

16.2
: we consider words of the form
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
(with an implicit decomposition as in
Remark

16.7
) and define a new specialization of elongated words sequences, as well as concepts of independent runs, types and dips, and some new characteristics.
We then prove certain properties of words that have these characteristics.
The proofs of this section, however, are more involved than those of
Section

16.2
, and make use of baseline shifts (
Section

12.2
) and flattening (
Section

12.3
).

In this section we fix a finite alphabet
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{0}_{\infty}
– that is, without rebase letters (and also without jump letters). In addition, we require that
\Gamma_{0}^{0}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
(i.e.,
\Gamma^{\prime}
contains the original letters of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
).
We discuss these assumption when it becomes relevant.
Let
W_{\max}
be the maximal weight appearing in a transition over
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

18.1
A Condition for Unbounded Potential

Our starting point is now an elongated words sequence
words
(note that we drop the assumption that
|w_{3}|=m
added in
Definition

16.10
). Unlike
Section

16.2.1
, in this section we do not focus on each state reachable after
w_{1}
, but rather on the configurations visited after
w_{1}w_{2}
.

Our first definition is a variant of independent runs.

Definition 18.1

(Configuration-Independent Runs)
.

Consider a word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and two seamless runs
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
over it. We say that
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are
configuration-independent on
w_{3}
if there exists
G\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every prefix
v
of
w_{3}
we have
\rho_{2}(w_{1}w_{2}v)-\rho_{1}(w_{1}w_{2}v)>G
.
In this case we say that
\rho_{2}
is
above
\rho_{1}
with gap
G
.

We say that
\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{1}
are
not within gap
G
when the order is not important.

A set
\{\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{k}\}
of runs is
configuration-independent with gap
G
if the runs are independent in pairs with gap
G
. A singleton is independent by definition.

Note that since we now focus on all the runs starting from
\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2})
, it follows that the maximal number of independent runs is at most
|S|
.

We proceed with an analogue of sparse words. Since we now look at general
words
, we define the gaps between runs as a function of
|w_{3}|
instead of a function of
m
.
In particular, when we apply the definition, we define the gap explicitly as an expression in
|w_{3}|
(see e.g.,
Lemma

18.8
below).

Definition 18.2

(
\ell
-Sparse
K
-Gap Words)
.

For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
K:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
we say that an elongated word sequence
words
is
\ell
-sparse
with
K
-gap if for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
there is a set
I
of independent runs with gap
K(|w_{3}|)

such that
|I|=\ell
.

In the following, we consider
decompositions
of
w_{3}
. Intuitively, a decomposition of
w_{3}
is simply a concatenation
w_{3}=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, such that the number of segments increases with
m
. We later impose further conditions on these decompositions, but for now they only serve as parts of definitions.

Definition 18.3

(Decomposed Words Sequence)
.

A sequence
words
is
decomposed
if for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
there is an associated decomposition
w_{3}=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{d(m)}
such that
\lim_{m\to\infty}d(m)=\infty
and all the
u_{i}
are nonempty.

As usual, we assume that a decomposed word sequence has a fixed associated decomposition function, so we can refer to “the decomposition of
\textsf{words}(m)
” for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Our next definitions are variants of a
G
-cover (
Definition

16.14
).

Definition 18.4

(Configuration
G
-Cover)
.

Consider an
\ell
-sparse decomposed sequence
words
.
For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
let
I=\{\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell}\}
be the corresponding
\ell
-sparse independent runs.

For
G\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that
words
is a
G
-cover
if for every prefix
u
of
w_{3}
and configuration
\boldsymbol{c}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}u)
, for every state
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}u)
we have
\min\{|\boldsymbol{c}(q)-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{i}(w_{1}w_{2}u))|:1\leq i\leq\ell\}\leq G
.

The next cover definition differs from
Definition

18.4
in that we require the cover to hold only at end points of the decomposition segments.

Definition 18.5

(Decomposition
G
-Cover)
.

Consider an
\ell
-sparse decomposed sequence
words
.
For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
with decomposition
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)}
let
I=\{\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell}\}
be the corresponding
\ell
-sparse independent runs.

For
G\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that
words
is a
decomposition
G
-cover
if for every
0\leq j\leq k
and configuration
\boldsymbol{c_{j}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{j})
, for every state
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{j})
we have
\min\{|\boldsymbol{c_{j}}(q)-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{i}(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{j}))|:1\leq i\leq\ell\}\leq G
.

As mentioned above, our definitions here focus on reachable states. Nonetheless, the presence of ghost runs still introduces complications. The following definition (when applicable) enables us to mitigate the effect of these runs.

Definition 18.6

(Ghost-Free Decomposition)
.

Consider a decomposed sequence
words
.
For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
with decomposition
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)}
we say that
words
is
ghost-free
if there are no ghost runs from
w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}
to
w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i}
for every
1\leq i<d(m)
.
(note that there is no requirement on the last segment, which can be thought of as a “remainder”).

Note the this requirement is not only that there are no ghost runs spanning the entire
w_{3}
, but that even when focusing on a single segment in the decomposition, there is no seamless ghost run on it. In particular, a ghost-free decomposition has no ghost runs on any concatenation
u_{i}\cdots u_{j}
for any
i\leq j
(this follows from the fact that an infix of a ghost run is also a ghost run, as noted after
Definition

11.19
).
Still, there could be ghost runs that start e.g., in the middle of segment
u_{i}
and are cut in the middle of
u_{i+1}
.

Our next requirement of the decomposition is more involved, and assumes there exists a run whose gap from the minimal states gets smaller.

Definition 18.7

(

Decreasing-Gap Fair Decomposition)
.

Consider a decomposed word sequence
words
and assume there exists a function
b:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}b(n)=\infty
such that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, there is some seamless run
\rho
on
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
on such that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w_{1}w_{2}))+\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})>\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}))+\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3})+b(m)
.

We say that the decomposition is
decreasing-gap fair
(with respect to
\rho
and
b(n)
) if there is a function
\widetilde{b}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widetilde{b}(n)=\infty
such that the decomposition
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)}
satisfies for every
1\leq i<d(m)
that

\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}))+\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})>\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i}))+\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})+\widetilde{b}(m)

(note that there is no requirement on the last segment, which can be thought of as a “remainder”).

Intuitively, a decreasing-gap fair decomposition guarantees that not only the number of segments increases, but also the gap between the minimal state and
\rho
decreases significantly with every segment (except possibly the last segment). We remark that the expressions “
+\uppsi
” should be read as “difference from the minimal weight”, since the charge is the inverse of the minimal weight. This idea is depicted in
Fig.

27

Figure 27
:
For visual simplicity, we assume
\rho
is constant
0
. The overall charge in every segment decreases (with respect to
\rho
), where the minimal charge for every segment is at its end. The segments are marked by the first time the charge crosses the threshold.

In this section we also make use of the
\mathsf{diff\_type}
, always starting from
s_{0}
.
We remind that for a word
x
we have
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(x)=\langle\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{x}}),\leq_{x},\mathsf{near}_{s_{0},G}(x)\rangle
where
\leq_{x}
is the relative order of the states according to
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}
and
\mathsf{near}_{s_{0},G}
specifies which states are assigned values within gap
G
of each other by
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}
. Recall that the (finite) set of all
\mathsf{diff\_type}
is denoted
\mathsf{DIFF\_TYPES}
.

Main result of this section – overview

Consider a decomposed sequence
words
that satisfies enough of the “nice” properties above (i.e., it is sparse with a large gap, has a cover, is ghost-free, and has a decreasing-gap fair decomposition).
Our main result in this section is that under this assumption, the potential (
\upphi
, see
Definition

13.2
) over
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
is unbounded.

A high-level overview of this result is given in
Section

6.2
, and is depicted in
Fig.

9
.
We now give a slightly more technical overview, followed by the complete proof.

Recall that for a word
w
with a seamless baseline run, the
potential

\upphi(w)
is the gap between the maximal dominant state and the baseline, which in turn means that there is some suffix
z
(over
\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty}
) such that reading
z
from the dominant state yields finite weight, but all states lower than the dominant state (after reading
w
) do not yield finite-weight run on
z
.

Under the assumptions on
words
, consider
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
for some very large
m
. In particular, we take
m
large enough so that the decomposition has a repetition of
\mathsf{diff\_type}
in some infix
x
of
w_{3}
(this is possible since the number of segments increases with
m
).

By our assumption, the minimal run
\rho_{\min}
gets close to some seamless run
\rho
. For intuition purposes, it’s useful to think of the minimal run as increasing and on
\rho
as decreasing (
Fig.

9(a)
).
Thus, there are significantly-decreasing runs, and in particular we find such runs on
x
(note that for this we need the properties of the fair decomposition).

We then
shift
our perspective so that the most decreasing seamless run
\rho_{\ssearrow}
becomes baseline (see Step 2). We observe that
\rho_{\ssearrow}
is then far above the minimal run, which is now highly increasing. Moreover, there are now no decreasing runs (
Fig.

9(b)
).

We then show (Step 3) that under sufficient assumptions,
x
actually induces a stable cycle
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
(this is the more technical part of the proof).
Now, since the minimal run increases so much, it follows that it has no grounded pairs, and therefore attains weight
\infty
upon reading
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
(see Step 4
12
12

12

In
Fig.

9
we combine Steps 3 and 4, so there is an offset in the numbering, i.e. Step 5 here is Step 4 in the figure.
, depicted in
Fig.

9(c)
).
Intuitively, we are already close to showing that the potential is unbounded, since we have a large gap between runs, and the lower runs jump to
\infty
. However, the precise definition of potential requires the gap to be from the baseline run, not the minimal run (indeed, if e.g.,
\rho_{\ssearrow}
is the baseline and contains the dominant state, the potential would be
0
).

We therefore want to make the minimal run the baseline. Thus, in Step 5 we
shift
again, this time making the prefix of the minimal run up to
x
the baseline (
Fig.

9(d)
). Then, all that remains (almost) is to construct a suffix that would attain finite value from the shifted
\rho_{\ssearrow}
, but not from minimal run. This is achieved by using a
jump letter
and
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
.

A caveat to the above is that it uses the general alphabet
\Gamma^{\prime}
, whereas we want to show the unboundedness of the potential already over
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
. Therefore, already in Step 1 we
flatten
the prefix before
x
. This guarantees that all the letters before
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
are from
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
, a property that is maintained by shifts.

Lemma 18.8

(

Decomposed Words to Unbounded Potential)
.

Assume there exists
G,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and a decomposed word sequence
words
that is
\ell
-sparse with gap

4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
and a ghost-free decomposition
G
-cover.
