In addition, assume the decomposition is decreasing-gap fair with respect to a seamless run
\rho
and function
b:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
.
Then
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma^{0}_{0})^{*}\}=\infty
.

Proof.

Let
P\in\mathbb{N}
, we construct a word over
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
whose potential is at least
P
. Let
G,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
words
be as in the premise of the lemma, and
b,\widetilde{b}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\rho
as per
Definition

18.7
.
Let
d:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be the decomposition length function, i.e., for
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
we have
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)}
.

Take
m\in\mathbb{N}
large enough so that
m>P
,
d(m)>|\mathsf{DIFF\_TYPES}_{s_{0},G}|+1
and
\widetilde{b}(m)>4G
. Since both
d
and
\widetilde{b}
are increasing, this is possible.
Consider the word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}=\textsf{words}(m)
and let

w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)}
be its decreasing-gap fair decomposition.

By the pigeonhole principle, there exist
0\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<d(m)
such that

\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i_{1}})=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i_{2}})

(Observe that we take
d(m)
large enough so that we can ignore the last segment, which is a remainder).

Denote

\widehat{x_{0}}=w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i_{1}}
(the reason for the hat is that we modify
\widehat{x_{0}}
to obtain a “simpler”
x_{0}
below),
x=u_{i_{1}+1}\cdots u_{i_{2}}
and
x_{1}=u_{i_{2}+1}\cdots u_{d(m)}w_{4}
.

Step 1: flatten the prefix before
x
.

Recall that
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
, i.e., has no rebase letters. It follows that the letters in
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
are either in
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
or are nested cacti.
Let
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(\widehat{x_{0}}x)}
, we now define
x_{0}=\mathsf{flatten}(\widehat{x_{0}}\wr F)
.
By
Definition

12.23
, it follows that in order to obtain
x_{0}
, we only apply the cactus unfolding of
Definition

11.12
. This implies that no jump letters are introduced in
x_{0}
(indeed, those are introduced only in the case of rebase removal). Thus,
x_{0}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
.
In the following we wish to reason about
x_{0}xx_{1}
instead of
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
.
However, the main difference between the words is that due to the flattening, we now have
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},x_{0})=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
.
Consider the configurations
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{0}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},\widehat{x_{0}})
and
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},x_{0})
, and similarly

12.24

. Additionally, for

\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(s)\geq 2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(\widehat{x_{0}}x)}
.

ACCOUNTING: ACCOUNTING

And in particular ghost states attain higher values than states reachable by
\widehat{x_{0}}
. In the following, we only use the fact that these are higher, and not the concrete gap size.

Let
\rho_{\min}

be the minimal-weight independent run on
w_{3}
among the assigned set of independent runs on
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
(recall that by
Definition

18.1
the independence is measured on the
w_{3}
infix). In particular,
\rho_{\min}
is seamless.
Note that since
x
is a concatenation of segments from the fair decomposition, then the gap between the minimal states and
\rho

decreases significantly upon reading
x
.
We claim that this implies the gap between
\rho
and
\rho_{\min}
also significantly decreases.
Intuitively, this holds because
\rho_{\min}
remains close to the minimal state (by the
G
-cover property).

Formally, let
q\in\arg\min\{\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(p)\mid p\in S\}
. By

Lemma

12.24

we also have
q\in\arg\min\{\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{0}}(p)\mid p\in S\}
. In particular,
\uppsi(x_{0})=-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q)
.
Since we assume that the independent runs form a
G
-cover, then the minimal state
q
must by within distance
G
from
\rho_{\min}
. That is,
|\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q)-\rho_{\min}(x_{0})|\leq G
. Similarly,
\uppsi(x_{0}x)=-\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q^{\prime})
where
q^{\prime}\in\arg\min\{\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(p)\mid p\in S\}
and
|\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q^{\prime})-\rho_{\min}(x_{0}x)|\leq G
.

Since
\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}))+\uppsi(x_{0})>\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}x))+\uppsi(x_{0}x)+\widetilde{b}(m)

we have by the triangle inequality that

\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\min}(x_{0}))>\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\min}(x_{0}x))+\widetilde{b}(m)-2G

(note that we replace e.g.,
\uppsi(x_{0})
with
-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\min}(x_{0}))
, since the charge is the inverse of the minimal weight).
In fact we can increase the bound to
(i_{2}-i_{1})\widetilde{b}(m)
, but the bound we state suffices.
Rearranging, we get that

\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\min}(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\min}(x_{0}))>\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}))+\widetilde{b}(m)-2G

i.e.,
\rho_{\min}
is significantly increasing on
x
relatively to
\rho
.

Step 2: baseline shift to sharpest decrease.

Define
\rho_{\ssearrow}
to be the seamless run that has the sharpest decrease on
x
.
Specifically,
\rho_{\ssearrow}
is the seamless run that minimizes
\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}))
among all seamless runs
\eta
on
x_{0}x
. Note that
\rho_{\ssearrow}
is not
\rho_{\min}
, as at least
\rho
is certainly decreasing more sharply than
\rho_{\min}
by the above (in case of several such runs, we choose one arbitrarily).

We now perform a baseline shift (see
Section

12.2
) on
\rho_{\ssearrow}
. Observe that
\rho_{\ssearrow}
is an entire run on
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, and therefore this baseline shift acts on all runs on
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
.
Since
\rho_{\ssearrow}
has the sharpest decrease on
x
, and since shifts maintain the gaps between runs (
Corollary

12.14
), we have that all the shifted runs are non-decreasing. That is, for every run
\eta
on
\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}x}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}]
it holds that

\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}](x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}](x_{0}))\geq 0

(18.1)

Indeed, by
Corollary

12.13
we now have that
\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{\ssearrow}}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}]
is the baseline run (and in particular remains at weight
0
). Therefore, by
Corollary

12.14
we have

\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}x))=\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}](x_{0}x))-0

and similarly

\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}))=\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}](x_{0}))-0

By subtracting the equations we get

\begin{split}&\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}](x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}](x_{0}))=\\
&\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}))+\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}))=\\
&\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}))-(\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0})))\geq 0\end{split}

Where the last inequality is because
\rho_{\ssearrow}
minimizes
(\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0})))
by definition.

To avoid the cumbersome notation of baseline shifts in the following, we wish to reuse the names
x
,
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, and the runs
\rho_{\min},\rho_{\ssearrow}
and
\rho
.
To justify this abuse, we notice that since baseline shifts preserve the run structure (
Corollary

12.14
), then the shifts of the independent runs of
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
retain the properties of being
\ell
-sparse with gap
4G+(|S|+1)|w_{3}|
and being a ghost-free
G
-cover.
In particular, we now have that
\rho_{\ssearrow}
is the baseline run (and in particular remains at weight
0
).

Step 3:
x
induces a stable cycle.

Since all the runs on
x
are nonnegative (
Eq.

18.1
) and
x
yields a repetition of configurations, we gain the intuition that
x
should induce a stable cycle. We show this is indeed the case. The difficult part is to show that there are no negative cycles induced by
x^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The proof relies on the fact that there are large gaps between the runs, and that the runs are a
G
-cover. Broadly, this is a similar proof to
Proposition

16.23
.

Let
S^{\prime}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},x_{0})
, we claim that
(S^{\prime},x)
is a stable cycle. Let
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},x_{0})
as before and
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},x_{0}x)
.
Since
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(\widehat{x_{0}})=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(\widehat{x_{0}}\cdot x)
, it follows that
S^{\prime}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},x_{0}\cdot x)
. Indeed, we have
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},x_{0})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{0}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{x}})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},x_{0}\cdot x)
, and by
Section

11.4
this also implies the equivalent ghost states. Thus, we have in particular that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},x)\subseteq S^{\prime}
, so
(S^{\prime},x)
is a reflexive cycle.

The next step (as per
Definition

10.5
) is to show that for the baseline state
s\in S^{\prime}
we have that
s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},x^{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and that
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{n},s\to s)=0
.
Recall that after the baseline shift, we have that
\rho_{\ssearrow}
is a baseline run on
x
and has weight
0
, in particular
\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow})=0
, and therefore
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{n}_{\ssearrow})=0
, so
x^{n}
has a cycle of weight
0
.

It remains to show that there are no negative cycles in
(S^{\prime},x^{n})
for any
n
. That is, for every
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
, we want to show that
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{n},s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime})\geq 0
.
We start with an overview of how this is proved.
Recall that our baseline shift operation ensures that
\rho_{\ssearrow}
has weight
0
, and
\rho_{\ssearrow}
before the rebase has the strongest decrease, so all seamless runs after rebase are non-negative (on
x
).
This, however, does not immediately extend to
x^{n}
. Indeed, it could conceptually be that there are e.g., (non-seamless) negative runs
r_{1}\xrightarrow{x}r_{2}
and
r_{2}\xrightarrow{x}r_{1}
, but then their concatenation yields a negative seamless cycle on
x^{2}
.

We therefore take an approach similar to the proof of
Lemma

16.15
. Specifically, we notice that the equivalence of
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}
before and after reading
x
implies that we can partition these configurations to ordered sub-configurations
V_{1},\cdots V_{k}
such that all the “interesting” transitions are from
V_{i}
are to
V_{i}
. We then show that after reading
x
, the actual values of the elements in the configurations of each
V_{i}
does not decrease (due to
\rho_{\ssearrow}
being the baseline). This allows us to conclude there are no negative cycles.
For the ghost-states, we use the ghost-freeness assumption to conclude there cannot be negative cycles on them.
We proceed with the formal details.

We remind of the following notations of Step 1.
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{0}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
and
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},x_{0})
. Also denote
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{x}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}}x)
and
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},x_{0}x)
.
Strictly speaking, note that in Step 1 these configurations are defined before our baseline shift of Step 2. However, the gaps are maintained by the shift, so this overloading is justified.

Since
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(\widehat{x_{0}})=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(\widehat{x_{0}}x)
, and by the preservation of the reachable set, we have that for every
s\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}}x)
that
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(s)=\mathsf{mwt}(x_{0},s_{0}\to s)
and
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(s)=\mathsf{mwt}(x_{0}x,s_{0}\to s)
.
Put simply – the configurations correctly track the weights of all states reachable by
\widehat{x_{0}}
(we deal later with the remaining states, namely
S^{\prime}\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
).

We partition
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
as
V_{1}\cup\ldots\cup V_{k}
according to
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(\widehat{x_{0}})
, similarly to the proof of
Lemma

16.15
. Specifically, we write
p\equiv q
if
|\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(p)-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q)|\leq 2G
. Since
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
is a
G
-cover, this is an equivalence relation. We then define
V_{1},\ldots,V_{k}
to be the equivalence classes of
\sim
, ordered by
\leq_{\boldsymbol{c_{0}}}
, i.e., all the states in
V_{1}
are lower than those of
V_{2}
, etc.
By the equivalence of
\mathsf{diff\_type}
, we have that the partition induces by
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}
is the same as that of
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}
.

Still similarly to
Lemma

16.15
, since
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
is
\ell
-sparse with a huge gap, we have that if
p\in V_{i}
,
q\in V_{j}
and there is a run
\eta:p\xrightarrow{x}q
, then
i\geq j
. That is, there are no runs that go “up” between the sets (as the gap is to large to be bridged by
x
, so this would “drag down”
q
).
Moreover, if
q\in V_{j}
there exists some
p\in V_{j}
such that
p\xrightarrow{x}q
, i.e., there must exist a predecessor of
q
in
V_{j}
(otherwise all predecessors of
q
come from higher sets, so
q
would be higher). Additionally, there must exist such
p\in V_{j}
such that
\eta:p\xrightarrow{x}q
is seamless (indeed, runs from higher sets to
q
cannot be seamless).

We claim that for every
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
it holds that
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q)\leq\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q)
. Intuitively,
x
only induces increasing behaviors.
To show this, let
1\leq i\leq k
such that
q\in V_{i}
. Let
q^{0}_{i}
be the minimal state in
V_{i}
(i.e.,
q^{0}_{i}\in\arg\min\{\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q)\mid q\in V_{i}\}
).

Let
p^{0}_{i}
be a state in
V_{i}
such that there exists a seamless run
\eta:p^{0}_{i}\xrightarrow{x}q^{0}_{i}
. Recall that our baseline shift ensures there are no seamless negative runs. It follows that
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q^{0}_{i})=\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(p^{0}_{i})+\mathsf{wt}(\eta)\geq\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(p^{0}_{i})\geq\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q^{0}_{i})
, where the last inequality is because we know that
q^{0}_{i}
is the minimal in
V_{i}
.
Therefore, the claim holds for
q^{0}_{i}
.
For every other state
q\in V_{i}
we have that
q\sim q^{0}_{i}
, and therefore
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q^{0}_{i})=\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q^{0}_{i})
, so
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q^{0}_{i})-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q^{0}_{i})\geq 0
.

Notice that the argument above only assumes that the gaps are larger than
|x|
. Recall, however, that we originally start with gaps larger than
(|S|+1)|w_{3}|\geq(|S|+1)|x|
(this includes the gap from the ghost states, which is even larger). It therefore follows that we can repeat this argument for up to
|S|
times, and obtain that the weights of each state is increased after reading
x^{n}
for any
n\leq|S|
.

Now, assume by way of contradiction that there is a negative cycle
\eta:q\xrightarrow{x^{n}}q
such that
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We first notice that we can assume
n\leq|S|
(otherwise we can find an inner
x^{n^{\prime}}
-cycle that is either itself a negative cycle, or is positive and we can shorten the original cycle). This, however, implies that
\mathsf{mwt}(x_{0},s_{0}\to q)>\mathsf{mwt}(x_{0}x^{n},s_{0}\to q)
, which is a contradiction to our observation above.

It remains to show that there are no negative cycles stemming from ghost-states.
In fact, we prove that there are no cycles (on
x^{n}
) on ghost states at all, negative or not.
This follows from ghost-freeness assumed in the lemma, as follows.
Assume by way of contradiction that there exists
g\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
and
\eta:g\xrightarrow{x^{n}}g
. We decompose
\eta
as
\eta:g\xrightarrow{x}g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{n-1}}g
according to the state reached after
x
.
Since
x
spans at least one
u_{i}
segment by definition, it follows from ghost-freeness (
Definition

18.6
) that
\rho
is not seamless already before
g^{\prime}
, i.e., we can write
x=y_{1}y_{2}
such that
\eta:g\xrightarrow{y_{1}}h\xrightarrow{y_{2}}g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{n-1}}g
and we can assume
h\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}}y_{1})
.

There is a slightly delicate point here: it could be that
\eta
is not seamless but that the run that “undercuts” it is also from a ghost-state. However, we can then repeat this argument until eventually reaching a “minimal” ghost run, after which the undercutting run indeed uses a state in
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}}y_{1})
.

Since
\eta
is a run, and in particular has finite weight, it follows that its suffix
\eta^{\prime}:h\xrightarrow{y_{2}}g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{n-1}}g
is also finite weight, and since
h\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}}y_{1})
, we have that
g\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}}x^{n})
, but
x
preserves the set of reachable states (by the equivalence of
\mathsf{diff\_type}
), so it follows that
g\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
, in contradiction to the assumption that
g\notin\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
.

We conclude that
(S^{\prime},x)
is a stable cycle, and so
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
is a cactus letter.

Step 4: replace
x
with
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
.

We now focus on the word
x_{0}\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
.
Our goal is to show that the minimal run after
x_{0}
, upon reading
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
, “jumps” to
\infty
.
Intuitively, due to the
increase of the minimal run, we can show that the minimal class
V_{1}
(of states equivalent to the minimal run) does not contain any grounded pairs of
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
(see
Definition

10.7
). Hence, there are no finite-value transitions on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
from this set.

Formally, recall from Step 1 that the minimal independent run (on
x
)
\rho_{\min}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x_{0}}s_{1}\xrightarrow{x}s_{2}
satisfies

\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\min}(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\min}(x_{0}))>\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}))+\widetilde{b}(m)-2G>2G

(18.2)

Where the last inequality is since after the baseline shift we have that
\rho
is non-decreasing, so
\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}))\geq 0
, and by our choice of
\widetilde{b}(m)>4G
.

Let
V_{1}
be the minimal equivalence class of states defined in Step 3. Due to the
G
-cover property, we have that
s_{1}\in V_{1}
and for each
s^{\prime}\in V_{1}
we have
|\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(s_{1})-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(s^{\prime})|\leq G
and similarly
|\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(s_{1})-\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(s^{\prime})|\leq G
. Consequently, for every
s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime}\in V_{1}
we have by the triangle inequality and
Eq.

18.2
that

\mathsf{mwt}(x,s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime\prime})>2G-2G=0
.

We now claim that for every
s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime}\in V_{1}
we have that
(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},x)
. In order to prove this, we use the contrapositive of
Lemma

10.11
: it suffices to show that

\mathsf{mwt}(x^{4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime\prime})>\mathsf{mwt}(x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}},s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime\prime})
, since if
(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})
were a grounded pair, these weights would be equal.

By our observation above, we
almost
have that
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{k+1},s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime\prime})>\mathsf{mwt}(x^{k},s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime\prime})
. Indeed, we already have that any runs on
x
between states in
V_{1}
are strictly increasing. However, a-priori it could be that after enough repetitions of
x
, runs from e.g.,
V_{2}
would decrease and become the minimal on
x^{k}
.
However, we now recall that the gap between the independent runs is greater than
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|>(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|x|
. It follows that with each repetition of
x
, the gap between runs from
V_{1}
and from
V_{2}
(or higher) can become closer by at most
2|x|W_{\max}
. In particular, after
4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
iterations, the gap is still at least

(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|x|-8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|x|>0

where we assume
|S|\geq 2
(which holds if the original WFA
\mathcal{A}
has at least one state).
and therefore no runs starting at
V_{2}
or higher can become minimal. We thus conclude that
(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})\notin\textsc{GrnPairs}(S^{\prime},x)
.

It follows (by
Definition

11.1
) that
\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},x},V_{1}\to S)=\infty
, i.e., all states in
V_{1}
“jump to infinity” upon
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
.

Step 5: baseline shift to minimal run

After completing Step 4, we are now at the following setting: upon reading the prefix
x_{0}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
, the next letter is
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
, and all states in
V_{1}
cannot read it, i.e, jump to
\infty
.

Intuitively, this almost shows that the potential of
x_{0}
is at least
P
– there is a run much higher than
V_{1}
, and all the states from
V_{1}
go to
\infty
. However, our precise definition of potential requires that this happens with the baseline run. We therefore need to perform some baseline shifts. Unfortunately, this baseline shift makes
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
“unreadable” after the shift, as it now has the wrong format. We fix this by introducing jump letters. We proceed with the details.

Consider the seamless run
\mu
that ends with minimal weight on
x_{0}
, and perform a baseline shift
only on
x_{0}
with respect to
\mu
. Note that
\mu
ends in
V_{1}
(since the minimal state is there).

We now have that
\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu}/{\mu}]
is the baseline run on
\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\mu}]
, and we denote
\boldsymbol{c_{1}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\mu}])
.
Recall that
m>P
(this is chosen before Step 1).

In the following, we still wish to reason about the
V_{j}
partition. However, following the baseline shift, the state have changed. Understanding the change and showing that it is very manageable requires us to dig slightly deeper into the states reached after
x_{0}
and after
\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\mu}]
.

Specifically, since
x_{0}
is obtained by flattening
\widehat{x_{0}}
, there are no ghost states. That is,
the states reachable after
x_{0}
are
S^{\prime}=\{(p,q,T)\mid p\in T\}
for some
q,T
with
q\in T\subseteq Q
(as per the construction of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
in
Section

9
). Note that this is the same
S^{\prime}
as in the cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
.

After baseline shift (
Proposition

12.12
), the states reachable when reading
\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\mu}]
are

S^{\prime\prime}=\{(p,q^{\prime},T)\mid p\in T\}
for some
q^{\prime}\in T
.

Apart from the change in the baseline component, however, there is no change in the state components or their gaps. We can therefore denote
\mathsf{bshift}[{V_{j}}/{\mu}]
when we wish to refer to the set of states corresponding to
V_{j}
after the baseline shift.
We can now proceed with showing the potential is unbounded.

Since the gap between independent runs is much larger than
m
, then in particular all states in
\mathsf{bshift}[{V_{j}}/{\mu}]
with
j>1
are at least
P
above any state in
\mathsf{bshift}[{V_{1}}/{\mu}]
, after reading
\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\mu}]
.
An important detail here is that any reachable state after
x_{0}
that is not in any of the
V_{j}
, i.e., in
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},x_{0})
, is still reachable, and the minimal weight to it is above all the
V_{j}
, and in particular these states also have a huge gap from
V_{1}
.

In order to conclude that
\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\mu}])>P
, it suffices (see
Definitions

13.2
and
13.1
) to find a suffix
z
such that for some state
s\in S^{\prime\prime}
such that

\mathsf{mwt}(z,s\to S)<\infty
and for every
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime\prime}
with
\boldsymbol{c_{1}}(s^{\prime})<\boldsymbol{c_{1}}(s)
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(z,s\to S)=\infty
.
Crucially, the suffix
z
need not be jump-free or over
\Gamma^{0}_{0}
.

Note that
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
has no transitions of finite weight from states in
S^{\prime\prime}
due to the “wrong” baseline component
q^{\prime}
.
Consider therefore the jump letter
\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,q^{\prime},T)\to(\cdot,q,T)}
as per
Definition

11.15
and the suffix
z=\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,q^{\prime},T)\to(\cdot,q,T)}\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
.

We first claim that there are no finite-weight runs from any
s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{bshift}[{V_{1}}/{\mu}]
on
z
. Indeed, for every state
s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{bshift}[{V_{1}}/{\mu}]
, write
s^{\prime}=(p,q^{\prime},T)
, then after reading
\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,q^{\prime},T)\to(\cdot,q,T)}
, the next state is (deterministically)
(p,q,t)
, and the weight of the transition is
0
. However, this is now a state in
V_{1}
, and therefore has no finite-weight transition on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
, as per Step 4.

All that remains is to show that there is some reachable state
s
for which
\mathsf{mwt}(z,s\to S)<\infty
.
Indeed, we can then take the minimal such state, and conclude the potential is above
P
.

The immediate candidate for
s
is the baseline state of
S^{\prime}
. That is, take
(q,q,T)\in S^{\prime}
and consider the corresponding state
(q,q^{\prime},T)\in S^{\prime\prime}
, then after reading
\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,q^{\prime},T)\to(\cdot,q,T)}
we arrive at
(q,q,T)
with the same weight, and since
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
is a cactus letter and its baseline state is
(q,q,T)
, then
\mathsf{mwt}((q,q,T),\alpha_{S^{\prime},x})=0
. In particular, we have that
(q,q,T)\notin V_{1}
, so
(q,q^{\prime},T)\notin\mathsf{bshift}[{V_{1}}/{\mu}]
. We conclude that
\mathsf{mwt}((q,q^{\prime},T),\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,q^{\prime},T)\to(\cdot,q,T)}\alpha_{S^{\prime},x})=0<\infty
, and therefore as mentioned above, we conclude that
\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\mu}])>P
, where
\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\mu}]\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
, as required.
∎

We conclude this section with one more definition, to be used later on, and some simple corollaries of it.
It is an analogue of
D
-dip words (
Definition

16.13
), now requiring that there are no large dips only on an infix of
w_{3}
, as follows.

Definition 18.9

(Infix
D
-dip)
.

For
D\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that
words
is
Infix
D
-dip
if for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists some
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
such that we can write
w_{3}=xvy
with
|v|>n
and for every
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}x)
and run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{x}S
it holds that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)>-D
.

Clearly there is a lot of similarity between Infix
D
-dip and the
D
-dip words of
Definition

16.13
. As we now show, the former implies the latter. Intuitively, this is simple: just select the words that witness the infix
D
-dip property, and rewrite them as concatenations so that the infix
v
becomes
w_{3}^{\prime}
.

Note that the central result of
Section

16
, namely
Corollary

16.26
, shows that
D
-dip words implies the existence of a separated increasing infix. These observations are central tools in our final proof (
Section

20
).

Proposition 18.10
.

Let
D\in\mathbb{N}
. If
words
is Infix
D
-dip, then there exists a faithful restriction (see
Definition

16.9
)
\mathsf{xwords}
of
words
such that
\mathsf{xwords}
is an exact words sequence (
Definition

16.10
) and is
D
-dip.

Proof.

We define
\mathsf{xwords}
as follows. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, by
Definition

18.9
let
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and we can write
w_{3}=xvy
with
|v|>n
, where for every
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}x)
and run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{x}S
it holds that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)>-D
.
Define
\mathsf{xwords}(n)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1}
,
w^{\prime}_{2}=w_{2}x
,
w^{\prime}_{3}=v[1,n]
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=v[n,|v|]yw_{4}
. By definition,
\mathsf{xwords}
is a faithful restriction of
words
. Since we define
w^{\prime}_{3}=v[1,n]
, we have
|w_{3}|=n
, so
\mathsf{xwords}
is indeed an exact words sequence. In addition, the property of
v
is exactly the
D
-dip property of
Definition

16.13
(and in particular holds for any prefix of
v
, namely
w^{\prime}_{3}
), so we are done.
∎

Combining
Proposition

18.10
with
Corollary

16.26
, we have the following.

Corollary 18.11
.

Let
D\in\mathbb{N}
. If
words
is Infix
D
-dip, then there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and a decomposition
w_{3}=u^{\prime}xyv^{\prime}
such that
uxyv
is a separated increasing infix from
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})
for
u=w_{2}u^{\prime}
and
v=v^{\prime}w_{4}
.

18.2
Leveled Words and Discharging Words

In this section we focus on two types of
words
sequences. The first, dubbed
leveled words
, models the case where the charge remains bounded.
It’s dual, dubbed
discharging words
, models the case where the charge significantly decreases along
w_{3}
(i.e., the minimal run is increasing).

We show that the existence of either type of words sequence (under very mild assumptions) implies that either the potential is unbounded, or the sequence has the
D
-dip property. These properties are central to our reasoning in the final proof (
Section

20
).

18.2.1
Leveled Words Sequences

An elongated words sequence
words
is leveled if, intuitively, the charge along the
w_{3}
infix remains in some constant width “band”.

Definition 18.12

(
\kappa
-Leveled Words Sequence)
.

For
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, a function from
\mathbb{N}
to
\Gamma^{\prime*}
is a
\kappa
-leveled words sequence
, denoted
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}:\mathbb{N}\to\Gamma^{\prime*}
, if it is an elongated words sequence, and for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and prefix
u
of
w_{3}
it holds that

|\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u)|\leq\kappa
.

That is, in a
\kappa
-leveled word sequence, the charge on the
w_{3}
infix remains within a band of “width”
\kappa
around the charge at
w_{2}
. Note that in particular, the charge at
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
is also within this band. Also note that this is in particular an elongated words sequence, so the length of
w_{3}
increases. The behavior of leveled words is depicted in
Fig.

28
.

Figure 28
:
A leveled word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
. The charge upon reading the infix
w_{3}
stayes bounded within
\kappa
of its starting point.

Our main technical result in this section that the existence of leveled words with large gap between independent runs implies that either the potential is unbounded, or the sequence has the infix
D
-dip property (
Definition

18.9
).
This result is proved by induction on the number of independent runs. Before proving the claim, we present a subcase of it, used in the induction.

Proposition 18.13
.

Let
\ell,G\in\mathbb{N}
and consider a leveled words sequence
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
that is
\ell
-sparse with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
and is a configuration
G
-cover. Further assume there exists
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
m
with
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, all ghost runs on
w_{3}
are of length at most
l
.
Then either
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
, or
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

If
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
, we are done. We therefore assume this is not the case. Thus, by the converse of
Definition

18.9
, for every
D,n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
m
with
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
such that we can write
w_{3}=xvy
with
|v|>n
and there exists
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}x)
and a run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{x}S
with
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\leq-D
.

Our goal now is to obtain from
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
another leveled sequence
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
that has a decreasing-gap fair decomposition (
Definition

18.7
). Intuitively, we obtain the decreasing gap using the decreasing runs on
v
above.

We define
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
as follows. For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
m^{\prime}>m
such that for
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m^{\prime})=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
we can write
w_{3}=xvy
with
|v|>(|S|+1)m+l
(recall that
l
is the bound on the length of a ghost run) and there exists
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}x)
and a run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{v}S
with
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\leq-W_{\max}((|S|+1)m+l)
.
Since there are no ghost runs of length greater than
l
over
w_{3}
, it follows that
\rho
reaches a reachable state
q
within
l
steps from
p
. We then still have a run
\rho^{\prime}
over a suffix
v^{\prime}
of
v
such that
\rho^{\prime}:q\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}}S
with
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime})\leq-W_{\max}(|S|+1)m
. Indeed,
\rho
can lose at most
W_{\max}l
weight within
l
steps.
Recall that for decreasing-gap fair decompositions we need a
seamless
decreasing run (
Definition

18.7
). Intuitively, the existence of a long-enough decreasing run clearly implies the existence of a decreasing seamless run, since a run cannot decrease without “dragging down” with it some seamless run.

Formally, if
\rho^{\prime}
has an infix of length at least
m
that is decreasing and seamless, we redefine
v^{\prime}
as that infix and we are done. Otherwise, decompose
\rho^{\prime}
into
|S|+1
segments by the first time
\rho^{\prime}
loses an additional
W_{\max}m
weight.
Since there are at most
|S|
seamless runs, it follows that
\rho^{\prime}
intersects the same seamless runs at least twice at the end of these segments, but then this seamless run is decreasing at least as much as
\rho^{\prime}
, so there is a seamless run decreasing by at least
W_{\max}m
, as required. We reuse
v^{\prime}
to denote the corresponding suffix, and write

v=v_{0}v^{\prime}
.

Define
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}(m)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1},w^{\prime}_{2}=w_{2}xv_{0},w^{\prime}_{3}=v^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=yw_{4}
.
Denote by
\kappa
the charge band of
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
.
Observe that
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
is a
2\kappa
-leveled word sequence: the infixes
v^{\prime}
have increasing length, and since the words in
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
are equal as concatenations to words from
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
, then the bounded-charge property is preserved, with the modification that previously all charges were within gap
\kappa
of
\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})
, whereas now it is possible that e.g.,
\uppsi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})=\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})-\kappa
, so the remaining charge is within distance at most
2\kappa
.
Moreover,
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
is
\ell
-sparse with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
and a configuration
G
-cover. Indeed, since we choose
v^{\prime}
that are shorter than their corresponding
|w_{3}|
, the gap requirement that is already met in
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
is even larger with respect to
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
, and the decomposition
G
-cover is implied by the configuration
G
-cover.

It remains to show that
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
has a decreasing-gap fair decomposition. To this end, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
consider
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}(m)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
as above, where by construction there exists
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})
and a run
\rho:q\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}_{3}}S
with
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\leq-W_{\max}m
and
|w^{\prime}_{3}|>m
.
We decompose
w^{\prime}_{3}=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}
as follows.
Let
d(m)
be the maximal number such that the following ordered indices are distinct:

0=i_{0}\leq i_{1}\leq\ldots\leq i_{d(m)}<|w_{3}|
where for every
1\leq k\leq d(m)
we have

i_{k}=\min\{j\mid\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}[1,i_{k-1}]))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}[1,j]))\geq\sqrt{m}W_{\max}\}

We then set
u_{k}=w^{\prime}_{3}[i_{k-1}+1,i_{k}]
for all
1\leq k\leq d(m)
and
u_{d(m)+1}
the remaining suffix.

Observe that for every
1\leq k\leq d(m)
we now have by definition that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1}))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k}))\geq\sqrt{m}W_{\max}
. Therefore, since the charge remains within a
2\kappa
band, we have that

\begin{split}&\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1}))+\uppsi(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1}))\geq\\
&\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k}))+\sqrt{m}W_{\max}+\uppsi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k})-2\kappa\end{split}

Moreover, since with each letter the run
\rho
loses at most
W_{\max}
weight, then the number of segments in the decomposition is (roughly)
\sqrt{m}
, i.e.,
\lim_{m\to\infty}d(m)=\infty
.

Thus,
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
has a decreasing-gap fair decomposition with
\widetilde{b}(m)=\sqrt{m}W_{\max}-2\kappa
.
We conclude that
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

18.8
, and therefore
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
, so we are done.
∎

We are now ready for the main inductive argument.

Lemma 18.14

(

Leveled Words and Gap to Unbounded Potential (or
D
-Dip))
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and consider a leveled words sequence
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
that is
\ell
-sparse with gap
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
. Then either
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
, or
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

The proof is by reverse induction on
\ell
, and shares common elements with the proof of
Lemma

16.24
.

Base case:
\ell=|S|

Recall that the maximal number of configuration-independent runs is
|S|
(see
Definition

18.1
).
In this case, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
we have that in every configuration in
w_{3}
, each state belongs to one of the independent runs.
In particular,
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
is a configuration
G
-cover with
G=0
, and is also
\ell
-sparse by assumption, with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
(since
G=0
).

Moreover, since
\ell=|S|
, then in particular every state is reachable along any prefix of
w_{3}
, and therefore there are no ghost runs at all along
w_{3}
.
Thus, the conditions of
Proposition

18.13
are met, so either
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
has the infix
D
-dip property for some
D
, or
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
, and we are done.

Inductive case:
\ell<|S|

The induction is split into three subcases.

Case 1: long ghost runs.

In this case we assume that there are unboundedly long ghost runs. Specifically, assume that for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
there is some
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and we can write
w_{3}=xuy
such that
|u|\geq l
and there exists
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}x)\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}x)
and a ghost run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{u}S
.
Intuitively, while this ghost run is not a real run on
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, it becomes a real run if we flatten the prefix up to it. Moreover, since flattening guarantees that ghost runs are very high, this implies the existence of another independent run, so we can apply the induction.

Formally, we define a new sequence
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
as follows. For every
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
let
m>m^{\prime}
be such that
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
has a ghost run as above with
|u|>m^{\prime}
. Define
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}(m^{\prime})=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with

w^{\prime}_{1}=\mathsf{flatten}(w_{1}w_{2}x\wr(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}|),w^{\prime}_{2}=\epsilon,w^{\prime}_{3}=y
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=yw_{4}
.
13
13

13

We remark that unlike
Section

16
, the roles of
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
here are combined, so it is fine selecting
w_{2}=\epsilon
and moving its content to
w_{1}
(or vice versa). We keep the four-part formalism for uniformity.

Similarly to
Lemma

18.8
, since our alphabet does not contain rebase letters, by
Definition

12.23
the resulting prefix
x^{\prime}_{1}
is over
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
.
Denote
\boldsymbol{c}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{init}},w_{1}w_{2}x)
and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{init}},w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})
, then by

Lemma

12.24

we have that
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q)
for every
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2})
, and

\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(p)\geq(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}|+\max\{\boldsymbol{c}(p^{\prime})\mid p^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})\}
.
Specifically, the entire run
\rho
from
p
is above all the existing
\ell
independent runs with gap at least
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
.
It follows that
\rho
can be added as an independent run. Thus,
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
has
\ell+1
independent runs. Moreover, note that
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
remains a leveled words sequence, since the minimal states are always reachable and therefore maintain their weight after flattening.
We can therefore apply the induction hypothesis on
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
. By the hypothesis, if
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
, then we are done.

Otherwise, we get that
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
has the
D
-dip property. Recall, however, that we need to show
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
has the
D
-dip property. Fortunately, observe that
D
-dip is “local” in that it only considers runs over the relevant infix, and since the
D
-dip infixes of
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
also appear in
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
, so we can indeed show this as follows.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider
m^{\prime}
such that
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m^{\prime})=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
has an infix
u
with
w^{\prime}_{3}=xuy
with
|u|\geq n
and for every
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}x)
and run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{u}S
we have
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)>-D
(these exist by the
D
-dip property, see
Definition

18.9
).
By the construction of
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
, there exists
m>m^{\prime}
such that
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and
w^{\prime}_{3}
is an infix of
w_{3}
. Moreover, while
w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}x
differs from the corresponding prefix
z
of
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m)
due to the flattening, it still holds that
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},z)=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}x)
by the flattening procedure.
It follows that the same condition on
u
holds also in
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
. Therefore,
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
also has the infix
D
-dip property, and we are done.

In the following cases, we therefore assume there is a bound
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that all ghost runs on infixes of any
w_{3}
are of length at most
l
.

Case 2: a diverging run on a suffix of
w_{3}
.

In this case we essentially assume that the independent runs are not a
G
-cover for any
G
, and use this to construct a new independent run from states that stay far away from all independent runs. We then apply the induction hypothesis. This is very similar to the setting in
Fig.

25
, where instead of starting from a single state, we start from a configuration. Specifically, we assume the divergence occurs in some long-enough prefix of
w_{3}
.

Formally, assume that for every
G\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m>2G
with
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and we can write
w_{3}=uv
with
|u|\geq\frac{1}{2}|w_{3}|
such that for
\boldsymbol{c}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}u)
there exists a state
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})
for which
\boldsymbol{c}(q)
is not withing gap
G
of any independent run.

We define a new sequence
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
as follows. For every
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, take some
G>2W_{\max}m^{\prime}+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}m^{\prime}

and let
m>2m^{\prime}
large enough such that the gap of
q
as above is at least
G
.
Write
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
with
w_{3}=uv
and
q
as above.
We can now use the fact that all seamless runs change their weight by at most
W_{\max}
at each step. Consider the seamless run
\rho
leading up to
q
, and write
u=u_{1}u_{2}
with
|u_{2}|=m^{\prime}
(this is possible since
|u|\geq\frac{1}{2}|w_{3}|\geq\frac{1}{2}m>m^{\prime}
).
Since
q
is not within gap
G
of any independent run, for every prefix
u^{\prime}
of
u_{2}
it holds that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}u^{\prime}))
is not within gap
G-2W_{\max}m^{\prime}>(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}m^{\prime}
of any independent run.

We can therefore define
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m^{\prime})=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
by
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1},w^{\prime}_{2}=w_{2}u_{1},w^{\prime}_{3}=u_{2},w^{\prime}_{4}=vw_{4}
. As above, this is a leveled words sequence, since the charge requirement remains valid. Moreover, we now have
\ell+1
independent runs satisfying the gap constraints. We can therefore apply the induction hypothesis. As in Case 1, if the potential is unbounded then we are done, and if
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
has the infix
D
-dip property, this lifts back to
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
(here we do not even have to consider the flattening).

Case 3:
G
-cover on suffix of
w_{3}
.

We now consider the complement of Case 2, still under the assumption that all ghost runs are of length at most
l
.
Specifically, we assume that there exists
G\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
m>2G
with
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, for every decomposition
w_{3}=uv
with
|u|\geq\frac{1}{2}|w_{3}|
and for every state
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}u)
, the weight of
q
is within gap
G
of some independent run. We therefore have a
G
-cover of the “second half” of
w_{3}
.
Our goal is now to apply
Proposition

18.13
. For this, we only need to define a sequence where the
w_{3}
infix corresponds to these “second halves”. A slight caveat is that
Proposition

18.13
requires a larger gap than
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
has. This, however, is an “artificial” problem: the gaps in
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
grow with
m
, so we just need to pick words that are further away, so we have larger gaps.

Formally, we proceed as follows. For every
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, let
m=2(m^{\prime}+G)
with
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and write
w_{3}=uv
with
|v|=m^{\prime}
(in particular
|u|\geq\frac{1}{2}|w_{3}|
). Define
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(m^{\prime})=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
by
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1},w^{\prime}_{2}=w_{2}u,w^{\prime}_{3}=v,w^{\prime}_{4}=w_{4}
. We observe that
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
is still a leveled words sequence, since the charge constraints do not change. We claim that
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
is
\ell
-sparse with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
. Indeed, recalling that
m=2(m^{\prime}+G)
, the gap guaranteed by
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
is

\begin{split}&(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|\geq(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}2(m^{\prime}+G)\geq\\
&4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}m^{\prime}=4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w^{\prime}_{3}|\end{split}

Finally,
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
has a configuration
G
-cover, as we discuss above.

We can therefore apply
Proposition

18.13
to
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
and we conclude that either
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
has the infix
D
-dip property, or
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
. Again, the infix
D
-dip property lifts back to
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
, so we are done.
Note that the last case is not inductive, but direct (indeed, it can be thought of as a different base case).
∎

Recall by the definition of sparse words (
Definition

16.11
), that if we consider a set with a single independent run (i.e.,
\ell=1
), then this set is vacuously sparse with any size of gap. We can then already apply
Lemma

18.14
. Indeed, this aligns precisely with the inductive proof above: we start with just the seamless baseline run. If it is a
G
-cover, we are done. Otherwise show that there is another independent run, and so on. Thus, in order to use
Definition

16.11
, we only need the leveled sequence, and not the gap requirement, giving us the main tool of this section.

Corollary 18.15

(

Leveled Words to Unbounded Potential or
D
-Dip)
.

Consider a
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
sequence, then either
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
, or
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

18.2.2
Discharging Words Sequences

A counterpart to leveled words are
discharging words
, where the charge significantly decreases.

Definition 18.16

(

Discharging Words Sequence)
.

An elongated words sequence
words
is a
discharging words sequence
, denoted
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}:\mathbb{N}\to\Gamma^{\prime*}
, if for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
the following hold:

•

\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3})>m
(the minimal states get much closer to the baseline run upon reading
w_{3}
).

•

For every prefix
v
of
w_{3}
we have
\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}v)\geq\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3})
(the minimal state stays below its value at
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
).

We illustrate the “shape” of
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
in
Fig.

29
.

Figure 29
:
A discharging word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
. The infix
w_{3}
brings the charge much closer to the baseline, being at its closest at the end of
w_{3}
.
The orange dots signify each segment in the decomposition, i.e., the first time the charge decreases by another
\sqrt{m}
.

Unless stated otherwise, in the following when we write
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
we implicitly assume the function is a discharging words sequence.

Discharging words sequences have a natural notion of decomposition: since the charge between
w_{1}w_{2}
and
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
decreases by at least
m
, we can mark each index where it decreases by
\sqrt{m}
, and thus obtain roughly
\sqrt{m}
segments, each of length roughly
\sqrt{m}
. This is similar to the proof of
Proposition

18.13
.
Intuitively, each segment in the decomposition can be thought of as “zooming in” on
w_{3}
and finding a self-similar structure. Indeed, the characteristic of the
w_{3}
infixes is that the charge decreases along them, while they get longer, and this is exactly the property satisfied by the infixes in the decomposition.

Unfortunately, things are not quite so simple, since the charge can behave in nasty ways. Specifically, it could be that
\uppsi(x)
is much larger than
\uppsi(x\sigma)
for some
x
, in case the minimal run on
x
cannot continue with
\sigma
, thus making the minimal state much higher. Put more succinctly, this behavior is the opposite of
Definition

13.5
.
In such cases, the decomposition we propose may have much less than
\sqrt{m}
segments.

Nonetheless, we define this decomposition, and show that under sufficient assumptions (namely
Definition

13.5
), it behaves well, i.e., it is a fair decreasing-gap decomposition.
Keeping in mind that the general goal of this section is to ultimately prove that the potential is unbounded, then by
Lemma

13.6
we already know that if the charge does not have bounded decrease, the potential is unbounded. Therefore, we need not be worried about this too much.

Definition 18.17

(

Discharging Decomposition)
.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, let
d(m)
be the maximal number such that the following ordered indices are distinct:

0=i_{0}\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\ldots\leq i_{d(m)}<|w_{3}|
where for every
1\leq k\leq d(m)
we have

i_{k}=\min\{j\mid\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}[1,i_{k-1}])-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}[1,j])>\sqrt{m}\}
.

The
discharging decomposition
of
w_{3}
is then
w_{3}=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}
where
u_{k}=w_{3}[i_{k-1}+1,i_{k}]
for every
1\leq k\leq d(m)
, and
u_{d(m)+1}=w_{3}[i_{d(m)+1},|w_{3}|]
.

For every
1\leq k\leq d(m)
we then have:

•

\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k})>\sqrt{m}
.

•

For every prefix
v
of
u_{1}\cdots u_{k}
it holds that

\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}v)\geq\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k})

Observe that the indices in
Definition

18.17
are indeed ordered, since we always take the first index after which the charge decreases by a certain amount.

We now show that if
\uppsi
behaves nicely, then the discharging decomposition is a decreasing-gap fair decomposition. Specifically, we require that the charge has bounded decrease (as per
Definition

13.5
).
Recall that otherwise, we have by
Lemma

13.6
that the potential is unbounded, which we utilize later on in the proof.

Proposition 18.18
.

Let
B\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
is such that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
we have that
\uppsi
has
B
-Bounded Decrease on the infix
w_{3}
(as per
Definition

13.5
).
Then the discharging decomposition is a decreasing-gap fair decomposition (as per
Definition

18.7
).

Proof.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
. Write
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}
.

We claim that the discharging decomposition is a decreasing-gap fair decomposition with respect to the baseline run
\rho_{0}
. Note that the baseline run has weight
0
in all prefixes. Therefore, we need to show that the length of the
u_{i}
increases with
m
, that
d(m)
increases with
m
, and that
\uppsi
decreases by some function
\widetilde{b}(m)
between each segment (as per
Definition

18.7
, instantiated with
\rho_{0}
) except for the last “remainder” segment.

The latter requirement is trivial by the
Definition

18.17
– the segments are defined such that

\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})>\sqrt{m}

i.e, their charge gap is at least
\sqrt{m}
.

By the bounded-decrease charge property of
Definition

13.5
, we inductively have that for every segment
u_{i}
(indeed, every infix of
w_{3}
) it holds that

\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})<|u_{i}|B

Combining this with the charge gap above, we have that
|u_{i}|>\sqrt{m}/B
, and in particular the length of
u_{i}
increases with
m
.

We turn to show that
d(m)
increases. We initially claim that

\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})\leq\sqrt{m}+B

Indeed write
u_{i}=v\cdot\sigma
where
\sigma
is the last letter, then

\begin{split}&\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v\sigma)=\\
