&\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v)+\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v)-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v\sigma)\leq\\
&\sqrt{m}+B\end{split}

Where the last inequality follows from
Definition

13.5
and from the fact that
u_{i}=v\sigma
is minimal with the gap property in
Definition

18.17
(i.e.,
\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdot u_{i-1}v)\leq\sqrt{m}
otherwise
v
would have been selected instead of
u_{i}
).

We notice that for segment
u_{d(m)+1}
we do not provide bounds, as it is essentially a “remainder”. Trivially, we have
\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)+1})\leq\sqrt{m}
(otherwise we would have another segment).

By the definition of
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
we now have that

\sum_{i=1}^{d(m)+1}(\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i}))=\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3})\geq m

and on the other hand by the above we have

\sum_{i=1}^{d(m)+1}(\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i}))\leq d(m)(\sqrt{m}+B)+\sqrt{m}

We therefore have

d(m)\geq\frac{m-\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{m}+B}

so
\lim_{m\to\infty}d(m)=\infty
, as required.
∎

Proposition

18.18
lays the ground to use
Lemma

18.8
: if the charge has bounded decrease, then the decomposition is fair, so under the appropriate conditions, the potential is unbounded. That is, we have the following.

Corollary 18.19
.

If there exists
G,\ell,B\in\mathbb{N}
and a discharging word sequence
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
that satisfies the following:

1.

\uppsi
has
B
-bounded decrease on
w_{3}
for every
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, and

2.

\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
(with the discharging decomposition) is a ghost-free decomposition
G
-cover and is
\ell
-sparse with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
,

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
todo:

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=

ACCOUNTING: ACCOUNTING

then
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma^{0}_{0})^{*}\}=\infty
.

Our main result of the section is again an inductive lemma (similar to
Lemma

18.14
), guaranteeing either a
D
-dip or unbounded potential, given a discharging words sequence.

Lemma 18.20

(

Discharging and Gap to Unbounded Potential (or
D
-Dip))
.

Let
\ell,B\in\mathbb{N}
and consider a discharging words sequence
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
that is
\ell
-sparse with gap
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
.
Also assume
\uppsi
has
B
-bounded decrease on
w_{3}
for every
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
.
Then either
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
or
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
has the infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by reverse induction on
\ell
. Again, there are similarities to the proofs of
Lemmas

18.14
and
16.24
.

Base case:
\ell=|S|

Recall that the maximal number of configuration-independent runs is
|S|
(see
Definition

18.1
).
In this case, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
we have that in every configuration in
w_{3}
, each state belongs to one of the independent runs.
In particular,
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
is a configuration
G
-cover with
G=0
, and is also
\ell
-sparse by assumption, with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
(since
G=0
).

Moreover, since
\ell=|S|
, then in particular every state is reachable along any prefix of
w_{3}
, and therefore there are no ghost runs at all along
w_{3}
.
Thus, the conditions of
Corollary

18.19
are met, so
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
, and we are done.

As a side-note, observe that for this case we do not actually need the assumption that
\uppsi
has bounded decrease, as this readily follows from the fact that there are
|S|
independent runs.

Inductive case:
\ell<|S|

For the inductive case, we start by decomposing
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
to its discharging decomposition (
Definition

18.17
)
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)}u_{d(m)+1}
.
Since
\uppsi
has
B
-bounded decrease, then by
Proposition

18.18
, this decomposition is a decreasing-gap fair decomposition.
We now split to three subcases.

Case 1: a diverging run on a segment

In this case we essentially assume that the independent runs are not a decomposition
G
-cover for any
G
, and use this to construct a new independent run from states that stay far away from all independent runs. We then apply the induction hypothesis. This starts similar to the analogous case in
Lemma

18.14
, but differs in that the charge constraints are not necessarily met, and requires further case analysis.

Formally, assume that for every
G\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m>2G
with
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)}u_{d(m)+1}
such that there exist
1\leq i\leq d(m)
where for

\boldsymbol{c_{i}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})
there is a state
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{i}})
for which
\boldsymbol{c_{i}}(q)
is not withing gap
G
of any independent run.
That is, we assume that
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)
does not have a decomposition
G
-cover (
Definition

18.5
).

By the assumption, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
1\leq i\leq d(m)
and
q
above it holds that
q
is not within gap
2W_{\max}n+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}n
of any independent run, and
|u_{i}|>n
.
In particular, there is a seamless runs
\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i}}q
such that for every split
u_{i}=v_{0}v
with
|v|\leq n
we have that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0}))
is not within gap
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}n
of any independent run.

With these notations, we divide into cases, depending on the behavior of
\uppsi
on
u_{i}
.

Case 1a: increasing charge

In this case, we assume that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m>n
and
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
with
u_{i}
,
q
and
\rho
as above such that there is a split
u_{i}=v_{0}v
with

\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0}v)>n
. That is, there is a suffix
v
of
w_{3}
on which the charge decreases significantly. Note the similarity to the condition of a discharging words sequence (
Definition

18.16
).

We therefore construct a discharging words sequence
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
by defining
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}(n)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1},w^{\prime}_{2}=w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0},w^{\prime}_{3}=v
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=u_{i+1}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}w_{4}
.

Since
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
is already a discharging words sequence, and
u_{i}
is a segment in the decomposition, then by the minimality criterion of
Definition

18.17
, we have that the charge along
u_{i}
does not exceed
\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})
. Therefore, combined with the condition on
v
above, we have that
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
satisfies both the conditions of
Definition

18.16
, so it is a discharging words sequence.

Moreover, as we observed above, the run
\rho
on the suffix
v
can be added as an independent run with the gap required in the induction assumption. Note that the remaining runs certainly have large enough gaps, since
m>n
. We therefore satisfy the induction assumption, so by the induction hypothesis, either the potential is unbounded (and we are done), or
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

By an identical argument to
Lemma

18.14
, we can lift this
D
-dip property to
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
itself, and we are done.

Case 1b: non-increasing charge

Complementing Case 1a, we now assume that there exists
\kappa
such that for every
m>\kappa
and
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
with
u_{i}
,
q
and
\rho
as above, every split
u_{i}=v_{0}v
satisfies

\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0}v)\leq\kappa

Our goal in this case is to obtain from these segments a leveled words sequence. Indeed, the condition above is almost the condition for leveled words, but is missing the absolute value.
However, since
u_{i}
is a
minimal
infix of
w_{3}
upon which the charge decreases enough, as per
Definition

18.17
, it follows that
\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0}v)>0
, so we can add the absolute value and assume
|\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0}v)|\leq\kappa
.

We construct a sequence
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
as follows: for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
take
m
for which
|u_{i}|>n
(recall that the segments increase in length by
Proposition

18.18
). Define
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}(n)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1},w^{\prime}_{2}=w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}
,
w^{\prime}_{3}=u_{i}
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=u_{i+1}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}w_{4}
.
By the above, we have that
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
is indeed a leveled words sequence. Moreover, it inherits from
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
the properties of being
\ell
-sparse and having gap
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
.
We can therefore invoke
Lemma

18.14
, and we obtain that either the potential is unbounded, or
\rightsquigarrow\!\!\mathsf{words}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
. As with the case above, an identical argument to
Lemma

18.14
shows that this lifts to
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
, so we are done.

Case 2: long ghost runs

The next case is not mutually exclusive to Case 1, so we assume Case 1 does not hold (even though we do not use this fact).
We assume that there are infinitely many
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and decomposition
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}
such that there exists a ghost run over an entire segment
u_{i}
. More precisely, there exists
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})
and a ghost run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{u_{i}}S
.

Our approach in this case is similar to that taken in
Lemma

18.14
– we flatten the prefix up to
u_{i}
, thus making the ghost run a real run that satisfies the gap constraints. Thus, we can apply induction.

Formally, we define a new sequence
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
as follows. For every
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
let
m>m^{\prime}
be such that
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
has a ghost run on
u_{i}
, and we require

\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})-\uppsi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})>m^{\prime}

(18.3)

Note that we can require this by since
u_{i}
is a segment in a discharging decomposition, as per
Definition

18.17
.
Define
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}(m^{\prime})=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with

w^{\prime}_{1}=\mathsf{flatten}(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}\wr(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{3}|)
,
w^{\prime}_{2}=\epsilon
,
w^{\prime}_{3}=u_{i}
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=u_{i}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}w_{4}
.

Since our alphabet does not contain rebase letters, by
Definition

12.23
the resulting prefix
x^{\prime}_{1}
is over
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
.
Denote
\boldsymbol{c}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{init}},w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})
and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{init}},w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})
, then by

Lemma

12.24

we have that
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q)
for every
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2})
, and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(p)
is much larger than any finite entry in
\boldsymbol{c}
(according to the flattening constant above).
Specifically, the entire run
\rho
from
p
is above all the existing
\ell
independent runs with gap at least
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
.
It follows that
\rho
can be added as an independent run. Thus,
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
has
\ell+1
independent runs. Moreover, note that
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
remains a discharging words sequence. Indeed,
since the minimal states and baseline states are always reachable and therefore maintain their weight after flattening. In particular, by
Eq.

18.3

u_{i}
satisfies the discharge constraints of
Definition

18.16
(to be more precise, it satisfies the second requirement by the minimality constraint of
Definition

18.17
).

We can therefore apply the induction hypothesis on
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
. By the hypothesis, if
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
, then we are done. Otherwise, we get that
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}
has the
D
-dip property, and we again repeat the argument in
Lemma

18.14
to lift this to
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
(note that here the argument is the involved case therein, since we use flattening) and we are done.

Case 3: Ghost free
G
-cover

Our final case assumes both Case 1 and Case 2 do not hold. Since Case 2 does not hold, there are at most finitely many
m
for which there are ghost runs over segments in the decomposition.
We slightly strengthen this to assume there are no ghost runs over any segments at all. This is possible since we can replace
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
by duplicating
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)
for some very large
m
into
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(i)
for all
i\leq m
. Note that this does not interfere with the Infix
D
-dip property, which anyway refers to infinitely many infixes and ignores the replacement of finitely many elements.
We therefore assume that the discharging decomposition of
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
is Ghost Free, as per
Definition

18.6
.

Next, since Case 1 does not hold, then there exists
G\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
m>2G
with
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}
, for every
1\leq i\leq d(m)
and
\boldsymbol{c_{i}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})
, every state
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{i}})
is within gap
G
from some independent run.
As above, we can assume this holds in fact for every
m
, by possibly replacing finitely many elements of
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
.
Thus, the discharging decomposition of
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
is a decomposition
G
-cover (as per
Definition

18.5
).

We can now almost apply
Corollary

18.19
, except the gap required there is slightly bigger than the gap in
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
. Identically to
Lemma

18.14
, this is an artificial problem, and can be resolved by shifting the elements of
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
, i.e., defining
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}^{\prime}(m)=\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m+G)
, which increases the bound by the necessary
4G
.

We can therefore invoke
Corollary

18.19
to conclude that the potential is unbounded, and we are done.
∎

In order to eliminate the dependency on bounded-decrease of charge, we observe that if for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
the charge upon reading
w_{3}
does not have
B
-bounded decrease, then we can apply
Lemma

13.6
with the word
u=w_{1}w_{2}x
and
\sigma\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
for an appropriate prefix
x\sigma
of
w_{3}
(upon which the charge decreases by
B
). We then get the the potential over
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
is unbounded.
Combining this with
Lemma

18.20
we conclude with the following.

Corollary 18.21
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. If there exists a discharging words sequences
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
that is
\ell
-sparse with gap
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
. Then either
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
or
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
has the infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

Similarly to
Corollary

18.15
for leveled words, we can start with a set of independent runs of size
1
, that vacuously has a large gap, and obtain our main tool of this section.

Corollary 18.22

(

Discharging Words to Unbounded Potential or
D
-Dip)
.

Consider a
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
sequence, then either
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
, or
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

19
On Potential and Type-1 Witnesses

In
Section

18
we discuss the behavior of the charge, and relate it to unbounded potential.
In this section, we complete the picture by relating unbounded potential to the existence of a type-1 witness.
In broad strokes, this section is very similar to
Section

18
, and we reuse its proof structures (as well as specific techniques).
However, there are many small differences that present new complications, and are therefore treated differently. We emphasize them as they occur.
Due to the similarities, we reuse some terms from
Section

18
, but add a “
\upphi
” symbol to them, to emphasize their connection to potential.

Fix a finite alphabet
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{0}_{\infty}
– that is, without rebase letters (and also without jump letters). In addition, we require that
\Gamma_{0}^{0}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
(i.e.,
\Gamma^{\prime}
contains the original letters of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
).
Let
W_{\max}
be the maximal weight appearing in a transition over
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

19.1
A Condition for Type-1 Witness

In this section we introduce a notion of fair decompositions with respect to potential, and use it to establish a condition for the existence of a type-1 witness, analogously to
Section

18.1
.
Our starting point remains elongated words sequence (
Definition

16.9
) and its related definitions (e.g., sparsity, independent runs, gaps, etc.).

Our first notion is an analogue of decreasing-gap fair decompositions (
Definition

18.7
). In this case, however, the potential is increasing (with respect to some run). Note that unlike charge, the potential is generally not attained by the minimal run, and therefore the intuition of “gap” somewhat loses its meaning. Indeed, this later causes some case analysis that is not present in the charge setting.

Definition 19.1

(

Increasing-Potential Fair Decomposition)
.

Consider a decomposed word sequence

words
and assume there exists a function
b:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}b(n)=\infty
such that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, there is some seamless run
\rho
on
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
on such that
\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3})-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}))>\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})+b(m)-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w_{1}w_{2}))
.

We say that the decomposition is
increasing-potential fair
(with respect to
\rho
and
b(n)
) if there is a function
\widetilde{b}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widetilde{b}(n)=\infty
such that the decomposition
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)}
satisfies for every
1\leq i<d(m)
that

\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})+\widetilde{b}(m)-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}))\leq\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i}))

(there is no requirement on the last “remainder”segment).

We prove an analogue of
Lemma

18.8
, this time showing that an increasing-potential fair decomposition (with enough “nice” properties) implies the existence of a type-1 witness.

Lemma 19.2

(

Increasing Potential Decomposition to Witness)
.

Assume there exists
G,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and a decomposed word sequence
words
that is
\ell
-sparse with gap

4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
and a ghost-free decomposition
G
-cover.

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
disable,inline,color=red!25,size=

ACCOUNTING: ACCOUNTING

In addition, assume the decomposition is increasing-potential fair with respect to a seamless run
\rho
and function
b:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
.
Then
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness.

Proof.

Let
G,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
words
be as in the premise of the lemma, and
b,\widetilde{b}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\rho
as per
Definition

18.7
.
Let
d:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be the decomposition length function, i.e., for
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\textsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
we have
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)}
.

Take
m\in\mathbb{N}
large enough so that
d(m)>|\mathsf{DIFF\_TYPES}_{s_{0},G}|+1
and
\widetilde{b}(m)>4G
. Since both
d
and
\widetilde{b}
are increasing, this is possible.
Consider the word
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}=\textsf{words}(m)
and let

w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)}
be its decreasing-gap fair decomposition.

By the pigeonhole principle, there exist
0\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<d(m)
such that

\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i_{1}})=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i_{2}})

(Observe that we take
d(m)
large enough so that we can ignore the last segment, which is a remainder).

Denote

\widehat{x_{0}}=w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i_{1}}
(the reason for the hat is that we modify
\widehat{x_{0}}
to obtain a “simpler”
x_{0}
below),
x=u_{i_{1}+1}\cdots u_{i_{2}}
and
x_{1}=u_{i_{2}+1}\cdots u_{d(m)}w_{4}
.

Step 1: flatten the prefix before
x
.

Recall that
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
, i.e., has no rebase letters. It follows that the letters in
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
are either in
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
or are (nested) cacti. Let
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(\widehat{x_{0}}x)}
, we define
x_{0}=\mathsf{flatten}(\widehat{x_{0}}\wr F)
.
By
Definition

12.23
, it follows that in order to obtain
x_{0}
, we only apply the cactus unfolding of
Definition

11.12
. This implies that no jump letters are introduced in
x_{0}
(indeed, those are introduced only in the case of rebase removal). Thus,
x_{0}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
.
In the following we wish to reason about
x_{0}xx_{1}
instead of
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
.
However, the main difference between the words is that due to the flattening, we now have
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},x_{0})=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
.
Consider the configurations
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{0}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},\widehat{x_{0}})
and
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},x_{0})
, and similarly
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{x}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},\widehat{x_{0}}x)
and
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},x_{0}x)
.
By
Lemma

12.24
for every
s,s^{\prime}\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
we have
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{0}}(s)-\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{0}}(s^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(s)-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(s^{\prime})
. Additionally, for
r\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
and
r\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
we have

\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(r)-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(s)\geq 2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(\widehat{x_{0}}x)}

ACCOUNTING: ACCOUNTING

and in particular ghost states attain higher values than states reachable by
\widehat{x_{0}}
. In the following, we only use the fact that these are higher, and not the concrete gap size.

We claim that for every prefix
x^{\prime}
of
x
we have that
\upphi(\widehat{x_{0}}x^{\prime})=\upphi(x_{0}x^{\prime})
(and in particular the potential is defined in both).
Indeed, since flattening in our case (of
\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
) amounts to repeated unfolding, then by
Lemma

14.4
either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness (and in particular a type-1 witness), in which case we are done, or
\upphi(\widehat{x_{0}}x^{\prime})=\upphi(x_{0}x^{\prime})
.

Step 2: baseline shift to sharpest decrease

In this step, we use the fact that the potential is increasing with respect to
\rho
upon reading
x
, and we shift the runs so that the potential becomes increasing in value (i.e., with respect to the baseline). Moreover, our shifting ensures that all runs are non-decreasing, which is used in Step 3.

Define
\rho_{\ssearrow}
to be the seamless run that has the sharpest decrease on
x
. Specifically,
\rho_{\ssearrow}
is the seamless run that minimizes
\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}))
among all seamless runs
\eta
on
x_{0}x
.
If there are several such runs, we arbitrarily choose one.

Recall that the increasing-potential assumption with respect to
\rho
means that
\rho
is decreasing relatively to the potential. Therefore,
\rho_{\ssearrow}
is also decreasing relatively to the potential, as it has the sharpest decrease among all runs.

We now perform a baseline shift of
x_{0}x
on
\rho_{\ssearrow}
, identically to
Lemma

18.8
.
Since
\rho_{\ssearrow}
has the sharpest decrease on
x
, and since shifts maintain the gaps between runs (
Corollary

12.14
), we have that all the shifted runs are non-decreasing. That is, for every run
\eta
on
\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}x}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}]
it holds that

\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}](x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}](x_{0}))\geq 0

(19.1)

Indeed, by
Corollary

12.13
we now have that
\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho_{\ssearrow}}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}]
is the baseline run (and in particular remains at weight
0
). Therefore, by
Corollary

12.14
we have

\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}x))=\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}](x_{0}x))-0

and similarly

\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}))=\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}](x_{0}))-0

By subtracting the equations we get

\begin{split}&\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}](x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\eta}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}](x_{0}))=\\
&\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}))+\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}))=\\
&\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}))-(\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0})))\geq 0\end{split}

Where the last inequality is because
\rho_{\ssearrow}
minimizes
\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\eta(x_{0}))
by definition.

Moreover, since the potential is also shifted (see
Proposition

13.8
), we have in particular that for every prefix
x^{\prime}
of
x
it holds that
\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}x^{\prime}}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}])=\upphi(x_{0}x^{\prime})-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}x^{\prime}))
.
Recall that by the increasing-potential fair decomposition and by our choice of
\widetilde{b}(m)>4G
we have

\upphi(x_{0}x)-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}x))>\upphi(x_{0})-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}))+4G
, and by rearranging:
\upphi(x_{0}x)-\upphi(x_{0})>\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}))+4G
.
Since
\rho_{\ssearrow}
has the sharpest decrease, then by the gap-preservation of baseline shift (
Corollary

12.14
) and by the above we have

\begin{split}&\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}x}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}])-\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}])=\\
&\upphi(x_{0}x)-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}x))-\upphi(x_{0})+\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}))>\\
&\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}x))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{0}))+4G-\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}x))+\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow}(x_{0}))\geq 4G\end{split}

where the last inequality is obtained by plugging in
\eta=\rho
in the inequalities above.
We conclude that

\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}x}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}])-\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho(x_{0}x)}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}])>\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}])-\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho(x_{0})}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}])+4G

That is,
\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}x}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}]
maintains the property that its potential increases upon reading
x
from
x_{0}
by at least
4G
with respect to
\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}]
.

Finally, note that since
\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}]
is non-decreasing (as all the shifted runs are), then in particular
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho(x_{0}x)}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}])-\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho(x_{0})}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}])\geq 0
, and therefore by the inequality above we conclude that the potential increases (a lot) upon reading
\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}x}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}]
.

\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}x}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}])>\upphi(\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{0}}/{\rho_{\ssearrow}}])+4G

(19.2)

A crucial detail to notice at this point is that since
x
may contain cactus letters, then its baseline shift may contain
rebase letters
(as per
Section

12.2
). Thus, the alphabet after the shift is
\Gamma_{\infty}^{1}
, which is why the result of this section is a type-1 witness, rather than type-0.

To avoid the cumbersome notation of baseline shifts in the following, we reuse the names
x_{0},x
,
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, and the runs
\rho_{\ssearrow}
and
\rho
for their baseline-shifted counterpart. This is sound, since the potential property is maintained, as we show above, and
the properties of being
\ell
-sparse with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
and being a ghost-free decomposition
G
-cover are retained by the preservation of run structure as per
Corollary

12.14
.

Step 3:
x
induces a stable cycle.

We remark that this step is identical, word-for-word, with the same step in
Lemma

18.8
. The reason for the duplication is that we utilize the details of the proof in Step 4 (in particular, the partition to
V_{j}
sub-configurations).

Since all the runs on
x
are nonnegative (
Eq.

19.1
) and
x
yields a repetition of configurations, we gain the intuition that
x
should induce a stable cycle. We show this is indeed the case. The difficult part is to show that there are no negative cycles induced by
x^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The proof relies on the fact that there are large gaps between the runs, and that the runs are a
G
-cover. Broadly, this is a similar proof to
Proposition

16.23
.

Let
S^{\prime}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},x_{0})
, we claim that
(S^{\prime},x)
is a stable cycle. Let
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},x_{0})
as before and
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},x_{0}x)
.
Since
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(\widehat{x_{0}})=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(\widehat{x_{0}}\cdot x)
, it follows that
S^{\prime}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},x_{0}\cdot x)
. Indeed, we have
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},x_{0})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{0}})=\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{x}})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},x_{0}\cdot x)
, and by
Section

11.4
this also implies the equivalent ghost states. Thus, we have in particular that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(S^{\prime},x)\subseteq S^{\prime}
, so
(S^{\prime},x)
is a reflexive cycle.

The next step (as per
Definition

10.5
) is to show that for the baseline state
s\in S^{\prime}
we have that
s\in\textsc{MinStates}(S^{\prime},x^{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and that
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{n},s\to s)=0
.
Recall that after the baseline shift, we have that
\rho_{\ssearrow}
is a baseline run on
x
and has weight
0
, in particular
\mathsf{wt}(\rho_{\ssearrow})=0
, and therefore
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{n}_{\ssearrow})=0
, so
x^{n}
has a cycle of weight
0
.

It remains to show that there are no negative cycles in
(S^{\prime},x^{n})
for any
n
. That is, for every
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
, we want to show that
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{n},s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime})\geq 0
.
We start with an overview of how this is proved.
Recall that our baseline shift operation ensures that
\rho_{\ssearrow}
has weight
0
, and
\rho_{\ssearrow}
before the rebase has the strongest decrease, so all seamless runs after rebase are non-negative (on
x
).
This, however, does not immediately extend to
x^{n}
. Indeed, it could conceptually be that there are e.g., (non-seamless) negative runs
r_{1}\xrightarrow{x}r_{2}
and
r_{2}\xrightarrow{x}r_{1}
, but then their concatenation yields a negative seamless cycle on
x^{2}
.

We therefore take an approach similar to the proof of
Lemma

16.15
. Specifically, we notice that the equivalence of
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}
before and after reading
x
implies that we can partition these configurations to ordered sub-configurations
V_{1},\cdots V_{k}
such that all the “interesting” transitions are from
V_{i}
are to
V_{i}
. We then show that after reading
x
, the actual values of the elements in the configurations of each
V_{i}
does not decrease (due to
\rho_{\ssearrow}
being the baseline). This allows us to conclude there are no negative cycles.
For the ghost-states, we use the ghost-freeness assumption to conclude there cannot be negative cycles on them.
We proceed with the formal details.

We remind of the following notations of Step 1.
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{0}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
and
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},x_{0})
. Also denote
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}_{x}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}}x)
and
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},x_{0}x)
.
Strictly speaking, note that in Step 1 these configurations are defined before our baseline shift of Step 2. However, the gaps are maintained by the shift, so this overloading is justified.

Since
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(\widehat{x_{0}})=\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(\widehat{x_{0}}x)
, and by the preservation of the reachable set, we have that for every
s\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}}x)
that
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(s)=\mathsf{mwt}(x_{0},s_{0}\to s)
and
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(s)=\mathsf{mwt}(x_{0}x,s_{0}\to s)
.
Put simply – the configurations correctly track the weights of all states reachable by
\widehat{x_{0}}
(we deal later with the remaining states, namely
S^{\prime}\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
).

We partition
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
as
V_{1}\cup\ldots\cup V_{k}
according to
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}(\widehat{x_{0}})
, similarly to the proof of
Lemma

16.15
. Specifically, we write
p\equiv q
if
|\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(p)-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q)|\leq 2G
. Since
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
is a
G
-cover, this is an equivalence relation. We then define
V_{1},\ldots,V_{k}
to be the equivalence classes of
\sim
, ordered by
\leq_{\boldsymbol{c_{0}}}
, i.e., all the states in
V_{1}
are lower than those of
V_{2}
, etc.
By the equivalence of
\mathsf{diff\_type}
, we have that the partition induces by
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}
is the same as that of
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}
.

Still similarly to
Lemma

16.15
, since
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
is
\ell
-sparse with a huge gap, we have that if
p\in V_{i}
,
q\in V_{j}
and there is a run
\eta:p\xrightarrow{x}q
, then
i\geq j
. That is, there are no runs that go “up” between the sets (as the gap is to large to be bridged by
x
, so this would “drag down”
q
).
Moreover, if
q\in V_{j}
there exists some
p\in V_{j}
such that
p\xrightarrow{x}q
, i.e., there exist a predecessor of
q
in
V_{j}
(otherwise all predecessors of
q
come from higher sets, so
q
would be higher). Additionally, there must exist such
p\in V_{j}
such that
\eta:p\xrightarrow{x}q
is seamless (indeed, runs from higher sets to
q
cannot be seamless).

We claim that for every
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
it holds that
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q)\leq\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q)
. Intuitively,
x
only induces increasing behaviors.
To show this, let
1\leq i\leq k
such that
q\in V_{i}
. Let
q^{0}_{i}
be the minimal state in
V_{i}
(i.e.,
q^{0}_{i}\in\arg\min\{\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q)\mid q\in V_{i}\}
).

Let
p^{0}_{i}
be a state in
V_{i}
such that there exists a seamless run
\eta:p^{0}_{i}\xrightarrow{x}q^{0}_{i}
. Recall that our baseline shift ensures there are no seamless negative runs. It follows that
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q^{0}_{i})=\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(p^{0}_{i})+\mathsf{wt}(\eta)\geq\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(p^{0}_{i})\geq\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q^{0}_{i})
, where the last inequality is because we know that
q^{0}_{i}
is the minimal in
V_{i}
.
Therefore, the claim holds for
q^{0}_{i}
.
For every other state
q\in V_{i}
we have that
q\sim q^{0}_{i}
, and therefore
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q^{0}_{i})=\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q^{0}_{i})
, so
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q)-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q)=\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q^{0}_{i})-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q^{0}_{i})\geq 0
.

Notice that the argument above only assumes that the gaps are larger than
|x|
. Recall, however, that we originally start with gaps larger than
(|S|+1)|w_{3}|\geq(|S|+1)|x|
(this includes the gap from the ghost states, which is even larger). It therefore follows that we can repeat this argument for up to
|S|
times, and obtain that the weights of each state is increased after reading
x^{n}
for any
n\leq|S|
.

Now, assume by way of contradiction that there is a negative cycle
\eta:q\xrightarrow{x^{n}}q
such that
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We first notice that we can assume
n\leq|S|
(otherwise we can find an inner
x^{n^{\prime}}
-cycle that is either itself a negative cycle, or is positive and we can shorten the original cycle). This, however, implies that
\mathsf{mwt}(x_{0},s_{0}\to q)>\mathsf{mwt}(x_{0}x^{n},s_{0}\to q)
, which is a contradiction to our observation above.

It remains to show that there are no negative cycles stemming from ghost-states.
In fact, we prove that there are no cycles (on
x^{n}
) on ghost states at all, negative or not.
This follows from ghost-freeness assumed in the lemma, as follows.
Assume by way of contradiction that there exists
g\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
and
\eta:g\xrightarrow{x^{n}}g
. We decompose
\eta
as
\eta:g\xrightarrow{x}g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{n-1}}g
according to the state reached after
x
.
Since
x
spans at least one
u_{i}
segment by definition, it follows from ghost-freeness (
Definition

18.6
) that
\rho
is not seamless already before
g^{\prime}
, i.e., we can write
x=y_{1}y_{2}
such that
\eta:g\xrightarrow{y_{1}}h\xrightarrow{y_{2}}g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{n-1}}g
and we can assume
h\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}}y_{1})
.

There is a slightly delicate point here: it could be that
\eta
is not seamless but that the run that “undercuts” it is also from a ghost-state. However, we can then repeat this argument until eventually reaching a “minimal” ghost run, after which the undercutting run indeed uses a state in
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}}y_{1})
.

Since
\eta
is a run, and in particular has finite weight, it follows that its suffix
\eta^{\prime}:h\xrightarrow{y_{2}}g^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{n-1}}g
is also finite weight, and since
h\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}}y_{1})
, we have that
g\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}}x^{n})
, but
x
preserves the set of reachable states (by the equivalence of
\mathsf{diff\_type}
), so it follows that
g\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
, in contradiction to the assumption that
g\notin\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},\widehat{x_{0}})
.

We conclude that
(S^{\prime},x)
is a stable cycle, and so
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
is a cactus letter.

Step 4: obtaining a type-
1
witness.

Continuing with the notations of Step 3, consider a maximal-dominant state
q_{x}
of
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},x_{0}x)
, and let
z
be a corresponding suffix such that

\mathsf{mwt}(z,q_{x}\to S)<\infty
and for every
q^{\prime}
with
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q^{\prime})<\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q)
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(z,q^{\prime}\to S)=\infty
.
Assume
q_{x}\in V_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq k
.
Consider also a maximal-dominant state
q_{0}
of
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},x_{0})
.

Intuitively, this step proceeds as follows. We first show that we can assume
q_{0}=q_{x}
, and in particular
q_{0}\in V_{i}
as well. That is, the potential before reading
x
stems from the same
V_{i}
sub-configuration as the potential after
x
. In fact, we show that the same suffix
z
works for
q_{0}
.
Then, since the potential increases on
x
(by Step 1), we conclude that the sub-configuration corresponding to
V_{i}
also increases. We use this to show by a pumping argument that all the runs on
x
from
V_{i}
to
V_{i}
are between non-grounded pairs of
(S^{\prime},x)
.
This means that after replacing
x
with
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
, the configuration
V_{i}
no longer yields runs to
V_{i}
, only to “lower”
V_{j}
. We then consider the suffix
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}z
from
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}
, and show that with this suffix, all the states up to and including
V_{i}
attain weight
\infty
.
Finally, this shows that either
q_{0}
is not maximal-dominating (which is a contradiction) or all states go to
\infty
, meaning that
(x_{0},x,z)
is a (type-1) witness.
We now proceed with the formal details.

We start by claiming that we can assume
q_{0}=q_{x}
.
That is, we show that
q_{x}
is maximal-dominant in
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}
.
We already have that
\mathsf{mwt}(z,q_{x}\to S)<\infty
. Consider
q^{\prime}
with
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q^{\prime})<\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q_{x})
. Since
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}
and
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}
have the same
\mathsf{diff\_type}_{s_{0},G}
, then
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q^{\prime})<\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q_{x})
, and therefore we also have
\mathsf{mwt}(z,q^{\prime}\to S)=\infty
. Thus,
q_{x}
is dominant in
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}
. To show it is also maximal dominant, we repeat the same argument only going from
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}
to
\boldsymbol{x}
: assume
q^{\prime\prime}
satisfies
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q^{\prime\prime})>\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(q_{x})
, but
q^{\prime\prime}
is dominant with suffix
z^{\prime\prime}
. Then an identical argument shows that
q^{\prime\prime}
is dominant in
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}
, and by the order preservation, it is also higher than
q_{x}
in
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}
, contradicting the fact that
q_{x}
is maximal dominant.

Recall by
Eq.

19.2
that (under the baseline-shifted notations for
x_{0},x
) we have
\upphi(x_{0}x)>\upphi(x_{0})+4G
. Specifically, let
P=\upphi(x_{0}x)-\upphi(x_{0})
, then
P>4G
and
\boldsymbol{c_{x}}(q_{x})=\upphi(c_{0})(q_{x})+P
. We now intuitively show that pumping
x
must keep increasing the sub-configuration
V_{i}
, and therefore there are no grounded pairs from
V_{i}
to itself.
To show this, consider a minimal-weight run

\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x_{0}}p_{0}\xrightarrow{x}p_{1}\xrightarrow{x}\cdots\xrightarrow{x}p_{k-1}\xrightarrow{x}p_{k}
for
p_{k}\in V_{i}
on
x^{k}
with
k\leq 4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
.
From Step 3 we know that there are no transitions on
x
from
V_{j}
to
V_{i}
if
j<i
, and that for every state in
V_{i}
there is a transition from some state in
V_{i}
.
Since
\rho
is minimal-weight to
p_{k}
, it is in particular seamless. By the gap and cover criteria in the lemma, for every
p^{\prime}\in V_{j}
with
j>i
we have

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(p^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(p_{0})>4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|-2G=\\
&2G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|>8{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|x|\geq 2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(x^{k})}\end{split}

Where the

and
V_{j}
, but the gap in the premise is between independent runs (which form a

-cover), and the last inequality is because

.

We now see that all the
p_{m}
are in
V_{i}
, otherwise there is a run from a higher

, but such a run cannot reach as low as
V_{i}
due to the gap.
More precisely, since

for every

(as there is a run through the
V_{i}
, and any run through higher

cannot be seamless due to the gap).

Assume by way of contradiction that there are

. By

\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{0}},x^{4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})(r)\leq 2G+\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{0}},x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})(r)

-cover, as it also starts from

).

\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{0}},x^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})(r)=\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(r)+2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}P

Plugging these into the inequality above, we have:

\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(r)+4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}P\leq 2G+\boldsymbol{c_{0}}(r)+2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}P
, so

, but

Intuitively, this is similar to the situation depicted in

We conclude that for every

we have

.
Now consider the suffix

from
\boldsymbol{c_{0}}
. For every

with

, we have that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(p,\alpha_{S^{\prime},x})\subseteq\bigcup_{j^{\prime}\leq j}V_{j^{\prime}}
, and since
z
has no finite-weight runs from any
V_{j^{\prime}}
lower than
V_{i}
, we have

\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}z,p\to S)=\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}z,p\xrightarrow{\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}}\bigcup_{j^{\prime}\leq j}V_{j}\xrightarrow{z}S)=\infty

Moreover, for every
q\in V_{i}
we have
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(q,\alpha_{S^{\prime},x})\in\bigcup_{j^{\prime}<i}V_{j^{\prime}}
(note the strict inequality). This is because there are no grounded pairs from
V_{i}
to itself, so all the transitions on
\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}
go to strictly lower
V_{j^{\prime}}
sets. In particular, we again have

\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}z,q\to S)=\infty
.

Recall that
q_{0}\in V_{i}
is maximal dominant. Therefore, if for some
q^{\prime\prime}\in V_{i^{\prime}}
for
i^{\prime}>i
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}z,q^{\prime\prime}\to S)<\infty
, this would contradict the maximal-dominance of
q_{0}
. We therefore conclude that for every
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{0}})
we have
\mathsf{mwt}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}z,q\to S)=\infty
.

We thus have the following setting:
\mathsf{mwt}(x_{0}\cdot x\cdot z,s_{0}\to S)<\infty
, and
\mathsf{mwt}(s_{0}\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}z,s_{0}\to S)=\infty
, thus fulfilling Requirement 4 of
Definition

14.1
, showing that
(x_{0},x,z)
is a witness (the other properties are immediate by the fact that
x
is a stable cycle and preserves the reachability set).

As mentioned in Step 2, due to the baseline shift we might have
x\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{1})^{*}
. Since
x_{0}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
due to the flattening in Step 1, we conclude that this is a type-1 witness.
∎

19.2
\upphi
-Leveled Words and
\upphi
-Discharging Words

In this section we present the potential analogue to leveled and discharging words, as well as the corresponding theorems of
Section

18.2
. Again, the overall structure is the same, but there are technical differences.

19.2.1
\upphi
-Leveled Words Sequences

An elongated words sequence
words
is
\upphi
-leveled if, intuitively, the potential along the
w_{3}
infix remains in some constant width “band”.

Definition 19.3

(
\kappa
-
\upphi
-Leveled Words Sequence)
.

For
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, a function from
\mathbb{N}
to
\Gamma^{\prime*}
is a
\kappa
-
\upphi
-leveled words sequence
, denoted
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}:\mathbb{N}\to\Gamma^{\prime*}
, if it is an elongated words sequence, and for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and prefix
u
of
w_{3}
it holds that

|\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u)|\leq\kappa
.

We say that a sequence is
\upphi
-leveled if it is
\kappa
-
\upphi
-leveled for some
\kappa
.

Proposition 19.4
.

Let
\ell,G\in\mathbb{N}
and consider a
pot
-leveled words sequence
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
that is
\ell
-sparse with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
and is a configuration
G
-cover. Further assume there exists
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
m
with
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, all ghost runs on
w_{3}
are of length at most
l
.
Then either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, or
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

The proof is nearly identical to that of
Proposition

18.13
, with only minor changes between charge and potential, and with eventually calling upon
Lemma

19.2
to obtain a type-1 witness.

If
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
, we are done. We therefore assume this is not the case. That is, by the converse of
Definition

18.9
, for every
D,n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
m
with
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
such that we can write
w_{3}=xvy
with
|v|>n
and there exists
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}x)
and a run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{x}S
with
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\leq-D
.

Our goal now is to obtain from
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
another
\upphi
-leveled sequence
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
that has an increasing-potential fair decomposition (
Definition

19.1
). Intuitively, we obtain the increasing potential using the decreasing runs on
v
above.
Note that despite the counterintuitive phrasing (increasing v.s. decreasing), this is still similar to the
\uppsi
case, as there also a decreasing charge essentially means an increasing minimal run.

We define
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
as follows. For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
m^{\prime}>m
such that for
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m^{\prime})=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
we can write
w_{3}=xvy
with
|v|>(|S|+1)m+l
(recall that
l
is the bound on the length of a ghost run) and there exists
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}x)
and a run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{v}S
with
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\leq-W_{\max}((|S|+1)m+l)
.
Since there are no ghost runs of length greater than
l
over
w_{3}
, it follows that
\rho
reaches a reachable state
q
within
l
steps from
p
. We then still have a run
\rho^{\prime}
over a suffix
v^{\prime}
of
v
such that
\rho^{\prime}:q\xrightarrow{v^{\prime}}S
with
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime})\leq-W_{\max}(|S|+1)m
. Indeed,
\rho
can lose at most
W_{\max}l
weight within
l
steps.
Recall that for increasing-potential fair decompositions we need a
seamless
run (
Definition

19.1
). Intuitively, the existence of a long-enough decreasing run clearly implies the existence of a decreasing seamless run, since a run cannot decrease without “dragging down” with it some seamless run.

Formally, if
\rho^{\prime}
has an infix of length at least
m
that is decreasing and seamless, we redefine
v^{\prime}
as that infix and we are done. Otherwise, decompose
\rho^{\prime}
into
|S|+1
segments by the first time
\rho^{\prime}
loses an additional
W_{\max}m
weight.
Since there are at most
|S|
seamless runs, it follows that
\rho^{\prime}
intersects the same seamless runs at least twice at the end of these segments, but then this seamless run is decreasing at least as much as
\rho^{\prime}
, so there is a seamless run decreasing by at least
W_{\max}m
, as required. We reuse
v^{\prime}
to denote the corresponding suffix, and write

v=v_{0}v^{\prime}
.

Define
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}(m)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1},w^{\prime}_{2}=w_{2}xv_{0},w^{\prime}_{3}=v^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=yw_{4}
.
Denote by
\kappa
the potential band of
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
.
Observe that
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
is a
2\kappa
-
\upphi
-leveled word sequence: the infixes
v^{\prime}
have increasing length, and since the words in
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
are equal as concatenations to words from
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
, then the bounded-charge property is preserved, with the modification that previously all potentials were within gap
\kappa
of
\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})
, whereas now it is possible that e.g.,
\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})=\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})-\kappa
, so the remaining charge is within distance at most
2\kappa
.
Moreover,
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
is
\ell
-sparse with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
and a configuration
G
-cover. Indeed, since we choose
v^{\prime}
that are shorter than their corresponding
|w_{3}|
, the gap requirement that is already met in
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
is even larger with respect to
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
, and the decomposition
G
-cover is implied by the configuration
G
-cover.

It remains to show that
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
has an increasing-potential fair decomposition.
Intuitively, this follows because the potential stays within a band, whereas
\rho
is decreasing, and therefore the difference of
\rho
from the potential must increase.
Formally, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
consider
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}(m)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
as above, where by construction there exists
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})
and a run
\rho:q\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}_{3}}S
with
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)\leq-W_{\max}m
and
|w^{\prime}_{3}|>m
.
We decompose
w^{\prime}_{3}=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}
as follows.
Let
d(m)
be the maximal number such that the following ordered indices are distinct:

0=i_{0}\leq i_{1}\leq\ldots\leq i_{d(m)}<|w_{3}|
where for every
1\leq k\leq d(m)
we have

i_{k}=\min\{j\mid\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}[1,i_{k-1}]))-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}[1,j]))\geq\sqrt{m}W_{\max}\}

We then set
u_{k}=w^{\prime}_{3}[i_{k-1}+1,i_{k}]
for all
1\leq k\leq d(m)
and
u_{d(m)+1}
the remaining suffix.

Observe that for every
1\leq k\leq d(m)
we now have by definition that

\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1})-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k}))\geq\sqrt{m}W_{\max}

or equivalently

-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1})+\sqrt{m}W_{\max}\leq-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k}))

Also, since the potential remains within a
2\kappa
band, we have that

\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1})-2\kappa\leq\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k})
. Thus, we have

\begin{split}&\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1})+\sqrt{m}W_{\max}-2\kappa-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1}))\leq\\
&\upphi(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k})-\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k}))\end{split}

Moreover, since with each letter the run
\rho
loses at most
W_{\max}
weight, then the number of segments in the decomposition is (roughly)
\sqrt{m}
, i.e.,
\lim_{m\to\infty}d(m)=\infty
.

Thus,
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
has an increasing-potential fair decomposition with
\widetilde{b}(m)=\sqrt{m}W_{\max}-2\kappa
.
We conclude that
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

19.2
, and therefore
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, and we are done.
∎

We are now ready for the main inductive argument.

Lemma 19.5
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and consider a
\upphi
-leveled words sequence
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
that is
\ell
-sparse with gap
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
. Then either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, or
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

The proof is by reverse induction on
\ell
, and is very similar to that of
Lemma

18.14
, with the exception of the fact that for flattening in the inductive step, we must use
Lemma

14.4
, which is less straightforward than the case of charge.

Base case:
\ell=|S|

Recall that the maximal number of configuration-independent runs is
|S|
(see
Definition

18.1
).
In this case, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
we have that in every configuration in
w_{3}
, each state belongs to one of the independent runs.
In particular,
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
is a configuration
G
-cover with
G=0
, and is also
\ell
-sparse by assumption, with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
(since
G=0
).

Moreover, since
\ell=|S|
, then in particular every state is reachable along any prefix of
w_{3}
, and therefore there are no ghost runs at all along
w_{3}
.
Thus, the conditions of
Proposition

19.4
are met, so either
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
has the infix
D
-dip property for some
D
, or
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, and we are done.

Inductive case:
\ell<|S|

The induction is split into three subcases.

Case 1: long ghost runs.

In this case we assume that there are unboundedly long ghost runs. Specifically, assume that for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
there is some
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and we can write
w_{3}=xuy
such that
|u|\geq l
and there exists
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}x)\setminus\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}x)
and a ghost run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{u}S
.
Intuitively, while this ghost run is not a real run on
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, it becomes a real run if we flatten the prefix up to it. Moreover, since flattening guarantees that ghost runs are very high, this implies the existence of another independent run, so we can apply the induction.

Formally, we define a new sequence
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
as follows. For every
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
let
m>m^{\prime}
be such that
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
has a ghost run as above with
|u|>m^{\prime}
. Define
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}(m^{\prime})=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with

w^{\prime}_{1}=\mathsf{flatten}(w_{1}w_{2}x\wr(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}|),w^{\prime}_{2}=\epsilon,w^{\prime}_{3}=y
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=yw_{4}
.
14
14

14

We remark that unlike
Section

16
, the roles of
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
here are combined, so it is fine selecting
w_{2}=\epsilon
and moving its content to
w_{1}
(or vice versa). We keep the four-part formalism for uniformity.

Similarly to
Lemma

19.2
, since our alphabet does not contain rebase letters, by
Definition

12.23
the resulting prefix
x^{\prime}_{1}
is over
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
.
Denote
\boldsymbol{c}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{init}},w_{1}w_{2}x)
and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{init}},w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})
, then by

Lemma

12.24

we have that
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q)
for every
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2})
, and

\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(p)\geq(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}|+\max\{\boldsymbol{c}(p^{\prime})\mid p^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})\}
.
Specifically, the entire run
\rho
from
p
is above all the existing
\ell
independent runs with gap at least
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
.
It follows that
\rho
can be added as an independent run. Thus,
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
has
\ell+1
independent runs.

In order to apply the induction we need to show that
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
remains a
\upphi
-leveled words sequence. This, however, might not be the case. Indeed, the flattening may have changed the potential. Fortunately, since our alphabet is
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
, then in particular it does not contain rebase or jump letters. This means that flattening amounts to repeated unfolding of cactus letters (
Definitions

12.15
and
12.23
). Thus, we can apply
Lemma

14.4
and get that either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness (and in particular a type-1 witness), or we have that the potential remains unchanged after flattening, so
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
remains a
\upphi
-leveled words sequence.
We can therefore apply the induction hypothesis on
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
. By the hypothesis, if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, then we are done.

Otherwise, we get that
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
has the
D
-dip property. Recall, however, that we need to show
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
has the
D
-dip property. Fortunately, observe that
D
-dip is “local” in that it only considers runs over the relevant infix, and since the
D
-dip infixes of
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
also appear in
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
, so we can indeed show this as follows.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider
m^{\prime}
such that
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m^{\prime})=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
has an infix
u
with
w^{\prime}_{3}=xuy
with
|u|\geq n
and for every
