by the flattening procedure.
It follows that the same condition on
u
holds also in
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
. Therefore,
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
also has the infix
D
-dip property, and we are done.

In the following cases, we therefore assume there is a bound
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that all ghost runs on infixes of any
w_{3}
are of length at most
l
.

Case 2: a diverging run on a suffix of
w_{3}
.

In this case we essentially assume that the independent runs are not a
G
-cover for any
G
, and use this to construct a new independent run from states that stay far away from all independent runs. We then apply the induction hypothesis. This is very similar to the setting in
Fig.

25
, where instead of starting from a single state, we start from a configuration. Specifically, we assume the divergence occurs in some long-enough prefix of
w_{3}
.

Formally, assume that for every
G\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m>2G
with
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and we can write
w_{3}=uv
with
|u|\geq\frac{1}{2}|w_{3}|
such that for
\boldsymbol{c}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}u)
there exists a state
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c})
for which
\boldsymbol{c}(q)
is not withing gap
G
of any independent run.

We define a new sequence
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
as follows. For every
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, take some
G>2W_{\max}m^{\prime}+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}m^{\prime}

and let
m>2m^{\prime}
large enough such that the gap of
q
as above is at least
G
.
Write
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
with
w_{3}=uv
and
q
as above.
We can now use the fact that all seamless runs change their weight by at most
W_{\max}
at each step. Consider the seamless run
\rho
leading up to
q
, and write
u=u_{1}u_{2}
with
|u_{2}|=m^{\prime}
(this is possible since
|u|\geq\frac{1}{2}|w_{3}|\geq\frac{1}{2}m>m^{\prime}
).
Since
q
is not within gap
G
of any independent run, for every prefix
u^{\prime}
of
u_{2}
it holds that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}u^{\prime}))
is not within gap
G-2W_{\max}m^{\prime}>(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}m^{\prime}
of any independent run.

We can therefore define
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m^{\prime})=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
by
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1},w^{\prime}_{2}=w_{2}u_{1},w^{\prime}_{3}=u_{2},w^{\prime}_{4}=vw_{4}
. As above, this is a
\upphi
-leveled words sequence, since the potential requirement remains valid on infixes.
Moreover, we now have
\ell+1
independent runs satisfying the gap constraints. We can therefore apply the induction hypothesis. As in Case 1, if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness then we are done, and if
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
has the infix
D
-dip property, this lifts back to
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
(here we do not even have to consider the flattening).

Case 3:
G
-cover on suffix of
w_{3}
.

We now consider the complement of Case 2, still under the assumption that all ghost runs are of length at most
l
.
Specifically, we assume that there exists
G\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
m>2G
with
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, for every decomposition
w_{3}=uv
with
|u|\geq\frac{1}{2}|w_{3}|
and for every state
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}u)
, the weight of
q
is within gap
G
of some independent run. We therefore have a
G
-cover of the “second half” of
w_{3}
.
Our goal is now to apply
Proposition

19.4
. For this, we only need to define a sequence where the
w_{3}
infix corresponds to these “second halves”. A slight caveat is that
Proposition

19.4
requires a larger gap than
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
has. This, however, is an “artificial” problem: the gaps in
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
grow with
m
, so we just need to pick words that are further away, so we have larger gaps.

Formally, we proceed as follows. For every
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, let
m=2(m^{\prime}+G)
with
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and write
w_{3}=uv
with
|v|=m^{\prime}
(in particular
|u|\geq\frac{1}{2}|w_{3}|
). Define
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(m^{\prime})=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
by
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1},w^{\prime}_{2}=w_{2}u,w^{\prime}_{3}=v,w^{\prime}_{4}=w_{4}
. We observe that
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
is still a leveled words sequence, since the potential constraints do not change. We claim that
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
is
\ell
-sparse with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
. Indeed, recalling that
m=2(m^{\prime}+G)
, the gap guaranteed by
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
is

\begin{split}&(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|\geq(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}2(m^{\prime}+G)\geq\\
&4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}m^{\prime}=4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w^{\prime}_{3}|\end{split}

Finally,
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
has a configuration
G
-cover, as we discuss above.

We can therefore apply
Proposition

19.4
to
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
and we conclude that either
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
has the infix
D
-dip property, or
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness. Again, the infix
D
-dip property lifts back to
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
, so we are done.
Note that the last case is not inductive, but direct (indeed, it can be thought of as a different base case).
∎

As a potential-counterpart to
Corollary

18.15
for leveled words, we have the main tool for
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
(by applying
Lemma

19.5
with
\ell=1
, for which the gap is unbounded).

Corollary 19.6
.

Consider a
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
sequence, then either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, or
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

19.2.2
\upphi
-Discharging Words Sequences

A counterpart to
\upphi
-leveled words are
\upphi
-discharging words
, where the potential significantly increases. We remark that while the naming scheme may seem confusing (i.e., “discharging” is used for “increasing potential”), recall that in the charge case, a decreasing charge amounts to an increasing minimal run (intuitively), so in this respect the scenarios are similar. We therefore keep the name, to maintain the analogy.

Definition 19.7

(
\upphi
-Discharging Words Sequence)
.

An elongated words sequence
words
is a
\upphi
-discharging words sequence
, denoted
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}:\mathbb{N}\to\Gamma^{\prime*}
, if for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
the following hold:

•

\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3})-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2})>m
(the potential significantly increases upon reading
w_{3}
).

•

For every prefix
v
of
w_{3}
we have
\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}v)\leq\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3})
(the potential does not exceed its level on
w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}
).

Similarly to the discharging words setting (
Section

18.2.2
), we introduce a specialized notion of decomposition for
\upphi
-discharging words, whereby in each segment the potential increases.
Recall that for the charge setting, when using the decomposition in
Definition

18.17
, we encounter the problem that the charge may have unbounded “jumps”. To overcome this, we resort there to assuming the bounded charge decrease property of
Definition

13.5
.

Fortunately, in this section things are simpler: the potential always has bounded increase, as per
Lemma

13.3
. This means that we get simplified assumptions when reasoning about
\upphi
-discharging decompositions, as follows.

Definition 19.8

(
\upphi
-Discharging Decomposition)
.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
, let
d(m)
be the maximal number such that the following ordered indices are distinct:

0=i_{0}\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\ldots\leq i_{d(m)}<|w_{3}|
where for every
1\leq k\leq d(m)
we have

i_{k}=\min\{j\mid\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}[1,j])-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}[1,i_{k-1}])>\sqrt{m}\}
.

The
discharging decomposition
of
w_{3}
is then
w_{3}=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}
where
u_{k}=w_{3}[i_{k-1}+1,i_{k}]
for every
1\leq k\leq d(m)
, and
u_{d(m)+1}=w_{3}[i_{d(m)+1},|w_{3}|]
.

For every
1\leq k\leq d(m)
we then have:

•

\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k})-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k-1})>\sqrt{m}
.

•

For every prefix
v
of
u_{1}\cdots u_{k}
it holds that

\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}v)\leq\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{k})

As mentioned above, due to the bounded growth of the potential, we readily have that the
\upphi
-discharging decomposition is an increasing potential decomposition, as follows.

Proposition 19.9
.

Let
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
be a
\upphi
-discharging sequence, then its
\upphi
-discharging decomposition is an increasing-potential fair decomposition (as per
Definition

19.1
).

Proof.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
. Write
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}
.

We claim that the
\upphi
-discharging decomposition is an increasing-potential fair decomposition with respect to the baseline run
\rho_{0}
. Note that the baseline run has weight
0
in all prefixes. Therefore, we need to show that the length of the
u_{i}
increases with
m
, that
d(m)
increases with
m
, and that
\upphi
increases by some function
\widetilde{b}(m)
between each segment (as per
Definition

19.1
, instantiated with
\rho_{0}
) except for the last “remainder” segment.

The latter requirement is trivial by the
Definition

19.8
– the segments are defined such that

\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})>\sqrt{m}

i.e, their potential increase is at least
\sqrt{m}
.

By
Lemma

13.3
, the potential can grow by at most
B
upon reading a letter, for some constant
B
(that depends on the finite alphabet
\Gamma^{\prime}
).
We then inductively have that for every segment
u_{i}
(indeed, every infix of
w_{3}
) it holds that

\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})<|u_{i}|B

Combining this with the potential difference above, we have that
|u_{i}|>\sqrt{m}/B
, and in particular the length of
u_{i}
increases with
m
.

We turn to show that
d(m)
increases. We initially claim that

\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})\leq\sqrt{m}+B

Indeed write
u_{i}=v\cdot\sigma
where
\sigma
is the last letter, then

\begin{split}&\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v\sigma)-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v)=\\
&\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v\sigma)-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v)+\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v)-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})\leq\\
&\sqrt{m}+B\end{split}

Where the last inequality follows from
Lemma

13.3
and from the fact that
u_{i}=v\sigma
is minimal with the gap property in
Definition

19.8
(i.e.,
\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v)-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdot u_{i-1})\leq\sqrt{m}
otherwise
v
would have been selected instead of
u_{i}
).

We notice that for segment
u_{d(m)+1}
we do not provide bounds, as it is essentially a “remainder”.
Trivially, we have
\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)+1})-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)})\leq\sqrt{m}
(otherwise we would have another segment).

By the definition of
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
we now have that

\sum_{i=1}^{d(m)+1}(\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}))=\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3})-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}w_{3})\geq m

and on the other hand by the above we have

\sum_{i=1}^{d(m)+1}(\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}))\leq d(m)(\sqrt{m}+B)+\sqrt{m}

We therefore have

d(m)\geq\frac{m-\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{m}+B}

so
\lim_{m\to\infty}d(m)=\infty
, as required.
∎

Proposition

19.9
allows us to weaken the conditions in
Lemma

19.2
to require only that the sequence is
\upphi
-discharging, since this now implies an increasing-potential fair decomposition. Thus, we have the following.

Corollary 19.10
.

If there exists
G,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and a
\upphi
-discharging word sequence
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
whose discharging decomposition is a ghost-free decomposition
G
-cover and is
\ell
-sparse with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
,

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
disable,inline,color=red!25,size=
todo:

disable,inline,color=red!25,size=

ACCOUNTING: ACCOUNTING

then
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness.

Our main result of the section is again an inductive lemma analogous to
Lemma

18.20
, guaranteeing either a
D
-dip or a type-1 witness, given a
\upphi
-discharging words sequence.

Lemma 19.11

(

Potential Discharge and Gap to Witness or
D
-Dip)
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and consider a
\upphi
-discharging words sequence
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
that is
\ell
-sparse with gap
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
.
Then
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, or
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
has the infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by reverse induction on
\ell
. Again, the proof is nearly identical to that of
Lemma

18.20
, but has some tweaks where potential differs from charge (in particular in Case 2, which requires flattening).

Base case:
\ell=|S|

Recall that the maximal number of configuration-independent runs is
|S|
(see
Definition

18.1
).
In this case, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
we have that in every configuration in
w_{3}
, each state belongs to one of the independent runs.
In particular,
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
is a configuration
G
-cover with
G=0
, and is also
\ell
-sparse by assumption, with gap
4G+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
(since
G=0
).

Moreover, since
\ell=|S|
, then in particular every state is reachable along any prefix of
w_{3}
, and therefore there are no ghost runs at all along
w_{3}
.
Thus, the conditions of
Corollary

19.10
are met,
so
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness.

Inductive case:
\ell<|S|

For the inductive case, we start by decomposing
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
to its discharging decomposition (
Definition

19.8
)
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)}u_{d(m)+1}
, and by
Proposition

19.9
this decomposition is an increasing-potential fair decomposition.
We now split to three subcases.

Case 1: a diverging run on a segment

In this case we essentially assume that the independent runs are not a decomposition
G
-cover for any
G
, and use this to construct a new independent run from states that stay far away from all independent runs. We then apply the induction hypothesis.

This starts similar to the analogous case in
Lemma

19.5
, but differs in that the potential constraints are not necessarily met, and requires further case analysis.

Formally, assume that for every
G\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m>2G
with
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)}u_{d(m)+1}
such that there exist
1\leq i\leq d(m)
where for

\boldsymbol{c_{i}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})
there is a state
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{i}})
for which
\boldsymbol{c_{i}}(q)
is not withing gap
G
of any independent run.
That is, we assume that
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)
does not have a decomposition
G
-cover (
Definition

18.5
).

By the assumption, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
1\leq i\leq d(m)
and
q
above it holds that
q
is not within gap
2W_{\max}n+(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}n
of any independent run, and
|u_{i}|>n
.
In particular, there is a seamless runs
\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i}}q
such that for every split
u_{i}=v_{0}v
with
|v|\leq n
we have that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0}))
is not within gap
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}n
of any independent run.

With these notations, we divide into cases, depending on the behavior of
\upphi
on
u_{i}
.

Case 1a: increasing potential

In this case, we assume that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m>n
and
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
with
u_{i}
,
q
and
\rho
as above such that there is a split
u_{i}=v_{0}v
with

\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0}v)-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0})>n
.
That is, there is a suffix
v
of
w_{3}
on which the potential increases significantly. Note the similarity to the condition of a
\upphi
-discharging words sequence (
Definition

19.7
).

We therefore construct a
\upphi
-discharging words sequence
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
by defining
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}(n)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1},w^{\prime}_{2}=w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0},w^{\prime}_{3}=v
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=u_{i+1}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}w_{4}
.

Since
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
is already a
\upphi
-discharging words sequence, and
u_{i}
is a segment in the decomposition, then by the minimality criterion of
Definition

19.8
, we have that the charge along
u_{i}
does not exceed
\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})
. Therefore, combined with the condition on
v
above, we have that
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
satisfies both the conditions of
Definition

19.7
, so it is a discharging words sequence.

Moreover, as we observed above, the run
\rho
on the suffix
v
can be added as an independent run with the gap required in the induction assumption. Note that the remaining runs certainly have large enough gaps, since
m>n
. We therefore satisfy the induction assumption, so by the induction hypothesis, either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness (and we are done), or
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

By an identical argument to
Lemma

19.5
, we can lift this
D
-dip property to
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
itself, and we are done.

Case 1b: non-increasing potential

Complementing Case 1a, we now assume that there exists
\kappa
such that for every
m>\kappa
and
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
with
u_{i}
,
q
and
\rho
as above, every split
u_{i}=v_{0}v
satisfies

\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0}v)-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0})\leq\kappa
.

Our goal in this case is to obtain from these segments a
\upphi
-leveled words sequence. Indeed, the condition above is almost the condition for
\upphi
-leveled words, but is missing the absolute value.
However, since
u_{i}
is a
minimal
infix of
w_{3}
upon which the potential increases enough, as per
Definition

19.8
, it follows that
\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0}v)-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0})>0
, so we can in fact add the absolute value and assume

|\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0}v)-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}v_{0})|\leq\kappa

We construct a sequence
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
as follows: for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
take
m
for which
|u_{i}|>n
(recall that the segments increase in length by
Proposition

19.9
). Define
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}(n)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with
w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1},w^{\prime}_{2}=w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}
,
w^{\prime}_{3}=u_{i}
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=u_{i+1}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}w_{4}
.
By the above, we have that
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
is indeed a leveled words sequence. Moreover, it inherits from
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
the properties of being
\ell
-sparse and having gap
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
.
We can therefore invoke
Lemma

19.5
, and we obtain that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, or
{\rightsquigarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!_{\upphi}\,\mathsf{words}}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
. As with the case above, an identical argument to
Lemma

19.5
shows that this lifts to
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
, so we are done.

Case 2: long ghost runs

The next case is not mutually exclusive to Case 1, so we assume Case 1 does not hold (even though we do not use this fact).
We assume that there are infinitely many
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and decomposition
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}
such that there exists a ghost run over an entire segment
u_{i}
. More precisely, there exists
p\in\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})
and a ghost run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{u_{i}}S
.

Our approach in this case is similar to that taken in
Lemma

19.5
– we flatten the prefix up to
u_{i}
, thus making the ghost run a real run that satisfies the gap constraints. Thus, we can apply induction.

Formally, we define a new sequence
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
as follows. For every
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
let
m>m^{\prime}
be such that
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
has a ghost run on
u_{i}
, and we require

\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})-\upphi(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})>m^{\prime}

(19.3)

Note that we can require this by since
u_{i}
is a segment in a
\upphi
-discharging decomposition, as per
Definition

19.8
.
Define
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}(m^{\prime})=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with

w^{\prime}_{1}=\mathsf{flatten}(w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1}\wr(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{3}|)
,
w^{\prime}_{2}=\epsilon
,
w^{\prime}_{3}=u_{i}
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=u_{i}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}w_{4}
.

Since our alphabet does not contain rebase letters, by
Definition

12.23
the resulting prefix
x^{\prime}_{1}
is over
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
.
Denote
\boldsymbol{c}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{init}},w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i-1})
and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{init}},w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2})
, then by

Lemma

12.24

we have that
\boldsymbol{c}(q)=\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(q)
for every
q\in\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},w_{1}w_{2})
, and
\boldsymbol{c^{\prime}}(p)
is much larger than any finite entry in
\boldsymbol{c}
(according to the flattening constant above).
Specifically, the entire run
\rho
from
p
is above all the existing
\ell
independent runs with gap at least
(|S|+1)4{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}W_{\max}|w_{3}|
.
It follows that
\rho
can be added as an independent run. Thus,
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
has
\ell+1
independent runs.

Now, however (and unlike
Lemma

18.20
), it is not necessarily the case that the potential is maintained after flattening. We therefore resort again to
Lemma

14.4
and obtain that either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness (and in particular type-1), or indeed the potential is unchanged by the flattening.
In the latter case, we have that
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
remains a discharging words sequence. In particular, by
Eq.

19.3

u_{i}
satisfies the discharge constraints of
Definition

19.7
(to be more precise, it satisfies the second requirement by the minimality constraint of
Definition

19.8
).

We can therefore apply the induction hypothesis on
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
. By the hypothesis, if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, then we are done. Otherwise, we get that
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}
has the
D
-dip property, and we again repeat the argument in
Lemma

19.5
to lift this to
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
(note that here the argument is the involved case therein, since we use flattening) and we are done.

Case 3: Ghost free
G
-cover

Our final case assumes both Case 1 and Case 2 do not hold. Since Case 2 does not hold, there are at most finitely many
m
for which there are ghost runs over segments in the decomposition.
We slightly strengthen this to assume there are no ghost runs over any segments at all. This is possible since we can replace
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
by duplicating
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)
for some very large
m
into
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(i)
for all
i\leq m
. Note that this does not interfere with the Infix
D
-dip property, which anyway refers to infinitely many infixes and ignores the replacement of finitely many elements.
We therefore assume that the
\upphi
-discharging decomposition of
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
is Ghost Free, as per
Definition

18.6
.

Next, since Case 1 does not hold, then there exists
G\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
m>2G
with
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and
w_{3}=u_{1}\cdots u_{d(m)+1}
, for every
1\leq i\leq d(m)
and
\boldsymbol{c_{i}}=\mathsf{next\_conf}(\boldsymbol{c_{\texttt{init}}},w_{1}w_{2}u_{1}\cdots u_{i})
, every state
q\in\mathsf{supp}(\boldsymbol{c_{i}})
is within gap
G
from some independent run.
As above, we can assume this holds in fact for every
m
, by possibly replacing finitely many elements of
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
.
Thus, the
\upphi
-discharging decomposition of
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
is a decomposition
G
-cover (as per
Definition

18.5
).

We can now almost apply
Corollary

19.10
, except the gap required there is slightly bigger than the gap in
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
. Identically to
Lemma

19.11
, this is an artificial problem, and can be resolved by shifting the elements of
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
, i.e., defining
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}^{\prime}(m)={\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m+G)
, which increases the bound by the necessary
4G
.

We can therefore invoke
Corollary

19.10
, and conclude that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, and we are done.
∎

As a potential-counterpart to
Corollary

18.22
for discharge words, we have the main tool for
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
(by applying
Lemma

19.11
with
\ell=1
, for which the gap is unbounded).

Corollary 19.12

(

Potential Discharge to Witness or
D
-Dip)
.

Consider a
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
sequence, then either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, or
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.

20
A Nondeterminizable WFA Has a Witness

We are finally ready to prove our main result, namely that if
\mathcal{A}
is a nondeterminizable WFA, then
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a witness. We then use this to conclude the proof that determinizability of WFA is decidable.

Theorem 20.1
.

Consider a WFA
\mathcal{A}
. If
\mathcal{A}
is nondeterminizable, then
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness.

We prove this theorem in the remainder of this section.
Technically, this result combines all the tools developed thus far. For an overview, see
Section

4.3
.

Henceforth, fix a WFA
\mathcal{A}
, and assume
\mathcal{A}
is nondeterminizable.

20.1
The Potential is Unbounded (Or There is a Witness)

The first step of our proof is to show that if
\mathcal{A}
is nondeterminizable, then the potential is unbounded. More precisely, we prove the following.

Lemma 20.2

(

Nondeterminizability implies Unbounded Potential or Witness)
.

Consider a WFA
\mathcal{A}
. If
\mathcal{A}
is nondeterminizable, then either
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
, or
\mathcal{A}
has a type-0 witness.

We prove the lemma in the remainder of the section.
since
\mathcal{A}
is nondeterminizable, then by
Lemma

9.8
we have that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is also nondeterminizable.
Our starting point is to use the gap-criterion of
Theorem

8.4
. Specifically, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
n
-gap witness
x_{n},y_{n}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
(
Definition

8.3
).
That is, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exist seamless runs
\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x_{n}y_{n}}S
and
\mu:s_{0}\xrightarrow{x_{n}}S
such that
\mathsf{mwt}(x_{n}y_{n},s_{0}\to S)=\mathsf{wt}(\rho)
while
\mathsf{mwt}(x_{n},s_{0}\to S)=\mathsf{wt}(\mu)
and
\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x_{n}))-\mathsf{wt}(\mu(x_{n}))\geq n
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we now perform a baseline shift (
Section

12.2
) so that
\rho
becomes the baseline (see
Fig.

30
). Since the alphabet is
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
, then this is maintained by the baseline shift. Specifically, we consider
\rho^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{\rho}/{\rho}]
,
\mu^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{\mu}/{\rho}]
,
x_{n}^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{x_{n}}/{\rho}]
and
y_{n}^{\prime}=\mathsf{bshift}[{y_{n}}/{\rho}]
.
By
Corollaries

12.14
and
12.13
, we have that the gap property above is maintained, and
\rho^{\prime}
is the baseline run (and therefore has weight
0
on all prefixes).
That is,
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{\prime}_{n}y^{\prime}_{n},s_{0}\to S)=\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime})=0
while
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{\prime}_{n},s_{0}\to S)=\mathsf{wt}(\mu^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x^{\prime}_{n}))-\mathsf{wt}(\mu(x^{\prime}_{n}))=-\mathsf{wt}(\mu(x^{\prime}_{n}))\geq n
.

Figure 30
:
Baseline shift on
\rho
. Note that we do not assume
\mu^{\prime}
actually continues after
x^{\prime}_{n}
, but this is the more interesting case.

By the gap criterion, and since
\mu^{\prime}
is a minimal run on
x^{\prime}_{n}
, we now see that
\uppsi(x^{\prime}_{n})=-\mathsf{wt}(\mu(x^{\prime}_{n}))\geq n
. However, since
\rho^{\prime}
is minimal on
x^{\prime}_{n}y^{\prime}_{n}
, we also have
\uppsi(x^{\prime}_{n}y^{\prime}_{n})=0
. That is, there is a significant discharge upon reading
y_{n}
.
Intuitively, we would like to obtain from this sequence a discharging words sequence, as these almost guarantee unbounded potential (
Corollary

18.22
).
The major barrier we face is that instead of unbounded potential, we might also get a
D
-dip, at which point we would be stuck.

To avoid this problem, we need to work much harder, and use our framework of cactus budding, as follows.
For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we replace
y^{\prime}_{n}
by a word
z^{\prime}_{n}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{\infty})^{*}
(i.e., containing cactus letters of any rank, but not rebase or jump letters), such that
\uppsi(x^{\prime}_{n}z^{\prime}_{n})=0
and

\mathsf{cost}(z^{\prime}_{n})

is minimal
with this property.
Note that since
y^{\prime}_{n}
satisfies the charge constraint, then there indeed exist such words
z^{\prime}_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
(possibly
y^{\prime}_{n}
itself, or otherwise some more elaborate words over
\Gamma_{0}^{\infty}
).
Intuitively, we allow “folding” infixes of
y^{\prime}_{n}
into cacti, as long as this reduces the overall cost of the obtained word.

Looking a few steps ahead, the idea is that
z^{\prime}_{n}
cannot have
D
-dip infixes, as those would imply the existence of separated increasing infixes, which in turn imply that we can perform cactus budding inside
z^{\prime}_{n}
, thus reducing the cost.
Unfortunately, since we now work over
(\Gamma_{0}^{\infty})^{*}
, there is no guarantee that the
z^{\prime}_{n}
are constructed from a finite alphabet, which is a requirement for discharging sequences. So we need to first reduce the setting to a finite alphabet.

To this end, we define a function that selects sub-cacti of a certain depth from the root of a cactus, as follows.

Definition 20.3
.

For a word
w\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
we define its
k
-deep sub-cacti

\mathsf{sub}_{k}(w)\subseteq\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
inductively (both on
k
and on the structure of
w
) as follows.

•

For
k=0
, if
w=\sigma\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
is a letter (either cactus or not) then
\mathsf{sub}_{0}(\sigma)=\sigma
.

•

For
k>0
, if
w=\sigma\in\Gamma_{0}^{0}
is a (non-cactus) letter, then
\mathsf{sub}_{k}(\sigma)=\emptyset
.

•

For
k>0
, if
w=\alpha_{S^{\prime},x}\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
is a cactus letter, then
\mathsf{sub}_{k}(\alpha_{S^{\prime},x})=\mathsf{sub}_{k-1}(x)
.

•

If
w=\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{n}\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{0})^{*}
then

\mathsf{sub}_{k}(\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{n})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{sub}_{k}(\sigma_{i})
.

Intuitively, we “dig” into the cactus letters for exactly
k
times, and select the set of letters that appear at
exactly
that depth (cactus or not). If we cannot expand a certain branch to depth
k
, we ignore it (output
\emptyset
for that branch).

Example 3
.

To illustrate this selection, recall
Example

2
, then in the notations therein we have:

\begin{split}&\mathsf{sub}_{0}(ab\alpha_{3}aa)=\{a,b,\alpha_{3}\}\\
&\mathsf{sub}_{1}(ab\alpha_{3}aa)=\mathsf{sub}_{0}(\alpha_{3})=\{a,b,\alpha_{4},\alpha_{2}\}\\
&\mathsf{sub}_{2}(ab\alpha_{3}aa)=\mathsf{sub}_{1}(\alpha_{3})=\mathsf{sub}_{0}(\alpha_{4})\cup\mathsf{sub}_{0}(\alpha_{2})=\{b\}\cup\{a,b,\alpha_{1}\}=\{a,b,\alpha_{1}\}\\
&\mathsf{sub}_{3}(ab\alpha_{3}aa)=\mathsf{sub}_{2}(\alpha_{3})=\mathsf{sub}_{1}(\alpha_{4})\cup\mathsf{sub}_{1}(\alpha_{2})=\emptyset\cup\mathsf{sub}_{0}(\alpha_{1})=\{a\}\end{split}

Now, since all the
z^{\prime}_{n}
have minimal cost, then by
Corollary

12.11
we have that every cactus letter appearing in
z^{\prime}_{n}
has depth at most
|S|
. Indeed, otherwise there is some cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
appearing in
z^{\prime}_{n}
that is the root of a depth
|S|+1
cactus chain, but then we can replace it with a less-deep cactus chain with the same transition weights, and reduce the overall cost of
z^{\prime}_{n}
.
In particular, this means that
\mathsf{sub}_{|S|}(z^{\prime}_{n})\subseteq\Gamma_{0}^{0}
, as it does not contain cactus letters.
Thus,
|\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{|S|}(z^{\prime}_{n})|<\infty
.
We now take
k\leq|S|
to be the minimal such that
|\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{k}(z^{\prime}_{n})|<\infty
.
That is, we dig deep enough so that the alphabet of the inner cacti (in all the
z^{\prime}_{n}
) becomes finite.

Before proceeding, we wish to claim that the
z^{\prime}_{n}
have unbounded length, as
n
increases. This, however, might not be the case (e.g., it could be that all
z^{\prime}_{n}
are a single letter, that does not allow the minimal run to continue).
However, in this case we claim that
\uppsi
does not have bounded decrease, and therefore we are under the conditions of
Lemma

13.6
, which immediately implies that the potential is unbounded, concluding the proof of
Lemma

20.2
.

More precisely, assume that
|z^{\prime}_{n}|<C
for some fixed
C\in\mathbb{N}
, and assume by way of contradiction that
\uppsi
has the
B
bounded-decrease property of
Definition

13.5
. Thus, we have
\uppsi(x^{\prime}_{n})-\uppsi(x^{\prime}_{n}z^{\prime}_{n})<B|z^{\prime}_{n}|\leq BC
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, but this is in contradiction to the fact that
\uppsi(x^{\prime}_{n})-\uppsi(x^{\prime}_{n}z^{\prime}_{n})\geq n
by the definition of
x^{\prime}_{n}
and
z^{\prime}_{n}
.
We therefore conclude that the sequence of
z^{\prime}_{n}
is unbounded in length.
We now split to two cases:

First case:
k=0
.

In this case, all the
z^{\prime}_{n}
are already constructed from some finite alphabet
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
. This makes things easy for us. We define the function
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
by choosing for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
a large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
|z^{\prime}_{n}|>n
(as we show above, the
z^{\prime}_{n}
are unbounded in length), and defining
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
with
w_{1}=x^{\prime}_{n}
,
w_{2}=\epsilon
,
w_{3}=z^{\prime}_{n}
and
w_{4}=\epsilon
.

We now apply our main result for discharging sequences, namely
Corollary

18.22
, and get that either
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
, concluding the proof of
Lemma

20.2
, or
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
has the Infix
D
-dip property for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
. In the latter case, we proceed to either reach a contradiction, or obtain a witness, as follows.

There is no need to remember what the
D
-dip property is at this point, as we immediately apply

Proposition

18.10
and then
Corollary

16.26
to conclude that
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
has a separated increasing infix. More precisely, there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and a decomposition
w_{3}=u^{\prime}xyv^{\prime}
such that
uxyv
is a separated increasing infix from
S^{\prime}=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},w_{1})
for
u=w_{2}u^{\prime}
and
v=v^{\prime}w_{4}
.
By the construction of
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
, we have in fact that
w_{1}=x^{\prime}_{n}
and
w_{3}=z^{\prime}_{n}
, and
u^{\prime}=u,v^{\prime}=v
.
There is also no need to remember what an increasing infix is, only that we can now apply cactus budding, as per
Lemma

16.6
.
The conditions of
Lemma

16.6
then tell us that either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness, in which case we conclude the proof of
Theorem

20.1
, or we can proceed to replace
z^{\prime}_{n}
with the word
z^{\prime\prime}_{n}=u\alpha_{B,x}v
where
B=\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(S^{\prime},u)
, and we have that
\mathsf{cost}(z^{\prime}_{n})>\mathsf{cost}(z^{\prime\prime}_{n})
, and that for every
r\in S^{\prime},t\in S
it holds that
\mathsf{mwt}(z^{\prime}_{n},r\to t)\leq\mathsf{mwt}(z^{\prime\prime}_{n},r\to t)
.

Since
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{\prime}_{n}z^{\prime}_{n},s_{0}\to S)=0
, and since
\alpha_{B,x}
maintains a seamless baseline run, then by the inequality above we have that

\begin{split}&0\geq\mathsf{mwt}(x^{\prime}_{n}z^{\prime\prime}_{n},s_{0}\to S)=\mathsf{mwt}(x^{\prime}_{n}z^{\prime\prime}_{n},s_{0}\xrightarrow{x^{\prime}_{n}}S^{\prime}\xrightarrow{z^{\prime\prime}_{n}}S)=\\
&\mathsf{mwt}(x^{\prime}_{n},s_{0}\to S^{\prime})+\mathsf{mwt}(z^{\prime\prime}_{n},S^{\prime}\to S)\geq\mathsf{mwt}(x^{\prime}_{n},s_{0}\to S^{\prime})+\mathsf{mwt}(z^{\prime}_{n},S^{\prime}\to S)=\\
&\mathsf{mwt}(x^{\prime}_{n}z^{\prime\prime}_{n},s_{0}\xrightarrow{x^{\prime}_{n}}S^{\prime}\xrightarrow{z^{\prime}_{n}}S)=\mathsf{mwt}(x^{\prime}_{n}z^{\prime}_{n},s_{0}\to S)=0\end{split}

so
\mathsf{mwt}(x^{\prime}_{n}z^{\prime\prime}_{n},s_{0}\to S)=0
and in particular
\uppsi(x^{\prime}_{n}z^{\prime\prime}_{n})=0
. This, however, is a contradiction to the choice of
z^{\prime}_{n}
as minimal cost with this property (since
\mathsf{cost}(z^{\prime}_{n})>\mathsf{cost}(z^{\prime\prime}_{n})
).
We conclude that
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
cannot have the Infix
D
-dip property, so the potential is unbounded in this case.

Second case:
k>0
.

Recall that
k
is the minimal number such that

|\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{k}(z^{\prime}_{n})|<\infty
. Since
k>0
, then by its minimality, we have that
|\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{k-1}(z^{\prime}_{n})|=\infty
.
In particular, since
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
is finite, we have that
\Upsilon=\left(\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{k-1}(z^{\prime}_{n})\right)\setminus\Gamma_{0}^{0}
is an infinite set of cactus letters. Consider the set of “inner words” of these cactus letters, i.e., the set
\Xi=\{w\mid\exists S^{\prime}\subseteq S,\ \alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\in\Upsilon\}
. By
Definition

20.3
we have that
\bigcup_{w\in\Xi}\mathsf{sub}_{0}(w)\subseteq\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{k}(z^{\prime}_{n})
, and in particular, this is a finite set, meaning that the alphabet of the words in
\Xi
is finite. Since
\Xi
itself is an infinite set (since
\Upsilon
is infinite), it follows that there are arbitrarily long words in
\Xi
.
Moreover, since there are only finitely many options for sets
S^{\prime}
, it follows that there exists a fixed
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is a word
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}\in\Upsilon
with
|w_{n}|>n
.

We claim that
S^{\prime}
is also reachable by some word
v_{\texttt{init}}
, i.e.,
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},v_{\texttt{init}})=S^{\prime}
.
Intuitively, this is because
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}
is read along some
z^{\prime}_{m}
, so all we need is the prefix up to it. However, this only guarantees reaching a subset of
S^{\prime}
. We fix this by flattening.

Formally, each
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}
is a letter appearing in some
z^{\prime}_{m}
, and there are finite-weight runs on
z^{\prime}_{m}
. Thus, the prefix
v^{\prime\prime}
up to the occurrence of
w_{n}
in some
z^{\prime}_{m}
reaches a state
s\in S^{\prime}
from which
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}
is read. In particular, we have
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},v^{\prime\prime})=S
(by
Definition

11.17
, of ghost states).
Then, we can define
v_{\texttt{init}}=\mathsf{flatten}(v^{\prime\prime}\wr F)
for
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(v^{\prime\prime})}
, and obtain by
Lemma

12.24
that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},v_{\texttt{init}})=S^{\prime}
.

We therefore arrive in the following setting: there is a sequence of words
\{(v_{\texttt{init}}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}})\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
with
v_{\texttt{init}}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
, as well as
|w_{n}|>n
and
w_{n}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a finite alphabet (namely
\Gamma^{\prime}=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{k}(z^{\prime}_{n})
).

We now unfold each
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}
as follows. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and set
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(v_{\texttt{init}}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}})}
. Define
v_{0}=\mathsf{unfold}(v_{\texttt{init}},\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}},\epsilon\wr F)=v_{\texttt{init}}w_{n}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
.
By
Proposition

13.9
, we have that
\uppsi(v_{0})=\uppsi(v_{\texttt{init}}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}})
.
By
Remark

12.16
, we can arbitrarily increase
M_{0}
and the following still holds. It particular, for
M_{0}+1
we also have
\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})=\uppsi(v_{\texttt{init}}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}})
, and therefore
\uppsi(v_{0})=\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
.
Consider therefore the sequence of prefixes

v_{0},v_{0}w_{n},v_{0}w_{n}^{2},\ldots,v_{0}w_{n}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}

then there exists
0\leq i<2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
such that
\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i})\geq\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i+1})
. Indeed, otherwise the charge strictly increases, contradicting the equality of charge between
v_{0}
and
v_{0}w_{n}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
.

We again construct an elongating words sequence
words
as follows. For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
n
be large enough such that
|w_{n}|>n
(recall that we prove above that there is such
n
), and choose
i
as above, i.e., such that
\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i})\geq\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i+1})
.
Define
\textsf{words}(m)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with
w^{\prime}_{1}=v_{0}w_{n}^{i}
,
w^{\prime}_{2}=\epsilon
,
w^{\prime}_{3}=w_{n}
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=\epsilon
.

We now analyze two possible scenarios.

•

If

is a leveled sequence (
Definition

18.12
), then by
Corollary

, either the potential is unbounded, concluding the proof of

, or

has the Infix

-dip property. In this case, we can reach a similar contradiction as in the

case, as follows.

Since

has the Infix

it has a separated increasing infix. Specifically, there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\textsf{words}(m)=v_{0}w_{n}^{i}\cdot\epsilon\cdot w_{n}\cdot\epsilon
and a decomposition

that is a separated increasing infix from
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},v_{0}w_{n}^{i})=S^{\prime}
(as above, this follows from
w^{\prime}_{2}=w^{\prime}_{4}=\epsilon
).
Recall that
w_{n}
is a word such that
15
15

15

\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}
actually appears in a sub-cactus of some
z^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
, and it may be that
n^{\prime}\neq n
. For clarity we unify them, as neither have any implication on the proof.

\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}\in\mathsf{sub}_{k-1}(z^{\prime}_{n})
.

By expanding
Definition

20.3
, we can write
z^{\prime}_{n}
as a cactus chain (
Definition

12.1
), i.e., there is a cactus chain

\Theta_{1}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{k-1},y_{k}}

such that
z^{\prime}_{n}=x_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y_{1}}z_{1}
for some words
x_{1},y_{1},z_{1}
, and
(S^{\prime}_{k-1},y_{k})=(S^{\prime},w_{n})
.
Then, since
w_{n}=uxyz
is an increasing infix, then
\Theta_{1}
is a
Pre-bud chain
(
Definition

17.7
).
We can therefore invoke
Lemma

17.8
, and obtain that the corresponding Post-bud chain
\Theta_{2}
is superior to
\Theta_{1}
.
In particular, the first element of
\Theta_{2}
, denoted
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime\prime}_{1}}
is superior to
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y_{1}}
.

Then,
Proposition

17.4
gives us that
0=\uppsi(x^{\prime}_{n}z^{\prime}_{n})=\uppsi(x^{\prime}_{n}\cdot x_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y_{1}}y_{1})\geq\uppsi(x^{\prime}_{n}\cdot x_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime\prime}_{1}})y_{1})\geq 0
(since the charge is non-negative).
Thus,
\uppsi(x^{\prime}_{n}\cdot x_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime\prime}_{1}})y_{1})=0
.
Note, however, that
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime\prime}_{1}}
is obtained by propagating the replacement of
w_{n}
with
u\alpha_{B,x}y
through the chain, and by
Lemma

16.6
we have
\mathsf{cost}(w_{n})>\mathsf{cost}(u\alpha_{B,x}y)
(which also propagates through the chain), meaning that
\mathsf{cost}(z^{\prime}_{n})>\mathsf{cost}(x_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime\prime}_{1}}y_{1})
.
But this is a contradiction to the assumption that
z^{\prime}_{n}
has minimal cost with this property, so we are done.

•

The second case is when
words
is not a leveled sequence. We claim that in this case we can find a faithful restriction of
words
(
Definition

16.9
) that is a discharging sequence, and then we can follow the same reasoning as above. Intuitively, not being leveled means that the charge fluctuates “wildly”, and in particular becomes very high. Since it is non-negative and must end in
0
, then either it drops abruptly, violating the
B
-bounded decrease property, in which case we have unbounded potential, or it decreases slowly in which case we have a discharging sequence.

Formally, since
words
is not leveled, then for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\textsf{words}(m)=v_{0}w_{n}^{i}\cdot\epsilon\cdot w_{n}\cdot\epsilon
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and a prefix
w^{\prime}_{n}
of
w_{n}
such that
|\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i})-\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n})|>B
. Write
w_{n}=w^{\prime}_{n}w^{\prime\prime}_{n}
.

–

If for every
B
there exists such
m,n
for which
\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i})-\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n})>B
, then we can readily construct a discharging words sequence
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
by setting
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m^{\prime})=v_{0}w_{n}^{i}\cdot\epsilon\cdot w^{\prime}_{n}\cdot w^{\prime\prime}_{n}
, where we take
n
large enough so that the discharge is greater than
m^{\prime}
. Note that we can assume
w^{\prime}_{n}
increase in length, since the charge is assumed to have bounded-decrease, as discussed above.

–

If for every
B
there exists such
m,n
for which
\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n})-\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i})>B
, recall that by our choice of
i
, we have
\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i})\geq\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i+1})=\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n}w^{\prime\prime}_{n})
, and therefore
we have
\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n})-\uppsi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n}w^{\prime\prime}_{n})>B
.
Since the charge has bounded-decrease, as we already argue before the
k=0
case, we have that the
w^{\prime\prime}_{n}
suffixes in this case have unbounded length.
Then we can again construct a discharging
16
16

16

A nit-picking details is that we need to take
w^{\prime\prime}_{n}
to be the suffix upon which the discharge is maximal, to conform with
Definition

18.16
.
words sequence
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
by setting
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m^{\prime})=v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n}\cdot\epsilon\cdot w^{\prime\prime}_{n}\cdot\epsilon
, where we take
n
large enough so that the discharge is greater than
m^{\prime}
.

Therefore, we have a discharging sequence
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
such that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
it holds that
w^{\prime}_{3}
is an infix (in fact, either a suffix or a prefix) of
w_{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
By our main result for discharging sequences, namely
Corollary

18.22
, we again have that either the potential is unbounded, concluding the proof of
Lemma

20.2
, or
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}
has the Infix
D
-dip property.
In the latter case, we proceed identically to the leveled-words case above: we obtain an increasing infix within
w_{n}
, therefore we have a Pre-bud chain of lower cost, which contradicts the cost-minimality of
z^{\prime}_{n}
.

All in all, we conclude that in all cases that did not reach a contradiction, either we have a type-0 witness, or
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
. This completes the proof of
Lemma

20.2
.

20.2
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
Has a Witness

We are now ready for the second (and more substantial) part of the proof of
Theorem

20.1
.
From a high-level perspective, this part is very similar to the first part in
Section

20.1
. That is, we start with a sequence of words over
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
that has some property (previously unbounded gaps, now unbounded potential). We then replace them with their minimal-cost variants over
\Gamma_{0}^{\infty}
.
We proceed to find a finite alphabet deep enough in these words, and from there obtain discharging or leveled sequences.
While the proof of this part does not seem more complicated, we emphasize that the lemma used in this part all pertain to potential, which uses the more difficult parts of our toolbox (e.g.,
Section

19.2
, and most of
Section

17
).

Lemma 20.4

(

Unbounded Potential Implies Witness)
.

Consider a WFA
\mathcal{A}
. If
\sup\{\upphi(w)\mid w\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}\}=\infty
, then
\mathcal{A}
has a type-1 witness.

We prove the lemma in the remainder of the section.

Since the potential is unbounded, there is a sequence of words
\{z_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that
\upphi(w_{z})>n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Intuitively, we would like to obtain from this sequence a potential-discharging words sequence (
Definition

19.7
), as these almost guarantee a witness (
Corollary

19.12
).
We again face the barrier that instead of a witness, we might also get a
D
-dip. However, we are now experienced and ready for the challenge.

Adhering to the naming scheme of
Section

20.2
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we replace
z_{n}
by a word
z^{\prime}_{n}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{\infty})^{*}
(i.e., containing cactus letters of any rank, but not rebase or jump letters), such that
\upphi(z^{\prime}_{n})>n
and
\mathsf{cost}(z^{\prime}_{n})

is minimal
with this property.
Note that since
z_{n}
satisfies the potential constraint, then there indeed exist such words
z^{\prime}_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
(possibly
z_{n}
itself, or otherwise some more elaborate words over
\Gamma_{0}^{\infty}
).
Intuitively, we allow “folding” infixes of
z_{n}
into cacti, as long as this reduces the overall cost of the obtained word.

Now, since all the
z^{\prime}_{n}
have minimal cost, then by
Corollary

12.11
we have that every cactus letter appearing in
z^{\prime}_{n}
has depth at most
|S|
. Indeed, otherwise there is some cactus letter
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}
appearing in
z^{\prime}_{n}
that is the root of a depth
|S|+1
cactus chain, but then we can replace it with a less-deep cactus chain with the same transition weights, and reduce the overall cost of
z^{\prime}_{n}
.
In particular, this means that
\mathsf{sub}_{|S|}(z^{\prime}_{n})\subseteq\Gamma_{0}^{0}
, as it does not contain cactus letters.
Thus,
|\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{|S|}(z^{\prime}_{n})|<\infty
.
We now take
k\leq|S|
to be the minimal such that
|\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{k}(z^{\prime}_{n})|<\infty
.
That is, we dig deep enough so that the alphabet of the inner cacti (in all the
z^{\prime}_{n}
) becomes finite.

Unlike
Section

20.1
, we cannot claim at this point that the lengths of the
z^{\prime}_{n}
are unbounded.
Indeed, the bounded-growth property of the potential (
Lemma

13.3
) applies only over a finite alphabet, which is not the case at this point.

We split to identical cases as in
Section

20.1
.

First case:
k=0
.

In this case, all the
z^{\prime}_{n}
are already constructed from some finite alphabet
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma_{\infty}^{0}
. In particular, we can now apply the bounded-growth property of
Lemma

13.3
, and get that the
z^{\prime}_{n}
are unbounded in length as
n
increases.

We define the function
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
(
Definition

19.7
) by choosing for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
a large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
|z^{\prime}_{n}|>n
and defining
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
with
w_{1}=\epsilon
,
w_{2}=\epsilon
,
w_{3}=z^{\prime}_{n}
and
w_{4}=\epsilon
. Note that
Definition

19.7
also requires that the potential does not exceed
\upphi(z^{\prime}_{n})
on any prefix of
w_{3}
.
This requirement is satisfied because
z^{\prime}_{n}
is assumed to have minimal cost, and if we could drop a suffix of it while increasing the potential, this would decrease the cost.

We now apply our main result for potential-discharging sequences, namely

Corollary

19.12
and get that either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, concluding the proof of
Lemma

20.4
or
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
has the Infix-
D
-dip property.

In the latter case, we proceed to either reach a contradiction, or obtain a witness, as follows.
We apply
Proposition

18.10
and then
Corollary

16.26
to conclude that
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
has a separated increasing infix. More precisely, there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\lightning\!\mathsf{words}(m)=w_{1}w_{2}w_{3}w_{4}
and a decomposition

that is a separated increasing infix from

).

We can now apply cactus budding, as per
Lemma

16.6
.
The conditions of
Lemma

16.6
then tell us that either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness, in which case we conclude the proof of

, or we can proceed to replace
z^{\prime}_{n}
with the word
z^{\prime\prime}_{n}=u\alpha_{B,x}v
where

, and we have that
\mathsf{cost}(z^{\prime}_{n})>\mathsf{cost}(z^{\prime\prime}_{n})
, as well as
\upphi(z^{\prime}_{n})\leq\upphi(z^{\prime\prime}_{n})
. This yields a contradiction to the minimal cost of

, and we are done.

We remark that the entire framework of increasing infixes (
Section

16
) is developed to obtain these two inequalities.

Second case:
k>0
.

Recall that
k
is the minimal number such that

|\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{k}(z^{\prime}_{n})|<\infty
. Since
k>0
, then by its minimality, we have that
|\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{k-1}(z^{\prime}_{n})|=\infty
.
In particular, since
\Gamma_{0}^{0}
is finite, we have that
\Upsilon=\left(\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{k-1}(z^{\prime}_{n})\right)\setminus\Gamma_{0}^{0}
is an infinite set of cactus letters. Consider the set of “inner words” of these cactus letters, i.e., the set
\Xi=\{w\mid\exists S^{\prime}\subseteq S,\ \alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\in\Upsilon\}
. By
Definition

20.3
we have that
\bigcup_{w\in\Xi}\mathsf{sub}_{0}(w)\subseteq\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{k}(z^{\prime}_{n})
, and in particular, this is a finite set, meaning that the alphabet of the words in
\Xi
is finite. Since
\Xi
itself is an infinite set (since
\Upsilon
is infinite), it follows that there are arbitrarily long words in
\Xi
.
Moreover, since there are only finitely many options for sets
S^{\prime}
, it follows that there exists a fixed
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is a word
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}\in\Upsilon
with
|w_{n}|>n
.

We claim that
S^{\prime}
is also reachable by some word
v_{\texttt{init}}
, i.e.,
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},v_{\texttt{init}})=S^{\prime}
.
Intuitively, this is because
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}
is read along some
z^{\prime}_{m}
, so all we need is the prefix up to it. However, this only guarantees reaching a subset of
S^{\prime}
. We fix this by flattening.

Formally, each
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}
is a letter appearing in some
z^{\prime}_{m}
, and there are finite-weight runs on
z^{\prime}_{m}
. Thus, the prefix
v^{\prime\prime}
up to the occurrence of
w_{n}
in some
z^{\prime}_{m}
reaches a state
s\in S^{\prime}
from which
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}
is read. In particular, we have
\delta_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}(s_{0},v^{\prime\prime})=S
(by
Definition

11.17
, of ghost states).
Then, we can define
v_{\texttt{init}}=\mathsf{flatten}(v^{\prime\prime}\wr F)
for
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(v^{\prime\prime})}
, and obtain by
Lemma

12.24
that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(s_{0},v_{\texttt{init}})=S^{\prime}
.

We therefore arrive in the following setting: there is a sequence of words
\{(v_{\texttt{init}}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}})\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
with
v_{\texttt{init}}\in(\Gamma_{0}^{0})^{*}
, as well as
|w_{n}|>n
and
w_{n}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a finite alphabet (namely
\Gamma^{\prime}=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{sub}_{k}(z^{\prime}_{n})
).

We now unfold each
\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}
(but we are wary of the differences between charge and potential, and recall that potential does not behave quite as nicely with unfolding).
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and set
F>2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(v_{\texttt{init}}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}})}
. Define
v_{0}=\mathsf{unfold}(v_{\texttt{init}},\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}},\epsilon\wr F)=v_{\texttt{init}}w_{n}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{0}}
.
By
Lemma

14.4
, we have that either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-0 witness, concluding the proof of
Lemma

20.4
, or
\upphi(v_{0})=\upphi(v_{\texttt{init}}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}})
.
By
Remark

12.16
, we can arbitrarily increase
M_{0}
and the following still holds. It particular, for
M_{0}+1
we also have
\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})=\upphi(v_{\texttt{init}}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}})
, and therefore
\upphi(v_{0})=\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}})
.
Consider therefore the sequence of prefixes

v_{0},v_{0}w_{n},v_{0}w_{n}^{2},\ldots,v_{0}w_{n}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}

then there exists
0\leq i<2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}
such that
\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i})\leq\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i+1})
. Indeed, otherwise the potential strictly decreases, contradicting the equality of potential between
v_{0}
and
v_{0}w_{n}^{2{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{m}}}}
.

We again construct an elongating words sequence
words
as follows. For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
n
be large enough such that
|w_{n}|>n
(recall that we prove above that there is such
n
), and choose
i
as above, i.e., such that
\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i})\leq\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i+1})
.
Define
\textsf{words}(m)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
with
w^{\prime}_{1}=v_{0}w_{n}^{i}
,
w^{\prime}_{2}=\epsilon
,
w^{\prime}_{3}=w_{n}
and
w^{\prime}_{4}=\epsilon
.

We now analyze two possible scenarios.

•

If
words
is a
\upphi
-leveled sequence (
Definition

19.3
), then by
Corollary

19.6
, either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, concluding the proof of

, or

has the Infix

-dip property. In this case, we can reach a similar contradiction as in the

case, as follows.

Since

has the Infix

-dip property, then by

it has a separated increasing infix. Specifically, there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\textsf{words}(m)=v_{0}w_{n}^{i}\cdot\epsilon\cdot w_{n}\cdot\epsilon
and a decomposition

that is a separated increasing infix from
