(as above, this follows from
w^{\prime}_{2}=w^{\prime}_{4}=\epsilon
).
Recall that
w_{n}
is a word such that
17
17

17

\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}
actually appears in a sub-cactus of some
z^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
, and it may be that
n^{\prime}\neq n
. For clarity we unify them, as neither have any implication on the proof.

\alpha_{S^{\prime},w_{n}}\in\mathsf{sub}_{k-1}(z^{\prime}_{n})
.

By expanding
Definition

20.3
, we can write
z^{\prime}_{n}
as a cactus chain (
Definition

12.1
), i.e., there is a cactus chain

\Theta_{1}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{k-1},y_{k}}

such that
z^{\prime}_{n}=x_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y_{1}}z_{1}
for some words
x_{1},y_{1},z_{1}
, and
(S^{\prime}_{k-1},y_{k})=(S^{\prime},w_{n})
.
Then, since
w_{n}=uxyz
is an increasing infix, then
\Theta_{1}
is a
Pre-bud chain
(
Definition

17.7
).
We can therefore invoke
Lemma

17.8
, and obtain that the corresponding Post-bud chain, which starts with
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{1}}
, satisfies
\upphi(z^{\prime}_{n})=\upphi(x_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y_{1}}z_{1})\leq\upphi(x_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{1}}z_{1})
.

Note, however, that
\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{1}}
is obtained by propagating the replacement of
w_{n}
with
u\alpha_{B,x}y
through the chain, and by
Lemma

16.6
we have
\mathsf{cost}(w_{n})>\mathsf{cost}(u\alpha_{B,x}y)
(which also propagates through the chain), meaning that
\mathsf{cost}(z^{\prime}_{n})>\mathsf{cost}(x_{1}\alpha_{S^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{1}}z_{1})
.
But this is a contradiction to the assumption that
z^{\prime}_{n}
has minimal cost with this property, so we are done.

•

The second case is when
words
is not a
\upphi
-leveled sequence. We claim that in this case we can find a faithful restriction of
words
(
Definition

16.9
) that is a
\upphi
-discharging sequence, and then we can follow the same reasoning as above. Intuitively, not being leveled means that the potential fluctuates “wildly” (and can become very large or very low, unlike charge). Recall that
words
is over a finite alphabet (due to the minimality assumption on
k
, see
\Gamma^{\prime}
above). In particular, we have that the potential satisfies the bounded-growth property of
Lemma

13.3
. This becomes useful below.

Since
words
is not leveled, then for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
\textsf{words}(m)=v_{0}w_{n}^{i}\cdot\epsilon\cdot w_{n}\cdot\epsilon
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and a prefix
w^{\prime}_{n}
of
w_{n}
such that
|\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i})-\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n})|>B
. Write
w_{n}=w^{\prime}_{n}w^{\prime\prime}_{n}
.

–

If for every
B
there exists such
m,n
for which
\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n})-\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i})>B
, then we can readily construct a
\upphi
-discharging words sequence
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
by setting
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m^{\prime})=v_{0}w_{n}^{i}\cdot\epsilon\cdot w^{\prime}_{n}\cdot w^{\prime\prime}_{n}
, where we take
n
large enough so that the discharge is greater than
m^{\prime}
. Specifically, we can assume
w^{\prime}_{n}
grows unboundedly due to the bounded-growth property of the potential (
Lemma

13.3
).

–

If for every
B
there exists such
m,n
for which
\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i})-\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n})>B
, then by the choice of
i
we have
\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i})\leq\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i+1})=\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n}w^{\prime\prime}_{n})
.
Thus, we have
\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n}w^{\prime\prime}_{n})-\upphi(v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n})>B
.
Since the potential has bounded-growth over this finite alphabet, as we already argue before the
k=0
case, we have that the
w^{\prime\prime}_{n}
suffixes in this case have unbounded length.
Then we can again construct a
\upphi
-discharging
18
18

18

A nit-picking details is that we need to take
w^{\prime\prime}_{n}
to be the suffix upon which the discharge is maximal, to conform with
Definition

19.7
.
words sequence
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
by setting
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m^{\prime})=v_{0}w_{n}^{i}w^{\prime}_{n}\cdot\epsilon\cdot w^{\prime\prime}_{n}\cdot\epsilon
, where we take
n
large enough so that the discharge is greater than
m^{\prime}
.

Therefore, we have a
\upphi
-discharging sequence
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
such that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}(m)=w^{\prime}_{1}w^{\prime}_{2}w^{\prime}_{3}w^{\prime}_{4}
it holds that
w^{\prime}_{3}
is an infix (in fact, either a suffix or a prefix) of
w_{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
By our main result for
\upphi
-discharging sequences, namely
Corollary

19.12
, we again have that either
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness, concluding the proof of
Lemma

20.4
, or
{\lightning\!^{\upphi}\!\!\mathsf{words}}
has the Infix
D
-dip property.
In the latter case, we proceed identically to the
\upphi
-leveled words case above: we obtain an increasing infix within
w_{n}
, therefore we have a Pre-bud chain of lower cost, which contradicts the cost-minimality of
z^{\prime}_{n}
.

All in all, we conclude that in all cases that did not reach a contradiction,
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has a type-1 witness and the proof of
Lemma

20.4
.
Combining this with
Lemma

20.2
, we conclude the proof of
Theorem

20.1
.

20.3
Determinizability of WFAs is Decidable

Having proved a sound and complete characterization of nondeterminizable WFAs by witnesses, we can now use the fact that checking a witness is decidable, to conclude the decidability of determinization.

Theorem 20.5
.

Determinizability of WFA is decidable.

Proof.

We show that both determinizability and its complement are in RE, and so the determinizability problem is in
\mathrm{RE}\cap\mathrm{coRE}=R
, i.e., decidable.

Determinizability is in
\mathrm{RE}

Given a WFA
\mathcal{A}
, we can decide whether it is determinizable by enumerating all deterministic WFAs, and for every deterministic WFA
\mathcal{D}
, check whether
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{D}
are equivalent. The latter is decidable by
[
1
]
(the so-called
N,D
Equality
Problem
therein. Crucially relying on one of the automata being deterministic).

If we find an equivalent deterministic automaton, we are done. Otherwise we continue.

We remark that we can improve upon this brute force algorithm, where instead of trying every deterministic automaton, we enumerate every size of gap
G
, and construct a deterministic automaton as per the proof of
Theorem

8.4
. Essentially, we try bigger and bigger gaps, and if
\mathcal{A}
is determinizable then eventually we will reach its gap and obtain the equivalent deterministic automaton.

Determinizability is in
\mathrm{coRE}

By
Theorem

20.1
, if
\mathcal{A}
is nondeterminizable, then it has a type-1 witness. We therefore enumerate every tuple
(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})
with
w_{1},w_{2}\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{1}
and
w_{3}\in\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty}
(including jump letters in
w_{3}
), and for every such tuple check whether it is a witness using
Theorem

14.3
.

Note that in order to enumerate all tuples, we need to increase the
length
of the words, the
cactus depth
, and the
rebase rank
. However, this can be done in parallel (equivalently, the tuple can be guessed nondeterministically).
∎

Funding

This research was supported by the ISRAEL SCIENCE FOUNDATION (grant No. 989/22).

Appendix A
A Characterization of Determinizability by Gaps

In this section we prove a characterization of determinizability by means of “gaps” between runs.
Since we use this result in
Appendix

B
for proving that we can omit initial and final weights (as well as accepting states), we prove it also for the general model of WFAs with initial and final weights.
Note that our model can be thought of as having initial weights in
\{0,\infty\}^{Q}
(with only a single state weighted
0
), and the final weights are just
\boldsymbol{0}
.

We now define explicitly and precisely the model of WFA with initial and final weights, and give the full details of the reduction.

Weighted Automata with Initial and Final Weights

A
(\min,+)
Weighted Automaton with initial and final weights
(WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
for short) is a tuple
\mathcal{A}=\langle Q,\Sigma,\texttt{init},\Delta,\texttt{fin}\rangle
with the same components as a WFA, except that

\texttt{init},\texttt{fin}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}^{Q}
are
Q
-indexed vectors denoting for each state its
initial weight
and
final weight
, respectively.

For the purpose of this section, we assume
\Sigma
is
finite
. We do, however, comment on what happens for infinite
\Sigma
in
Remark

A.3
.

A WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
is
deterministic
if
|\{q\mid\texttt{init}(q)\neq\infty\}|=1
and for every
\sigma\in\Sigma
and
p\in Q
there exists at most one transition
(p,\sigma,c,q)
with
c\neq\infty
.

For a run
\rho:p\xrightarrow{x}q
in a WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
, the sum of its weights along the transitions is denoted
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)
(similarly to WFAs). We also introduce some additional notations for WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
, as follows.

•

\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}(\rho)=\texttt{init}(p)+\mathsf{wt}(\rho)
and
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}(x,Q_{1}\to Q_{2})=\min\{\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}(\rho)\mid\rho:Q_{1}\xrightarrow{x}Q_{2}\}
.

•

\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}(\rho)=\mathsf{wt}(\rho)+\texttt{fin}(q)
and
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}(x,Q_{1}\to Q_{2})=\min\{\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}(\rho)\mid\rho:Q_{1}\xrightarrow{x}Q_{2}\}
.

•

\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}(\rho)=\texttt{init}(p)+\mathsf{wt}(\rho)+\texttt{fin}(q)
and
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}(x,Q_{1}\to Q_{2})=\min\{\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}(\rho)\mid\rho:Q_{1}\xrightarrow{x}Q_{2}\}
.

We denote
Q_{0}=\{q\mid\texttt{init}(q)<\infty\}
and
F=\{q\mid\texttt{fin}(q)<\infty\}
.
As usual, we assume all automata we deal with a trim. For WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
, this means that every state is reachable from
Q_{0}
and in addition can reach
F
.

Intuitively, we show that
\mathcal{A}
is determinizable if and only if there is a bound of how large a run can grow above the current minimal run, and still become minimal with an appropriate suffix. The intuition for the proof is that a deterministic equivalent must, in some sense, keep track of the differences in values between all runs that may still become minimal.

Our main result in this section is the following.

Theorem A.1
.

A trim WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
is determinizable if and only if there exists a bound
B\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every word
x\in\Sigma^{*}
and state
q\in Q
, if there exists
y\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}(x\cdot y,Q_{0}\to F)=\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}(x\cdot y,Q_{0}\xrightarrow{x}q\xrightarrow{y}F)<\infty
, then
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}(x,Q_{0}\to q)-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}(x,Q_{0}\to Q)\leq B
.

Remark A.2
.

It is easy to see that the statement of
Theorem

A.1
coincides with that of
Theorem

8.4
in when the WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
is actually a WFA.
It should be noted, however, that when we say “determinizable” for a WFA, we mean that the equivalent deterministic is also a WFA (rather than a WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
). Otherwise one may worry that the determinization of a WFA in this characterization is a WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
. We explain why this holds in the proof.

Remark A.3

(On Infinite Alphabets)
.

The proof that the existence of a bound implies determinizability does not rely on the alphabet begin finite, and therefore holds for infinite alphabets as well. We use this in
Corollary

12.26
.

The converse direction, however, does not hold. Yet, the proof in the converse direction can be adapted to state that if
\mathcal{A}
is determinizable, then for every finite subset of the alphabet, there exists a bound for words over this subset. While we do not make use of this, it is interesting to note.

We prove
Theorem

A.1
in the remainder of this section. We remark that the final weights, combined with our sub-goal in
Remark

A.2
, add some annoying technicalities to the proof. In order to understand the proof, we recommend assuming that all final weights are
0
, and applying this whenever they are discussed.

A.1
First Direction: a Bound Implies Determinizability

For the first direction, assume there exists a bound
B
as specified in the theorem. We construct an equivalent deterministic WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}

\mathcal{D}=\langle Q^{\prime},\Sigma,\texttt{init}^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},\texttt{fin}^{\prime}\rangle
.
We start with the intuition for the construction. Consider a word
x\in\Sigma^{*}
. The WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}

\mathcal{D}
keeps track of minimal run of
\mathcal{A}
on
x
, but also keeps track of the difference between the minimal run and higher runs, provided that these runs are no more than
B
above the minimal one. The condition in the theorem ensures that once a run becomes higher than
B
above the minimal one, it can no longer become minimal on any suffix, and can therefore be safely ignored.
In order to track the runs, the minimal run is always normalized to
0
, and this normalization is accounted for by the weight of the transition.

We start with some definitions and notations. Let
\max\texttt{fin}=\max\{|\texttt{fin}(q)|\mid q\in Q\}
.
For a function
f:Q\to\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
, we define
f|_{B}\in Q^{\prime}
by setting
f|_{B}(q)=f(q)
if
f(q)\leq B
, and
f|_{B}(q)=\infty
otherwise, and we define
\mathsf{shift_{0}}(f)
to be the function
\mathsf{shift_{0}}(f)(q)=f(q)-\min\{f(p)\mid p\in Q\}
, i.e., we shift all values of
f
such that the minimum is
0
. We now turn to formally define the construction.

•

The state space is
Q^{\prime}=(\{0,\ldots,M\}\cup\{\infty\})^{Q}\times\{-\max\texttt{fin},\ldots,\max\texttt{fin}\}
. Intuitively, each state corresponds to a configuration, shifted to
0
(and ignoring states whose minimal run thus far has weight more than
B
above the minimum), as well as a number representing the final weight that was incurred in the last step.

•

In order to define the initial state, we consider two cases.

–

If every state
q
with
\texttt{init}(q)<\infty
has
\inf(q)=\infty
(i.e., no accepting initial states), then
The initial vector
\texttt{init}^{\prime}
assigns weight
\texttt{init}(q)
to the single state
(\mathsf{shift_{0}}(\texttt{init})|_{B},0)
.

–

If there exists
q\in Q
such that
\texttt{init}(q)<\infty
and
\texttt{fin}(q)<\infty
(i.e., the word
\epsilon
receives a finite weight), let
q\in Q
be the state for which
\texttt{init}(q)+\texttt{fin}(q)
is minimal.
The initial vector
\texttt{init}^{\prime}
assigns weight
\texttt{init}(q)+\texttt{fin}(q)
to the single state
(\mathsf{shift_{0}}(\texttt{init})|_{B},\texttt{fin}(q))
.

•

For
(g,c)\in Q^{\prime}
, the final weight is
0
if there exists
q\in Q
such that
\texttt{fin}(q)<\infty
and
g(q)<\infty
(i.e., if
g
“contains” an accepting state), and otherwise the final weight is
\infty
.

Observe that if
\mathcal{A}
is a WFA, then
\texttt{init}^{\prime}
has entries in
\{0,\infty\}
with only one entry of
0
(and
\texttt{fin}^{\prime}
always has entries in
\{0,\infty\}
by our construction).
Thus,
\mathcal{D}
is also a WFA in this case (c.f.,
Remark

A.2
).

•

The transitions are defined as follows.
Consider a state
(g,c)\in Q^{\prime}
and letter
\sigma\in\Sigma
. We first define an intermediate function
g^{\prime}:Q\to\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}
by setting for every
q\in Q

g^{\prime}(q)=\min\{g(p)+d\mid p\in Q,\ (p,\sigma,d,q)\in\Delta\}

We then consider
g^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{shift_{0}}(g^{\prime})|_{B}
, and split to cases similarly to the initial states.

–

If
\texttt{fin}(q)=\infty
for every
q
with
g^{\prime\prime}(q)<\infty
, we have the transition

((g,c),\sigma,\min\{g^{\prime}(q)\mid q\in Q\}-c,(g^{\prime\prime},0))\in\Delta^{\prime}

–

If there exists
q\in Q
with
\texttt{fin}(q)<\infty
and
g^{\prime\prime}(q)<\infty
, let
q\in Q
be the state for which
g^{\prime\prime}(q)+\texttt{fin}(q)
is minimal. We have the transition

((g,c),\sigma,\min\{g^{\prime}(q)\mid q\in Q\}-c+\texttt{fin}(q),(g^{\prime\prime},\texttt{fin}(q)))\in\Delta^{\prime}

(all other transitions from
g
with
\sigma
have weight
\infty
, so
\mathcal{D}
is deterministic).

We explain the intuition and prove the correctness of the construction (namely that
\mathcal{D}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{A}
). The proof proceeds by induction on the length of a give word
x
.

For the base case,
x=\epsilon
and we consider the initial state. By definition, the configuration at the initial state is
g_{0}=\mathsf{shift_{0}}(\texttt{init})|_{B}
.
If
\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\epsilon)=\infty
, then the initial weight is
0
, but the initial state
(g_{0},0)
has final weight
\infty
.

Otherwise,
\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\epsilon)=\infty
and the initial state
(g_{0},\texttt{fin}(q))
has weight
\texttt{init}(q)+\texttt{fin}(q)=\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\epsilon)
for the appropriate state
q
.

From this, we extract our induction hypothesis. Consider the run
\rho_{x}
of
\mathcal{D}
on
x
, then:

1.

\rho_{x}
ends in state
(\mathsf{shift_{0}}(g_{x})|_{B},c_{x})
with
g_{x}(q)
being the minimal value of a run on
x
that ends in
q
, provided this run is at most
B
above the overall minimal run thus far.

2.

If
\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x)<\infty
, then
c_{x}=\texttt{fin}(q)
for the state
q
such that
g_{x}(q)+\texttt{fin}(q)=\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x)
(i.e., the state which induces the minimal accepting run, including final weight), and in addition
\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\rho)=g_{x}(q)+c_{x}
.

3.

If
\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x)=\infty
, then
c_{x}=0
and
\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\rho)=\min\{g_{x}(q)\mid q\in Q\}
.

By the above, the induction hypothesis holds for
\epsilon
. The definition of the transition relation easily shows that this carries through the induction step, as we now show.

Consider a word
x\sigma
, and assume the hypothesis above for
\rho_{x}
, which ends in
(\mathsf{shift_{0}}(g_{x})|_{B},c_{x})
. Note that we cannot directly refer to
g_{x}
, as it has been normalized and clipped at
B
. Let
(h,d)
be the state reached by
\mathcal{D}
after reading
\sigma
from
(\mathsf{shift_{0}}(g_{x})|_{B},c_{x})
.
Consider the intermediate function
g^{\prime}
defined in
\Delta^{\prime}
above. Observe that
g^{\prime}(q)
is the minimal added weight from
g(q^{\prime})
via a transition to
q
. By the premise of the theorem, states whose minimal run is at more than
B
above the minimal run, cannot yield minimal runs on any suffix. By the induction hypothesis, the states for which
g_{x}(q)=0
represent the minimal runs, and therefore all states more than
B
above are marked as
\infty
in
g_{x}
anyway. It follows that
g^{\prime}(q)
represents the minimal weight of a run that ends in
q
and might still become a minimal run.

The remaining two conditions also follow from the definition of
\Delta^{\prime}
: if there is a state with finite final weight, then
h
accumulates the final weight of the state yielding the minimal run. Otherwise
h
accumulates
0
(on top of the weight lost by shifting).
Observe that in
\Delta^{\prime}
, the transition accounts for
-c
, since if
g
already accounted for some final weight, this has to be removed (as the run did not end in
g
).
Put simply:
\mathcal{D}
tracks the correct minimal weight to any run that might still become minimal.
We then have that
(h,d)
satisfies the conditions of the induction hypothesis, which concludes the claim.

By the hypothesis, when the run ends, the accumulated weight is exactly
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x)
, so we are done.

A.2
Second Direction: Determinizability Implies a Bound

For this section, assume
\mathcal{A}=\langle Q,\Sigma,\texttt{init},\Delta,\texttt{fin}\rangle
has an equivalent (trim) deterministic WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}

\mathcal{D}=\langle Q^{\prime},\Sigma,\texttt{init}^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},\texttt{fin}^{\prime}\rangle
, we show that there exists a bound
B
as in the theorem.
Recall that we denote by
Q_{0},F
(resp.
Q^{\prime}_{0},F^{\prime}
) the initial and final states (i.e., those with finite initial and final weight) in
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{D}
respectively.

Denote by
M
the maximal absolute value of a weight appearing in either
\mathcal{A}
or
\mathcal{D}
(as a transition, initial or final weight).

We start by showing that
\mathcal{D}
must “roughly” track the same weights as the minimal run of
\mathcal{A}
on any word.

Proposition A.4
.

For every word
x\in\Sigma^{*}
, if
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to Q^{\prime})<\infty
, we have that
|\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to Q^{\prime})-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to Q)|\leq M\cdot(|Q|+1)
.

Proof.

Intuitively, there exists a short suffix from which
xz
must be accepted with finite weight (since the automata are trim), and since
\mathcal{D}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{A}
, it cannot deviate more than
|z|M
away from the value assigned by
\mathcal{A}
.

Formally, let
q\in Q
such that
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to Q)=\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to q)
, and let
z\in\Sigma^{*}
be a minimal-length word such that
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z,q\to F)<\infty
. Such a word induces a cycle-free run (otherwise it is not minimal), and
|z|<|Q|
(the strict inequality is because
z
induces a run with
|z|+1
states).

We then have that

\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(xz,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to Q^{\prime})=\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(xz,Q_{0}\to F)\leq\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z,q\to F)

Observe that
|\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z,q\to F)|\leq M\cdot|Q|
(since
|z|<|Q|
, but one more weight is incurred as the final weight).
Thus, we have
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(xz,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to Q^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to q)+M\cdot|Q|
.

On the other hand, by our choice of
q
, for every other
q^{\prime}\in Q
we have that
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to q^{\prime})\geq\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to q)
, and in particular for the state
q^{\prime}
along a minimal run of
\mathcal{A}
on
xz
we have

\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(xz,Q_{0}\to F)=\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to q^{\prime})+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z,q^{\prime}\to F)\geq\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to q)-M\cdot|Q|

so
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(xz,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to Q^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to q)-M\cdot|Q|
.

Combining the two inequalities, we have

|\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to Q^{\prime})-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to Q)|\leq M\cdot|Q|
.
The last step is to notice that since
\mathcal{D}
is deterministic, we have that

|\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to Q^{\prime})-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to Q^{\prime})|\leq M
(as they differ by at most the final weight).
By the triangle inequality, we conclude that

|\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to Q^{\prime})-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to Q)|\leq M\cdot(|Q|+1)

∎

We proceed with the proof of the existence of a bound
B
.
Assume by way of contradiction that there does not exist such a bound
B
.
That is, for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a word
x\in\Sigma^{*}
, a state
q\in Q
and
y\in\Sigma^{*}
such that

•

\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x\cdot y,Q_{0}\to F)=\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x\cdot y,Q_{0}\xrightarrow{x}q\xrightarrow{y}F)<\infty
, but

•

\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to q)-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to Q)>B
.

Let
B>(2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q^{\prime}|+(|Q|+1))\cdot 2\cdot M
be some large constant, and take
x,q,y
as above.
Let
p\in Q^{\prime}
the state of
\mathcal{D}
after reading
x
, i.e.,

\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(xy,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to F^{\prime})=\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to p)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{D}}(y,p\to F^{\prime})

(A.1)

By our choice of
q
, we have that

\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(xy,Q_{0}\to F)=\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to F)

(A.2)

Combining these equations with
Proposition

A.4
, we have the following:

\begin{split}&\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{D}}(y,p\to F^{\prime})=_{(1)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(xy,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to F^{\prime})-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to p)\\
&=_{(2)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(xy,Q_{0}\to F)-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to p)\\
&=_{(3)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to F)-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to p)\\
&\geq_{(4)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to F)-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,Q_{0}\to Q)-M(|Q|+1)\\
&>_{(5)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to F)+B-M(|Q|+1)\end{split}

(A.3)

Where

(1)

is just a rearrangement of
Eq.

A.1
.

(2)

is because
\mathcal{D}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{A}
.

(3)

is by
Eq.

A.2
.

(4)

is an application of
Proposition

A.4
.

(5)

is our assumption on
q
above (by way of contradiction).

Next, consider the set
S_{x}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(x)
of states reachable (by finite weight runs) on
x
in
\mathcal{A}
and recall that the run of
\mathcal{D}
on
x
reaches state
p
. Since
B>(2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q^{\prime}|+(|Q|+1))\cdot 2\cdot M
, it follows that
|x|>2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q^{\prime}|+(|Q|+1)>2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q^{\prime}|
. Indeed, with each letter read, the minimal and maximal runs can diverge by at most
2M
, so our assumption on the size of the gap cannot be captured with a shorter
x
.

It follows, however, that there exists
x^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{*}
with
|x|\leq 2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q^{\prime}|
such that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(x^{\prime})=S_{x}
and the run of
\mathcal{D}
on
x^{\prime}
reaches
p
. This is by e.g., removing simultaneous cycles in
\mathcal{D}
and the (Boolean) subset-construction of
\mathcal{A}
.

We can now readily reach a contradiction as follows. On the one hand we use the lower bound on
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{D}(y,p\to F^{\prime})
in
Eq.

A.3
to get

\begin{split}&\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x^{\prime}y,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to F^{\prime})=\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x^{\prime},Q^{\prime}_{0}\to p)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{D}}(y,p\to F^{\prime})\\
&\geq\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x^{\prime},Q^{\prime}_{0}\to p)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to F)+B-M(|Q|+1)\end{split}

(A.4)

On the other hand we have

\begin{split}&\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x^{\prime}y,Q^{\prime}_{0}\to F^{\prime})=_{(1)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x^{\prime}y,Q_{0}\to F)\\
&\leq_{(2)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x^{\prime},Q_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to F)\\
&\leq_{(3)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(x^{\prime},Q_{0}\to Q)+2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q^{\prime}|\cdot 2\cdot M+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to F)\\
&\leq_{(4)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(x^{\prime},Q^{\prime}_{0}\to p)+M(|Q|+1)+2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q^{\prime}|\cdot 2\cdot M+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to F)\end{split}

where

(1)

is because
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{D}
are equivalent.

(2)

is because the minimal run is at most the one that goes through
q
.

(3)

is because the run to
q
can diverge by at most
|x^{\prime}|\cdot 2\cdot M
from the minimal run.

(4)

is by
Proposition

A.4
.

Combining this inequality with
Eq.

A.4
and cancelling equal terms, we get

B\leq 2M(|Q|+1)+2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q^{\prime}|\cdot 2\cdot M

which contradicts our choice of
B
.
We conclude that there exists a bound
B
on the gap, as per the theorem statement.

Appendix B
No Need for Initial and Final Weights, nor Accepting States

In this section we show that the determinization problem for WFAs with initial and final weights (WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
) reduces to the determination problem for our model, which only has one initial state (and in particular
\texttt{init}\in\{0,\infty\}^{Q}
) and all states are accepting (i.e.,
\texttt{fin}=\{0\}^{Q}
).
The precise definition of WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
can be found in
Appendix

A
(we also rely on the results proved there, namely
Theorem

A.1
).

The intuition for getting rid of the initial and final weights is simple: we add two letters
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{f}\}
to the alphabet, to denote the start and the finish of a word, respectively. Upon reading
\mathsf{s}
and
\mathsf{f}
, the automaton incurs the weights described by the initial and final states, respectively.
This, however, still leaves us with accepting states, which turn out to pose a bigger obstacle.
Nonetheless, we show that if all states are made accepting after the aforementioned additions, the obtained WFA is still equi-determinizable to the original WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
(but they are not equivalent in their quantitative language).

Lemma B.1
.

The determinization problem for WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}
is reducible (in logarithmic space) to the determinization problem for WFAs.

We prove the lemma in the remainder of this section.
Consider a WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}

\mathcal{A}=\langle Q,\Sigma,\texttt{init},\Delta,\texttt{fin}\rangle
, and assume
\mathcal{A}
is trim (otherwise remove non-reachable states and states that cannot reach
F
).

We construct a WFA
\mathcal{B}=\langle Q\cup\{s_{0},s_{f}\},\Sigma\cup\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{f}\},s_{0},\eta\rangle
where
s_{0},s_{f}\notin Q
and
\mathsf{s},\mathsf{f}\notin\Sigma
. The transitions are defined as follows:

•

For
p,q\in Q
and
\sigma\in\Sigma
we have
(p,\sigma,c,q)\in\eta
iff
(p,\sigma,c,q)\in\Delta
.

•

For
q\in Q
we have
(s_{0},\mathsf{s},\texttt{init}(q),q)\in\eta
and
(q,\mathsf{f},\texttt{fin}(q),s_{f})\in\eta
.

•

The remaining transitions are with weight
\infty
.

This construction can clearly be implemented in logarithmic space.
We prove that
\mathcal{A}
is determinizable if and only if
\mathcal{B}
is determinizable.

Observe that by the construction of
\mathcal{B}
, we have for every word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
that

\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(w)=\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{s}\cdot w\cdot\mathsf{f})

(B.1)

B.1
If
\mathcal{B}
is Determinizable, then
\mathcal{A}
is Determinizable.

For the first (and easy) direction, assume
\mathcal{B}
is determinizable, and let
\mathcal{D}=\langle Q_{\mathcal{D}},\Sigma\cup\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{f}\},q_{0},\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}\rangle
be an equivalent deterministic trim WFA.
Note that we can assume
q_{0}
has no incoming transitions (otherwise a word with more than one
\mathsf{s}
can have finite weight, unless all words have cost
\infty
, which is a degenerate case).
Similarly, we can assume that once
\mathsf{f}
is read, a unique state
q_{f}
is reached, from which there are no outgoing transitions (indeed, no final weights can be accumulated upon leaving
q_{f}
). We can assume
q_{0}\neq q_{f}
, otherwise the only accepted word in
\mathcal{A}
is
\epsilon
, which is again degenerate (since
q_{f}
has no outgoing transitions).

Define a deterministic WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\langle Q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}},\Sigma,\texttt{init}^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}},\texttt{fin}^{\prime}\rangle
as follows. The states are
Q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}=Q_{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\{q_{0},q_{f}\}
.
Let
q\in Q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}
be the unique state such that
(q_{0},\mathsf{s},c,q)\in\Delta
with
c\neq\infty
(since
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is deterministic, and recall that
q\neq q_{0}
).
Define
\texttt{init}^{\prime}(q)=c
, and for every
q^{\prime}\neq q
we set
\texttt{init}^{\prime}(q^{\prime})=\infty
.
For the final vector, for every
q\in Q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}

set
\texttt{fin}(q)=c
where
(q,\mathsf{f},c,q_{f})\in\Delta
.
For the remaining transitions, we have that
\Delta^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}=\Delta\cap(Q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}\times\Sigma\times\mathbb{Z}_{\infty}\times Q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}})
(i.e., we keep only transitions on
Q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}
and over
\Sigma
).

We claim that
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}(w)=\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{A}}(w)
for every
w\in\Sigma^{*}
. By
Eq.

B.1
, it is enough to prove that
\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}(w)=\mathsf{wt}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{s}\cdot w\cdot\mathsf{f})
, but the latter is immediate from the construction, since reading
\mathsf{s}
is simulated by the initial weight, and reading
\mathsf{f}
by the final weights. Note that even though we made all states accepting in
\mathcal{B}
, this does not play a role here, since we only consider (in
\mathcal{B}
) words padded with
\mathsf{s}
and
\mathsf{f}
.

B.2
If
\mathcal{A}
is Determinizable, then
\mathcal{B}
is Determinizable

We now turn to the harder direction. Assume
\mathcal{A}
is determinizable and consider an equivalent deterministic WFA
{}_{\text{i-f}}

\mathcal{D}=\langle Q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}},\Sigma,\texttt{init}^{\prime},\Delta_{\mathcal{D}},\texttt{fin}^{\prime}\rangle
. Let
q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}
be the single state with
\texttt{init}^{\prime}(q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}})<\infty
, i.e., the initial state of
\mathcal{D}
, and
F_{\mathcal{D}}
the accepting states (i.e., those with finite
\texttt{fin}^{\prime}
).
Denote
Q^{\prime}=Q\cup\{s_{0},s_{f}\}
, and assume by way of contradiction that
\mathcal{B}
is not determinizable.

By the characterization in
Theorem

A.1
, we have that for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
there exist words
x,y\in(\Sigma\cup\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{f}\})^{*}
and a state
q\in Q^{\prime}
such that

•

\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{B}}(x\cdot y,s_{0}\to Q^{\prime})=\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{B}}(x\cdot y,s_{0}\xrightarrow{x}q\xrightarrow{y}Q^{\prime})<\infty
, but

•

\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,s_{0}\to q)-\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,s_{0}\to Q^{\prime})>B
.

Observe that we do not need to specify whether the expressions above refer to initial/final weights, since
\mathcal{B}
is a WFA. Moreover, we look at reachability to
Q^{\prime}
instead of
F
, since in a WFA all states are accepting.
We refer to
x,y,q
above as a
B
-gap witness for
\mathcal{B}
.

The proof follows similar lines to
Section

A.2
, with some crucial changes.

Denote by
M
be the maximal absolute value of a weight appearing in
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
or
\mathcal{D}
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
is determinizable, then by
Theorem

A.1
, it has a bound
B^{\prime}
on the gaps.

Let
B>\max\{B^{\prime}+1,2M(2|Q|+1+2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q_{\mathcal{D}}|)\}
, and let
x,y,q
be a
B
-gap witness for
\mathcal{B}
.

If there is such a witness
x,y,q
with
x=\mathsf{s}u
and
y=v\mathsf{f}
with
u,v\in\Sigma^{*}
, then
u,v,q
is also a
B
-gap witness for
\mathcal{A}
. Indeed, every run of
\mathcal{B}
on
\mathsf{s}u
corresponds to a run of
\mathcal{A}
on
u
(i.e., the runs are identical up to omitting
s_{0}
in the former), and every run of
\mathcal{B}
on
v\mathsf{f}
corresponds to a run of
\mathcal{A}
on
v
that reaches an accepting state (up to omitting
s_{f}
in the former). This, however, is a contradiction, since
B>B^{\prime}
.

Assuming the gap witness is not of the form above, we note that any
B
-gap witnesses for
\mathcal{B}
must have
x=\mathsf{s}u
for some
u\in\Sigma^{*}
. Indeed, otherwise either
x=\epsilon
or
x
gets weight
\infty
in
\mathcal{B}
. Therefore,
y\in\Sigma^{*}
(i.e., cannot contain
\mathsf{s}
or
\mathsf{f}
), otherwise
x\cdot y
would get weight
\infty
in
\mathcal{B}
.

Since
\mathcal{A}
is trim, there exists a word
z\in\Sigma^{*}
with
|z|<|Q|
such that
\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(yz,q\to F)<\infty
. Thus, we have
|\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{B}}(z\mathsf{f},Q^{\prime}\to Q^{\prime})|<|Q|M
.
Since
x,y,q
is a
B
-gap witness for
\mathcal{B}
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{B}}(xyz\mathsf{f},s_{0}\to Q^{\prime})\geq\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{B}}(xy,s_{0}\to Q^{\prime})+\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{B}}(z\mathsf{f},Q^{\prime}\to Q^{\prime})\\
&\geq\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,s_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{B}}(y,q\to Q^{\prime})-|Q|M\end{split}

By the construction of
\mathcal{B}
from
\mathcal{A}
(and recall that
x=\mathsf{s}u
), this implies that

\begin{split}&\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(uyz,Q_{0}\to F)\geq\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(uy,Q_{0}\to Q)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z,Q\to Q)\\
&\geq\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,Q_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to Q)-|Q|M\end{split}

On the other hand,

\begin{split}&\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(uyz,Q_{0}\to F)\leq\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,Q_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(yz,q\to F)\\
&\leq\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,Q_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to Q)+|Q|M\end{split}

Combining the two inequalities, we have

|\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(uyz,Q_{0}\to F)-(\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,Q_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to Q))|\leq|Q|M

(B.2)

Let
p\in Q_{\mathcal{D}}
be the state of
\mathcal{D}
after reading
u
. Thus,

\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(uyz,q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\to F_{\mathcal{D}})=\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(u,q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\to p)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}(yz,p\to F_{\mathcal{D}})

(B.3)

Combining these equations with
Proposition

A.4
, we have the following:

\begin{split}&\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{D}}(yz,p\to F_{\mathcal{D}})=_{(1)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(uyz,q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\to F_{\mathcal{D}})-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(u,q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\to p)\\
&=_{(2)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(uyz,Q_{0}\to F)-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(u,q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\to p)\\
&\geq_{(3)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,Q_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to Q)-|Q|M-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(u,q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\to p)\\
&\geq_{(4)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,Q_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to Q)-|Q|M-\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,Q_{0}\to Q)-M(|Q|+1)\\
&>_{(5)}\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to Q)-|Q|M+B-M(|Q|+1)\\
&=_{(6)}\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to Q)+B-M(2|Q|+1)\end{split}

(B.4)

Where

(1)

is just a rearrangement of
Eq.

B.3
.

(2)

is because
\mathcal{D}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{A}
.

(3)

is by
Eq.

B.2
as a lower bound.

(4)

is an application of
Proposition

A.4
.

(5)

is because
(x,y,q)
is a
B
-gap witness for
\mathcal{B}
, and
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,Q_{0}\to q)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,s_{0}\to q)
and
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,Q_{0}\to Q)=\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,s_{0}\to Q^{\prime})
.

(6)

is just rearranging.

Next, consider the set
S_{u}=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(u)
of states reachable (by finite weight runs) on
u
in
\mathcal{A}
and recall that the run of
\mathcal{D}
on
u
reaches state
p
.
Since
B>2M(2|Q|+1+2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q_{\mathcal{D}}|)
,
it follows in particular that
|u|>2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q_{\mathcal{D}}|
. Indeed, with each letter read, the minimal and maximal runs can diverge by at most
2M
, so our assumption on the size of the gap cannot be captured with a shorter
u
.

It follows, however, that there exists
u^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{*}
with
|u|\leq 2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q_{\mathcal{D}}|
such that
\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(u^{\prime})=S_{u}
and the run of
\mathcal{D}
on
u^{\prime}
reaches
p
. This is by e.g., removing simultaneous cycles in
\mathcal{D}
and the (Boolean) subset-construction of
\mathcal{A}
.

We can now reach a contradiction as follows. On the one hand we use the lower bound on
\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{D}(yz,p\to F_{\mathcal{D}})
in
Eq.

B.4
to get

\begin{split}&\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(u^{\prime}yz,Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\to F_{\mathcal{D}})=\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(u^{\prime},q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\to p)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{D}}(yz,p\to F_{\mathcal{D}})\\
&\geq\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(u^{\prime},q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\to p)+\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to Q)+B-M(2|Q|+1)\end{split}

(B.5)

On the other hand we have

\begin{split}&\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{D}}(u^{\prime}yz,q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\to F_{\mathcal{D}})=_{(1)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{if}}_{\mathcal{A}}(u^{\prime}yz,Q_{0}\to F)\\
&\leq_{(2)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(u^{\prime},Q_{0}\to q)+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(yz,q\to F)\\
&\leq_{(3)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{A}}(u^{\prime},Q_{0}\to Q)+2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q_{\mathcal{D}}|\cdot 2\cdot M+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(yz,q\to F)\\
&\leq_{(4)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(u^{\prime},q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\to p)+M(|Q|+1)+2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q_{\mathcal{D}}|\cdot 2\cdot M+\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{f}}_{\mathcal{A}}(yz,q\to F)\\
&\leq_{(5)}\mathsf{m}\mathsf{wt}^{+\mathsf{i}}_{\mathcal{D}}(u^{\prime},q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\to p)+M(|Q|+1)+2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q_{\mathcal{D}}|\cdot 2\cdot M+\mathsf{mwt}_{\mathcal{A}}(y,q\to F)+M|Q|\end{split}

(B.6)

where

(1)

is because
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{D}
are equivalent.

(2)

is because the minimal run is at most the one that goes through
q
.

(3)

is because the run to
q
can diverge by at most
|u^{\prime}|\cdot 2\cdot M
from the minimal run.

(4)

is by
Proposition

A.4
.

(5)

is because
|z|<|Q|
.

Combining
Eqs.

B.5
and
B.6
and cancelling equal terms, we get

B-M(2|Q|+1)\leq M(|Q|+1)+2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q_{\mathcal{D}}|\cdot 2\cdot M+M|Q|

which simplifies to

B\leq 2M(2|Q|+1+2^{|Q|}\cdot|Q_{\mathcal{D}}|)

and this contradicts our choice of
B
.
We conclude that
\mathcal{B}
is determinizable (as this was our assumption by way of contradiction).

Appendix C
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
is Determinizable if and only if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is Determinizable

In this section we prove
Theorem

12.25
.

We start with the trivial direction: if
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
is determinizable, then let
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}^{\infty}
be an equivalent deterministic WFA. We restrict
\mathcal{D}
to the alphabet
\Gamma
and obtain a deterministic WFA that is equivalent to
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
. Thus,
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is determinizable.

The converse is the involved part. Assume
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
is not determinizable. By
Theorems

8.4
and
A.3
we have that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}^{\infty}
has unbounded gaps. More precisely, for every
B\in\mathbb{N}
there exist words
x,y\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty})^{*}
and a state
s\in S
such that
\mathsf{mwt}(x\cdot y,s_{0}\to S)=\mathsf{mwt}(xy,s_{0}\xrightarrow{x}s\xrightarrow{y}S)
(and is finite), but
\mathsf{mwt}(x,s_{0}\to s)-\mathsf{mwt}(x,s_{0}\to S)>B
(where the latter difference can be infinite or finite, but
\mathsf{mwt}(x,s_{0}\to S)
is clearly finite due to the run on
xy
).

We claim that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
also has unbounded gaps. We start by showing this for
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
with jump letters, and proceed to remove jump letters afterwards.

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
with Jump Letters is Not Determinizable

To this end, we need to show the property above holds already for words over
\Gamma^{*}
(with jump letters).
Naturally, we obtain such words using the
flattening
operation (
Definition

12.23
).

Consider
B\in\mathbb{N}
and let
x,y\in(\Gamma_{\infty}^{\infty})^{*}
as above.
Recall that for a word
u
the value
{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u)}
is an upper-bound on the absolute value of the change in weight that a run can incur upon reading
u
.
In the following we show that
\mathsf{flatten}(xy\wr 2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)})
can be split to two words
x^{\prime}\cdot y^{\prime}
such that the gap condition above holds.
To show this, we proceed by induction over the sequence of operations induced by the flattening inductive definition of
Definition

12.23
.

Base case:

For the base case we already have
xy\in\Gamma^{*}
, so we are done.

Cactus case:

For the cactus case, assume
xy=u\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}v
, and let
\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{xy}S
be a seamless run such that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\mathsf{mwt}(xy,s_{0}\to S)
.
Let
z=\mathsf{unfold}(u,\alpha_{S^{\prime},w},v|2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(u\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}v)})
.
By
Proposition

12.17
, there is a seamless run
\rho^{\prime}:s_{0}\xrightarrow{z}S
such that
for every prefix
h
of
xy
there is a corresponding prefix
z^{\prime}
of
z
such that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime}(z^{\prime}))=\mathsf{wt}(\rho(h))
, and moreover
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime})=\mathsf{wt}(\rho)
.
In particular,
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime})=\mathsf{mwt}(z,s_{0}\to S)
. Indeed, otherwise by
Proposition

12.18
, there is a seamless run of lower weight also on
xy
, which contradicts the minimality of
\rho
.

Consider
\rho(x)
, then by
Proposition

12.17
there is a prefix
z^{\prime}
of
z
that corresponds to
x
.
Since
\mathsf{mwt}(x,s_{0}\to S)<\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x))-B
, then again by
Proposition

12.17
there exists a seamless run
\mu:s_{0}\xrightarrow{z^{\prime}}S
with

\mathsf{wt}(\mu)=\mathsf{mwt}(x,s_{0}\to S)<\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x))-B=\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime}(z^{\prime}))-B

which is exactly the gap constraint we wished to show (by replacing
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime}(z^{\prime}))
with
\mathsf{mwt}(z^{\prime},s_{0}\to s)
for the last state of
\rho^{\prime}(z^{\prime})
).

Rebase case:

In this case, assume
xy=u\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r}v
and let
z=\mathsf{rebase\_rm}(u,\beta_{S^{\prime},w,s\to r},v)
.

By
Corollary

12.22
, the gaps between any two seamless runs on
z
after reading each prefix is the same as the gap between the corresponding seamless runs on
xy
. Thus, we obtain a gap of more than
B
after the prefix that corresponds to
x
.

To spell out this argument further, recall that
z=u\mathsf{jump}_{t_{1}\to t_{p}}\alpha_{S^{\prime},w}\mathsf{jump}_{t_{p}\to t_{2}}v
as per
Definition

12.20
.
Consider the seamless run
\rho:s_{0}\xrightarrow{xy}S
such that
\mathsf{wt}(\rho)=\mathsf{mwt}(xy,s_{0}\to S)
then the run

\rho^{\prime}
obtained from
\rho
as per
Propositions

12.21
and
12.22
satisfies
\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime})=\mathsf{mwt}(z,s_{0}\to S)
. Indeed, if there is a run with smaller weight, then the reverse direction of
Corollary

12.22
would yield a run with lower weight than
\rho
on
xy
.
Similarly, consider the minimal seamless run
\mu^{\prime}
on the prefix
x^{\prime}
of
z
that corresponds to
x
(which by the same argument corresponds to a minimal seamless
\mu
run on
x
).

Corollary

12.22
now guarantees that there exists a constant
c
such that

\mathsf{wt}(\rho^{\prime}(x^{\prime}))-\mathsf{wt}(\mu^{\prime}(x^{\prime}))=\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x))+c-(\mathsf{wt}(\mu(x))+c)=\mathsf{wt}(\rho(x))-\mathsf{wt}(\mu(x))>B

where the last inequality is because both
\rho
and
\mu
are seamless and minimal. We thus conclude the inductive claim.

We conclude that
\mathsf{flatten}(xy\wr 2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)})
also satisfies the gap constraints, and since
\mathsf{flatten}(xy\wr 2{\mathsf{max\_eff}(xy)})
is over the alphabet
\Gamma^{*}
with jump letters, we conclude by
Theorem

8.4
that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
with jump letters is not determinizable.

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
without Jump Letters is Not Determinizable

Having showed that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
with jump letters is not determinizable, we now turn to remove jump letters.
Conceptually, this is simple: jump letters do not change the weight of any run, and do not introduce nondeterminism. Thus, they can simply be cut from a run. Indeed, their only purpose is for reasoning about baseline runs.

Technically, the problem that arises from jump letters is that the baseline component might not form a legal run of
\mathcal{A}
, thus preventing us from simply dropping those letters. This is illustrated in
Fig.

31
.

Figure 31
:
The jump transition from
(q_{2},p_{2},T_{2})
to
(q_{2},p^{\prime}_{2},T_{2})
enables the next transition on
t_{2}
, which can only be taken from the baseline component
p^{\prime}_{2}
. Thus, simply removing the jump letter (and continuing from
(q_{2},p_{2},T_{2})
) would not yield a legal run.

We therefore first shift the baseline run (akin to
Section

9.1
) so that all jump transitions do not actually change the baseline and can therefore be dropped.
As in the case of flattening, we treat jump letters inductively, one by one, and show that the gap property is maintained upon each removal.

Consider a word
x\cdot\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p,T)\to(\cdot,p^{\prime},T)}\cdot y
where
x
has no jump letters, and denote
x=(p_{0},\sigma_{1},c_{1},p_{1})\cdots(p_{k-1},\sigma_{k},c_{k},p_{k})
(as the letters in
\Gamma
are transitions in
\mathcal{A}
).

Since
p^{\prime}\in T
(otherwise the jump letter is not defined, c.f.,
Definition

11.15
) and
T=\delta_{\mathbb{B}}(q_{0},\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{k})
(in the original WFA
\mathcal{A}
), it follows that there exists a seamless run
\rho_{x}:q_{0}\xrightarrow{\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{k}}p^{\prime}
.
Denote
\rho_{x}=(p^{\prime}_{0},\sigma_{1},e_{1},p^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,(p^{\prime}_{k-1},\sigma_{k},e_{k},p^{\prime}_{k})
with
p^{\prime}_{k}=p^{\prime}
.
We now think of
\rho_{x}
as a word over
\Gamma
. We show that if
x\cdot\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p,T)\to(\cdot,p^{\prime},T)}\cdot y
is a
B
-gap witness for
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
with jump letters, then
\rho_{x}\cdot y
is also a
B
-gap witness (and note that
\rho_{x}\cdot y
has one fewer jump letter, so we can proceed inductively).

Consider a run
\mu
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on
x\cdot\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p,T)\to(\cdot,p^{\prime},T)}\cdot y
, then we can write
\mu=t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{k},t^{\prime},r_{1},\ldots,r_{|y|}
where for
1\leq i\leq k
we have

t_{i}=((q_{i-1},p_{i-1},T_{i-1}),(p_{i-1},\sigma_{i},c_{i},p_{i}),f_{i},(q_{i},p_{i},T_{i}))
and
t^{\prime}=((q_{k},p,T),\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p,T)\to(\cdot,p^{\prime},T)},(q_{k},p^{\prime},T)
(indeed, by the fact that the jump letter has defined transitions, we have that
p_{k}=p
). We leave
r_{1},\ldots,r_{|y|}
without particular notation.

We obtain from
\mu
the run
\eta=t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{k},r_{1},\ldots,r_{|y|}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on
\rho_{x}\cdot y
where for every
1\leq i\leq k
we have

t_{i}=((q_{i-1},p^{\prime}_{i-1},T_{i-1}),(p^{\prime}_{i-1},\sigma_{i},e_{i},p^{\prime}_{i}),d_{i},(q_{i},p^{\prime}_{i},T_{i}))
, where
d_{i}
is determined by the transitions in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
(we elaborate on
d_{i}
below).

Note that
\eta
is indeed a legal run on
\rho_{x}\cdot y
, since the last state reached by
\eta
after reading
\rho_{x}
is
(q_{k},p^{\prime},T)
, which is the same state reached by
\rho
after reading
x\cdot\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p,T)\to(\cdot,p^{\prime},T)}
.

Consider now the weight
d_{i}
of the transition above. By the definition of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
in
Section

9
, we have that
d_{i}=d^{\prime}_{i}-e_{i}
, where
d^{\prime}_{i}
is the weight of the unique transition
(q_{i-1},\sigma_{i},d^{\prime}_{i},q_{i})
. By the same token, we also have
f_{i}=c_{i}-d^{\prime}_{i}
in the transitions of
\mu
. It follows that
f_{i}-d_{i}=c_{i}-e_{i}
. In particular, the difference
f_{i}-d_{i}
does not depend on the first component of the states visited by
\mu
and
\eta
, and depends only on
\rho_{x}
and on
\mu
.

Moreover, note that the correspondence between
\mu
and
\eta
is bijective. Indeed, given a run
\eta
on
\rho_{x}\cdot y
, we can obtain from it a run
\mu
on
x\cdot\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p,T)\to(\cdot,p^{\prime},T)}\cdot y
by replacing the baseline component to that dictated by
x
, and using
\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p,T)\to(\cdot,p^{\prime},T)}
to connect the run to
y
(essentially retracing our construction bottom-up).

Therefore, we fix
\rho_{x}
and get that this bijection between runs also shifts the weight of the runs by the same constants
f_{i}-d_{i}
. That is, for two runs
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
on
x\cdot\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p,T)\to(\cdot,p^{\prime},T)}\cdot y
and their corresponding
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
, for every prefix
z
of
x
we have
\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{1}(z))-\mathsf{wt}(\mu_{1}(z))=\mathsf{wt}(\eta_{1}(z))-\mathsf{wt}(\eta_{2}(z))
.

In particular, this result applies to prefixes of
x\cdot\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p,T)\to(\cdot,p^{\prime},T)}\cdot y
, and it follows that if
x\cdot\mathsf{jump}_{(\cdot,p,T)\to(\cdot,p^{\prime},T)}\cdot y
is a
B
-gap witness, then the gaps are maintained also for
\rho_{x}\cdot y
, and thus
\rho_{x}\cdot y
is also a
B
-gap witness.

We can thus proceed by induction until no jump letters are used, and conclude that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is not determinizable.

Appendix D
PSPACE-Hardness of Determinizability

We prove that the determinizability problem is PSPACE-hard by reduction from the universality problem for NFAs.

Intuitively, given an NFA
\mathcal{A}
, we construct a WFA
\mathcal{B}
by giving weight
0
to all the transitions of
\mathcal{A}
, adding a new letter
\#
, and connecting all the non-accepting states of
\mathcal{A}
with
\#
to a WFA component that is not determinizable, and gains weight above
0
. From accepting states we connect
\#
to a
0
-sink.

Formally, given an NFA
\mathcal{A}=\langle Q,\Sigma,\Delta,Q_{0},F\rangle
(where
\Delta\subseteq Q\times\Sigma\times Q
is a non-weighted transition relation),
we define a WFA
\mathcal{B}=\{Q\cup\{q_{a},q_{b},q_{\top}\},\Sigma\cup\{\#,a,b\},\Delta^{\prime},Q_{0},\{q_{a},q_{b},q_{\top}\}\}
with
q_{a},q_{b}
being fresh states and
\#,a,b
fresh letters, and the following transitions:

\begin{split}&\Delta^{\prime}=\{(p,\sigma,0,q)\mid(p,\sigma,q)\in\Delta\}\cup\\
&\{(q,\#,0,q_{a}),(q,\#,q_{b})\mid q\in Q\setminus F\}\cup\\
&\{(q,\#,0,q_{\top})\mid q\in F\}\cup\\
&\{(q_{a},a,0,q_{a}),(q_{a},b,1,q_{a}),(q_{b},a,1,q_{b}),(q_{b},b,0,q_{b}),(q_{\top},a,0,q_{\top}),(q_{\top},b,0,q_{\top})\}\end{split}

It is now easy to prove that
L(\mathcal{A})=\Sigma^{*}
if and only if
\mathcal{B}
is determinizable.
For the first direction, if
L(\mathcal{A})=\Sigma^{*}
, then for every
w\in\Sigma^{*}\cdot\#\cdot\{a+b\}^{*}
we have
\mathcal{B}(w)=0
, since there is a run on the
\Sigma^{*}
prefix that ends in an accepting state, which proceeds to gain weight
0
in the
q_{\top}
component.
Words in
\Sigma^{*}
get weight
0
by construction, and all other words do not have finite weight. Thus, every word gets weight either
0
or
\infty
, and in particular has bounded gaps, and by
Theorem

8.4
is determinizable.

Conversely, if
L(\mathcal{A})\neq\Sigma^{*}
, then let
w\in\Sigma^{*}
be a non-accepted word. It follows that after reading
w
we arrive at the
q_{a},q_{b}
component. This component is not determinizable (which can either be seen by the gaps characterization, or directly from
[
7
]
). It is easy to see that the same proof carries also with the
w
prefix, so
\mathcal{B}
is not determinizable.
∎
